\title{\Huge\bfseries $q$-Series and Inverse $q$-Analogs\\[8pt]
\Large A Proof-Oriented Synthesis}
\author{Human-readable research synthesis for\\
\small Vladimir Reshetnikov's \texttt{ProveIt} repository}
\date{Consolidated repository edition: 31 August 2026}

\begin{document}
\frontmatter
\hypersetup{pageanchor=false}
\maketitle
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
The finite and infinite $q$-Pochhammer symbols and the Gaussian, or
$q$-binomial, coefficients form a small set of notational primitives from
which a remarkable amount of mathematics can be built.  They simultaneously
encode weighted subsets, partitions, lattice paths, finite-field geometry,
$q$-difference operators, basic hypergeometric summations, theta products,
cyclotomic arithmetic, geometric interpolation, and the Fabius--Rvachev
functions.

This volume gives one proof-oriented account of both the forward theory and
its branch-aware inverse problems.  The forward parts develop the algebraic,
combinatorial, analytic, arithmetic, and asymptotic structure of $q$-series;
the inverse part develops regular and ramified reversion, Bell-polynomial and
Lagrange--Good coefficient engines, Lambert-$W$ asymptotic transfer,
finite- and infinite-product branch atlases, $q$-special-function inversion,
certified numerical enclosures, and parameter recovery from Fabius--Rvachev
observables.  Repeated source results are stated once with the strongest
proved hypotheses.  Every retained theorem, lemma, proposition, and corollary
has a human-readable proof; genuinely open claims occur only in explicitly
labelled conjecture environments.  The appendices distinguish exact Lean
counterparts, partial formal infrastructure, human-proved frontier results,
source records, and conjectures.

\chapter*{Preface and source map}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface and source map}
The aim is not merely to list identities, but to expose the mechanisms that repeatedly generate them.  Three such mechanisms dominate:
\begin{enumerate}
\item finite products split into residue classes, blocks, or initial and tail factors;
\item coefficient extraction turns product factorizations into convolution identities;
\item functional equations determine analytic $q$-series from one initial value.
\end{enumerate}
Once these mechanisms are visible, many formulas that initially appear unrelated become instances of the same principle.

The supplied archive was used throughout.  In particular, the weighted subset framework of Konvalina motivates \Cref{chap:weighted}; Kupershmidt's treatment of the $q$-Newton binomial informs \Cref{chap:qcalculus,chap:qnewton}; Straub's seminar on $q$-binomial congruences is reflected in \Cref{chap:cyclotomic}; the formalized $q$-series project of Lau, Lee, and Ono motivates the algebraic-versus-analytic distinctions in \Cref{chap:formalization} and supplies a modern Bailey-pair route to the Rogers--Ramanujan identities; and the central $q$-binomial paper of Chern, Dilcher, and Jiu is developed, with a complete proof, in \Cref{chap:central}.  The archive's reference chapters and encyclopedia snapshots were checked against the standard sources listed in the bibliography.  The current \emph{ProveIt} Fabius-function documentation~\cite{ReshetnikovProveIt2026}, including its main exposition and non-formalized research-frontier drafts, supplies the geometric-node interpolation rows, exact residual moments, half-base identities, formal filters, and dyadic Thue--Morse connections expanded independently in \Cref{chap:geometric-filters,chap:halfbase-thuemorse,chap:geometric-uniform-fabius}.  Online research also led to the recent automated identity-translation framework of Zhong and Zhao~\cite{ZhongZhao2026} and the Isabelle formalization of combinatorial $q$-analogues by Eberl~\cite{Eberl2024QAnalogues}.  Additional material was drawn from the classical books and articles of Andrews, Askey, Bailey, Gasper, Rahman, Rota, Goldman, Reiner, Stanton, White, and others, as well as the NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions.

\section*{Two regimes for $q$}
Throughout the finite algebraic chapters, $q$ may be an indeterminate and identities are understood in a polynomial or rational-function ring.  In analytic chapters, unless stated otherwise,
\[
  q\in\ComplexNumbers,\qquad 0<\AbsoluteValue q<1.
\]
For the Jackson integral and positivity statements we usually assume $0<q<1$.
In the finite-geometry sections only, a capital $Q$ denotes the cardinality of a
finite field, so there $Q$ is a prime power and $Q>1$.  Every other use of $Q$
is redeclared in its own local scope (for example, as a complementary nome or a
theta-series base) and does not inherit the finite-field meaning.

\section*{Conventions}
Empty products equal $1$, empty sums equal $0$, and
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=0\qquad\text{when }k<0\text{ or }k>n
\]
for nonnegative integral $n$.  Multiple parameters are compressed as
\[
 (a_1,\ldots,a_r;q)_n=\prod_{j=1}^r(a_j;q)_n.
\]
The notation $(a;q)_\infty$ is analytic when $\AbsoluteValue q<1$; when formal power series are intended, that will be said explicitly.

\tableofcontents

\mainmatter

\part{The finite algebra}

\chapter{The algebra of \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-shifted factorials}\label{chap:pochhammer}
\index{$q$-Pochhammer symbol|see{$q$-shifted factorial}}
\index{$q$-shifted factorial}

\section{Definitions and first examples}

\begin{definition}\label{qg:def-qshifted-factorial}
For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ the \emph{$q$-shifted factorial}, or \emph{$q$-Pochhammer symbol}, is
\[
 (a;q)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^j),\qquad (a;q)_0=1.
\]
For parameters $a_1,\dots,a_r$ we write
\[
 (a_1,\dots,a_r;q)_n=\prod_{i=1}^r(a_i;q)_n.
\]
When $0<\AbsoluteValue q<1$, the infinite symbol is
\[
 (a;q)_\infty=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(1-aq^j).
\]
\end{definition}

The word ``shifted'' refers to the geometric progression $a,aq,aq^2,\ldots$.  The first few values are
\[
 (a;q)_1=1-a,
 \qquad
 (a;q)_2=(1-a)(1-aq),
 \qquad
 (a;q)_3=(1-a)(1-aq)(1-aq^2).
\]
Important specializations include
\[
 (q;q)_n=\prod_{j=1}^n(1-q^j),
 \qquad
 (-q;q)_n=\prod_{j=1}^n(1+q^j),
 \qquad
 (a;q^r)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{rj}).
\]

\section{Concatenation, dissection, and reversal}

\begin{proposition}[Concatenation law]\label{prop:concat}
For $m,n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\[
 (a;q)_{m+n}=(a;q)_m(aq^m;q)_n.
\]
Consequently, whenever the denominator is nonzero,
\[
 \frac{(a;q)_{m+n}}{(a;q)_m}=(aq^m;q)_n.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Split the defining product at $j=m$:
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{m+n-1}(1-aq^j)
 =\left(\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(1-aq^j)\right)
  \left(\prod_{j=m}^{m+n-1}(1-aq^j)\right).
\]
In the second product put $j=m+r$, where $0\le r<n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Dissection into residue classes]\label{prop:dissection}
For integers $r,n\ge0$,
\[
 (a;q)_{rn}=\prod_{s=0}^{r-1}(aq^s;q^r)_n.
\]
If $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and $r\ge1$, then also
\[
 (a;q)_\infty=\prod_{s=0}^{r-1}(aq^s;q^r)_\infty.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every integer $j$ with $0\le j<rn$ has a unique representation $j=s+rt$ with $0\le s<r$ and $0\le t<n$.  Reordering the finite product gives the first identity; when $r=0$ or $n=0$ both sides are empty products.  Equivalently, one may induct on $n$: passing from $rn$ to $r(n+1)$ appends one full period $(aq^{rn};q)_r=\prod_{s<r}\bigl(1-aq^{s}(q^r)^{n}\bigr)$, whose $r$ factors are exactly the next factors of the $r$ residue-class products.  This is the argument used by the formalization, which is valid over every commutative ring.

For the infinite identity, $(a;q)_{rn}\to(a;q)_\infty$ along the multiples of $r$ by \Cref{thm:poch-entire}, while $(aq^s;q^r)_n\to(aq^s;q^r)_\infty$ for each residue $s$ because $\AbsoluteValue{q^r}<1$; the finite dissection therefore passes to the limit.  (Alternatively, the factors are absolutely summably close to $1$ because $\sum_{j\ge0}\AbsoluteValue{aq^j}<\infty$, so the product may be regrouped into the $r$ residue classes.)
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Dissection with a remainder]\label{cor:dissection-remainder}
\index{dissection!with a remainder}
Let $r\ge1$ and $n\ge0$ be integers, let $0\le u\le r$, and set $N=rn+u$.  Then
\[
 (a;q)_N
 =\prod_{s=0}^{u-1}(aq^s;q^r)_{n+1}
  \prod_{s=u}^{r-1}(aq^s;q^r)_n .
\]
For $u=0$ this is \Cref{prop:dissection}, and for $u=r$ it is \Cref{prop:dissection} at length $n+1$ (the second product being empty).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Division with remainder by $r$ is a bijection between the nonnegative integers $j$ and the pairs $(s,\ell)$ with $0\le s<r$ and $\ell\ge0$, given by $j=s+r\ell$.  Fix a residue $s$ and count the $\ell$ for which $j=s+r\ell$ lies in the exponent set $\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$ of the defining product.  The condition $s+r\ell<rn+u$ reads
\[
 r\ell<rn+(u-s).
\]

If $s<u$, then $0<u-s\le u<r$, so
\[
 rn<rn+(u-s)\le rn+r=r(n+1).
\]
As $r\ell$ runs through the multiples of $r$, the inequality $r\ell<rn+(u-s)$ therefore holds exactly for $\ell=0,1,\ldots,n$, which is $n+1$ values.

If $s\ge u$, then $u-s\le0$, so the inequality implies $r\ell<rn$, that is $\ell\le n-1$; conversely every such $\ell$ satisfies $s+r\ell\le s+r(n-1)=rn+(s-r)<rn\le N$.  Hence $\ell$ ranges over $0,\ldots,n-1$, which is $n$ values.

The defining product is finite and its factors commute, so it may be reordered according to the residue of $j$ modulo $r$:
\[
 (a;q)_N
 =\prod_{s=0}^{u-1}\ \prod_{\ell=0}^{n}\bigl(1-aq^{s}(q^{r})^{\ell}\bigr)\;
  \prod_{s=u}^{r-1}\ \prod_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1-aq^{s}(q^{r})^{\ell}\bigr).
\]
Each inner product is by definition a $q^r$-shifted factorial with argument $aq^s$, namely $(aq^s;q^r)_{n+1}$ in the first family and $(aq^s;q^r)_n$ in the second.  This is the asserted identity.  When $u=0$ the first family is empty and the second has all $r$ residues, recovering \Cref{prop:dissection}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:dissection-remainder-checks}
Two consistency checks are worth recording.  The total number of linear factors on the right is
\[
 u(n+1)+(r-u)n=rn+u=N,
\]
as it must be, since $(a;q)_N$ is a product of $N$ linear factors in $a$.  At the other extreme $n=0$ the corollary reads $(a;q)_u=\prod_{s=0}^{u-1}(aq^s;q^r)_1=\prod_{s=0}^{u-1}(1-aq^s)$, again the definition.  Combining the corollary with \Cref{prop:concat} in the form $(a;q)_{rn+u}=(a;q)_{rn}(aq^{rn};q)_u$ and dividing by \Cref{prop:dissection} recovers the identity $\prod_{s=0}^{u-1}(1-aq^{s+rn})=(aq^{rn};q)_u$, so the two decompositions agree.
\end{remark}

The finite clauses are exactly
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_dissection} and
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_dissection_remainder} in
\path{QPochhammerDissection.lean}.  Both hold over every commutative ring; the
first allows all natural $r,n$, and the second assumes only $u\le r$, hence is
stronger at the harmless endpoint $u=r$.  The infinite clause of
\Cref{prop:dissection} is exactly
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_dissection} in
\path{QPochhammerInfinite.lean}: it holds in every complete normed
commutative ring with $\lVert1\rVert=1$, under $\lVert q\rVert<1$ and
$0<r$.  Thus the two labelled rows are Lean-proved; this promotion does not
extend to any neighboring complex-order or logarithmic-derivative result.

\begin{proposition}[Base reversal]\label{prop:base-reversal}
For $n\ge0$ and nonzero $a,q$ in a field,
\[
 (a;q)_n=(-a)^n q^{\binom n2}(a^{-1};q^{-1})_n.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 (a;q^{-1})_n=(-a)^nq^{-\binom n2}(a^{-1};q)_n.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Factor $-aq^j$ from each term:
\[
 1-aq^j=-aq^j\bigl(1-a^{-1}q^{-j}\bigr).
\]
Multiplying for $0\le j<n$ gives the factor
\(
 (-a)^nq^{0+1+\cdots+(n-1)}=(-a)^nq^{\binom n2}.
\)
The remaining product is $(a^{-1};q^{-1})_n$.  The assumptions
$a\ne0$ and $q\ne0$ are exactly what makes the two displayed inverses
legitimate; equivalently one may formulate the same proof for unit parameters
over a commutative ring.
\end{proof}

\section{Negative indices}

The concatenation law suggests the only extension to negative integers that preserves
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{m+n}
 =\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{m}
  \GeneralizedQPochhammer{aq^m}{q}{n}.
\]

\begin{definition}\label{qg:def-negative-qshifted-index}
For $n\ge1$, define
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{-n}
 =\frac{1}{\QPochhammer{aq^{-n}}{q}{n}},
\]
provided no factor in the denominator vanishes.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Closed form for a negative index]\label{prop:negative-index}
For $n\ge1$ and $a\ne0$,
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{-n}
 =\frac{(-1)^n a^{-n}q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}
        {\QPochhammer{q/a}{q}{n}}.
\]
Moreover, for all integers $m,n$ for which both sides are defined,
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{m+n}
 =\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{m}
  \GeneralizedQPochhammer{aq^m}{q}{n}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By definition,
\[
 (aq^{-n};q)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{-n+j}).
\]
Factor $-a q^{-n+j}$ from each factor.  Their product is
\[
 (-a)^nq^{-n^2+\binom n2}=(-a)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}},
\]
and the remaining product is
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-a^{-1}q^{n-j})=(q/a;q)_n.
\]
Taking the reciprocal proves the formula.  The extended concatenation identity follows by cancelling the overlapping finite factors; the cases in which one of $m,n,m+n$ is negative reduce directly to the definition.
\end{proof}

% ed.: remark added to record the generality of the formalization.
\begin{remark}[Signed interval products]\label{rem:negative-index-formalization}
The formalization organizes all integer indices around the signed interval
products $I(u,v)=\prod_{u\le j<v}(1-aq^j)$ for $u\le v$ and
$I(v,u)=I(u,v)^{-1}$, so that $(a;q)_n=I(0,n)$ and $(aq^m;q)_n=I(m,m+n)$
for all $m,n\in\IntegerNumbers$.  The single \emph{cocycle identity}
$I(u,w)=I(u,v)\,I(v,w)$ then holds for all integers $u,v,w$ as soon as the
two factors on the right are nonzero (unconditionally when $u\le v\le w$),
and it contains the extended concatenation law, the shift
$(a;q)_{n+1}=(a;q)_n(1-aq^n)$ for every $n\in\IntegerNumbers$, and the
closed form above.  Nonvanishing of the two factors is exactly the
``both sides defined'' proviso of the proposition.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[A terminating factor]\label{cor:qminusN}
Let $q$ be a nonzero element of a field.  If $0\le k\le N$ and
$(q;q)_{N-k}\ne0$, then
\[
 (q^{-N};q)_k=(-1)^kq^{-Nk+\binom k2}
 \frac{(q;q)_N}{(q;q)_{N-k}}.
\]
Independently of that denominator condition, $(q^{-N};q)_k=0$ for $k>N$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The denominator-cleared identity
\[
 q^{Nk}(q^{-N};q)_k
 =(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}(q^{N-k+1};q)_k
\]
holds for $k\le N$ once $q\ne0$.  Concatenation gives
\[
 (q;q)_N=(q;q)_{N-k}(q^{N-k+1};q)_k.
\]
Division by the nonzero product $q^{Nk}(q;q)_{N-k}$ gives the displayed
quotient.  For $k>N$, the defining product contains its index-$N$ factor
$1-q^{-N}q^N=0$; here only $q\ne0$ is needed.
\end{proof}

\section{Partial fractions and logarithmic derivatives}

\begin{theorem}[Finite partial-fraction decomposition]\label{thm:partial-fraction}
Assume $q^i\ne q^j$ for $0\le i<j\le n$.  Then
\[
 \frac{1}{(z;q)_{n+1}}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}
 \frac{(-1)^j q^{\binom{j+1}{2}}}
 {(q;q)_j(q;q)_{n-j}}\,
 \frac{1}{1-zq^j}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $q=0$, pairwise distinctness of $q^0,\ldots,q^n$ forces $n\le1$, and the
formula is immediate in the two possible cases.  Assume henceforth that
$q\ne0$.  The poles of the left-hand side are the distinct points
$z=q^{-j}$, $0\le j\le n$, and the coefficient of $(1-zq^j)^{-1}$ in its
partial-fraction expansion is
\[
 A_j=\left.\frac{1-zq^j}{(z;q)_{n+1}}\right|_{z=q^{-j}}
 =\frac{1}{\prod_{r\ne j}(1-q^{r-j})}.
\]
For $r<j$, put $s=j-r$ and obtain
\[
 \prod_{r=0}^{j-1}(1-q^{r-j})
 =\prod_{s=1}^{j}(1-q^{-s})
 =(-1)^jq^{-\binom{j+1}{2}}(q;q)_j.
\]
The factors with $r>j$ multiply to $(q;q)_{n-j}$.  Thus $A_j$ is the
displayed coefficient.  After subtracting the proposed right-hand side, all
finite poles are removable.  The difference is therefore a polynomial; it is
$O(z^{-1})$ at infinity, so it is identically zero.
\end{proof}

% ed.: remark added to record the division-free form proved in Lean.
\begin{remark}[The polynomial form]\label{rem:partial-fraction-formalization}
Clearing denominators, the theorem is the identity
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}
 \frac{(-1)^jq^{\binom{j+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_j(q;q)_{n-j}}
 \prod_{\substack{0\le i\le n\\ i\ne j}}(1-zq^i)=1
\]
of polynomials in $z$, which is what the formalization proves, by Lagrange
interpolation at the $n+1$ nodes $z=q^{-j}$: both sides have degree at most
$n$, and at $z=q^{-j}$ only the $j$th summand survives, with value
$A_j\prod_{r\ne j}(1-q^{r-j})=1$.  The only hypothesis needed is
$(q;q)_n\ne0$, that is $q^m\ne1$ for $1\le m\le n$; for $q\ne0$ this is the
displayed distinctness of $1,q,\ldots,q^n$, and for $q=0$ it holds
automatically (the sum then reduces to its $j=0$ term).  The rational
identity follows for every $z$ off the poles.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Logarithmic derivatives]\label{prop:logder-finite}
Away from its zeros,
\[
 \frac{\partial}{\partial a}\log(a;q)_n
 =-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{q^j}{1-aq^j}.
\]
If $a=a(x)$ is differentiable, then
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential x}\log(a(x);q)_n
 =-a'(x)\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{q^j}{1-a(x)q^j}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the logarithm of the finite product term by term.
\end{proof}

\section{Infinite products and Lambert series}

\begin{theorem}[Convergence, zero set, and simplicity of the zeros]\label{thm:poch-entire}
Let $\AbsoluteValue q<1$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The product $(a;q)_\infty$ converges locally uniformly as a function of $a\in\ComplexNumbers$ and therefore defines an entire function.  For every $n\ge0$,
\[
 (a;q)_\infty=(a;q)_n\,(aq^n;q)_\infty .
\]
\item $(a;q)_\infty=0$ if and only if $aq^j=1$ for some $j\ge0$.  Thus for $q\ne0$ the zeros are precisely
\[
 a=q^{-j},\qquad j=0,1,2,\ldots,
\]
while for $q=0$ the only zero is $a=1$.
\item Every zero is simple, with an explicit derivative: if $a_0q^j=1$, then
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential a}(a;q)_\infty\Big|_{a=a_0}
 =-q^{j}\,(a_0;q)_j\,(a_0q^{j+1};q)_\infty\ne0,
\]
and for $q\ne0$, $a_0=q^{-j}$, this equals
\[
 (-1)^{j+1}q^{-\binom j2}\,(q;q)_j\,(q;q)_\infty .
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) On a compact set $K\subset\ComplexNumbers$ let $M=\max_{a\in K}\AbsoluteValue a$.  Then
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sup_{a\in K}\AbsoluteValue{a q^j}
 \le \frac{M}{1-\AbsoluteValue q}<\infty.
\]
The standard convergence criterion for infinite products shows that
\(
 \prod_j(1-aq^j)
\)
converges uniformly on $K$; equivalently, one may apply uniform convergence to the tails after choosing an analytic branch of the logarithm near $1$.  Uniform limits of holomorphic functions are holomorphic, so the limit is entire.  The concatenation identity is the limit, as $N\to\infty$, of the finite concatenation $(a;q)_{n+N}=(a;q)_n(aq^n;q)_N$ of \Cref{prop:concat}, since both $(a;q)_{n+N}\to(a;q)_\infty$ and $(aq^n;q)_N\to(aq^n;q)_\infty$.

(ii) If $aq^j=1$, one factor vanishes.  Conversely, if no factor vanishes, then the tail product is nonzero because the sum of the absolute deviations from $1$ converges, while the finite initial product is nonzero.  For $q\ne0$ the condition $aq^j=1$ reads $a=q^{-j}$; for $q=0$ only the factor $j=0$ can vanish, at $a=1$.

(iii) Removing the $j$-th factor by (i) at $n=j+1$ gives
\[
 (a;q)_\infty=(1-aq^j)\,g_j(a),
 \qquad g_j(a):=(a;q)_j\,(aq^{j+1};q)_\infty ,
\]
with $g_j$ entire.  Differentiating at $a_0$, where $1-a_0q^j=0$, leaves only $-q^j g_j(a_0)$.  This is nonzero: $(a_0;q)_j=\prod_{i<j}(1-q^{i-j})$ has no vanishing factor because $\AbsoluteValue{q^{i-j}}>1$ for $i<j$, and $(a_0q^{j+1};q)_\infty=(q;q)_\infty\ne0$ by (ii).  For $q\ne0$ the closed form follows from $(q^{-j};q)_j=(-1)^jq^{-\binom{j+1}2}(q;q)_j$ (\Cref{cor:qminusN} with $N=k=j$), since $j-\binom{j+1}2=-\binom j2$.
\end{proof}

This theorem has an exact Lean counterpart in
\path{FabiusFunction.QPochhammerEntire}.  For a fixed complex nome with
$\Norm q<1$, the declaration
\texttt{hasProdLocallyUniformly\_complexQPochhammerInf} proves locally
uniform convergence on the whole $a$-plane and
\texttt{complexQPochhammerInf\_differentiable} proves entirety.  The
division-free theorem \texttt{complexQPochhammerInf\_eq\_zero\_iff} says
that the product vanishes exactly when some factor $1-aq^j$ vanishes; it
remains valid at $q=0$.  Under the present theorem's hypothesis $q\ne0$,
\texttt{complexQPochhammerInf\_eq\_zero\_iff\_eq\_inv\_pow} gives the
literal lattice $a=(q^j)^{-1}$.  Finally,
\texttt{analyticOrderAt\_complexQPochhammerInf\_of\_eq\_zero} proves that
every zero has analytic order one.  Its proof splits the infinite product
into the first $j+1$ factors and a shifted tail; uniqueness of the vanishing
factor makes both complementary factors nonzero, leaving a nonzero analytic
unit times $a-q^{-j}$.  The Lean result is slightly stronger than the
displayed theorem because its division-free and simple-order layers also
cover $q=0$, where the sole zero is $a=1$.

\begin{remark}[The generality of the convergence statements]\label{rem:poch-entire-generality}
Only the norm of the coefficient ring enters into (i) and (ii).  The formalization defines $(a;q)_\infty=\prod'_{j\ge0}(1-aq^j)$ in an arbitrary topological commutative ring; for $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ in any complete normed commutative ring with $\AbsoluteValue1=1$ the factors are multipliable, the finite symbols $(a;q)_n$ converge to $(a;q)_\infty$, and the concatenation and dissection identities hold.  When the norm is moreover multiplicative, the zero criterion (ii) holds verbatim.  Over any complete, locally compact normed field the convergence is locally uniform in $a$, so $a\mapsto(a;q)_\infty$ is continuous; holomorphy and the derivative formula (iii) are stated for $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Complex orders}

Finite and negative integral indices extend naturally to a complex order once an infinite product is available.  Fix $0<\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and a branch of the logarithm at $q$, denoted by $\log q$, and write $q^\alpha=\exp(\alpha\log q)$.

\begin{definition}\label{def:complex-qshift}
For $\alpha\in\ComplexNumbers$, define
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}
 :=\frac{\QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\infty}},
 \tag{1.1}\label{eq:complex-qshift}
\]
whenever the denominator is nonzero.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Complex concatenation]\label{prop:complex-concat}
Whenever the displayed quantities are defined,
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha+\beta}
 =\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}
  \GeneralizedQPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\beta}.
 \tag{1.2}\label{eq:complex-concat}
\]
In particular,
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha+1}
 =(1-aq^\alpha)\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}.
 \tag{1.3}\label{eq:complex-shift}
\]
For integral $\alpha$, \eqref{eq:complex-qshift} agrees with the finite and negative-index definitions already given.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By definition,
\[
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\beta}
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\infty}}
  \frac{\QPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{aq^{\alpha+\beta}}{q}{\infty}}
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{aq^{\alpha+\beta}}{q}{\infty}}.
\]
This is \eqref{eq:complex-concat}.  Taking $\beta=1$ gives \eqref{eq:complex-shift}.  If $\alpha=n\ge0$, the tail factorization
\(
 \QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}
 =\QPochhammer{a}{q}{n}\QPochhammer{aq^n}{q}{\infty}
\)
recovers the finite product.  If $\alpha=-n<0$, the same relation, applied after shifting by $-n$, gives
\(
 \GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{-n}=1/\QPochhammer{aq^{-n}}{q}{n}.
\)
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Derivative with respect to the order]\label{prop:order-derivative}
Fix $a\in\ComplexNumbers$ with $\QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}\ne0$, and let
$U\subset\ComplexNumbers$ be open with
\[
 aq^{\alpha+j}\ne1\qquad(\alpha\in U,\ j\ge0).
\]
Then $\alpha\mapsto\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}$ is holomorphic and nonvanishing on $U$, and
its logarithmic derivative is
\[
 \frac{\partial_\alpha\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}}
      {\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}}
 =(\log q)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{aq^{\alpha+j}}{1-aq^{\alpha+j}}.
 \tag{1.4}\label{eq:order-derivative}
\]
Equivalently, the left-hand side is
$\partial_\alpha\log\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}$ for every local holomorphic branch of the
logarithm.  The series converges locally uniformly on $U$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $G(\alpha)=\QPochhammer{aq^\alpha}{q}{\infty}$.  The infinite-product theorem and the
displayed nonvanishing condition show that $G$ is holomorphic and nonzero on
$U$.  If $K\Subset U$, then
$\sup_{\alpha\in K}\AbsoluteValue{aq^\alpha}<\infty$.  Choose $J$ so that
$\AbsoluteValue{aq^{\alpha+j}}\le\tfrac12$ for $\alpha\in K$ and $j\ge J$.
The corresponding summands are bounded by
$2\sup_K\AbsoluteValue{aq^\alpha}\,\AbsoluteValue q^j$; the finitely many
earlier denominators have a positive minimum on $K$.  Thus the series
converges uniformly on $K$, and termwise logarithmic differentiation of the
normally convergent product gives
\[
 \frac{G'(\alpha)}{G(\alpha)}
 =-(\log q)\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{aq^{\alpha+j}}{1-aq^{\alpha+j}}.
\]
Since $\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{\alpha}
=\QPochhammer{a}{q}{\infty}/G(\alpha)$ and the numerator is nonzero and
independent of $\alpha$, the claimed formula follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Lambert-series expansion]\label{thm:lambert-log}
If $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and $\AbsoluteValue a<1$, then
\[
 \log(a;q)_\infty
 =-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a^m}{m(1-q^m)}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \frac{\partial}{\partial a}\log(a;q)_\infty
 =-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a^{m-1}}{1-q^m}
 =-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^j}{1-aq^j}.
\]
All series converge locally uniformly in $\AbsoluteValue a<1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $\AbsoluteValue a<1$, use $\log(1-u)=-\sum_{m\ge1}u^m/m$ and absolute convergence:
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\AbsoluteValue a^m\AbsoluteValue q^{jm}}{m}
 =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\AbsoluteValue a^m}{m(1-\AbsoluteValue q^m)}<\infty.
\]
Thus the order of summation may be exchanged:
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}\log(1-aq^j)
 =-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{a^m}{m}\sum_{j\ge0}q^{jm}.
\]
The geometric sum gives the first formula.  Local uniform convergence permits termwise differentiation.  Expanding $(1-aq^j)^{-1}$ geometrically gives the second form of the derivative.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Branches]\label{rem:lambert-log-branch}
The logarithm on the left is the sum $\sum_{j\ge0}\log(1-aq^j)$ of the principal logarithms of the factors, each of which lies in the disk $\AbsoluteValue{1-(1-aq^j)}<1$ where the Mercator series converges; the identity therefore fixes a definite branch, the one that is continuous in $a$ on $\AbsoluteValue a<1$ and vanishes at $a=0$.  The branch-free form is
\[
 \exp\Bigl(-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a^m}{m(1-q^m)}\Bigr)=(a;q)_\infty,
\]
which is how the formalization records the first display, over $\ComplexNumbers$, via the absolutely convergent double series.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Divisor sums]\label{cor:divisor-sum}
As a formal power series,
\[
 -q\frac{\Differential}{\Differential q}\log(q;q)_\infty
 =\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_1(n)q^n,
\]
where $\sigma_1(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}d$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Logarithmic differentiation gives
\[
 -q\frac{\Differential}{\Differential q}\log(q;q)_\infty
 =\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{j q^j}{1-q^j}
 =\sum_{j\ge1}\sum_{r\ge1}j q^{jr}.
\]
The coefficient of $q^n$ is the sum of all $j$ dividing $n$.
\end{proof}

\section{The dilogarithmic limit}

The singular regime $q\to1^-$ turns an infinite product into an exponential of order $(1-q)^{-1}$.  The dilogarithm is the natural leading term.

\begin{theorem}[Asymptotic expansion near $q=1$]\label{thm:poch-dilog}
Fix $0<\rho<1$ and an integer $N\ge1$.  Uniformly for $\AbsoluteValue a\le\rho$, as $t\to0^+$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \log(a;\EulerE^{-t})_\infty
 ={}&-\frac{\Polylogarithm_2(a)}{t}+\frac12\log(1-a)\\
 &-\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\frac{B_{2r}}{(2r)!}
 t^{2r-1}\Polylogarithm_{2-2r}(a)+O_{\rho,N}(t^{2N-1}),
\end{aligned}
\]
where $B_{2r}$ are Bernoulli numbers and
\(
 \Polylogarithm_s(a)=\sum_{m\ge1}a^m/m^s
\)
for $\AbsoluteValue a<1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:lambert-log},
\[
 \log(a;\EulerE^{-t})_\infty
 =-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{a^m}{m(1-\EulerE^{-mt})}.
\]
The Bernoulli expansion at the origin is
\[
 \frac1{1-\EulerE^{-x}}
 =\frac1x+\frac12+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}
 \frac{B_{2r}}{(2r)!}x^{2r-1}+R_N(x),
\]
with $R_N(x)=O_N(x^{2N-1})$ for $0\le x\le1$.  Substitution of the displayed polynomial terms gives respectively
\[
 -\frac1t\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{a^m}{m^2},
 \qquad
 -\frac12\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{a^m}{m}=\frac12\log(1-a),
\]
and the polylogarithmic terms in the statement.

It remains to bound the total remainder.  Split the $m$-sum at $m\le t^{-1}$.  In this range,
\[
 \sum_{m\le t^{-1}}\frac{\AbsoluteValue a^m}{m}\AbsoluteValue{R_N(mt)}
 \le C_Nt^{2N-1}\sum_{m\ge1}\rho^m m^{2N-2}
 =O_{\rho,N}(t^{2N-1}).
\]
For $m>t^{-1}$, subtracting finitely many asymptotic terms leaves a quantity bounded by a polynomial in $mt$ plus $(1-\EulerE^{-mt})^{-1}$; the factor $\rho^m$ makes the tail exponentially small in $1/t$, hence smaller than every power of $t$.  This proves the uniform remainder estimate.
\end{proof}

\section{Classical shifted factorials as a limit}

\begin{proposition}[Finite classical limit]\label{prop:classical-poch-limit}
Let $U$ be a sufficiently small simply connected neighborhood of $1$ not
containing $0$, and choose the holomorphic logarithm on $U$ with
$\log 1=0$.  For $q\in U$ put $q^x=\exp(x\log q)$.  Then, for fixed
$x\in\ComplexNumbers$ and $n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\[
 \lim_{q\to1}\frac{(q^x;q)_n}{(1-q)^n}
 =x(x+1)\cdots(x+n-1)=(x)_n.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $h=\log q$, so $h\to0$ and $q=\exp h$.  For every fixed
$u\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\[
 \frac{1-q^u}{1-q}=\frac{1-\exp(uh)}{1-\exp h}\longrightarrow u
\]
by the Taylor expansion of the exponential, also when $u=0$.  Applying this
to $u=x+j$ in
\[
 \frac{(q^x;q)_n}{(1-q)^n}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1-q^{x+j}}{1-q}
\]
proves the result.
\end{proof}

% ed.: remark added to record the generality of the formalization.
\begin{remark}[Only the derivative at $1$ enters]\label{rem:classical-limit-formalization}
The proof uses nothing about $q^x$ beyond $q^x\to1$ and
$(1-q^x)/(1-q)\to x$, that is, the value and the derivative of $q\mapsto q^x$
at $q=1$.  Accordingly the formalization proves the limit for an arbitrary
parameter family $a=f(q)$ in any nontrivially normed field: if $f(1)=1$ and
$f'(1)=c$, then $(f(q);q)_n/(1-q)^n\to(c)_n$ as $q\to1$, $q\ne1$, because
each factor $(1-f(q)q^j)/(1-q)$ is the difference quotient at $1$ of
$q\mapsto f(q)q^j$, whose derivative there is $c+j$.  The complex statement
above (principal branch) and its real analogue are the specializations
$f(q)=q^x$.
\end{remark}

\chapter{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-integers and Gaussian coefficients}\label{chap:gaussian}
\index{Gaussian coefficient}
\index{$q$-binomial coefficient|see{Gaussian coefficient}}

\section{Definitions}

\begin{definition}\label{qg:def-gaussian-coefficient}
For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ define the $q$-integer and $q$-factorial by
\[
 [n]_q=\frac{1-q^n}{1-q}=1+q+\cdots+q^{n-1},
 \qquad
 [n]_q!=\prod_{j=1}^{n}[j]_q.
\]
For $0\le k\le n$, the \emph{Gaussian coefficient} is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 =\frac{(q;q)_n}{(q;q)_k(q;q)_{n-k}}
 =\frac{[n]_q!}{[k]_q![n-k]_q!}.
\]
Outside $0\le k\le n$ it is defined to be $0$.
\end{definition}

The notation is initially a rational expression, but the recurrences below show that it is a polynomial in $q$ with nonnegative integral coefficients.

\begin{proposition}[Product forms]\label{prop:qbinom-products}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 =\frac{(q^{n-k+1};q)_k}{(q;q)_k}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1-q^{n-k+j}}{1-q^j}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Cancel $(q;q)_{n-k}$ from $(q;q)_n$ using \Cref{prop:concat}.
\end{proof}

\section{The two Pascal recurrences}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Pascal recurrences]\label{thm:qpascal}
For $1\le k\le n-1$,
\begin{align}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}+q^k\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q},\label{eq:qpascal1}\\
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=q^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}.\label{eq:qpascal2}
\end{align}
Both identities remain valid at the boundary under the zero convention.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put the two terms on the right of \eqref{eq:qpascal1} over the common denominator $(q;q)_k(q;q)_{n-k}$.  Their numerator is
\[
 (q;q)_{n-1}\bigl((1-q^k)+q^k(1-q^{n-k})\bigr)
 =(q;q)_{n-1}(1-q^n)=(q;q)_n.
\]
The proof of \eqref{eq:qpascal2} is the same, using
\(
 q^{n-k}(1-q^k)+(1-q^{n-k})=1-q^n.
\)
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Polynomiality and positivity]\label{cor:positivity}
For all $0\le k\le n$, the Gaussian coefficient $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ belongs to $\NaturalNumbers[q]$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $n$.  The boundary values are $1$.  Either recurrence in \Cref{thm:qpascal} expresses an interior coefficient as a sum of two polynomials with nonnegative integral coefficients.
\end{proof}

\section{Symmetry, reciprocity, and degree}

\begin{theorem}[Elementary structure]\label{thm:qbinom-structure}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{n-k}{q}$;
\item $\displaystyle \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}=q^{-k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$;
\item $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ has degree $k(n-k)$, constant term $1$, and leading coefficient $1$;
\item its coefficient sequence is palindromic.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Work first in the rational-function field \(\RationalNumbers(q)\).  Symmetry is immediate
from the factorial quotient.  For reciprocity, use
\[
 (q^{-1};q^{-1})_m=(-1)^mq^{-\binom{m+1}{2}}(q;q)_m
\]
for $m=n,k,n-k$.  The signs cancel and the exponent is
\[
 -\binom{n+1}{2}+\binom{k+1}{2}+\binom{n-k+1}{2}=-k(n-k).
\]
Both sides of the resulting cleared identity are polynomials, so the identity
holds identically, including at roots of unity where the factorial quotient
itself may display a removable \(0/0\).

The polynomiality already proved in \Cref{cor:positivity} makes the degree
calculation legitimate.  Since \((q;q)_m\) has degree \(\binom{m+1}{2}\),
constant term \(1\), and leading coefficient \((-1)^m\), the quotient in
\(\RationalNumbers(q)\) has degree
\[
 \binom{n+1}{2}-\binom{k+1}{2}-\binom{n-k+1}{2}=k(n-k),
\]
with constant and leading coefficients both equal to \(1\).  Finally,
reciprocity says
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=q^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}.
\]
Writing \(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{k(n-k)}c_jq^j\) and comparing powers gives
\(c_j=c_{k(n-k)-j}\), which is precisely palindromicity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Exact reciprocal core in Lean]
Items~\textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} have exact machine-checked counterparts.
Symmetry is \lean{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_symm}.  Reciprocity is
\lean{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_reciprocity_units} in
\path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean}: for every unit \(u\) in every commutative
semiring and all \(n,k\in\NaturalNumbers\), including the zero extension above the
diagonal,
\[
 u^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{u^{-1}}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{u}.
\]
The wrapper \lean{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_reciprocity} specializes this to a
nonzero element of an arbitrary semifield.  Unlike the quotient proof above,
the formal proof is a denominator-free induction on the two Pascal
recurrences, so it remains valid with zero divisors.  Items~\textup{(iii)} and
\textup{(iv)} are not yet represented as polynomial-degree and coefficient
theorems; consequently \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure} remains \statuspartial.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Odd reciprocal degree at \(q=-1\)]
\label{cor:qbinom-neg-one-odd}
Let \(R\) be a commutative ring.  If \(k(n-k)\) is odd, then
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{-1}=0 \qquad\text{in }R.
\]
In particular, for every \(a,b\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{2a}{2b+1}{-1}=0,
\]
where a column above the row is interpreted by the zero extension.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
First evaluate the universal integer Gaussian polynomial at \(-1\).
Reciprocity changes its value to its negative when \(k(n-k)\) is odd.  Hence
twice that integer is zero, and torsion-freeness of \(\IntegerNumbers\) makes the integer
itself zero.  The recursive Gaussian coefficient commutes with every semiring
homomorphism, so the canonical map \(\IntegerNumbers\to R\) transports this zero to every
commutative ring, including characteristic two.  For the displayed
specialization, if \(b<a\), then both \(2b+1\) and
\(2a-(2b+1)=2(a-b-1)+1\) are odd, so their product is odd.  If \(b\ge a\),
then \(2b+1>2a\) and the zero extension applies directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version]
The two statements are respectively
\lean{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_eq_zero_of_odd_degree} and
\lean{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero} in
\path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean}.  Both are total in the natural indices and
hold over every commutative ring.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Classical limit]\label{cor:qbinom-classical}
For fixed $n,k$,
\[
 \lim_{q\to1}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\binom nk.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the $q$-factorial form and $[j]_q\to j$.  Alternatively, and in the generality of an arbitrary topological semiring, the Gaussian coefficient is built from $q$ by finitely many additions, multiplications, and powers through the $q$-Pascal recurrence, hence is a continuous function of $q$; its value at $q=1$ is $\binom nk$ by the same recurrence, so the limit is that value.  This is the route taken by the formalization.
\end{proof}

\section{Multinomial coefficients and flags}

\begin{definition}
Let $r\in\PositiveIntegers$, let
$n_1,\dots,n_r\in\NaturalNumbers$, and put
$n=n_1+\cdots+n_r$.  Define
\[
 \QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\ldots,n_r}{q}
 =\frac{(q;q)_n}{(q;q)_{n_1}\cdots(q;q)_{n_r}}.
\]
The quotient denotes its uniquely cancelled polynomial in
$\IntegerNumbers[q]$, so removable singularities at roots of unity are filled
by polynomial evaluation.  The cases $r=1$ and
$n_1=\cdots=n_r=0$ both have value $1$.  Tuples outside the displayed
composition domain are undefined here; no implicit zero extension is used.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Factorization into binomials]\label{prop:qmult-factor}
Let $N_j=n_j+n_{j+1}+\cdots+n_r$.  Then
\[
 \QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\ldots,n_r}{q}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{r-1}\GaussianBinomial{N_j}{n_j}{q}.
\]
In particular, every $q$-multinomial coefficient is a polynomial with nonnegative integral coefficients.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write out the factorial ratios.  Consecutive numerator and denominator factors telescope:
\[
 \prod_{j=1}^{r-1}
 \frac{(q;q)_{N_j}}{(q;q)_{n_j}(q;q)_{N_{j+1}}}
 =\frac{(q;q)_n}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(q;q)_{n_j}}.
\]
Positivity follows from \Cref{cor:positivity}.
\end{proof}

\section{Adjacent ratios and efficient recurrences}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:adjacent-ratio}
Let $q$ lie in a field.  For $0\le k<n$, if
$\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\ne0$ and $1-q^{k+1}\ne0$, then
\[
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+1}{q}}{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}}
 =\frac{1-q^{n-k}}{1-q^{k+1}}.
\]
For the same range, if $\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}\ne0$ and
$1-q^{n-k}\ne0$, then
\[
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}}{\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}}
 =\frac{1-q^n}{1-q^{n-k}}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The denominator-free cross identities
\[
 (1-q^{k+1})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+1}{q}
 =(1-q^{n-k})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
\]
and
\[
 (1-q^{n-k})\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 =(1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}
\]
hold over every commutative ring and, by zero extension, are total in all
natural \(n,k\): on and above the row boundary both sides vanish.  In the
strict range \(k<n\), division over a field by the two explicitly assumed
nonzero denominators in each line gives the stated ratios.
\end{proof}

These ratios are useful numerically, but at roots of unity their denominators
may vanish and the quotients need not be defined.  The cross-multiplied
identities in the proof and the polynomial recurrences of
\Cref{thm:qpascal} remain valid there; unlike the quotients, the two cross
identities also include the row boundary and every above-row index.

\section{Coefficient moments}

Write
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{D}c_jq^j,
 \qquad D=k(n-k),
\]
and choose an exponent $X$ with probability
\(
 \Pr(X=j)=c_j/\binom nk.
\)
The combinatorial meaning of $X$ will be established in \Cref{chap:combinatorics}; its moments can already be found algebraically.

\begin{theorem}[Mean and variance]\label{thm:qbinom-moments}
For the preceding distribution,
\[
 \Expectation X=\frac{k(n-k)}2,
 \qquad
 \Variance(X)=\frac{k(n-k)(n+1)}{12}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 \left.\frac{\Differential}{\Differential q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\right|_{q=1}
 =\frac{k(n-k)}2\binom nk.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The palindromic identity $c_j=c_{D-j}$ immediately gives $\Expectation X=D/2$ and the derivative formula.

For the variance, use the moment-generating function
\[
 M(t)=\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{\EulerE^t}}{\binom nk}
 =\prod_{i=1}^{k}
 \frac{\EulerE^{(n-k+i)t}-1}{(n-k+i)t}
 \frac{it}{\EulerE^{it}-1}.
\]
The local expansion
\[
 \log\frac{\EulerE^{u}-1}{u}=\frac u2+\frac{u^2}{24}+O(u^3)
\]
gives
\[
 \log M(t)
 =\frac t2\sum_{i=1}^k(n-k)
 +\frac{t^2}{24}\sum_{i=1}^k\bigl((n-k+i)^2-i^2\bigr)+O(t^3).
\]
The first sum is $k(n-k)$.  The second equals
\[
 k(n-k)^2+2(n-k)\frac{k(k+1)}2
 =k(n-k)(n+1).
\]
Since the first and second derivatives of $\log M$ at $0$ are the mean and variance, respectively, the result follows.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Finite \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial theorems and inversion}\label{chap:finite-qbinomial}

\section{Euler's finite product expansions}

\begin{theorem}[Finite $q$-binomial theorem]\label{thm:finite-qbinomial}
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 (z;q)_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} z^k,\label{eq:finite-qbinomial-minus}\\
 (-z;q)_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} z^k.\label{eq:finite-qbinomial-plus}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
It is enough to prove the first identity; replace $z$ by $-z$ for the second.  The case $n=0$ is clear.  Suppose the formula holds for $n$.  Then
\[
 (z;q)_{n+1}=(1-zq^n)(z;q)_n.
\]
The coefficient of $z^k$ on the right is
\[
 (-1)^kq^{\binom k2}
 \left(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+q^{n-k+1}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}\right).
\]
By the second $q$-Pascal recurrence, the parenthesis is $\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}$.  This completes the induction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Alternating sums]\label{cor:alternating-qbinom}
For $n>0$,
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=0.
\]
More generally,
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2+mk}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=(q^m;q)_n.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Set $z=1$ and $z=q^m$, respectively, in \eqref{eq:finite-qbinomial-minus}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version]
Over every commutative ring, including \(q=0\), roots of unity, zero
divisors, and positive characteristic,
\lean{Fabius.sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating_mul_pow} proves the second
display for all \(m,n\in\NaturalNumbers\), and
\lean{Fabius.sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating} proves the first with its exact
row-zero boundary \(1\).  Both declarations are in
\path{QBinomialInversion.lean}; neither uses a quotient or cancellation
hypothesis.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Weighted subset sum]\label{cor:weighted-subsets}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\[
 \sum_{0\le i_1<\cdots<i_k\le n-1}q^{i_1+\cdots+i_k}
 =q^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 \sum_{1\le j_1<\cdots<j_k\le n}q^{j_1+\cdots+j_k}
 =q^{\binom{k+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Expand $\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1+zq^i)$ by choosing either $1$ or $zq^i$ from each factor, and compare the coefficient of $z^k$ with \eqref{eq:finite-qbinomial-plus}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version]
\sloppy
The zero-based and one-based formulas are respectively
\lean{Fabius.sum_pow_sum_powersetCard_eq_gaussianBinomial} and
\lean{Fabius.sum_pow_sum_powersetCard_Icc_eq_gaussianBinomial} in
\path{BitPositionQBinomial.lean}.  They are total in \(n,k\in\NaturalNumbers\) over every
commutative ring: the first uses \texttt{range n}, and the second literally
uses the interval \texttt{Icc 1 n}.  The latter is obtained by translating
each chosen index upward by one, which contributes the extra factor \(q^k\).
\end{remark}

\section{Reciprocal finite products}

\begin{theorem}[Reciprocal finite $q$-binomial theorem]\label{thm:reciprocal-finite}
For $n\ge0$ and $\AbsoluteValue z<1$,
\[
 \frac{1}{(z;q)_{n+1}}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}z^k.
\]
The identity also holds formally in $\IntegerNumbers[q][[z]]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let
\(
 H_n(z)=\sum_{k\ge0}\GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}z^k.
\)
The second $q$-Pascal recurrence gives
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}-q^n\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k-1}{q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k}{q}.
\]
Therefore
\[
 (1-zq^n)H_n(z)=H_{n-1}(z),
\]
where $H_0(z)=\sum_{k\ge0}z^k=(1-z)^{-1}$.  Iterating yields
\[
 H_n(z)=\frac1{(1-z)(1-zq)\cdots(1-zq^n)}.
\]
All operations are valid formally; analytically they are justified for $\AbsoluteValue z<1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version and analytic boundary]
For every commutative ring \(R\), every \(q\in R\), and every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\lean{Fabius.prod_one_sub_qPow_X_mul_gaussianBinomialGeneratingSeries} in
\path{SymmetricFunctionGenerating.lean} proves
\[
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{n}(1-q^jX)\right)
 \operatorname{PowerSeries.mk}\!\left(k\mapsto\GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}\right)=1
 \quad\text{in }R[[X]].
\]
This is denominator-free and total at \(q=0\), roots of unity, zero divisors,
positive characteristic, and the zero ring.  It proves the formal
power-series half of \Cref{thm:reciprocal-finite}; no current declaration here
evaluates that series at a complex \(z\) or establishes convergence for
\(\lvert z\rvert<1\).  The theorem therefore remains only partially
formalized as stated.
\end{remark}

\section{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Vandermonde convolution}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Vandermonde convolution]\label{thm:q-vandermonde}
For $m,n,r\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \GaussianBinomial{m+n}{r}{q}
 &=\sum_{j\in\IntegerNumbers}q^{(m-j)(r-j)}
 \GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r-j}{q},\label{eq:qvand1}\\
 &=\sum_{j\in\IntegerNumbers}q^{j(n-r+j)}
 \GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r-j}{q}.\label{eq:qvand2}
\end{align}
Only finitely many terms are nonzero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Factor
\[
 (-z;q)_{m+n}=(-z;q)_m(-zq^m;q)_n
\]
and use \eqref{eq:finite-qbinomial-plus}.  The coefficient of $z^r$ on the left is
\(
 q^{\binom r2}\GaussianBinomial{m+n}{r}{q}.
\)
The term with $j$ chosen from the first factor and $r-j$ from the second has exponent
\[
 \binom j2+m(r-j)+\binom{r-j}{2}.
\]
Subtracting $\binom r2$ gives $(m-j)(r-j)$, proving \eqref{eq:qvand1}.  Splitting the product in the reverse order gives \eqref{eq:qvand2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Central form]\label{cor:central-vandermonde}
For $m,n\ge0$,
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{m+n}{m}{q}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{\min(m,n)}
 q^{j^2}\GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Set $r=m$ in \eqref{eq:qvand1} and put $\ell=m-j$.  Then the exponent becomes $\ell^2$, while symmetry gives $\GaussianBinomial{m}{m-\ell}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{m}{\ell}{q}$ and the second factor is $\GaussianBinomial{n}{\ell}{q}$.
\end{proof}

% Chapter: \ref{chap:finite-qbinomial} ("Finite $q$-binomial theorems and
% inversion"), section "$q$-Vandermonde convolution".

\begin{corollary}[Shifted central form]\label{cor:shifted-central-vandermonde}
\index{$q$-Vandermonde convolution!shifted central form}
For $N\in\NaturalNumbers$ and $k\in\IntegerNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \GaussianBinomial{2N}{N+k}{q}
 =\sum_{\ell\in\IntegerNumbers}q^{\ell^2+\ell k}\,
 \GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell}{q}\,\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell+k}{q},
 \label{eq:shifted-central-vandermonde}
\end{equation}
where a Gaussian coefficient whose lower index falls outside $\SetOf{0,1,\dots,\text{upper index}}$ is read as $0$, so that only finitely many terms are nonzero.  The case $k=0$ is \Cref{cor:central-vandermonde} with $m=n=N$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If $\AbsoluteValue k>N$ both sides vanish: the left side because $N+k\notin\SetOf{0,\dots,2N}$, and the right side because a nonzero term requires both $0\le\ell\le N$ and $0\le\ell+k\le N$, which forces $\AbsoluteValue k\le N$.

Assume therefore $-N\le k\le N$ and set $r=N+k\in\SetOf{0,\dots,2N}$.  \Cref{thm:q-vandermonde}, in the form \eqref{eq:qvand1} with $m=n=N$, gives
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{2N}{N+k}{q}
 =\sum_{j\in\IntegerNumbers}q^{(N-j)(N+k-j)}
 \GaussianBinomial{N}{j}{q}\,\GaussianBinomial{N}{N+k-j}{q}.
\]
Substitute $\ell=N-j$.  Then $N+k-j=\ell+k$, so the exponent becomes $\ell(\ell+k)=\ell^2+\ell k$; the symmetry $\GaussianBinomial{N}{N-\ell}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell}{q}$ of \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}\,(i) turns the first Gaussian coefficient into $\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell}{q}$, and the second is $\GaussianBinomial{N}{\ell+k}{q}$.  This is \eqref{eq:shifted-central-vandermonde}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version]
\formalfile{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean} defines the zero-extended integer
coefficient and its four totality laws.
\begin{formalapilist}
\emph{Definition}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInt}

\smallskip
\emph{Cast, boundary, and reflection laws}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInt_ofNat}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInt_eq_zero_of_neg}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInt_eq_zero_of_lt}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInt_symm}
\end{formalapilist}
\formalfile{QBinomialVandermonde.lean} proves the signed formula in two total
forms.
\begin{formalapilist}
\emph{Finite range \(0\le\ell\le N\)}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_two_mul_int_shifted_central}

\smallskip
\emph{Literal finite sum over all \(\ell\in\IntegerNumbers\)}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_two_mul_int_shifted_central_finsum}
\end{formalapilist}
The Lean power is written
\(q^{\ell^{\natural}(\ell+k)^{\natural}}\), with
\(t^{\natural}=\max(t,0)\); every possibly nonzero term has
\(\ell,\ell+k\ge0\), so its exponent is exactly
\(\ell(\ell+k)\).  Thus the formal theorem is total in \(k\), including
\(\lvert k\rvert>N\), over every commutative semiring and at \(q=0\).
\end{remark}

\section{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial inversion}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-binomial inversion]\label{thm:qbinomial-inversion}
For sequences $(a_n)$ and $(b_n)$ in a commutative ring containing $q$, the relations
\[
 b_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} a_k
\]
are equivalent to
\[
 a_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} b_k.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute the first relation into the proposed inverse.  The coefficient of $a_j$ becomes
\[
 \sum_{k=j}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{k}{j}{q}.
\]
The factorial definition gives
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{k}{j}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n-j}{k-j}{q}.
\]
Put $r=n-k$.  The coefficient is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}
 \sum_{r=0}^{n-j}(-1)^rq^{\binom r2}\GaussianBinomial{n-j}{r}{q}.
\]
By \Cref{cor:alternating-qbinom}, this equals $0$ when $n>j$ and $1$ when $n=j$.  Thus the composition is the identity.  The same argument in the opposite order proves equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Matrix orthogonality]\label{cor:qbinomial-orthogonality}
For $0\le j\le n$,
\[
 \sum_{k=j}^{n}(-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{k}{j}{q}=\delta_{n,j}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This is the kernel computation in the preceding proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version and scaled strengthening]
The scalar algebra is formalized in \formalfile{QBinomialInversion.lean}.  Its
public surface consists exactly of the following definitions and theorems.
\begin{formalapilist}
\emph{Definitions}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialKernel}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInverseKernel}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialKernel}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel}

\smallskip
\emph{Theorems}\par
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_mul}\\
\formalname{Fabius.sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating_mul_pow}\\
\formalname{Fabius.sum_gaussianBinomial_alternating}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialKernel_left_orthogonality}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialKernel_right_orthogonality}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialKernel_left_orthogonality}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialKernel_right_orthogonality}
\end{formalapilist}
The chain identity already holds over every commutative semiring.  The alternating and
orthogonality results hold over every commutative ring.  Both multiplication
orders are stated on the total interval \texttt{Icc j n}, including the empty
case \(n<j\).  The scaled forward and inverse entries are
\[
 s^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},
 \qquad
 (-s)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},
\]
for an arbitrary independent \(s\); neither \(s\) nor \(q\) is assumed
nonzero or invertible.

The common finite-sum argument is isolated in
\formalfile{FiniteTriangularTransform.lean}, whose exhaustive public surface is
the definition \formalname{Fabius.lowerTriangularTransform} and the theorem
\formalname{Fabius.lowerTriangularTransform_comp}.  For
\texttt{[Semiring R] [AddCommMonoid M] [Module R M]}, the theorem converts a
total ordered kernel convolution on \texttt{Icc j n} into equality of the
whole composite sequence function with its input.  It assumes neither
commutativity of \(R\) nor additive inverses in \(M\).  The Gaussian kernels
are zero-extended above the row, so the empty case \(n<j\) fits this generic
total interface without a separate range hypothesis.

The module-valued sequence theorem is formalized in
\formalfile{QBinomialTransform.lean}, whose exact public surface is:
\begin{formalapilist}
\emph{Definitions}\par
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialTransform}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialInverseTransform}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialTransform}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomialInverseTransform}

\smallskip
\emph{Theorems}\par
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialInverseTransform_transform}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomialTransform_inverseTransform}\\
\formalname{Fabius.scaledGaussianBinomial_inversion}\\
\formalname{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_inversion}
\end{formalapilist}
The two forward-transform
definitions require only
\texttt{[Semiring R] [AddCommMonoid M] [Module R M]}; the two signed inverse
definitions require \texttt{[Ring R]} with the same target assumptions.
All four theorems require exactly
\texttt{[CommRing R] [AddCommMonoid M] [Module R M]}.  The first two give
both compositions as equalities of whole sequence functions and the last
two give the scaled and classical iff statements.  Taking \(M=R\) yields
\Cref{thm:qbinomial-inversion}; the formal theorem is stronger in allowing
arbitrary module-valued sequences and requires no topology or convergence.

\formalfile{QDifferenceAnnihilation.lean} formalizes the scaled characteristic
polynomial and the \(q\)-difference functional; its exact public surface
consists of four theorems.
\begin{formalapilist}
\formalname{Fabius.sum_scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel_mul_pow}\\
\formalname{Fabius.sum_gaussianBinomialInverseKernel_mul_geometric_pow}\\
\formalqtwo{\_eq\_map\_coeff\_mul}\\
\formalqtwo{\_eq\_zero\_of\_degree\_lt}
\end{formalapilist}
Writing
\[
 K^{-1}_{q,s}(n,k)=(-s)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},
\]
the first theorem is the homogeneous identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}K^{-1}_{q,s}(n,k)z^k
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(z-sq^j).                         \label{eq:scaled-qdiff-characteristic}
\end{equation}
It holds over every commutative ring for arbitrary \(q,s,z\).  With \(s=1\)
and \(z=q^d\), the second theorem gives the complete moment table
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}K^{-1}_{q,1}(n,k)(q^k)^d
 =\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(q^d-q^j),                         \label{eq:qdiff-moments}
\end{equation}
so the row annihilates every monomial of degree \(d<n\).

The last two theorems make the polynomial statement strictly stronger than
the scalar lemma usually quoted in Bailey inversion.  If \(A\) is a semiring,
\(R\) a commutative ring, \(\varphi\colon A\to R\) a semiring homomorphism,
and \(P\in A[X]\) has degree at most \(n\), then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}K^{-1}_{q,1}(n,k)\,P^{\varphi}(q^k)
 =\varphi([X^n]P)\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(q^n-q^j),          \label{eq:qdiff-top-coefficient}
\end{equation}
where \(P^{\varphi}\) denotes coefficientwise scalar extension.  If
\(\deg P<n\), the left side is zero.  These degree statements include
\(n=0\) and the zero polynomial.  Nodes may collide and the surviving factor
in \eqref{eq:qdiff-top-coefficient} may vanish: no division, distinctness,
nonzero or invertible base, domain, characteristic, topology, or convergence
hypothesis is present.

The global geometric-numerator bridge is owned by
\formalfile{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean}:
\begin{formalapilist}
\formalname{Fabius.geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_scaledGaussianBinomialInverseKernel}
\end{formalapilist}
It identifies the
denominator-free numerator with \(K^{-1}_{q,q}(n,k)\) for every pair of
natural indices, including indices above the diagonal; equivalently, its
generating polynomial is the \(s=q\) specialization of
\eqref{eq:scaled-qdiff-characteristic}.  This global identity does not remove
the field, finite-node injectivity, or in-range assumptions from separately
normalized Lagrange quotients.

Finally, \formalfile{QBinomialInversionSpecializations.lean} has exactly two
public definitions and four public theorems.
\begin{formalapilist}
\emph{Definitions}\par
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianResidualCoeff}\\
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianReconstructionCoeff}

\smallskip
\emph{Theorems}\par
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianResidualCoeff_eq}\\
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianReconstructionCoeff_eq}\\
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianReconstructionCoeff_residualCoeff_delta}\\
\formalname{Fabius.qGaussianResidualCoeff_reconstructionCoeff_delta}
\end{formalapilist}
At base
\(q^2\) and scale \(-q\), the coefficients are
\[
 (-q)^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^2},
 \qquad q^{(n-k)^2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^2},
\]
The two definitions and these two pointwise closed forms require only an
arbitrary, possibly noncommutative ring.  Exactly the two convolution-delta
theorems require a commutative ring, and prove that both total finite orders
are the Kronecker delta.
\end{remark}

\section{A \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Cauchy product identity}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Cauchy identity]\label{thm:q-cauchy}
For $u,v$ in a commutative ring,
\[
 (uv;q)_n
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} (u;q)_k v^k(v;q)_{n-k}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $n$.  The assertion is clear at $n=0$.  Let the right side be $C_n(u,v)$.  Use
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}=q^{n+1-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
\]
and split $C_{n+1}$ accordingly.  In the first part shift $k\mapsto k+1$, use
\((u;q)_{k+1}=(u;q)_k(1-uq^k)\), and combine with the second part after using
\((v;q)_{n+1-k}=(v;q)_{n-k}(1-vq^{n-k})\).
The bracket that remains is
\[
 (1-vq^{n-k})+vq^{n-k}(1-uq^k)=1-uvq^n,
\]
independent of $k$.  Therefore
\[
 C_{n+1}(u,v)=(1-uvq^n)C_n(u,v)=(uv;q)_{n+1}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Taking $u=0$ recovers the tautology $(0;q)_k=1$ inside a weighted expansion; taking special limits of $u$ or $v$ produces several finite convolution formulas used later in the proof of the $q$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz summation.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Formal version and strengthenings]
\formalfile{QBinomialCauchy.lean} provides the theorem and its compatibility
spelling.
\begin{formalapilist}
\formalname{Fabius.finite_qCauchy_identity}\\
\formalname{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_mul_eq_sum_gaussianBinomial}
\end{formalapilist}
It is stated for arbitrary \(q,u,v\) in every
commutative ring and every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), so it includes \(n=0\), \(q=0\), roots
of unity, positive characteristic, and zero divisors.  The proof is entirely
polynomial and assumes neither cancellation nor nonvanishing.  The reflected
strengthening \formalname{Fabius.finite_qCauchy_identity_reflected} states
\[
 (uv;q)_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 (v;q)_k v^{n-k}(u;q)_{n-k}.
\]
The same module defines the denominator-free \(q\)-Bernstein term
\[
 B^{(q)}_{n,k}(x)=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} x^k(x;q)_{n-k}
\]
as \formalname{Fabius.qBernsteinBasis} and proves the total partition of unity
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}B^{(q)}_{n,k}(x)=1
\]
as \formalname{Fabius.sum_qBernsteinBasis}.

Together with
\formalname{Fabius.finite_qCauchy_second_identity}, described at
\Cref{lem:cauchy-II}, these are exhaustively the one public definition and
five public theorems of \formalfile{QBinomialCauchy.lean}.
\end{remark}


\chapter{Weighted binomial coefficients and symmetric functions}\label{chap:weighted}
\index{weighted binomial coefficient}
\index{symmetric function}

The ordinary and Gaussian binomial coefficients are instances of a broader construction in which each available object carries a weight.  This point of view, developed systematically by Konvalina, places subset selection, multiset selection, Stirling numbers, and Gaussian coefficients in one framework.

\section{Elementary and complete weighted coefficients}

\begin{definition}
Let $\mathbf w=(w_1,\dots,w_n)$ be elements of a commutative ring.  Define
\begin{align*}
 C_k^n(\mathbf w)
 &=\sum_{1\le i_1<\cdots<i_k\le n}w_{i_1}\cdots w_{i_k},\\
 S_k^n(\mathbf w)
 &=\sum_{1\le i_1\le\cdots\le i_k\le n}w_{i_1}\cdots w_{i_k}.
\end{align*}
We set $C_0^n=S_0^n=1$.  Thus $C_k^n$ is the elementary symmetric function $e_k(w_1,\dots,w_n)$ and $S_k^n$ is the complete homogeneous symmetric function $h_k(w_1,\dots,w_n)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Weighted Pascal recurrences]\label{thm:weighted-pascal}
For $n,k\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 C_k^n(\mathbf w)&=C_k^{n-1}(\mathbf w)+w_nC_{k-1}^{n-1}(\mathbf w),\label{eq:weighted-C}\\
 S_k^n(\mathbf w)&=S_k^{n-1}(\mathbf w)+w_nS_{k-1}^{n}(\mathbf w).\label{eq:weighted-S}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A $k$-element subset of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ either omits $n$, contributing $C_k^{n-1}$, or contains $n$, in which case removal of $n$ leaves a $(k-1)$-element subset and the weight acquires a factor $w_n$.  This proves \eqref{eq:weighted-C}.

For multisets, separate those with no occurrence of $n$ from those with at least one occurrence.  Removing one occurrence of $n$ from the latter leaves an arbitrary multiset of size $k-1$ drawn from all $n$ types.  This proves \eqref{eq:weighted-S}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Generating products]\label{thm:weighted-generating}
One has
\begin{align}
 \prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+w_i z)
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}C_k^n(\mathbf w)z^k,\label{eq:weighted-e-gen}\\
 \prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac1{1-w_i z}
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}S_k^n(\mathbf w)z^k.\label{eq:weighted-h-gen}
\end{align}
The second identity is formal in $z$, or analytic when $\AbsoluteValue{w_i z}<1$ for all $i$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the first product, choosing $w_i z$ from exactly the factors indexed by a subset $I$ produces $z^{\AbsoluteValue I}\prod_{i\in I}w_i$.  Summing over subsets gives \eqref{eq:weighted-e-gen}.  For the second, expand each factor geometrically:
\[
 \frac1{1-w_i z}=\sum_{r_i\ge0}w_i^{r_i}z^{r_i}.
\]
The coefficient of $z^k$ sums over weak compositions $r_1+\cdots+r_n=k$, which are equivalent to multisets of size $k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Elementary--complete orthogonality]\label{cor:e-h-orthogonality}
For $m\ge0$,
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^jC_j^n(\mathbf w)S_{m-j}^n(\mathbf w)=\delta_{m,0}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Multiply
\(
 \prod_i(1-w_i z)
\)
by its reciprocal and compare the coefficient of $z^m$, using \Cref{thm:weighted-generating} with $z$ replaced by $-z$ in the elementary generating function.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version and exact boundary]
{\small
For a finite family \(a:\iota\to R\), the evaluator and its bridge to
Mathlib's universal \texttt{MvPolynomial.esymm} are
\begin{center}
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_eq_eval_esymm}.
\end{center}
The adjoining-variable recurrences are
\begin{center}
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_option_succ}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousEval_option_succ}.
\end{center}
They prove, respectively,
\[
 e_{r+1}(x,a)=x e_r(a)+e_{r+1}(a),
 \qquad
 h_{r+1}(x,a)=x h_r(x,a)+h_{r+1}(a).
\]
Thus both weighted Pascal recurrences of \Cref{thm:weighted-pascal} are exact,
including their zero-degree boundaries, over every commutative semiring.
\par}

{\footnotesize
The exhaustive public surface of
\path{SymmetricFunctionOrthogonality.lean} is its one definition above and
the following six theorems, all in namespace \texttt{Fabius}:
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_eq_eval_esymm}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_zero}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_comp_equiv}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_option_succ}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricEval_fin_succ}\\
\lean{sum_elementarySymmetricEval_mul_completeHomogeneousEval}.
\end{center}
\par}
The first five structural theorems hold over a commutative semiring; the last
proves exactly
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^j e_j(a)h_{m-j}(a)
 =\begin{cases}1,&m=0,\\0,&m>0,
 \end{cases}
\]
over every commutative ring, for every finite index type and every \(m\in\NaturalNumbers\).
It therefore includes the empty family and degree zero and uses no division,
domain, characteristic, or nonvanishing hypothesis.

The exhaustive public surface of
\path{SymmetricFunctionGenerating.lean} consists of the two definitions
\begin{center}
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries}
\end{center}
and the six theorems
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\lean{coeff_elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries}\\
\lean{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_eq_prod}\\
\lean{elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_neg_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeries_eq_invOfUnit_elementarySymmetricGeneratingSeries_neg}\\
\lean{prod_one_sub_qPow_X_mul_gaussianBinomialGeneratingSeries}.
\end{center}
The definitions, coefficient formulas, and finite product
\(E_a(X)=\prod_i(1+a_iX)\) hold over every commutative semiring.  Over every
commutative ring the remaining theorems prove
\[
 E_{-a}(X)H_a(X)=1,
 \qquad H_a(X)=\operatorname{invOfUnit}(E_{-a}(X),1),
\]
as well as the geometric-alphabet reciprocal identity recorded after
\Cref{thm:reciprocal-finite}.  Hence the polynomial elementary identity and
the formal-power-series version of the complete identity in
\Cref{thm:weighted-generating} are exact.  The theorem as labelled remains
\statuspartial{} because its analytic evaluation and convergence assertion
under \(\lvert w_i z\rvert<1\) has not been formalized.

The complementary finite-index interface in
\path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean} consists of the definition
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} and the six theorems
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\lean{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_empty}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert}\\
\lean{one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert}\\
\lean{prod_one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn}\\
\lean{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_eq_invOfUnit_prod}.
\end{center}
The coefficient, empty-family, and adjoining-variable recurrence results hold
over every commutative semiring.  Over every commutative ring, the remaining
results clear one or all finite linear denominators and identify the series
with Mathlib's canonical \texttt{PowerSeries.invOfUnit}; zero divisors are
allowed.  Thus this module and \path{SymmetricFunctionGenerating.lean} give
complementary formal proofs of the complete-product half of
\Cref{thm:weighted-generating}, while the latter also supplies the elementary
product and elementary--complete reciprocity.

There is also a finite-family analytic companion.  The sole public theorem
\lean{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_isBigO_pow} in
\path{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean} transfers coordinatewise
\(\BigO{g}\) bounds through every fixed degree \(r\): the corresponding
complete homogeneous evaluation is \(\BigO{g^r}\).  It includes degree zero
and requires no nonvanishing assumption on \(g\).  This asymptotic bound does
not evaluate either formal series at \(z\), establish its convergence, or
close the analytic clause of the labelled theorem.
\end{remark}

\section{Classical, Stirling, and Gaussian specializations}

\begin{theorem}[A specialization table]\label{thm:weighted-specializations}
The following identities hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $w_i=1$, then
\[
 C_k^n=\binom nk,
 \qquad
 S_k^n=\binom{n+k-1}{k}.
\]
\item If $w_i=q^{i-1}$, then
\[
 C_k^n=q^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q},
 \qquad
 S_k^n=\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k}{q}.
\]
\item For the unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind $c(n,k)$ and Stirling numbers of the second kind $\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}n\\k\end{smallmatrix}\right\}$,
\[
 c(n+1,k+1)=C_{n-k}^{n}(1,2,\dots,n),
\]
\[
 \left\{\begin{matrix}n+k\\n\end{matrix}\right\}
 =S_k^n(1,2,\dots,n).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $w_i=1$, $C_k^n$ counts $k$-subsets.  The multiset count is the stars-and-bars number $\binom{n+k-1}{k}$.

For $w_i=q^{i-1}$, the elementary identity is \Cref{cor:weighted-subsets}.  For the complete identity, \Cref{thm:weighted-generating} gives
\[
 \prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac1{1-zq^i}
 =\frac1{(z;q)_n}.
\]
The coefficient of $z^k$ is $\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k}{q}$ by \Cref{thm:reciprocal-finite}.

Finally,
\[
 x(x+1)\cdots(x+n)=\sum_{j=0}^{n+1}c(n+1,j)x^j.
\]
The coefficient of $x^{k+1}$ is $e_{n-k}(1,2,\dots,n)$.  The standard ordinary generating function
\[
 \prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac1{1-iz}
 =\sum_{k\ge0}\left\{\begin{matrix}n+k\\n\end{matrix}\right\}z^k
\]
follows from the recurrence for Stirling numbers of the second kind, or by induction on $n$; comparison with \eqref{eq:weighted-h-gen} proves the last formula.
\end{proof}

\section{Principal specialization of Schur polynomials}
\index{Schur polynomial}
\index{principal specialization}

The two Gaussian entries of \Cref{thm:weighted-specializations}(ii) are the
one-column and the one-row case of a single product formula.  Stating it
requires no symmetric-function theory beyond determinants, because a Schur
polynomial can be \emph{defined} by its bialternant.

\begin{definition}\label{def:schur-bialternant}
Let $m\ge1$ and let $\lambda=(\lambda_1\ge\lambda_2\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_m\ge0)$
be a partition with at most $m$ parts, padded by zeros.  Put
\[
 \mu_j=\lambda_j+m-j\quad(1\le j\le m),
 \qquad
 \delta=(m-1,m-2,\ldots,0),
\]
so that $\mu_1>\mu_2>\cdots>\mu_m\ge0$, and $\delta$ is the value of $\mu$ at
$\lambda=0$.  For indeterminates $x_1,\ldots,x_m$ set
\[
 a_\mu(x_1,\ldots,x_m)=\det\bigl(x_i^{\mu_j}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{m},
\]
and define the \emph{Schur polynomial}
\[
 s_\lambda(x_1,\ldots,x_m)
 =\frac{a_\mu(x_1,\ldots,x_m)}{a_\delta(x_1,\ldots,x_m)}.
\]
Finally put $n(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(j-1)\lambda_j$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Vandermonde alternant]\label{lem:vandermonde-alternant}
For indeterminates $y_1,\ldots,y_m$,
\[
 \det\bigl(y_i^{\,j-1}\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{m}
 =\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}(y_j-y_i).
\]
Consequently $a_\delta(x_1,\ldots,x_m)=\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}(x_i-x_j)$, and
$a_\delta$ divides $a_\mu$ in $\IntegerNumbers[x_1,\ldots,x_m]$, so $s_\lambda$ is a
polynomial with integer coefficients.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $m$; for $m=1$ both sides equal $1$.  Regard
$D=\det(y_i^{\,j-1})_{i,j=1}^{m}$ as a polynomial in $y_m$ of degree at most
$m-1$.  If $y_m=y_i$ for some $i<m$, two rows coincide and $D=0$; hence
$\prod_{i<m}(y_m-y_i)$ divides $D$, and by the degree bound
$D=c\prod_{i<m}(y_m-y_i)$ with $c$ free of $y_m$.  Expanding along the last
row, the coefficient of $y_m^{\,m-1}$ in $D$ is the cofactor at position
$(m,m)$, namely $\det(y_i^{\,j-1})_{i,j=1}^{m-1}$, which by induction equals
$\prod_{1\le i<j\le m-1}(y_j-y_i)$.  Multiplying proves the formula.

Reversing the order of the columns of $\bigl(x_i^{\,m-j}\bigr)_{i,j}$ turns
the exponents $m-j$ into $j-1$ and multiplies the determinant by the sign
$(-1)^{\binom m2}$ of the reversal permutation, so
\[
 a_\delta=(-1)^{\binom m2}\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}(x_j-x_i)
 =\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}(x_i-x_j).
\]
For the divisibility, interchanging $x_i$ and $x_j$ interchanges two rows of
$a_\mu$, so $a_\mu$ changes sign; setting $x_i=x_j$ therefore gives
$a_\mu=0$, and $x_i-x_j$ divides $a_\mu$ in $\IntegerNumbers[x_1,\ldots,x_m]$.  The
polynomials $x_i-x_j$ with $i<j$ are pairwise non-associate primes in that
unique factorization domain, so their product, which is $a_\delta$, divides
$a_\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Principal specialization of a Schur polynomial]\label{thm:schur-principal}
Let $\lambda$ have at most $m$ parts.  Then, as an identity in $\RationalNumbers(q)$,
\[
 s_\lambda(1,q,q^2,\ldots,q^{m-1})
 =q^{n(\lambda)}
 \prod_{1\le i<j\le m}
 \frac{1-q^{\,\lambda_i-\lambda_j+j-i}}{1-q^{\,j-i}}.
\]
The left side lies in $\IntegerNumbers[q]$, so by \Cref{cor:safe-specialization} the
identity persists at every complex $q$ once the right-hand quotient has been
reduced; as displayed it is to be read at those $q$ with $q^{\,d}\ne1$ for
$1\le d\le m-1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Specialize $x_i=q^{\,i-1}$, so that $x_i^{\mu_j}=q^{(i-1)\mu_j}$.  Writing
$y_j=q^{\mu_j}$, the matrix $\bigl(x_i^{\mu_j}\bigr)_{i,j}$ has entries
$y_j^{\,i-1}$, hence is the transpose of $\bigl(y_i^{\,j-1}\bigr)_{i,j}$.
A determinant is invariant under transposition, so
\Cref{lem:vandermonde-alternant} gives
\[
 a_\mu(1,q,\ldots,q^{m-1})
 =\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}\bigl(q^{\mu_j}-q^{\mu_i}\bigr).
\]
Because $\lambda$ is weakly decreasing we have $\mu_1>\cdots>\mu_m\ge0$, so
$\mu_i-\mu_j>0$ whenever $i<j$, and we extract from each difference the
\emph{smaller} power:
% ed.: extracting q^{\mu_j} rather than q^{\mu_i} produces no sign at all.
% ed.: The incoming survey's `factor the lower powers of q from each
% ed.: difference' left \binom m2 minus signs unaccounted for.
\[
 q^{\mu_j}-q^{\mu_i}=q^{\mu_j}\bigl(1-q^{\,\mu_i-\mu_j}\bigr).
\]
Hence
\[
 a_\mu(1,q,\ldots,q^{m-1})
 =q^{A(\mu)}\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}\bigl(1-q^{\,\mu_i-\mu_j}\bigr),
 \qquad
 A(\mu)=\sum_{1\le i<j\le m}\mu_j=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(j-1)\mu_j,
\]
the last equality because $\mu_j$ is contributed once for each $i<j$.
Applying this to $\mu=\delta$, where $\delta_i-\delta_j=j-i$, gives
\[
 a_\delta(1,q,\ldots,q^{m-1})
 =q^{A(\delta)}\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}\bigl(1-q^{\,j-i}\bigr),
\]
a nonzero element of $\RationalNumbers(q)$.  Dividing,
\[
 s_\lambda(1,q,\ldots,q^{m-1})
 =q^{\,A(\mu)-A(\delta)}
 \prod_{1\le i<j\le m}
 \frac{1-q^{\,\mu_i-\mu_j}}{1-q^{\,j-i}}.
\]
Finally $\mu_i-\mu_j=\lambda_i-\lambda_j+j-i$ and
\[
 A(\mu)-A(\delta)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(j-1)(\mu_j-\delta_j)
 =\sum_{j=1}^{m}(j-1)\lambda_j=n(\lambda).
\]
The left side is a polynomial with integer coefficients by
\Cref{lem:vandermonde-alternant}; the transfer to arbitrary complex $q$ is
\Cref{lem:polynomial-identity-principle} together with
\Cref{cor:safe-specialization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The one-column and one-row shadows]\label{cor:schur-e-h}
Let $m=n\ge1$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For $0\le k\le n$ and $\lambda=(1^k)=(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)$,
\[
 s_{(1^k)}(1,q,\ldots,q^{n-1})=q^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}.
\]
\item For $k\ge0$ and $\lambda=(k)$,
\[
 s_{(k)}(1,q,\ldots,q^{n-1})=\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k}{q}.
\]
\end{enumerate}
Since $s_{(1^k)}=e_k$ and $s_{(k)}=h_k$~\cite{Macdonald1995}, these are
exactly the two Gaussian entries of \Cref{thm:weighted-specializations}(ii),
that is, $C_k^n(\mathbf w)$ and $S_k^n(\mathbf w)$ at $w_i=q^{\,i-1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(i) Here $\lambda_i=1$ for $i\le k$ and $\lambda_i=0$ for $i>k$, so
$n(\lambda)=\sum_{i\le k}(i-1)=\binom k2$, and $\lambda_i-\lambda_j$ vanishes
except when $i\le k<j$, where it equals $1$.  The product of
\Cref{thm:schur-principal} therefore collapses to
\[
 \prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=k+1}^{n}
 \frac{1-q^{\,j-i+1}}{1-q^{\,j-i}}
 =\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1-q^{\,n-i+1}}{1-q^{\,k-i+1}},
\]
the inner product telescoping.  The numerators are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (1-q^{n})(1-q^{n-1})\cdots(1-q^{\,n-k+1})
   &=\frac{(q;q)_n}{(q;q)_{n-k}},\\
 (1-q^k)\cdots(1-q)&=(q;q)_k,
 \end{aligned}
\]
so the product is $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$.

(ii) Here $\lambda_1=k$ and $\lambda_j=0$ for $j\ge2$, so $n(\lambda)=0$ and
only the pairs $(1,j)$ contribute:
\[
 \prod_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1-q^{\,k+j-1}}{1-q^{\,j-1}}
 =\frac{(q;q)_{n+k-1}\big/(q;q)_k}{(q;q)_{n-1}}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n+k-1}{k}{q}.
 \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:schur-hypotheses}
Both hypotheses of \Cref{thm:schur-principal} are needed as stated.  If
$\lambda$ had more than $m$ parts, $\mu$ would not be strictly decreasing and
$a_\mu$ would vanish identically.  And the displayed quotient is meaningful
only where $q^{\,j-i}\ne1$ for all $1\le i<j\le m$; at the excluded roots of
unity, and at $q=1$, one must cancel first, exactly as prescribed by
\Cref{cor:safe-specialization}.  After cancellation the limit $q\to1$ is
\[
 s_\lambda(1,1,\ldots,1)
 =\prod_{1\le i<j\le m}\frac{\lambda_i-\lambda_j+j-i}{j-i},
\]
by $\lim_{q\to1}(1-q^a)/(1-q^b)=a/b$; this is Weyl's dimension formula for
$\mathrm{GL}_m$, and it stands to \Cref{thm:schur-principal} exactly as
\Cref{cor:qbinom-classical} stands to the Gaussian coefficient.
\end{remark}

\section{Weighted inversion}

\begin{theorem}[Symmetric-function inversion]\label{thm:weighted-inversion}
Let $(a_m)$ and $(b_m)$ be sequences.  The transformations
\[
 b_m=\sum_{j=0}^{m}S_{m-j}^n(\mathbf w)a_j
\]
and
\[
 a_m=\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{m-j}C_{m-j}^n(\mathbf w)b_j
\]
are inverse to each other.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The generating functions $A(z)=\sum a_mz^m$ and $B(z)=\sum b_mz^m$ satisfy
\[
 B(z)=A(z)\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-w_i z)^{-1}.
\]
Multiplication by $\prod_i(1-w_i z)$ gives the proposed inverse coefficient formula.  Equivalently, use the orthogonality in \Cref{cor:e-h-orthogonality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version and module-valued strengthening]
The exhaustive public surface of \path{SymmetricFunctionTransform.lean} is
the four definitions
\begin{center}
\lean{Fabius.completeHomogeneousKernel}\\
\lean{Fabius.signedElementaryKernel}\\
\lean{Fabius.completeHomogeneousTransform}\\
\lean{Fabius.signedElementaryTransform}
\end{center}
and the five theorems
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\lean{Fabius.completeHomogeneousKernel_left_orthogonality}\\
\lean{Fabius.completeHomogeneousKernel_right_orthogonality}\\
\lean{Fabius.signedElementaryTransform_completeHomogeneousTransform}\\
\lean{Fabius.completeHomogeneousTransform_signedElementaryTransform}\\
\lean{Fabius.weightedSymmetricFunction_inversion}.
\end{center}
For a finite family \(w:\iota\to R\), the complete-homogeneous kernel and
transform need only \texttt{[CommSemiring R]}; the signed-elementary kernel
