and transform require \texttt{[CommRing R]}.  Both kernel orthogonalities,
both inverse compositions, and the inversion iff use exactly
\texttt{[CommRing R] [AddCommMonoid M] [Module R M]} with
\texttt{[Fintype \(\iota\)]}.  The compositions are equalities of whole
sequence functions, so
\lean{Fabius.weightedSymmetricFunction_inversion} proves
\Cref{thm:weighted-inversion} exactly and strengthens it to arbitrary
module-valued sequences.

Both kernels are extended by zero above the diagonal, including the empty
outer interval.  Their composition proofs reuse
\lean{Fabius.lowerTriangularTransform_comp}; together with the definition
\lean{Fabius.lowerTriangularTransform}, these are exhaustively the one
theorem and one definition of \path{FiniteTriangularTransform.lean}.  The
generic theorem itself only assumes a semiring acting on an additive
commutative monoid and a total ordered finite-convolution identity.  No
division, topology, domain, characteristic, or nonvanishing condition occurs.
\end{remark}

\section{The subset--subspace analogy}

The choice $w_i=q^{i-1}$ is more than a convenient specialization.  It replaces the Boolean lattice of subsets by the lattice of subspaces over a finite field.  Ordinary binomial coefficients count subsets by cardinality; Gaussian coefficients count subspaces by dimension.  The two-term weighted recurrence mirrors the decomposition of a subspace according to its relation with a fixed hyperplane.  This analogy is made exact in \Cref{chap:finite-geometry}.

\chapter{Partitions, words, paths, and inversion statistics}\label{chap:combinatorics}
\index{partition!inside a rectangle}
\index{inversion statistic}
\index{binary word}

\section{Binary words}

Let $\mathcal W_{n,k}$ be the set of binary words of length $n$ containing $k$ ones.  Define
\[
\InversionNumber{w}=\#\SetOf{(i,j):i<j,\ w_i=1,\ w_j=0}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Inversion generating function]\label{thm:binary-inversions}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\[
\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal W_{n,k}}q^{\InversionNumber{w}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $F_{n,k}(q)$ denote the right side.  If the last letter is $1$, deletion of it gives a word in $\mathcal W_{n-1,k-1}$ and creates no new inversion.  If the last letter is $0$, deletion gives a word in $\mathcal W_{n-1,k}$ and the final zero forms an inversion with each of the $k$ ones, contributing a factor $q^k$.  Hence
\[
 F_{n,k}=F_{n-1,k-1}+q^kF_{n-1,k}.
\]
The boundary values are $1$, so $F_{n,k}$ and $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ obey the same recurrence and initial conditions.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Inversion-law interpretation]\label{cor:qbinom-inversion-law}
For $0\le k\le n$, the random variable in \Cref{thm:qbinom-moments} is the
inversion number of a uniformly random word in $\mathcal W_{n,k}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient of $q^j$ in \Cref{thm:binary-inversions} counts words with $j$ inversions, while $\AbsoluteValue{\mathcal W_{n,k}}=\binom nk$.
\end{proof}

\section{Partitions inside a rectangle}

A partition $\lambda=(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_k)$ is said to fit in a $k\times m$ rectangle if
\[
 m\ge\lambda_1\ge\cdots\ge\lambda_k\ge0.
\]
Its size is $\AbsoluteValue\lambda=\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_k$.

\begin{theorem}[Rectangular partition generating function]\label{thm:rectangle-partitions}
For $m,k\ge0$,
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q}
 =\sum_{\lambda\subseteq k\times m}q^{\AbsoluteValue\lambda}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Take a word $w\in\mathcal W_{m+k,k}$ and let the positions of its ones be
\(
 1\le i_1<\cdots<i_k\le m+k.
\)
Define
\[
 \lambda_j=m+j-i_j\qquad(1\le j\le k).
\]
Then $0\le\lambda_j\le m$ and
\[
 \lambda_j-\lambda_{j+1}=i_{j+1}-i_j-1\ge0.
\]
Thus $\lambda$ fits in the rectangle.  Conversely,
\(
 i_j=m+j-\lambda_j
\)
recovers a strictly increasing sequence of positions, so this is a bijection.  The number $\lambda_j$ is exactly the number of zeros to the right of the $j$th one, hence
\(
\AbsoluteValue\lambda=\InversionNumber{w}.
\)
Apply \Cref{thm:binary-inversions}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Conjugation and complement]\label{cor:partition-symmetries}
Conjugating Ferrers diagrams proves
\(
 \GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{m+k}{m}{q}.
\)
Taking the complement in the rectangle proves
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q^{-1}}=q^{-mk}\GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Conjugation exchanges a $k\times m$ rectangle with an $m\times k$ rectangle without changing size.  Complementation sends size $r$ to $mk-r$, so the generating polynomial is reciprocal of degree $mk$.
\end{proof}

\section{Lattice paths and area}

Encode a binary word $w$ by a path $P$ from $(0,0)$ to $(m,k)$, reading
$0$ as an east step and $1$ as a north step.  Define
\[
 \LatticePathArea{P}
 \DefinitionEquals
 \sum_{\substack{e\text{ an east step of }P}}
 \bigl(\text{starting height of }e\bigr).
\]
Equivalently, this is the number of unit cells in
$[0,m]\times[0,k]$ whose interiors lie below $P$, or the sum over north
steps of the number of later east steps.  Thus
$\LatticePathArea{P}=\InversionNumber{w}$ exactly.  In particular the path
$E^mN^k$ has area $0$, while $N^kE^m$ has area $mk$; no complementary-area
convention is implicit.

\begin{corollary}[Area generating function]\label{cor:path-area}
The polynomial $\GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q}$ is the generating function of monotone lattice paths from $(0,0)$ to $(m,k)$ by the preceding, explicitly oriented area:
\[
\GaussianBinomial{m+k}{k}{q}=\sum_P q^{\LatticePathArea{P}}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Under the word--path encoding, every north step contributes the number of
later east steps.  Summing over north steps gives $\InversionNumber{w}$ and,
by the equivalent definition above, $\LatticePathArea{P}$.
\end{proof}

\section{Durfee squares and the central convolution}

The largest square in the northwest corner of a partition diagram is its Durfee square.

\begin{remark}[Durfee-square proof of the central convolution]\label{rem:durfee-qvand}
The identity in \Cref{cor:central-vandermonde} has a direct partition proof.
A partition in an $m\times n$ rectangle with Durfee square of side $j$
consists of the $j\times j$ square, a partition to its right fitting in a
$j\times(n-j)$ rectangle, and a partition below it fitting in an
$(m-j)\times j$ rectangle.  By \Cref{thm:rectangle-partitions}, their
generating functions are
\[
 q^{j^2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{q}\GaussianBinomial{m}{j}{q}.
\]
The Durfee size is unique, so summing over $j$ counts every partition exactly once.
Thus the algebraic proof above and the Durfee decomposition prove one and the
same canonical result, rather than two separately labelled assertions.
\end{remark}

\section{Words with several letters}

Let $\mathcal W(n_1,\dots,n_r)$ be the words containing $n_i$ copies of the letter $i$, ordered by $1<\cdots<r$.  Let $\InversionNumber{w}$ count pairs $i<j$ of positions with $w_i>w_j$.

\begin{theorem}[Multiset inversion enumerator]\label{thm:qmult-inversions}
If $n=n_1+\cdots+n_r$, then
\[
 \QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\dots,n_r}{q}
 =\sum_{w\in\mathcal W(n_1,\dots,n_r)}q^{\InversionNumber{w}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose the positions of the largest letter $r$.  If their binary indicator has inversion number $s$, then $s$ counts precisely the inversions in which the larger letter is $r$.  The remaining positions contain a word on $1,\dots,r-1$, and its inversions are independent.  Therefore the generating function factors as
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{n_r}{q}
 \QMultinomial{n-n_r}{n_1,\dots,n_{r-1}}{q}.
\]
Iterating and using \Cref{prop:qmult-factor} proves the formula.
\end{proof}

\section{Rogers--Szeg\H{o} polynomials}

\begin{definition}
The Rogers--Szeg\H{o} polynomial is
\[
 H_n(z;q)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q} z^k.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Basic recurrence]\label{prop:rogers-szego-recurrence}
One has
\[
 H_{n+1}(z;q)=zH_n(z;q)+H_n(qz;q),
 \qquad H_0(z;q)=1.
\]
Moreover, if $\AbsoluteValue q<1$, then
\[
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_n(z;q)}{(q;q)_n}t^n
 =\frac1{(t;q)_\infty(zt;q)_\infty}
\]
for $\AbsoluteValue t<1$ and $\AbsoluteValue{zt}<1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Insert the first $q$-Pascal recurrence into $H_{n+1}$:
\[
 \sum_k\left(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}+q^k\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\right)z^k
 =zH_n(z;q)+H_n(qz;q).
\]
For the generating function, use
\[
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}}{(q;q)_n}=\frac1{(q;q)_k(q;q)_{n-k}}.
\]
Writing $n=k+j$ and separating the double sum gives
\[
 \left(\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{t^j}{(q;q)_j}\right)
 \left(\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(zt)^k}{(q;q)_k}\right).
\]
Each factor equals the reciprocal infinite product by Euler's identity, proved later in \Cref{cor:euler-infinite}; alternatively, the equality follows already by taking $n\to\infty$ in \Cref{thm:reciprocal-finite}.  This gives the stated product.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Three-term recurrence]\label{prop:rogers-szego-three-term}
\index{Rogers--Szeg\H{o} polynomial!three-term recurrence}
For $n\ge1$,
\[
 H_{n+1}(z;q)=(1+z)H_n(z;q)-z\,(1-q^n)H_{n-1}(z;q).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
% ed.: the incoming survey proved this by quoting a Pascal II instance in the
% ed.: indices (n-1,k-1),(n-1,k-2), which does not effect the reduction, and
% ed.: then appealing to ``a common factorial denominator''.  The argument below
% ed.: replaces that step by the ratio identity displayed second, after which
% ed.: the comparison is exactly eq:qpascal2.
Both sides are polynomials in $z$, so it suffices to compare the coefficient
of $z^k$ for every $k\in\IntegerNumbers$, using the convention $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=0$ for $k<0$ or
$k>n$.  On the left that coefficient is $\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}$; on the right it is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}-(1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}.
\]
We claim that for every $k\in\IntegerNumbers$ and every $n\ge1$,
\[
 (1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}=\bigl(1-q^{\,n+1-k}\bigr)\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}.
\]
Granting the claim, the right-hand coefficient equals
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+\Bigl(1-\bigl(1-q^{\,n+1-k}\bigr)\Bigr)\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+q^{\,n+1-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q},
\]
which is the second $q$-Pascal recurrence \eqref{eq:qpascal2} with $n$
replaced by $n+1$; by \Cref{thm:qpascal} that recurrence is valid for all $k$
under the zero convention, and the right-hand coefficient is therefore
$\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}$, as required.

To prove the claim, let first $1\le k\le n$, so that $k-1\ge0$ and
$n-k\ge0$ and both Gaussian coefficients are given by factorial quotients.
From $(q;q)_n=(1-q^n)(q;q)_{n-1}$ and
$(q;q)_{n-k+1}=(1-q^{\,n-k+1})(q;q)_{n-k}$,
\[
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}}{\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}}
 =\frac{(q;q)_n\,(q;q)_{n-k}}{(q;q)_{n-1}\,(q;q)_{n-k+1}}
 =\frac{1-q^n}{1-q^{\,n-k+1}},
\]
and cross-multiplication gives the claim.  For $k\le0$ both sides vanish,
since then $k-1<0$ and both Gaussian coefficients are zero.  For $k=n+1$ the
left side vanishes because $\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{n}{q}=0$ and the right side vanishes
because $1-q^{\,n+1-k}=1-q^0=0$.  For $k>n+1$ every term is zero.  This
exhausts all $k$ and completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Dilation formula]\label{cor:rogers-szego-dilation}
For $n\ge1$,
\[
 H_n(qz;q)=H_n(z;q)-z\,(1-q^n)H_{n-1}(z;q).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Compare the coefficients of $z^k$, $k\ge1$: on the left it is $q^k\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$, on the right $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}-(1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}$, and the two agree exactly when
\[
 (1-q^k)\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=(1-q^n)\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q},
\]
which is the claim established in the proof of \Cref{prop:rogers-szego-three-term} (read at the index $k$ in place of $k-1$; it is the product of the two cross identities of \Cref{prop:adjacent-ratio}).  The constant terms are both $1$.  Conversely, adding the dilation formula to the basic recurrence $H_{n+1}(z;q)=zH_n(z;q)+H_n(qz;q)$ gives the three-term recurrence at once, which is how the formalization organizes the three results: the basic recurrence is the second $q$-Pascal summation, the dilation formula is the column-adjacent cross identity read coefficientwise over every commutative ring, and the three-term recurrence is their sum.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:rogers-szego-three-term}
The recurrence of \Cref{prop:rogers-szego-recurrence} moves the argument, from
$z$ to $qz$; the one above does not.  It is therefore a three-term recurrence
in the index $n$ alone, of the shape familiar from orthogonal polynomial
sequences, and it evaluates $H_0,H_1,\ldots,H_N$ at a fixed numerical $z$ in
$\BigO(N)$ ring operations, without ever forming the Gaussian coefficients.  At
$q=0$ every $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ equals $1$ by \Cref{prop:extreme-specializations},
the recurrence becomes $H_{n+1}=(1+z)H_n-zH_{n-1}$, and its solution is the
geometric sum $1+z+\cdots+z^{n}$, as it must be.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Finite fields, Grassmannians, and rank enumeration}\label{chap:finite-geometry}
\index{finite field}
\index{Grassmannian}
\index{Schubert cell}

\section{Counting subspaces}

Write a prime power as $Q=p^e$, where $p$ is prime and
$e\in\PositiveIntegers$.  The symbol $\FiniteField_{Q}$ denotes a field with
$Q$ elements, unique up to field isomorphism but not by a distinguished
isomorphism.  For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$, $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}$ is its
$n$-fold Cartesian product and $n$-dimensional vector space; at $n=0$ this is
the one-element zero space.  For $r\in\NaturalNumbers$,
$\mathrm{GL}_r(\FiniteField_{Q})$ is the group of invertible
$r\times r$ matrices, with $\mathrm{GL}_0(\FiniteField_{Q})$ the trivial
group.

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian coefficients count subspaces]\label{thm:subspace-count}
Let $Q$ be a prime power.  The number of $k$-dimensional linear subspaces of $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}$ is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Count ordered linearly independent $k$-tuples in $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}$.  The first vector may be any nonzero vector, the second any vector outside the span of the first, and so on, giving
\[
 (Q^n-1)(Q^n-Q)\cdots(Q^n-Q^{k-1}).
\]
Each $k$-dimensional subspace has
\[
 (Q^k-1)(Q^k-Q)\cdots(Q^k-Q^{k-1})
\]
ordered bases.  Dividing and cancelling $Q^i$ in corresponding factors yields
\[
 \prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{Q^{n-i}-1}{Q^{k-i}-1}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1-Q^{n-k+j}}{1-Q^j}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Geometric Pascal recurrence]\label{thm:geometric-pascal}
Let $H$ be a fixed hyperplane in $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}$.  Then
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{Q}+Q^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{Q}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A $k$-subspace $U$ is either contained in $H$ or not.  The contained subspaces contribute $\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{Q}$.  If $U\not\subseteq H$, then $W=U\cap H$ has dimension $k-1$.  Fixing $W$, the quotient $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}/W$ has dimension $n-k+1$, and $H/W$ is a hyperplane.  The subspace $U/W$ is a line not contained in $H/W$.  The total number of lines in $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n-k+1}$ minus those in its hyperplane is
\[
 \frac{Q^{n-k+1}-1}{Q-1}-\frac{Q^{n-k}-1}{Q-1}=Q^{n-k}.
\]
There are $\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{Q}$ choices for $W$, proving the formula.
\end{proof}

\section{Flags and \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-multinomial coefficients}

\begin{theorem}[Partial flags]\label{thm:flag-count}
Let $d_i=n_1+\cdots+n_i$.  The number of flags
\[
 0=V_0\subset V_1\subset\cdots\subset V_r=\FiniteField_{Q}^{n},
 \qquad \dim(V_i/V_{i-1})=n_i,
\]
is
\[
 \QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\dots,n_r}{Q}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose $V_1$ in $\GaussianBinomial{n}{n_1}{Q}$ ways.  In the quotient $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}/V_1$, choose $V_2/V_1$ in $\GaussianBinomial{n-n_1}{n_2}{Q}$ ways, and continue.  The product is the factorization in \Cref{prop:qmult-factor}.
\end{proof}

\section{Matrices of fixed rank}

\begin{theorem}[Rank enumeration]\label{thm:rank-matrices}
The number of $m\times n$ matrices of rank $r$ over $\FiniteField_{Q}$ is
\[
 N_{m,n,r}(Q)
 =\GaussianBinomial{m}{r}{Q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r}{Q}\AbsoluteValue{\mathrm{GL}_r(\FiniteField_{Q})},
\]
where
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{\mathrm{GL}_r(\FiniteField_{Q})}=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(Q^r-Q^i).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 N_{m,n,r}(Q)
 =\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(Q^m-Q^i)(Q^n-Q^i)}
 {\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(Q^r-Q^i)}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every rank-$r$ matrix $A$ can be factored as $A=UV$, where $U$ is an $m\times r$ matrix of full column rank and $V$ is an $r\times n$ matrix of full row rank.  There are
\(
 \prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(Q^m-Q^i)
\)
choices for $U$ and
\(
 \prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(Q^n-Q^i)
\)
choices for $V$.  Two pairs $(U,V)$ and $(U',V')$ give the same $A$ exactly when
\[
 U'=UG,\qquad V'=G^{-1}V
\]
for a unique $G\in\mathrm{GL}_r(\FiniteField_{Q})$.  Thus every matrix is counted $\AbsoluteValue{\mathrm{GL}_r}$ times.  Substituting the subspace-count formulas gives the first expression.
\end{proof}

\section{Schubert cells and a topological meaning}

Let $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$ be the complex Grassmannian of $k$-planes in $\ComplexNumbers^n$.  Its Schubert cell decomposition is indexed by partitions inside a $k\times(n-k)$ rectangle.

\begin{theorem}[Poincar\'e polynomial of the Grassmannian]\label{thm:grassmann-poincare}
The integral cohomology of $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$ is concentrated in even degrees, and its Poincar\'e polynomial is
\[
 P_{\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)}(t)
 =\sum_{j\ge0}\RankOperator H^{2j}(\mathrm{Gr}(k,n);\IntegerNumbers)t^{2j}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{t^2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix the standard flag in $\ComplexNumbers^n$.  Every $k$-plane has a unique row-reduced echelon matrix.  Its pivot positions determine a binary word with $k$ ones, hence a partition $\lambda$ inside the $k\times(n-k)$ rectangle.  Once the pivots are fixed, the entries allowed to vary freely occupy exactly the boxes of $\lambda$; the corresponding stratum is therefore an affine complex space $\ComplexNumbers^{\AbsoluteValue\lambda}$.  Ordering the strata by inclusion of diagrams yields a CW decomposition with one real cell of dimension $2\AbsoluteValue\lambda$ for each $\lambda$.  Since all cells have even dimension, every cellular boundary map is zero.  Thus the rank of $H^{2j}$ is the number of such partitions of size $j$.  Apply \Cref{thm:rectangle-partitions} with $q=t^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The ``same polynomial'' principle]
For a prime power $Q$, evaluating $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ at $q=Q$ counts rational points of a Grassmannian over $\FiniteField_{Q}$.  Evaluating it at $q=t^2$ records the even Betti numbers of the complex Grassmannian.  This is an elementary instance of the broad principle that polynomial point counts over finite fields often shadow topological invariants over $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{remark}

\section{Growth over finite fields}

\begin{proposition}[Dimension-dominant bounds]\label{prop:qbinom-growth}
For $Q>1$ and $0\le k\le n$,
\[
 Q^{k(n-k)}
 \le \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}
 \le \frac{Q^{k(n-k)}}{(Q^{-1};Q^{-1})_\infty}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Rewrite the product as
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}
 =Q^{k(n-k)}
 \prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1-Q^{-(n-k+j)}}{1-Q^{-j}}.
\]
Each ratio is at least $1$ because $n-k+j\ge j$, proving the lower bound.  For the upper bound, discard the numerator factors and note
\[
 \prod_{j=1}^{k}(1-Q^{-j})\ge\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}(1-Q^{-j})=(Q^{-1};Q^{-1})_\infty.
\]
\end{proof}

\chapter{Symmetry and unimodality}\label{chap:unimodality}
\index{unimodality}
\index{$\mathfrak{sl}_2$}

Palindromicity follows immediately from reciprocity.  Unimodality is subtler: the coefficients rise toward the middle and then fall.  A concise conceptual proof comes from the representation theory of $\mathfrak{sl}_2$.

\section{A representation-theoretic lemma}

Let $\mathfrak{sl}_2(\ComplexNumbers)$ have generators $E,F,H$ with
\[
 [H,E]=2E,\qquad [H,F]=-2F,\qquad [E,F]=H.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Structure of finite-dimensional $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-modules]\label{lem:sl2-structure}
Every finite-dimensional complex $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-module is a direct sum of irreducibles $L_d$ indexed by $d\in\NaturalNumbers$.  The module $L_d$ has dimension $d+1$ and $H$-weights
\[
 d,d-2,\dots,-d,
\]
each with multiplicity one.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We include the standard argument.  Restrict a representation to the compact real form $\mathfrak{su}_2$.  A finite-dimensional Lie-algebra representation integrates to the simply connected compact group $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ by exponentiating the representing matrices; the Lie relations ensure consistency of the local exponentials, and simple connectedness removes monodromy.  Starting from any Hermitian inner product, average it over $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ with normalized Haar measure.  The result is invariant.  Hence the orthogonal complement of every invariant subspace is invariant, proving complete reducibility.

It remains to classify an irreducible module $V$.  In the invariant Hermitian structure, a suitable scalar multiple of $H$ is self-adjoint, so $H$ is diagonalizable.  Choose an eigenvector $v$ whose eigenvalue $d$ has maximal real part.  Since $E$ raises an $H$-weight by $2$, maximality gives $Ev=0$.  The commutation relations imply by induction
\[
 H F^jv=(d-2j)F^jv,
 \qquad
 E F^jv=j(d-j+1)F^{j-1}v.
\]
Because $V$ is finite-dimensional, let $m$ be maximal with $F^mv\ne0$.  Applying $E$ to $F^{m+1}v=0$ gives
\[
 (m+1)(d-m)F^mv=0,
\]
so $d=m\in\NaturalNumbers$.  The vectors $v,Fv,\dots,F^dv$ have distinct weights and are linearly independent.  Their span is invariant under $E,F,H$ and is nonzero; irreducibility makes it all of $V$.  The displayed weights and multiplicities follow.
\end{proof}

\section{Exterior powers and Gaussian coefficients}

Let $L_{n-1}$ have a weight basis $v_0,\dots,v_{n-1}$ with
\(
 Hv_i=(n-1-2i)v_i.
\)

For any finite-dimensional complex $H$-module with an integral weight
decomposition
\[
 V=\bigoplus_{r\in\IntegerNumbers}V_r,
 \qquad
 V_r=\SetOf{v\in V:Hv=rv},
\]
define its weight character, in the explicitly displayed formal invertible
variable $x$, by
\[
 \WeightCharacter{V}{x}
 \DefinitionEquals
 \sum_{r\in\IntegerNumbers}(\dim_{\ComplexNumbers}V_r)x^r
 \in\IntegerNumbers[x,x^{-1}].
\]
Only finitely many $V_r$ are nonzero.  In particular
$\WeightCharacter{0}{x}=0$, and repeated weights are counted through their
dimensions; no unprinted multiplicity or evaluation point is implicit.

\begin{proposition}[Exterior-power character]\label{prop:exterior-character}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\[
 \WeightCharacter{\bigwedge^kL_{n-1}}{x}
 =x^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{x^{-2}}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A basis of the exterior power consists of
\(
 v_{i_1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{i_k}
\)
with $0\le i_1<\cdots<i_k\le n-1$.  Its weight is
\[
 k(n-1)-2(i_1+\cdots+i_k).
\]
Therefore
\[
 \WeightCharacter{\bigwedge^kL_{n-1}}{x}=x^{k(n-1)}
 \sum_{i_1<\cdots<i_k}(x^{-2})^{i_1+\cdots+i_k}.
\]
Use \Cref{cor:weighted-subsets} with $q=x^{-2}$.  The prefactor becomes
\[
 x^{k(n-1)}x^{-2\binom k2}=x^{k(n-k)}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Unimodality of Gaussian coefficients]\label{thm:qbinom-unimodal}
If
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=c_0+c_1q+\cdots+c_Dq^D,
 \qquad D=k(n-k),
\]
then
\[
 c_0\le c_1\le\cdots\le c_{\Floor{D/2}}
\]
and $c_j=c_{D-j}$.  Thus the coefficient sequence is symmetric and unimodal.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Symmetry was proved in \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}.  By \Cref{lem:sl2-structure}, decompose
\[
 \bigwedge^kL_{n-1}\cong\bigoplus_{d\ge0}m_dL_d.
\]
All weights in this exterior power have the same parity as $D$.  The character of one summand $L_d$, placed inside the ambient sequence of weights
\[
 D,D-2,\dots,-D,
\]
contributes a consecutive block of ones centered at weight $0$: it is zero until weight $d$ is reached, then one at every step down to $-d$, then zero again.  As one moves from weight $D$ toward $0$, such a block can turn on but cannot turn off.  A nonnegative sum of these centered blocks therefore has weakly increasing multiplicities toward the middle.

By \Cref{prop:exterior-character}, the multiplicity of weight $D-2j$ is exactly $c_j$.  Hence $c_0\le c_1\le\cdots\le c_{\Floor{D/2}}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The proof explains more than the inequality: the successive differences $c_j-c_{j-1}$ record multiplicities of irreducible summands with prescribed highest weight.  Stronger strict-unimodality results require additional arguments and have a small family of exceptional cases; ordinary unimodality is exactly what the $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ decomposition supplies for free.
\end{remark}


\part{Infinite series and basic hypergeometric summation}

\chapter{The infinite \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial theorem}\label{chap:infinite-qbinomial}
\index{$q$-binomial theorem!infinite}

\section{A functional-equation proof}

\begin{theorem}[Infinite $q$-binomial theorem]\label{thm:infinite-qbinomial}
Let $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and $\AbsoluteValue z<1$.  Then
\[
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a;q)_n}{(q;q)_n}z^n
 =\frac{(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}.
\]
The convergence is locally uniform in $(a,z)$ on compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers\times\SetOf{\AbsoluteValue z<1}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 F(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}c_nz^n,
 \qquad c_n=\frac{(a;q)_n}{(q;q)_n}.
\]
Since $c_{n}/c_{n-1}=(1-aq^{n-1})/(1-q^n)\to1$, the radius of convergence is $1$ unless the series terminates; in every case it converges for $\AbsoluteValue z<1$.  The coefficient recurrence is equivalent to
\[
 (1-q^n)c_n=(1-aq^{n-1})c_{n-1}.
\]
Comparing coefficients shows that
\[
 (1-z)F(z)=(1-az)F(qz),\qquad F(0)=1.\label{eq:qbinom-fe}
\]
Now put
\(
 G(z)=(az;q)_\infty/(z;q)_\infty.
\)
Using $(z;q)_\infty=(1-z)(qz;q)_\infty$ gives the same functional equation and $G(0)=1$.

To prove uniqueness, iterate the functional equation:
\[
 F(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1-azq^j}{1-zq^j}F(q^Nz).
\]
As $N\to\infty$, $q^Nz\to0$, so $F(q^Nz)\to1$.  The finite product tends to $G(z)$.  Hence $F=G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Euler's two infinite identities]\label{cor:euler-infinite}
For $\AbsoluteValue q<1$,
\begin{align}
 (z;q)_\infty
 &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^nq^{\binom n2}}{(q;q)_n}z^n,
 \qquad z\in\ComplexNumbers,\label{eq:euler-product-series}\\
 \frac1{(z;q)_\infty}
 &=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^n}{(q;q)_n},
 \qquad \AbsoluteValue z<1.\label{eq:euler-reciprocal-series}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Set $a=0$ in \Cref{thm:infinite-qbinomial} to obtain \eqref{eq:euler-reciprocal-series}.  For \eqref{eq:euler-product-series}, start with the finite identity
\[
 (z;q)_N=\sum_{n=0}^{N}(-1)^nq^{\binom n2}\GaussianBinomial{N}{n}{q} z^n.
\]
For fixed $n$, $\GaussianBinomial{N}{n}{q}\to1/(q;q)_n$ as $N\to\infty$.  The limiting series converges for every $z$ because its successive-term ratio has magnitude asymptotic to $\AbsoluteValue z\AbsoluteValue q^n$.  To justify passage to the limit uniformly on $\AbsoluteValue z\le R$, note that
\[
 \left|\GaussianBinomial{N}{n}{q}\right|
 \le
 \frac{\prod_{j\ge1}(1+\AbsoluteValue q^j)}
      {\bigl(\inf_{r\ge0}\AbsoluteValue{(q;q)_r}\bigr)^{2}},
% ed.: squared the infimum --- the Gaussian coefficient has two
% ed.: shifted-factorial factors in its denominator, so the derivation
% ed.: bounds each by the infimum separately.
\]
where the denominator is positive because $(q;q)_r\to(q;q)_\infty\ne0$.  Thus the summands, extended by zero for $n>N$, are dominated by a constant multiple of $\AbsoluteValue q^{\binom n2}R^n$.  Dominated convergence, together with local uniform convergence of the products, now permits passage to the limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Both identities as stable limits of finite ones]\label{rem:qbinomial-finite-route}
The formalization proves \Cref{thm:infinite-qbinomial} itself, and not only \eqref{eq:euler-product-series}, by the finite-to-infinite mechanism of the preceding proof, over an arbitrary complete normed field.  Divide the finite $q$-Cauchy identity of \Cref{thm:q-cauchy}, with $u=a$ and $v=z$, by $(z;q)_N$:
\[
 \frac{(az;q)_N}{(z;q)_N}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{N}\GaussianBinomial{N}{k}{q}(a;q)_k z^k\,\frac{(z;q)_{N-k}}{(z;q)_N}.
\]
As $N\to\infty$ the left side tends to $(az;q)_\infty/(z;q)_\infty$, and the $k$-th summand tends to $(a;q)_kz^k/(q;q)_k$ by the fixed-column limit \eqref{eq:fixed-column-limit} and \Cref{thm:poch-entire}.  The summands are dominated, uniformly in $N$, by a geometric sequence in $k$: with
\[
 \mu:=(\AbsoluteValue q;\AbsoluteValue q)_\infty>0,
 \qquad
 M:=(-\AbsoluteValue q;\AbsoluteValue q)_\infty,
\]
one has $\mu\le\AbsoluteValue{(q;q)_n}\le M$ for every $n$, because $\AbsoluteValue{1-q^{j+1}}$ lies between $1-\AbsoluteValue q^{j+1}$ and $1+\AbsoluteValue q^{j+1}$ and the two real products are monotone in $n$; hence
\[
 \left|\GaussianBinomial{N}{k}{q}\right|\le\frac{M}{\mu^2}
 \qquad\text{for all }N\text{ and }k,
\]
and likewise $\AbsoluteValue{(a;q)_k}\le(-\AbsoluteValue a;\AbsoluteValue q)_\infty$, $\AbsoluteValue{(z;q)_{N-k}}\le(-\AbsoluteValue z;\AbsoluteValue q)_\infty$, and $\AbsoluteValue{(z;q)_N}\ge(\AbsoluteValue z;\AbsoluteValue q)_\infty>0$.  Tannery's theorem (dominated convergence for series) then yields the $q$-binomial theorem, and \eqref{eq:euler-reciprocal-series} is its case $a=0$.  This route uses no functional equation and no uniqueness argument; its only analytic input is the fixed-column limit.  It does not by itself give the local uniformity in $(a,z)$ asserted in \Cref{thm:infinite-qbinomial}, which the functional-equation proof supplies.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[A ratio expansion]\label{cor:ratio-expansion}
If $\AbsoluteValue{b_jz}<1$ for $1\le j\le s$, then
\[
 \frac{(a_1z;q)_\infty\cdots(a_rz;q)_\infty}
 {(b_1z;q)_\infty\cdots(b_sz;q)_\infty}
 =\sum_{N\ge0}c_Nz^N,
\]
where
\[
 c_N=\!\sum_{u_1+\cdots+u_r+v_1+\cdots+v_s=N}
 \prod_{i=1}^r
 \frac{(-1)^{u_i}q^{\binom{u_i}{2}}a_i^{u_i}}{(q;q)_{u_i}}
 \prod_{j=1}^s\frac{b_j^{v_j}}{(q;q)_{v_j}}.
\]
When $s=0$ there is no restriction on $z$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Each numerator factor has the entire Euler expansion, while the reciprocal
expansion of $(b_jz;q)_\infty^{-1}$ converges absolutely for
$\AbsoluteValue{b_jz}<1$.  Hence all finitely many series converge absolutely
in the stated common domain, so repeated Cauchy multiplication is legitimate.
Collecting the terms of total degree $N$ gives the displayed finite
convolution.
\end{proof}

\section{Formal and analytic interpretations}

\begin{proposition}[Formal version]\label{prop:formal-qbinomial}
Let $A$ be a commutative ring complete and separated for the $I$-adic
topology, let $q\in I$, and let $a\in A$.  Then the coefficientwise $I$-adic
products are defined in $A[[z]]$, $(z;q)_\infty$ is a unit there, and
\[
 \frac{(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{(a;q)_n}{(q;q)_n}z^n
\]
in $A[[z]]$.  Here every $(q;q)_n$ is a unit.  In particular, with $a$ and
$q$ indeterminates, the identity holds in
$\IntegerNumbers[a][[q]][[z]]$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $j\ge1$ the $j$th product factor differs from $1$ by a coefficient in
$I^j$.  Thus the finite products form coefficientwise $I$-adic Cauchy
sequences and have limits in $A[[z]]$.  Their constant coefficient in $z$ is
$1$, so $(z;q)_\infty$ is invertible.  Also $q^n\in I$, hence
\[
 (1-q^n)^{-1}=\sum_{r\ge0}q^{nr}
\]
converges $I$-adically, and therefore $(q;q)_n$ is a unit.

Write the quotient as $F(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}c_nz^n$.  Splitting off the initial
factors gives
\[
 (1-z)F(z)=(1-az)F(qz),\qquad c_0=1,
\]
and hence
\[
 (1-q^n)c_n=(1-aq^{n-1})c_{n-1},
 \qquad n\ge1.
\]
Because $1-q^n$ is a unit, this recurrence has a unique solution, namely
$c_n=(a;q)_n/(q;q)_n$.
\end{proof}

\section{Limits in the number of factors}

The stronger effective statement \Cref{thm:fixed-column-limit} below gives
both the fixed-column limit and its shifted form, together with a geometric
error term.  This is the stable finite-to-infinite passage used here.

\begin{remark}
The two Euler identities are therefore the stable limits of the finite product and reciprocal-product formulas.  This stability is one reason finite $q$-binomial identities so often serve as polynomial approximations to infinite $q$-series identities.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Basic hypergeometric series}\label{chap:basic-hypergeometric}
\index{basic hypergeometric series}

\section{Definition and convergence}

\begin{definition}\label{qg:def-basic-hypergeometric-series}
The basic hypergeometric series is
\[
 {}_r\phi_s\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}a_1,\dots,a_r\\ b_1,\dots,b_s\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(a_1,\dots,a_r;q)_n}{(q,b_1,\dots,b_s;q)_n}
 \left((-1)^nq^{\binom n2}\right)^{1+s-r}z^n,
\]
provided no denominator factor vanishes.  If one numerator parameter is $q^{-N}$, the series terminates at $n=N$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Elementary convergence classification]\label{prop:phi-convergence}
Assume the series does not terminate and the denominator parameters avoid poles.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item If $r\le s$, the series converges for every $z$.
\item If $r=s+1$, its radius of convergence is $1$.
\item If $r>s+1$, the terms generally fail to tend to zero for $z\ne0$; such series are normally interpreted only when terminating or formally.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $T_n$ be the $n$th summand.  Since $(1-aq^n)\to1$ for each fixed parameter,
\[
 \frac{T_{n+1}}{T_n}
 =z\,\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-a_iq^n)}
 {(1-q^{n+1})\prod_{j=1}^{s}(1-b_jq^n)}
 (-1)^{1+s-r}q^{n(1+s-r)}.
\]
The ratio tends to $0$ when $r\le s$, to $z$ when $r=s+1$, and has unbounded magnitude when $r>s+1$ and $z\ne0$.  Apply the ratio test.
\end{proof}

The infinite $q$-binomial theorem is the evaluation
\[
 {}_1\phi_0\!\left[\begin{matrix}a\\-\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\frac{(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}.
\]

% ---- Insert at the end of \Cref{chap:basic-hypergeometric},
% ---- \section{Definition and convergence}, after the displayed
% ---- ${}_1\phi_0$ evaluation (line 1746-1750) and before
% ---- \section{Heine's transformation}.

\index{basic hypergeometric series!classical limit}
\index{hypergeometric series!ordinary ${}_rF_{r-1}$}

When $s=r-1$ the factor $\bigl((-1)^nq^{\binom n2}\bigr)^{1+s-r}$ in the definition is identically $1$, so that
\[
 {}_r\phi_{r-1}\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}a_1,\dots,a_r\\ b_1,\dots,b_{r-1}\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{\QPochhammer{a_1}{q}{n}\cdots\QPochhammer{a_r}{q}{n}}
      {\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}\QPochhammer{b_1}{q}{n}\cdots\QPochhammer{b_{r-1}}{q}{n}}\,z^n .
\]
Under the scaling $a_i=q^{\alpha_i}$, $b_j=q^{\beta_j}$ this family degenerates as $q\to1^-$ to the ordinary hypergeometric series
\[
 {}_rF_{r-1}\!\left(
 \begin{matrix}\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_r\\ \beta_1,\dots,\beta_{r-1}\end{matrix};z\right)
 \DefinitionEquals\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(\alpha_1)_n\cdots(\alpha_r)_n}
      {n!\,(\beta_1)_n\cdots(\beta_{r-1})_n}\,z^n,
 \qquad
 (\alpha)_n=\alpha(\alpha+1)\cdots(\alpha+n-1),
\]
and -- this is the point of the next theorem -- the degeneration is valid for the full nonterminating series, not only for the terminating specializations tabulated in \Cref{app:limits}.

\begin{theorem}[Classical limit of a nonterminating ${}_r\phi_{r-1}$]\label{thm:classical-hypergeometric-limit}
Let $r\ge1$ and let $\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_r,\beta_1,\dots,\beta_{r-1}\in\ComplexNumbers$ satisfy
\[
 \beta_j\notin\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}\qquad(1\le j\le r-1).
\]
For $0<q<1$ let $q^{c}=\EulerE^{c\log q}$ with the real logarithm.  Then there is $q_0\in(0,1)$ such that for $q_0<q<1$ no denominator factor of
\[
 \mathscr H_q(z)\DefinitionEquals
 {}_r\phi_{r-1}\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{\alpha_1},\dots,q^{\alpha_r}\\
               q^{\beta_1},\dots,q^{\beta_{r-1}}\end{matrix};q,z\right]
\]
vanishes, and for every $\rho\in(0,1)$
\[
 \lim_{q\to1^-}\mathscr H_q(z)
 ={}_rF_{r-1}\!\left(
 \begin{matrix}\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_r\\ \beta_1,\dots,\beta_{r-1}\end{matrix};z\right)
\]
uniformly for $\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$.  Both sides are analytic in $\AbsoluteValue z<1$.  If no $\alpha_i$ lies in $\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}$, both series have radius of convergence exactly $1$: for the basic hypergeometric function $\mathscr H_q$ this is \Cref{prop:phi-convergence}(ii), and for ${}_rF_{r-1}$ the ratio of consecutive terms is
\[
 \frac{z\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\alpha_i+n)}{(n+1)\prod_{j=1}^{r-1}(\beta_j+n)}\longrightarrow z
 \qquad(n\to\infty).
\]
If some $\alpha_i$ is a nonpositive integer, both series terminate at the same
degree, and the asserted limit follows term by term; see
\Cref{rem:classical-limit-terminating}.
\end{theorem}

% ed.: the source omits the hypothesis $\beta_j\notin\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}$ (without
% ed.: which the limiting ${}_rF_{r-1}$ is undefined) and says nothing about the
% ed.: vanishing of the $q$-denominators; Step 1 supplies both.
% ed.: the source also asserts that on compact subdisks ``term ratios are
% ed.: uniformly bounded by a number below $1$ for $q$ sufficiently close to $1$''.
% ed.: This is false for small $n$: for $r=2$, $\alpha=(5,5)$, $\beta=(1)$, $z=2/5$
% ed.: the $n=0$ ratio tends to $z\cdot 5\cdot5/(1\cdot1)=10$ as $q\to1^-$.  The
% ed.: correct argument, given in Steps 3--4, bounds the ratios only for
% ed.: $m\ge m_0$ and absorbs the finitely many earlier terms into a constant.

\begin{proof}
Throughout put $\beta_r\DefinitionEquals1$, so that $\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}=\QPochhammer{q^{\beta_r}}{q}{n}$ and the $n$th summand
\[
 T_n(q)\DefinitionEquals
 \frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\QPochhammer{q^{\alpha_i}}{q}{n}}
      {\prod_{i=1}^{r}\QPochhammer{q^{\beta_i}}{q}{n}}\,z^n
\]
has equally many Pochhammer factors above and below.

\emph{Step 1: no denominator vanishes near $q=1$.}
A factor $1-q^{\beta+m}$ with $m\ge0$ vanishes exactly when $(\beta+m)\log q\in2\pi i\IntegerNumbers$.  Writing $\beta+m=\sigma+i\tau$ and using that $\log q$ is real and nonzero, this forces $\sigma=0$ together with $\tau\log q\in2\pi\IntegerNumbers$.  If $\tau=\Im\beta=0$, the two conditions say $\beta=-m$, which the hypothesis excludes for $\beta=\beta_1,\dots,\beta_{r-1}$ and which is false for $\beta_r=1$.  If $\Im\beta\ne0$, then $\tau\log q$ is a nonzero multiple of $2\pi$, whence $\AbsoluteValue{\log q}\ge2\pi/\AbsoluteValue{\Im\beta}$.  Taking
\[
 q_0=\max\SetOf{\exp\!\bigl(-2\pi/\AbsoluteValue{\Im\beta_j}\bigr):\Im\beta_j\ne0}
\]
(and $q_0$ arbitrary in $(0,1)$ if all $\beta_j$ are real) rules out every vanishing for $q_0<q<1$.

\emph{Step 2: termwise convergence.}
Fix $n$ and divide each of the $2r$ Pochhammer symbols by $(1-q)^n$; the powers of $(1-q)$ cancel, so
\[
 T_n(q)=
 \frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\QPochhammer{q^{\alpha_i}}{q}{n}(1-q)^{-n}}
      {\prod_{i=1}^{r}\QPochhammer{q^{\beta_i}}{q}{n}(1-q)^{-n}}\,z^n .
\]
By \Cref{prop:classical-poch-limit} the $i$th numerator factor tends to $(\alpha_i)_n$ and the $i$th denominator factor to $(\beta_i)_n$, with $(\beta_r)_n=(1)_n=n!$.  The hypothesis on the $\beta_j$ makes every $(\beta_i)_n$ nonzero, so
\[
 \lim_{q\to1^-}T_n(q)=t_n\DefinitionEquals
 \frac{(\alpha_1)_n\cdots(\alpha_r)_n}
      {n!\,(\beta_1)_n\cdots(\beta_{r-1})_n}z^n .
\]

\emph{Step 3: two elementary estimates.}
Put $t=-\log q>0$, so that $q^{w}=\EulerE^{-wt}$, and set $h(u)=1-\EulerE^{-ut}$ for $u\ge0$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item If $w=\sigma+i\tau$ with $\sigma>0$, then
\[
 h(\sigma)\le\AbsoluteValue{1-\EulerE^{-wt}}\le h(\sigma)+\AbsoluteValue\tau\,t .
\]
Indeed, with $\varrho=\EulerE^{-\sigma t}\in(0,1)$ and $\phi=-\tau t$,
$\AbsoluteValue{1-\varrho \EulerE^{i\phi}}^2=(1-\varrho)^2+2\varrho(1-\cos\phi)$.  Discarding the nonnegative second term gives the lower bound; using $1-\cos\phi\le\phi^2/2$ and $\varrho\le1$ gives $\AbsoluteValue{1-\varrho \EulerE^{i\phi}}^2\le(1-\varrho)^2+(\tau t)^2\le\bigl((1-\varrho)+\AbsoluteValue\tau t\bigr)^2$.
\item $h$ is increasing and concave on $[0,\infty)$ with $h(0)=0$, so $u\mapsto h(u)/u$ is nonincreasing and therefore
\[
 \frac{h(v)}{h(u)}\le\max\Bigl(1,\frac vu\Bigr)\qquad(u,v>0).
\]
Moreover $\EulerE^{ut}\ge1+ut$ gives $h(u)\ge ut/(1+ut)$, that is, $h(u)^{-1}\le(ut)^{-1}+1$.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Step 4: a majorant uniform in $q$ and in $\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$.}
Set $K=1+\max_{i}\AbsoluteValue{\alpha_i}+\max_{i}\AbsoluteValue{\beta_i}$ and fix an integer $m_0\ge2K$.  For $m\ge m_0$ the real numbers $\sigma_i=\Re\alpha_i+m$ and $\gamma_i=\Re\beta_i+m$ are at least $m/2>0$.  The ratio of consecutive summands is
\[
 \frac{T_{m+1}(q)}{T_m(q)}
 =z\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1-q^{\alpha_i+m}}{1-q^{\beta_i+m}},
\]
and for $m\ge m_0$ and every $t>0$, Step 3 gives
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{\frac{1-q^{\alpha_i+m}}{1-q^{\beta_i+m}}}
 \le\frac{h(\sigma_i)+\AbsoluteValue{\Im\alpha_i}t}{h(\gamma_i)}
 \le\max\Bigl(1,\frac{\sigma_i}{\gamma_i}\Bigr)
  +\AbsoluteValue{\Im\alpha_i}t\Bigl(\frac1{\gamma_it}+1\Bigr)
 \le1+\frac{6K}m+Kt,
\]
because $\max(1,\sigma_i/\gamma_i)\le1+\AbsoluteValue{\Re\alpha_i-\Re\beta_i}/\gamma_i\le1+4K/m$ and $\AbsoluteValue{\Im\alpha_i}/\gamma_i\le2K/m$.  Put $C=6rK$, fix $\rho\in(0,1)$, choose $t_1>0$ with $\EulerE^{rKt_1}\le\rho^{-1/2}$, and set $\theta=\rho^{1/2}<1$.  For $\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$, $0<t\le t_1$ and $m\ge m_0$,
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{\frac{T_{m+1}(q)}{T_m(q)}}
 \le\rho\Bigl(1+\frac{6K}m+Kt_1\Bigr)^{r}
 \le\rho\,\EulerE^{rKt_1}\EulerE^{C/m}
 \le\theta\,\EulerE^{C/m}.
\]
Multiplying these bounds and using $\sum_{m=m_0}^{n-1}m^{-1}\le1+\log(n/m_0)$,
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{T_n(q)}\le\AbsoluteValue{T_{m_0}(q)}\,\theta^{\,n-m_0}\,\EulerE^{C}\Bigl(\frac n{m_0}\Bigr)^{C}
 \qquad(n>m_0).
\]
By Step 2 the finitely many functions $T_0,\dots,T_{m_0}$ converge as $q\to1^-$, hence are bounded, say by $B$, on some interval $(q_1,1)$; the bound is uniform in $\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$ because $T_n(q)$ is $z^n$ times a function of $q$ alone.  Therefore, with
\[
 M_n\DefinitionEquals B\ \ (n\le m_0),
 \qquad
 M_n\DefinitionEquals B\,\EulerE^{C}m_0^{-C}\,n^{C}\theta^{\,n-m_0}\ \ (n>m_0),
\]
one has $\AbsoluteValue{T_n(q)}\le M_n$ for all $n\ge0$, all $\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$ and all $q$ with $\max\bigl(q_0,q_1,\EulerE^{-t_1}\bigr)<q<1$, while $\sum_{n\ge0}M_n<\infty$ because $\theta<1$ dominates the polynomial factor $n^{C}$.

\emph{Step 5: interchange.}
The series $\sum_nT_n(q)$ is dominated by the summable sequence $(M_n)$ uniformly in $q$, so it converges uniformly in $q$ on that interval; each term has the limit $t_n$ by Step 2.  Dominated convergence with respect to counting measure (Tannery's theorem) therefore gives
\[
 \lim_{q\to1^-}\sum_{n\ge0}T_n(q)=\sum_{n\ge0}t_n,
\]
which is the asserted limit.  % ed.: the draft concluded uniformity from a $z$-independent majorant
% ed.: alone, which gives only pointwise convergence in $z$; the head/tail
% ed.: split below is what actually delivers it.
For the uniformity, note first that letting $q\to1^-$ in
$\AbsoluteValue{T_n(q)}\le M_n$ gives $\AbsoluteValue{t_n}\le M_n$ as well.  Let $\varepsilon>0$ and
choose $N>m_0$ with $\sum_{n>N}M_n<\varepsilon/3$.  Writing $T_n(q)=z^ng_n(q)$ and
$t_n=z^ng_n(1^-)$, where $g_n$ does not involve $z$, one has for every
$\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$
\[
 \Bigl\lvert\sum_{n\ge0}T_n(q)-\sum_{n\ge0}t_n\Bigr\rvert
 \le\sum_{n\le N}\AbsoluteValue{g_n(q)-g_n(1^-)}+2\sum_{n>N}M_n,
\]
because $\AbsoluteValue z^n\le1$.  The first sum is a finite sum of quantities
independent of $z$, each tending to $0$ by Step 2, hence is $<\varepsilon/3$ once
$q$ is close enough to $1$.  The convergence is therefore uniform on
$\AbsoluteValue z\le\rho$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The terminating case]\label{rem:classical-limit-terminating}
If some $\alpha_i$ is a nonpositive integer $-N$, the numerator factor $\QPochhammer{q^{-N}}{q}{n}$ vanishes for $n>N$, the series is a finite sum, and neither the restriction $\AbsoluteValue z<1$ nor Steps 3--5 are needed: applying \Cref{prop:classical-poch-limit} to each of the $N+1$ terms suffices.  That elementary mechanism is what underlies the itemized list in \Cref{app:limits}; \Cref{thm:classical-hypergeometric-limit} is its nonterminating counterpart, and the analytic work in Steps 3--5 is exactly the price of dropping the terminating hypothesis.
\end{remark}

\section{Heine's transformation}

\begin{theorem}[Heine transformation]\label{thm:heine}
Initially for $\AbsoluteValue z<1$ and $\AbsoluteValue b<1$,
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[\begin{matrix}a,b\\c\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\frac{(b,az;q)_\infty}{(c,z;q)_\infty}
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[\begin{matrix}c/b,z\\az\end{matrix};q,b\right].
\]
The identity extends wherever both sides are defined by analytic continuation.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the tail-factor identity to write
\[
 \frac{(b;q)_n}{(c;q)_n}
 =\frac{(b;q)_\infty}{(c;q)_\infty}
 \frac{(cq^n;q)_\infty}{(bq^n;q)_\infty}.
\]
By the infinite $q$-binomial theorem,
\[
 \frac{(cq^n;q)_\infty}{(bq^n;q)_\infty}
 =\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(c/b;q)_k}{(q;q)_k}b^kq^{nk}.
\]
Insert this into the ${}_2\phi_1$ series and interchange the absolutely convergent sums:
\[
 \frac{(b;q)_\infty}{(c;q)_\infty}
 \sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(c/b;q)_k}{(q;q)_k}b^k
 \sum_{n\ge0}\frac{(a;q)_n}{(q;q)_n}(zq^k)^n.
\]
The inner sum is
\[
 \frac{(azq^k;q)_\infty}{(zq^k;q)_\infty}
 =\frac{(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}
 \frac{(z;q)_k}{(az;q)_k}.
\]
Collecting factors yields the claimed transformation.  Both sides are meromorphic functions of the parameters, so equality extends from the initial open domain by the identity theorem.
\end{proof}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Gauss sum}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Gauss summation]\label{thm:q-gauss}
If $\AbsoluteValue{c/(ab)}<1$, then
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}a,b\\c\end{matrix};q,\frac{c}{ab}\right]
 =\frac{(c/a,c/b;q)_\infty}{(c,c/(ab);q)_\infty}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $z=c/(ab)$ in \Cref{thm:heine}.  Then $az=c/b$, so one numerator parameter of the transformed series cancels its denominator parameter:
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[\begin{matrix}c/b,c/(ab)\\c/b\end{matrix};q,b\right]
 ={}_1\phi_0\!\left[\begin{matrix}c/(ab)\\-\end{matrix};q,b\right]
 =\frac{(c/a;q)_\infty}{(b;q)_\infty}.
\]
Multiplication by the prefactor in Heine's transformation gives the result.  The initially stronger restrictions are removed by analytic continuation within the convergence domain.
\end{proof}

\section{A second finite Cauchy identity}

\begin{lemma}[Cauchy convolution II]\label{lem:cauchy-II}
For $a,b,c$ in a commutative ring,
\[
 (ac;q)_n(bc;q)_n
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 (a;q)_k(b;q)_k c^k(c;q)_{n-k}(abcq^k;q)_{n-k}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply the $q$-Cauchy identity \Cref{thm:q-cauchy} with $u=bq^k$, $v=ac$, and length $n-k$:
\[
 (abcq^k;q)_{n-k}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n-k}{r}{q}
 (bq^k;q)_r(ac)^r(ac;q)_{n-k-r}.
\]
Insert this in the right side of the proposed identity.  Use
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n-k}{r}{q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n}{k+r}{q}\GaussianBinomial{k+r}{k}{q},
 \qquad
 (b;q)_k(bq^k;q)_r=(b;q)_{k+r}.
\]
Put $m=k+r$ and use
\[
 (c;q)_{n-k}=(c;q)_{n-m}(cq^{n-m};q)_{m-k}.
\]
The double sum becomes
\[
 \sum_{m=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{m}{q}(b;q)_m c^m(c;q)_{n-m}(ac;q)_{n-m}
 \sum_{k=0}^{m}\GaussianBinomial{m}{k}{q}(a;q)_k a^{m-k}
 (cq^{n-m};q)_{m-k}.
\]
In the inner sum replace $j=m-k$ and apply \Cref{thm:q-cauchy} with $u=cq^{n-m}$ and $v=a$; it equals $(acq^{n-m};q)_m$.  Hence
\[
 (ac;q)_{n-m}(acq^{n-m};q)_m=(ac;q)_n
\]
can be factored out.  The remaining sum is
\[
 (ac;q)_n\sum_{m=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{m}{q}(b;q)_m c^m(c;q)_{n-m}
 =(ac;q)_n(bc;q)_n
\]
by one final application of the $q$-Cauchy identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal version]
The displayed identity is
\lean{Fabius.finite_qCauchy_second_identity} in
\path{QBinomialCauchy.lean}.  It holds for arbitrary \(q,a,b,c\) in every
commutative ring and every \(n\in\NaturalNumbers\), including \(n=0\), \(q=0\), roots of
unity, positive characteristic, and rings with zero divisors.  The formal
proof expands and reindexes a finite triangle and applies the primary and
reflected finite \(q\)-Cauchy identities; it uses no quotient, cancellation,
or nonvanishing assumption.  This exact finite convolution does not by itself
formalize the quotient-bearing \(q\)-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz theorem that follows.
\end{remark}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz sum}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz summation]\label{thm:q-pfaff}
For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ and parameters for which all displayed
expressions are defined,
\[
 {}_3\phi_2\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}a,b,q^{-n}\\c,abq^{1-n}/c\end{matrix};q,q\right]
 =\frac{(c/a,c/b;q)_n}{(c,c/(ab);q)_n}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In \Cref{lem:cauchy-II}, replace its parameters by
\[
 a\mapsto a,\qquad b\mapsto b,\qquad c\mapsto \frac{c}{ab}.
\]
This gives
\[
 \frac{(c/a,c/b;q)_n}{(c,c/(ab);q)_n}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}(a,b;q)_k
 \left(\frac{c}{ab}\right)^k
 \frac{(c/(ab);q)_{n-k}(cq^k;q)_{n-k}}
 {(c,c/(ab);q)_n}.
\]
We show that the $k$th summand is the $k$th term of the stated ${}_3\phi_2$.  The tail identities give
\[
 \frac{(cq^k;q)_{n-k}}{(c;q)_n}=\frac1{(c;q)_k}.
\]
Also, reversal of the two finite products gives
\[
 (q^{-n};q)_k
 =(-1)^kq^{-nk+\binom k2}(q^{n-k+1};q)_k,
\]
\[
 (abq^{1-n}/c;q)_k
 =\left(-\frac{ab}{c}\right)^k
 q^{k(1-n)+\binom k2}
 (cq^{n-k}/(ab);q)_k.
\]
Therefore
\begin{align*}
 &\frac{(q^{-n};q)_k q^k}{(q;q)_k(abq^{1-n}/c;q)_k}
 \frac{(c/(ab);q)_n}{(c/(ab);q)_{n-k}}\\
 &\quad=\left(\frac{c}{ab}\right)^k
 \frac{(q^{n-k+1};q)_k}{(q;q)_k}
 \frac{(c/(ab);q)_n}
 {(c/(ab);q)_{n-k}(cq^{n-k}/(ab);q)_k}\\
 &\quad=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\left(\frac{c}{ab}\right)^k.
\end{align*}
Substitution yields exactly
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{(a,b,q^{-n};q)_k}{(q,c,abq^{1-n}/c;q)_k}q^k.
\]
\end{proof}

\section{The two \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Chu--Vandermonde sums}

\begin{corollary}[$q$-Chu--Vandermonde]\label{cor:q-chu}
For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$, wherever the displayed rational expressions are
defined,
\begin{align}
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},a\\c\end{matrix};q,\frac{cq^n}{a}\right]
 &=\frac{(c/a;q)_n}{(c;q)_n},\label{eq:qchu1}\\
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},a\\c\end{matrix};q,q\right]
 &=a^n\frac{(c/a;q)_n}{(c;q)_n}.
 \label{eq:qchu2}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For \eqref{eq:qchu1}, put $b=q^{-n}$ in the $q$-Gauss sum.  The infinite products telescope:
\[
 \frac{(cq^n,c/a;q)_\infty}{(c,cq^n/a;q)_\infty}
 =\frac{(c/a;q)_n}{(c;q)_n}.
\]
% ed.: the justification here formerly read ``Because the series terminates,
% ed.: no convergence restriction remains.''  Termination makes both sides
% ed.: DEFINED for all $(a,c)$, but it does not extend the EQUALITY, since the
% ed.: product side was produced under the $q$-Gauss hypothesis
% ed.: $\abs{cq^n/a}<1$.  What extends it is rationality plus agreement on a
% ed.: nonempty open set, as stated below.
On the nonempty seed domain $\AbsoluteValue{cq^n/a}<1$, substitution of
$b=q^{-n}$ is legitimate directly in \Cref{thm:q-gauss}; its convergence
hypothesis becomes precisely that inequality, and
$(q^{-n};q)_k=0$ for $k>n$.  Both sides of \eqref{eq:qchu1} are rational in
$(a,c)$, and they agree on this open set wherever the displayed denominators
are nonzero.  After taking their difference and clearing denominators, the
resulting polynomial vanishes on a nonempty open subset of
$\ComplexNumbers^2$, hence vanishes identically.  Thus the identity holds
wherever both sides are defined.  Termination alone would not give this: it
makes the two sides defined, not equal.

For \eqref{eq:qchu2}, let $b\to0$ in \Cref{thm:q-pfaff}, with its other numerator parameter equal to $a$.  Then $(b;q)_k\to1$ and $(abq^{1-n}/c;q)_k\to1$, so the left side becomes the displayed ${}_2\phi_1$.  On the right,
\[
 \frac{(c/b;q)_n}{(c/(ab);q)_n}\longrightarrow a^n
\]
by comparing the leading factors of the two degree-$n$ polynomials in $1/b$.
\end{proof}

% -----------------------------------------------------------------
% INSERTION 1 of 1 for this item.
% Chapter: \ref{chap:basic-hypergeometric} ("Basic hypergeometric series"),
% section "The two $q$-Chu--Vandermonde sums".
% Place immediately after the \end{proof} of \Cref{cor:q-chu}
% (MONOGRAPH line 1943) and before \section{A hierarchy of mechanisms}.
% -----------------------------------------------------------------



\begin{lemma}[Reversal of a terminating ${}_2\phi_1$]\label{lem:2phi1-reversal}
\index{basic hypergeometric series!reversal of a terminating sum}
Let $n\in\NaturalNumbers$, let $q\ne0$, and let $a$, $c$, $z$ be nonzero with
$(c;q)_n\ne0$ and $(q^{1-n}/a;q)_n\ne0$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},a\\c\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =(-1)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}z^{n}\frac{(a;q)_n}{(c;q)_n}\,
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},\,q^{1-n}/c\\[2pt] q^{1-n}/a\end{matrix};q,
 \,\frac{cq^{n+1}}{az}\right].
 \label{eq:2phi1-reversal}
\end{equation}
Both series terminate at $n$, so \eqref{eq:2phi1-reversal} is an identity between rational functions of $a$, $c$, $z$ over $\RationalNumbers(q)$; no convergence hypothesis enters.  The substitution $(a,c)\mapsto(q^{1-n}/c,\,q^{1-n}/a)$ is an involution, and applying \eqref{eq:2phi1-reversal} twice returns the original series.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $r=2$ and $s=1$, the factor $\bigl((-1)^jq^{\binom j2}\bigr)^{1+s-r}$ in the definition of ${}_2\phi_1$ equals $1$, so the left side is $\sum_{j=0}^{n}T_j$ with
\[
 T_j=\frac{(q^{-n};q)_j(a;q)_j}{(q;q)_j(c;q)_j}\,z^{j}.
\]
We reverse the order of summation, writing $j=n-i$ with $0\le i\le n$.

First record the reversal rule for a single finite shifted factorial.  Let $x\ne0$.  The concatenation law \Cref{prop:concat} gives $(x;q)_n=(x;q)_{n-i}(xq^{n-i};q)_i$, and base reversal \Cref{prop:base-reversal}, applied to the second factor, gives
\[
 (xq^{n-i};q)_i=(-x)^iq^{(n-i)i+\binom i2}\,(q^{1-n}/x;q)_i .
\]
Because $(n-i)i+\binom i2=ni-\binom{i+1}{2}$, these combine into
\begin{equation}
 (x;q)_{n-i}=(x;q)_n\,
 \frac{(-1)^i x^{-i}q^{\binom{i+1}{2}-ni}}{(q^{1-n}/x;q)_i}.
 \label{eq:poch-reversal-rule}
\end{equation}

Now apply \eqref{eq:poch-reversal-rule} to each of the four shifted factorials occurring in $T_{n-i}$.  The four bases $q^{-n},a,q,c$ reflect to $q,\,q^{1-n}/a,\,q^{-n},\,q^{1-n}/c$ respectively, the first and third of which are again members of the list (the reflection of $q^{-n}$ is $q$ and that of $q$ is $q^{-n}$, while $q^{1-n}/a$ and $q^{1-n}/c$ are new).  Explicitly,
\begin{align*}
 (q^{-n};q)_{n-i}&=(q^{-n};q)_n\,
 \frac{(-1)^iq^{\binom{i+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_i},
 &
 (a;q)_{n-i}&=(a;q)_n\,
 \frac{(-1)^ia^{-i}q^{\binom{i+1}{2}-ni}}{(q^{1-n}/a;q)_i},\\
 (q;q)_{n-i}&=(q;q)_n\,
 \frac{(-1)^iq^{\binom{i+1}{2}-ni-i}}{(q^{-n};q)_i},
 &
 (c;q)_{n-i}&=(c;q)_n\,
 \frac{(-1)^ic^{-i}q^{\binom{i+1}{2}-ni}}{(q^{1-n}/c;q)_i}.
\end{align*}
Form the quotient $\bigl[(q^{-n};q)_{n-i}(a;q)_{n-i}\bigr]\big/\bigl[(q;q)_{n-i}(c;q)_{n-i}\bigr]$.  The four factors $(-1)^i$ cancel in pairs.  The four powers of $q$ contribute
\[
 \binom{i+1}{2}
 +\left(\binom{i+1}{2}-ni\right)
 -\left(\binom{i+1}{2}-ni-i\right)
 -\left(\binom{i+1}{2}-ni\right)
 =ni+i,
\]
the scalars contribute $a^{-i}c^{\,i}$, and the reflected shifted factorials
contribute
\[
 \frac{(q^{-n};q)_i\,(q^{1-n}/c;q)_i}{(q;q)_i\,(q^{1-n}/a;q)_i}.
\]
Multiplying by $z^{n-i}=z^{n}z^{-i}$ and collecting the $i$-dependent
scalars as $\bigl(cq^{n+1}/(az)\bigr)^{i}$, we obtain
\[
 T_{n-i}
 =\frac{(q^{-n};q)_n(a;q)_n}{(q;q)_n(c;q)_n}\,z^{n}\;
 \frac{(q^{-n};q)_i\,(q^{1-n}/c;q)_i}
 {(q;q)_i\,(q^{1-n}/a;q)_i}
 \left(\frac{cq^{n+1}}{az}\right)^{i}.
\]
By \Cref{cor:qminusN} with $N=k=n$,
\[
 (q^{-n};q)_n=(-1)^nq^{-n^2+\binom n2}(q;q)_n
 =(-1)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n,
\]
so the constant in front is $(-1)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}z^{n}(a;q)_n/(c;q)_n$.  Summing over $0\le i\le n$ gives \eqref{eq:2phi1-reversal}.  The final assertion follows because the reflected parameters are $a'=q^{1-n}/c$ and $c'=q^{1-n}/a$, so that $c'q^{\,n+1}/(a'z')=\bigl[(q^{1-n}/a)q^{\,n+1}\bigr]\big/\bigl[(q^{1-n}/c)\cdot cq^{\,n+1}/(az)\bigr]=z$, and the reflection is therefore an involution on the parameter pair.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The second $q$-Chu--Vandermonde sum by reversal]\label{prop:qchu2-by-reversal}
Identity \eqref{eq:qchu2} is a formal consequence of \eqref{eq:qchu1}: it follows from \Cref{lem:2phi1-reversal} alone, without \Cref{thm:q-pfaff}, without \Cref{lem:cauchy-II}, and without any limiting process.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ and nonzero parameters $A,C$ with $(A;q)_n\ne0$ and $(C;q)_n\ne0$.  Put
\[
 a=\frac{q^{1-n}}{C},\qquad
 c=\frac{q^{1-n}}{A},\qquad
 z=\frac{cq^{n}}{a}=\frac{Cq^{n}}{A}.
\]
These are nonzero, and by \Cref{prop:base-reversal} the hypotheses of \Cref{lem:2phi1-reversal} hold: $(c;q)_n=(q^{1-n}/A;q)_n=(-A)^{-n}q^{-\binom n2}(A;q)_n\ne0$ and $(q^{1-n}/a;q)_n=(C;q)_n\ne0$.  Since $az=cq^{n}$, the reflected argument in \eqref{eq:2phi1-reversal} is $cq^{n+1}/(az)=q$, and the reflected parameters are $q^{1-n}/c=A$ and $q^{1-n}/a=C$.  Hence \eqref{eq:2phi1-reversal} reads
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},a\\c\end{matrix};q,\frac{cq^n}{a}\right]
 =(-1)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}
 \left(\frac{cq^{n}}{a}\right)^{n}
 \frac{(a;q)_n}{(c;q)_n}\,
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},A\\ C\end{matrix};q,q\right].
\]
% ed.: the ARTICLE asserts that reversal returns \eqref{eq:qchu2} at the SAME
% ed.: parameters $(a,c)$; it in fact returns it at the reflected pair
% ed.: $(q^{1-n}/c,\,q^{1-n}/a)$.  Since that substitution is an involution of
% ed.: $(\C^*)^2$, nothing is lost --- every admissible pair is reached --- but
% ed.: the reflection must be carried explicitly, as here.
The left side is $(c/a;q)_n/(c;q)_n$ by \eqref{eq:qchu1}, and $c/a=C/A$.  Solving for the remaining series and using $a/(cq^{n})=A/(Cq^{n})$,
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},A\\ C\end{matrix};q,q\right]
 =(-1)^nq^{\binom{n+1}{2}}
 \left(\frac{A}{Cq^{n}}\right)^{n}
 \frac{(C/A;q)_n}{(a;q)_n}.
\]
By \Cref{prop:base-reversal} once more, $(a;q)_n=(q^{1-n}/C;q)_n=(-C)^{-n}q^{-\binom n2}(C;q)_n$.  Substituting and cancelling $C^{n}$ and the two signs,
\[
 {}_2\phi_1\!\left[
 \begin{matrix}q^{-n},A\\ C\end{matrix};q,q\right]
 =q^{\binom{n+1}{2}+\binom n2-n^2}\,A^{n}\frac{(C/A;q)_n}{(C;q)_n}
 =A^{n}\frac{(C/A;q)_n}{(C;q)_n},
\]
because $\binom{n+1}{2}+\binom n2=\tfrac{n^2+n}{2}+\tfrac{n^2-n}{2}=n^2$.  This is \eqref{eq:qchu2} at the pair $(A,C)$, which was an arbitrary admissible pair.  The auxiliary condition $(A;q)_n\ne0$ excludes only a proper algebraic
subset of the parameter space, and both sides of \eqref{eq:qchu2} are
rational functions of $(A,C)$ there; agreeing off a proper algebraic
subset, they agree wherever both are defined.  This is the same
identity-theorem step used for \eqref{eq:qchu1} in the proof of
\Cref{cor:q-chu}.
% ed.: the draft justified this by \Cref{lem:polynomial-identity-principle}
% ed.: of chapter 20 -- a silent forward reference across eleven chapters,
% ed.: and to a lemma stated for a single variable $q$ over $\Q$ rather than
% ed.: for $(A,C)$ over $\C$.  Chapter 9's own idiom settles it with neither
% ed.: problem.
Every step used only finite products, so, after multiplication by $A^{-n}$ is undone and denominators are cleared, \eqref{eq:qchu2} holds over any commutative ring in which $q$ and $A$ are invertible.
\end{proof}

% -----------------------------------------------------------------
% AMENDMENT to \section{A hierarchy of mechanisms}, MONOGRAPH lines 1945--1960.
% Replace the second display and the closing paragraph by the following.
% -----------------------------------------------------------------

\section{A hierarchy of mechanisms}

The proofs in this chapter form a hierarchy:
\[
 \text{finite $q$-Pascal}
 \Longrightarrow \text{$q$-Cauchy}
 \Longrightarrow \text{$q$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz},
\]
while
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \text{infinite $q$-binomial}
 \Longrightarrow \text{Heine}
 \Longrightarrow \text{$q$-Gauss}\\
 \Longrightarrow \text{first $q$-Chu--Vandermonde}
 \Longrightarrow \text{second $q$-Chu--Vandermonde},
\end{gathered}
\]
the last arrow being \Cref{prop:qchu2-by-reversal}.  The finite chain is valid in a commutative ring after denominators are cleared.  The infinite chain requires convergence or a suitable complete formal topology, but only as far as its third arrow: the first $q$-Chu--Vandermonde sum is already a rational, hence purely algebraic, identity, as the proof of \Cref{cor:q-chu} records, and by \Cref{lem:2phi1-reversal} the fourth arrow is finite-product algebra.  Thus \eqref{eq:qchu2} does not depend on \Cref{thm:q-pfaff}, and the two derivations of it given here are genuinely independent.  This distinction becomes important in computer formalization; see \Cref{chap:formalization}.

\input{chapters/09_very_well_poised_summations}

\chapter{Bilateral series and Ramanujan's \texorpdfstring{${}_1\psi_1$}{1psi1} sum}\label{chap:bilateral}
\index{Ramanujan ${}_1\psi_1$ summation}
\index{bilateral basic hypergeometric series}

\section{Definition}

For integral $n$, negative $q$-shifted factorials are understood as in
\Cref{prop:negative-index}.
\begin{definition}[Bilateral basic hypergeometric series]
\label{qg:def-bilateral-basic-hypergeometric}
Define
\[
 {}_1\psi_1\!\left[\begin{matrix}a\\b\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}
   \frac{\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{n}}
        {\GeneralizedQPochhammer{b}{q}{n}}z^n.
\]
The positive tail behaves like a geometric series in $z$, while the negative tail behaves like a geometric series in $b/(az)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Convergence annulus]\label{prop:1psi1-convergence}
If denominator factors do not vanish, the ${}_1\psi_1$ series converges absolutely in
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{b/a}<\AbsoluteValue z<1.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $n\to+\infty$, the ratio of consecutive terms tends to $z$.  For the negative tail put $n=-m$ and use the negative-index formula.  The ratio of successive terms in $m$ tends to $b/(az)$.  The ratio test gives the annulus.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Ramanujan's ${}_1\psi_1$ summation]\label{thm:1psi1}
For $\AbsoluteValue{b/a}<\AbsoluteValue z<1$,
\[
 {}_1\psi_1\!\left[\begin{matrix}a\\b\end{matrix};q,z\right]
 =\frac{(q,b/a,az,q/(az);q)_\infty}
 {(b,q/a,z,b/(az);q)_\infty}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the formula for $b=q^{m+1}$, where $m\in\NaturalNumbers$.
In that case
$1/\GeneralizedQPochhammer{b}{q}{n}=0$ for $n<-m$ in the limiting sense,
so shift $n=k-m$:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}
 \frac{\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{n}}
      {\GeneralizedQPochhammer{q^{m+1}}{q}{n}}z^n
 &=z^{-m}\frac{\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{-m}}
                  {\GeneralizedQPochhammer{q^{m+1}}{q}{-m}}
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(aq^{-m};q)_k}{(q;q)_k}z^k\\
 &=z^{-m}\frac{(q;q)_m}{(aq^{-m};q)_m}
 \frac{(aq^{-m}z;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}.
\end{align*}
The second equality is the infinite $q$-binomial theorem.  Reverse the finite products:
\[
 (aq^{-m};q)_m=(-a)^mq^{-m(m+1)/2}(q/a;q)_m,
\]
\[
 (aq^{-m}z;q)_m=(-az)^mq^{-m(m+1)/2}(q/(az);q)_m.
\]
Since
\(
 (aq^{-m}z;q)_\infty=(aq^{-m}z;q)_m(az;q)_\infty,
\)
we obtain
\[
 \frac{(q;q)_m}{(q/a;q)_m}
 \frac{(q/(az);q)_m(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}.
\]
On the other hand, substituting $b=q^{m+1}$ in the proposed product and cancelling tails gives exactly the same expression:
\[
 \frac{(q;q)_\infty}{(q^{m+1};q)_\infty}
 \frac{(q^{m+1}/a;q)_\infty}{(q/a;q)_\infty}
 \frac{(q/(az);q)_\infty}{(q^{m+1}/(az);q)_\infty}
 \frac{(az;q)_\infty}{(z;q)_\infty}.
\]

For fixed $a,z$ with $\AbsoluteValue z<1$, both sides are analytic functions of $b$ in a neighborhood of $0$ contained in $\AbsoluteValue b<\AbsoluteValue{az}$.  Absolute convergence is locally uniform there by \Cref{prop:1psi1-convergence}.  For all sufficiently large $m$, the points $b=q^{m+1}$ lie in that neighborhood, and they accumulate at $0$.  The identity theorem therefore proves equality near $0$.  Both sides are meromorphic functions of $b$ throughout the disk $\AbsoluteValue b<\AbsoluteValue{az}$: the series gives the meromorphic continuation termwise on compacta avoiding denominator zeros, and the product quotient is visibly meromorphic.  Uniqueness of meromorphic continuation proves the identity everywhere in that disk where both sides are defined, which is precisely the asserted annulus for fixed $a$ and $z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Ramanujan's sum contains the infinite $q$-binomial theorem and several theta-product identities as limiting or specialized cases.  It is a model example of a bilateral identity: the second inequality in the convergence annulus is controlled by the positive tail, while the first is controlled by the negative tail.
\end{remark}


\chapter{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-difference calculus and the quantum binomial}\label{chap:qcalculus}
\index{$q$-derivative}
\index{Jackson integral}
\index{quantum plane}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-derivative}

Assume $q\ne1$.  For $x\ne0$ define
\[
 D_qf(x)=\frac{f(x)-f(qx)}{(1-q)x}.
\]
At $x=0$ one uses the continuous extension when it exists.

\begin{proposition}[Elementary rules]\label{prop:qderivative-rules}
For $n\ge1$,
\[
 D_qx^n=[n]_qx^{n-1}.
\]
The product rules are
\begin{align*}
 D_q(fg)(x)&=g(x)D_qf(x)+f(qx)D_qg(x),\\
 &=f(x)D_qg(x)+g(qx)D_qf(x).
\end{align*}
Moreover,
\[
 D_q\bigl(f(q^rx)\bigr)=q^r(D_qf)(q^rx)
\]
for integral $r$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The monomial identity follows from
\(
 (x^n-q^nx^n)/((1-q)x)=[n]_qx^{n-1}.
\)
For the first product rule, insert and subtract $f(qx)g(x)$ in
\(
 f(x)g(x)-f(qx)g(qx).
\)
For the second, insert and subtract $f(x)g(qx)$.  The scaling rule follows directly from the definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The polynomial $q$-derivative, division-free]\label{rem:qderivative-polynomial}
On polynomials the $q$-derivative needs neither $q\ne1$ nor $x\ne0$: it is the coefficientwise operator
\[
 D_q\Bigl(\sum_n a_nx^n\Bigr)=\sum_n a_n[n]_qx^{n-1},
 \qquad [n]_q=1+q+\cdots+q^{n-1},
\]
which is $R$-linear on $R[x]$ over every commutative semiring $R$ and satisfies the exact identity
\[
 (1-q)\,x\,(D_qp)(x)=p(x)-p(qx)
\]
over every commutative ring, because $(1-q)[n]_q=1-q^n$.  The product rules and the scaling rule $D_q\bigl(p(cx)\bigr)=c\,(D_qp)(cx)$, for an arbitrary scale $c$, hold in this generality; by bilinearity they reduce to monomials, where they are the addition law $[m+n]_q=[m]_q+q^m[n]_q$ of $q$-integers.  This is the form in which the formalization records \Cref{prop:qderivative-rules}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Leibniz rule]\label{thm:q-leibniz}
For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\[
 D_q^n(fg)(x)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 (D_q^kf)(q^{n-k}x)(D_q^{n-k}g)(x).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $n$.  The assertion is the ordinary product at $n=0$.  Apply $D_q$ to the $k$th term at level $n$.  By the product and scaling rules, it contributes
\[
 q^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 (D_q^{k+1}f)(q^{n-k}x)(D_q^{n-k}g)(x)
\]
to index $k+1$, and
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}(D_q^kf)(q^{n-k+1}x)(D_q^{n-k+1}g)(x)
\]
to index $k$.  The total coefficient of index $k$ is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+q^{n-k+1}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}
\]
by the second $q$-Pascal recurrence.
\end{proof}

\section{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Taylor and Newton interpolation}

Define the $q$-falling power based at $a$ by
\[
 \QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m}=\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(x-q^ja),
 \qquad \QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{0}=1.
\]

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:qfalling-derivative}
For $m\ge1$,
\[
 D_q\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m}
 =[m]_q\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m-1}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 D_q^j\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m}
 =\frac{[m]_q!}{[m-j]_q!}\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m-j}
\]
for $0\le j\le m$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $P_m(x)=\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{m}$.  Then
\[
 P_m(qx)=q^m(x-q^{-1}a)\prod_{j=0}^{m-2}(x-q^ja).
\]
Subtracting from
\(
 P_m(x)=(x-q^{m-1}a)\prod_{j=0}^{m-2}(x-q^ja)
\)
gives
\[
 P_m(x)-P_m(qx)=(1-q^m)xP_{m-1}(x).
\]
Divide by $(1-q)x$.  Iteration proves the second formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Taylor formula]\label{thm:q-taylor}
Assume $[j]_q\ne0$ for $1\le j\le N$.  If $f$ is a polynomial of degree at most $N$, then
\[
 f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(D_q^kf)(a)}{[k]_q!}
 \QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{k}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The polynomials $\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{k}$, $0\le k\le N$, are monic of distinct degrees and hence form a basis.  Write
\(
 f=\sum c_k\QFallingPower{x}{a}{q}{k}.
\)
Apply $D_q^j$ and set $x=a$.  By \Cref{lem:qfalling-derivative}, every term with $k>j$ still contains the factor $x-a$, terms with $k<j$ vanish after too many derivatives, and the $k=j$ term equals $c_j[j]_q!$.  Thus $c_j=(D_q^jf)(a)/[j]_q!$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
When the nodes $a,aq,aq^2,\ldots$ are distinct, this is a Newton interpolation formula on that geometric grid.  In every case covered by the theorem, its basis zeros follow a multiplicative rather than an additive progression.
\end{remark}

\section{The quantum plane}

\begin{theorem}[Noncommutative $q$-binomial theorem]\label{thm:quantum-binomial}
Let $X,Y$ belong to an associative algebra and satisfy
\[
 YX=qXY.
\]
Then
\[
 (X+Y)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}X^{n-k}Y^k.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $n$ and multiply on the right by $X+Y$.  Since $Y^kX=q^kXY^k$, the coefficient of $X^{n+1-k}Y^k$ becomes
\[
 q^k\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}
\]
by the first $q$-Pascal recurrence.  Multiplying on the left instead uses $YX^{m}=q^{m}X^{m}Y$ and produces the coefficient
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+q^{\,n+1-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q},
\]
the second $q$-Pascal recurrence; this is the route taken by the formalization.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Generality]\label{rem:quantum-binomial-generality}
Only two things are used: that $q$ commutes with $X$ and with $Y$, so that $YX^m=q^mX^mY$ and so that the Gaussian coefficients, being polynomials in $q$ with natural coefficients, commute with $X$ and $Y$; and the $q$-Pascal recurrence.  The theorem therefore holds in every semiring, with no commutativity assumption on the ambient ring and no requirement that $q$ be central beyond commuting with $X$ and $Y$; over a commutative ring it is the finite $q$-binomial theorem of \Cref{thm:finite-qbinomial} in reversed form.
\end{remark}

\index{quantum affine space}
\index{$q$-multinomial coefficient!as structure constants}

\begin{corollary}[Noncommutative $q$-multinomial theorem]\label{cor:quantum-multinomial}
Let $r\ge1$ and let $x_1,\dots,x_r$ be elements of an associative algebra over a commutative ring in which $q$ is a central element, and suppose that
\[
 x_jx_i=qx_ix_j
 \qquad(1\le i<j\le r).
\]
Then, for every $n\ge0$,
\[
 (x_1+\cdots+x_r)^n
 =\sum_{\substack{n_1,\dots,n_r\ge0\\ n_1+\cdots+n_r=n}}
 \QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\dots,n_r}{q}\,
 x_1^{n_1}x_2^{n_2}\cdots x_r^{n_r},
\]
where on the right the factors are written in increasing order of index.  Equivalently, the $q$-multinomial coefficients are exactly the structure constants that expand powers of $x_1+\cdots+x_r$ in the ordered monomial basis of the quantum affine $r$-space.
\end{corollary}

% ed.: the incoming survey states only that ``iterating the proof gives a
% ed.: $q$-multinomial theorem''.  The ordering of the monomials is part of the
% ed.: statement, not a convention, because $x_jx_i=qx_ix_j$ is imposed only for
% ed.: $i<j$; centrality of $q$ is likewise recorded explicitly here.

\begin{proof}
Induct on $r$.  For $r=1$ there is nothing to prove, and $r=2$ is \Cref{thm:quantum-binomial}.

Let $r\ge2$ and set $X=x_1$ and $Y=x_2+\cdots+x_r$.  Because $x_jx_1=qx_1x_j$ for every $j\ge2$ and $q$ is central,
\[
 YX=\sum_{j=2}^{r}x_jx_1
 =q\sum_{j=2}^{r}x_1x_j
 =qXY,
\]
so \Cref{thm:quantum-binomial} applies to the pair $(X,Y)$ and gives
\[
 (x_1+\cdots+x_r)^n
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\,x_1^{\,n-k}\,(x_2+\cdots+x_r)^{k}.
\]
Substituting $n_1=n-k$ and using the symmetry $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{n-k}{q}$ of \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}, this reads
\[
 (x_1+\cdots+x_r)^n
 =\sum_{n_1=0}^{n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{n_1}{q}\,x_1^{\,n_1}\,(x_2+\cdots+x_r)^{\,n-n_1}.
\]
The elements $x_2,\dots,x_r$ satisfy the same commutation relations among themselves, so the inductive hypothesis applies to them and gives, with $m=n-n_1$,
\[
 (x_2+\cdots+x_r)^{m}
 =\sum_{\substack{n_2,\dots,n_r\ge0\\ n_2+\cdots+n_r=m}}
 \QMultinomial{m}{n_2,\dots,n_r}{q}\,x_2^{n_2}\cdots x_r^{n_r}.
\]
Since the coefficients are central, substitution produces the ordered
monomials.  For the multi-index $(n_1,\dots,n_r)$, the coefficient is
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{n_1}{q}\QMultinomial{n-n_1}{n_2,\dots,n_r}{q}
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n_1}\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-n_1}}
 \cdot
 \frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-n_1}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n_2}\cdots\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n_r}}
 =\QMultinomial{n}{n_1,\dots,n_r}{q}
\]
by the definition of the $q$-multinomial coefficient preceding \Cref{prop:qmult-factor}; the factor $\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-n_1}$ telescopes away, exactly as in the proof of that proposition.  No factor was transposed at any stage after the expansion, so the monomials are produced in increasing order of index, as asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The same coefficient read combinatorially]\label{rem:quantum-multinomial-inversions}
Expanding $(x_1+\cdots+x_r)^n$ without using any relation produces the $r^n$ words $x_{w_1}x_{w_2}\cdots x_{w_n}$ with $w\in\{1,\dots,r\}^n$.  Sorting such a word into increasing order uses one adjacent transposition for each inversion of $w$, and each transposition contributes exactly one factor $q$; hence
\[
 x_{w_1}x_{w_2}\cdots x_{w_n}=q^{\InversionNumber{w}}x_1^{n_1}\cdots x_r^{n_r},
\]
where $n_i$ is the multiplicity of the letter $i$ in $w$.  Comparing coefficients with \Cref{cor:quantum-multinomial} recovers the multiset inversion enumerator of \Cref{thm:qmult-inversions}, and conversely that theorem proves \Cref{cor:quantum-multinomial} again.  The quantum affine space is therefore a purely algebraic encoding of the inversion statistic, just as \Cref{thm:quantum-binomial} encodes \Cref{thm:binary-inversions}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Factorization of $q$-exponentials]\label{cor:qexp-factor}
Assume $\AbsoluteValue q<1$.  Define
\[
 e_q(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^n}{[n]_q!}
 =\frac1{((1-q)x;q)_\infty},
 \qquad \AbsoluteValue{(1-q)x}<1,
\]
and
\[
 E_q(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{\binom n2}x^n}{[n]_q!}
 =(-(1-q)x;q)_\infty,
 \qquad x\in\ComplexNumbers.
\]
Then $e_q(x)E_q(-x)=1$ wherever the first series is defined.  If $YX=qXY$, then, as an identity of formal series in total degree,
\[
 e_q(X+Y)=e_q(X)e_q(Y).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The product formulas and the stated convergence domains follow from Euler's identities after replacing $z$ by $(1-q)x$.  Their product is $1$.  For the noncommutative factorization, apply \Cref{thm:quantum-binomial} degree by degree and use
\[
 \frac1{[n]_q!}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 =\frac1{[n-k]_q![k]_q!}.
\]
Reindex with $r=n-k$ to obtain the Cauchy product of $e_q(X)$ and $e_q(Y)$, with all $X$ factors preceding all $Y$ factors.  Since every total degree receives only finitely many contributions, the formal rearrangement is valid.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Eigenfunction properties]\label{prop:qexp-eigen}
Under the hypotheses and within the convergence domains of \Cref{cor:qexp-factor}, for scalar $\lambda$,
\[
 D_qe_q(\lambda x)=\lambda e_q(\lambda x),
 \qquad
 D_qE_q(\lambda x)=\lambda E_q(q\lambda x).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the series term by term and use $D_qx^n=[n]_qx^{n-1}$.
\end{proof}

\section{Jackson's \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-integral}

Assume $0<q<1$.  Define
\[
 \int_0^a f(t)\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{t}
 =(1-q)a\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q^nf(aq^n),
\]
whenever the series converges.

\begin{theorem}[Fundamental theorem of $q$-calculus]\label{thm:q-fundamental}
Let $\Omega\subset\ComplexNumbers$ satisfy $q\Omega\subset\Omega$, and
suppose
\[
 F(x)=(1-q)x\sum_{n\ge0}q^nf(xq^n)
\]
is defined for $x\in\Omega$, for example because the series converges locally
uniformly.  Then
\[
 D_qF(x)=f(x)\qquad(x\in\Omega\setminus\{0\}).
\]
If $0\in\Omega$ and $f$ is continuous at $0$, set $F(0)=0$; then $D_qF$
has the continuous extension $D_qF(0)=f(0)$.

Conversely, if $a\ne0$, $G$ is defined on
$\{0,a,aq,aq^2,\ldots\}$, and $G(aq^n)\to G(0)$, then the Jackson sum of
$D_qG$ converges and
\[
 \int_0^aD_qG(t)\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{t}=G(a)-G(0).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $x\ne0$, shifting the convergent series gives
\[
 F(qx)=(1-q)x\sum_{n\ge1}q^nf(xq^n).
\]
Thus $F(x)-F(qx)=(1-q)xf(x)$ and $D_qF(x)=f(x)$.  If $f$ is
continuous at $0$, this identity itself gives the asserted continuous
extension.

For the converse, the $N$th partial Jackson sum is exactly
\[
 \sum_{n=0}^{N}\bigl(G(aq^n)-G(aq^{n+1})\bigr)
 =G(a)-G(aq^{N+1}).
\]
Letting $N\to\infty$ proves both convergence and the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Jackson integration by parts]\label{cor:q-integration-parts}
Let $a\ne0$.  Suppose the two Jackson series below converge and
\[
 f(aq^N)g(aq^N)\longrightarrow f(0)g(0).
\]
Then
\[
 \int_0^a f(t)D_qg(t)\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{t}
 =f(a)g(a)-f(0)g(0)
 -\int_0^a g(qt)D_qf(t)\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{t}.
\]
In particular, the endpoint condition holds when $f$ and $g$ are continuous
at $0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For every $N$, the product rule gives the finite identity
\begin{align*}
 &(1-q)a\sum_{n=0}^{N}q^n
 \bigl[f(aq^n)D_qg(aq^n)+g(aq^{n+1})D_qf(aq^n)\bigr]\\
 &\qquad=\sum_{n=0}^{N}
 \bigl(f(aq^n)g(aq^n)-f(aq^{n+1})g(aq^{n+1})\bigr)\\
 &\qquad=f(a)g(a)-f(aq^{N+1})g(aq^{N+1}).
\end{align*}
Let $N\to\infty$ and use the assumed convergence and endpoint limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Monomials]
For $\Re(s)>-1$,
\[
 \int_0^a t^s\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{t}
 =(1-q)a^{s+1}\sum_{n\ge0}q^{n(s+1)}
 =\frac{a^{s+1}}{\GeneralizedQNumber{s+1}{q}},
\]
where $\GeneralizedQNumber{u}{q}=(1-q^u)/(1-q)$ for complex $u$.
\end{example}

\chapter{The \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-gamma and \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-beta functions}\label{chap:qgamma}
\index{$q$-gamma function}
\index{$q$-beta function}

Throughout this chapter $0<q<1$ unless stated otherwise.

\section{Definition and functional equation}

\begin{definition}
For $x>0$, define
\[
 \Gamma_q(x)
 =(1-q)^{1-x}\frac{(q;q)_\infty}{(q^x;q)_\infty}.
\]
With $q^z=\exp(z\log q)$, the same product quotient defines a
meromorphic function of $z\in\ComplexNumbers$; its possible poles occur where
$q^{z+j}=1$ for some $j\in\NaturalNumbers$.  Whenever complex arguments of
$\Gamma_q$ occur below, this meromorphic continuation is intended.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Basic properties]\label{thm:qgamma-basic}
For $x>0$,
\[
 \Gamma_q(1)=1,
 \qquad
 \Gamma_q(x+1)=\GeneralizedQNumber{x}{q}\Gamma_q(x).
\]
Consequently, for $n\in\PositiveIntegers$,
\[
 \Gamma_q(n)=[n-1]_q!.
\]
The recurrence extends as an identity of meromorphic functions of a
complex argument.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The value at $1$ is immediate.  The tail identity
\(
 (q^x;q)_\infty=(1-q^x)(q^{x+1};q)_\infty
\)
gives
\[
 \frac{\Gamma_q(x+1)}{\Gamma_q(x)}
 =(1-q)^{-1}(1-q^x)=\GeneralizedQNumber{x}{q}.
\]
Iteration gives the integral values.  The same tail identity is valid for
complex $x$, and hence proves the meromorphic recurrence wherever both
sides are finite; equality elsewhere follows in the sense of
meromorphic continuation.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Logarithmic derivatives and convexity]\label{prop:qgamma-convex}
For $x>0$,
\[
