$\FourierAngular{g}(-t)=\int_{\RealNumbers}\EulerE^{\ImaginaryUnit ty}g(y)\,\Differential y$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:up-sinc-product}
 \FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-t)
 =\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{2^k}\right).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:up-functional-differential}
 \RvachevUp'(y)
 =2\RvachevUp(2y+1)-2\RvachevUp(2y-1)
 \qquad(y\in\RealNumbers).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The random variable with density $\RvachevUp$ is
$Y=2X-1$.  Writing $V_j=2U_j-1$, we have independent uniform variables on $[-1,1]$ and
\[
 Y=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j-1}V_j.
\]
The characteristic function of $V_j$ is $\SincRad t$.  Independence therefore gives
\[
 \Expectation \EulerE^{itY}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\SincRad(t/2^{j+1}),
\]
which is \eqref{eq:up-sinc-product}.

From \eqref{eq:up-definition},
$f(x)=2\RvachevUp(2x-1)$.  Differentiate
\eqref{eq:fabius-global-derivative} to obtain
\[
 f'(x)=4f(2x)-4f(2x-1).
\]
Set $x=(y+1)/2$.  Because
$\RvachevUp'(y)=f'((y+1)/2)/4$, substitution of
$f(t)=2\RvachevUp(2t-1)$ gives
\[
 \RvachevUp'(y)
 =2\RvachevUp(2y+1)-2\RvachevUp(2y-1).
\]
The identity remains valid outside the support because all boundary derivatives vanish.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Where the $q$-objects enter]
The parameter $q$ controls four structures at once:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the geometric coordinate weights $(1-q)q^j$;
\item the factors $1-q^{2m}$ in the cumulants \eqref{eq:q-cumulants-uniform};
\item the geometric interpolation nodes and Gaussian residual moments of \Cref{chap:geometric-filters};
\item at $q=1/2$, the Mersenne products, dyadic Thue--Morse splines, and the Fabius--Rvachev functional equations.
\end{enumerate}
Thus the appearance of $q$-Pochhammer and Gaussian coefficients in exact Fabius formulas is not accidental: both arise from the same geometric scaling law.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Dyadic Gaussian--Thue--Morse structure and the values of \texorpdfstring{$F$}{F}}
\label{chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}
\index{Fabius function!dyadic values}
\index{Thue--Morse sequence!Gaussian blocks}

% ed.: this chapter is the editorial merge of the two closest sibling drafts of
% ed.: this monograph, q_pochhammer_q_binomial_article-2 (its chapter
% ed.: chap:dyadic-q) and q_pochhammer_q_binomial_monograph-3 (its five bridge
% ed.: chapters).  Their material was first deduplicated separately against
% ed.: Chapters 21--23, then against each other: of the 23 candidate results in
% ed.: the second, 4 were outright duplicates of the first, 8 overlapped and are
% ed.: stated here once in merged form, and 11 were independent.  Both siblings
% ed.: were deleted afterwards; git history is the archive.

The three preceding chapters built the geometric filters, the half-base
arithmetic and the geometric uniform law separately.  This chapter runs them
together in the one case where everything is exactly computable: the base
$q=1/2$, where the geometric uniform sum is the Fabius variable, the Prouhet
filter is the Thue--Morse sign, and the resulting closed formula evaluates
$F$ at every inverse power of two.


% ---- merged block M01 ----
\begin{proposition}[The up function is a Fabius tail]\label{prop:up-tail}
\index{Rvachev up function!tail representation}
For $\AbsoluteValue y\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:up-tail-reflection}
 \RvachevUp(y)=F(1-\AbsoluteValue y),
\end{equation}
and in particular, for $0\le t\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:up-survival}
 \RvachevUp(t)=1-F(t)=\Probability(X\ge t).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $-1\le y\le0$ and put $x=(y+1)/2\in[0,1/2]$.  By
\eqref{eq:fabius-local-derivative}, $f(x)=2F(2x)=2F(y+1)$, so
\eqref{eq:up-definition} gives
$\RvachevUp(y)=\tfrac12f(x)=F(1+y)=F(1-\AbsoluteValue y)$.

Differentiating the symmetry \eqref{eq:fabius-symmetry} gives $f(1-x)=f(x)$, so
$\RvachevUp(-y)=\tfrac12f((1-y)/2)=\tfrac12f((1+y)/2)=\RvachevUp(y)$;
thus $\RvachevUp$ is even and \eqref{eq:up-tail-reflection} holds on all of
$[-1,1]$.  Finally, for $0\le t\le1$ the symmetry \eqref{eq:fabius-symmetry} turns
$F(1-t)$ into $1-F(t)$, and $X$ has a density, so
$1-F(t)=\Probability(X>t)=\Probability(X\ge t)$.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M02 ----
\begin{corollary}[Tail form of the moments]\label{cor:up-moments}
For every $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:up-moment-integral}
 \Expectation X^n=n\int_0^1t^{n-1}\RvachevUp(t)\,\Differential t.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $X\ge0$, Tonelli's theorem applied to the nonnegative integrand
$nt^{n-1}\IndicatorOf{\SetOf{t<X}}$ gives
\[
 \Expectation X^n
 =\Expectation\int_0^Xnt^{n-1}\,\Differential t
 =\int_0^1nt^{n-1}\Probability(X\ge t)\,\Differential t,
\]
because $0\le X\le1$.  Now apply \eqref{eq:up-survival}.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M03 ----
% ed.: this block replaces the sibling lemma \texttt{lem:fabius-moment-dilation},
% ed.: which stated only the case $q=1/2$.  Its labels are retained on the
% ed.: corollary below, so that \Cref{lem:thue-morse-dilation-bridge},
% ed.: \Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} and \Cref{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic}
% ed.: need no re-referencing.  Two mechanical renames are required in that
% ed.: chain: $\Phi\mapsto\Lambda$ and $a_n\mapsto\alpha_n$.
Write
% ed.: REPLACES the preamble equations \eqref{eq:uniform-mgf-kernel},
% ed.: \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def} and \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf}
% ed.: with its proof (monograph block M03, up to but excluding
% ed.: \Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}, which is unchanged).  The merge adds to
% ed.: the filed theorem exactly what the incoming sibling item carried and the
% ed.: filed text did not: the parameter is now an arbitrary complex $q$ with
% ed.: $\abs q<1$, the transform is defined by the product rather than by an
% ed.: expectation, and the coefficients are recorded as rational functions of
% ed.: $q$.  All four equation labels and the theorem label are retained, so no
% ed.: reference elsewhere in the chapter changes.  The complex parameter is not
% ed.: a flourish: \Cref{prop:qF-negative-affine}, \Cref{thm:qF-germ-uniqueness}
% ed.: and \Cref{thm:qF-spectral} act on $\mathcal A_q$ outside $(0,1)$, and
% ed.: \Cref{thm:qF-moment-polynomial} expands $\alpha^{(q)}_n$ at $q=\infty$.

% ed.: this block replaces the sibling lemma \texttt{lem:fabius-moment-dilation},
% ed.: which stated only the case $q=1/2$.  Its labels are retained on the
% ed.: corollary below, so that \Cref{lem:thue-morse-dilation-bridge},
% ed.: \Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} and \Cref{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic}
% ed.: need no re-referencing.  Two mechanical renames are required in that
% ed.: chain: $\Phi\mapsto\Lambda$ and $a_n\mapsto\alpha_n$.
Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform-mgf-kernel}
 \Lambda(z)\DefinitionEquals\int_0^1\EulerE^{zu}\,\Differential u=\frac{\EulerE^z-1}{z},
 \qquad\Lambda(0)\DefinitionEquals1,
\end{equation}
an entire function with $\Lambda(z)=\sum_{r\ge0}z^r/(r+1)!$ and $\Lambda(z)=1+\BigO(z)$ at the
origin, whose zeros are exactly the points $2\pi\mathrm ik$ with $k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\SetOf{0}$; in
particular $\Lambda$ has no zero in the disc $\AbsoluteValue z<2\pi$.  For $q\in\ComplexNumbers$ with $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def}
 \mathcal A_q(z)\DefinitionEquals\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\Lambda\bigl((1-q)q^jz\bigr)
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\alpha^{(q)}_nz^n .
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Moment product, dilation equation, coefficient recurrence]
\label{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf}
\index{moment generating function}
\index{Mahler functional equation}
Let $q\in\ComplexNumbers$ with $\AbsoluteValue q<1$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the product in \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def} converges locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$
and defines an entire function with $\mathcal A_q(0)=1$;
\item $\mathcal A_q$ satisfies the dilation equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mgf-refinement}
 \mathcal A_q(z)=\Lambda\bigl((1-q)z\bigr)\,\mathcal A_q(qz),
\end{equation}
and is the unique formal power series with constant term $1$ that does so; its coefficients
satisfy $\alpha^{(q)}_0=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-moment-recurrence}
 (1-q^n)\,\alpha^{(q)}_n
 =\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\frac{(1-q)^{n-r}q^r}{(n-r+1)!}\,\alpha^{(q)}_r
 \qquad(n\ge1);
\end{equation}
\item consequently each $\alpha^{(q)}_n$ is a rational function of $q$ with rational
coefficients, its poles are roots of unity, and it is regular on the open unit disc;
\item for real $0<q<1$ the function $\mathcal A_q$ is the moment generating function of
$X_q$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mgf-product}
 \mathcal A_q(z)=\Expectation\,\EulerE^{zX_q}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\Lambda\bigl((1-q)q^jz\bigr),
 \qquad
 \alpha^{(q)}_n=\frac{\Expectation X_q^{\,n}}{n!},
\end{equation}
and $\varphi_q(t)=\mathcal A_q(it)$ for every real $t$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i)  On a compact set $K\subset\ComplexNumbers$ the arguments $(1-q)q^jz$ are $\BigO(\AbsoluteValue q^j)$ uniformly,
and $\Lambda(w)-1=\BigO(w)$ near the origin, so
$\sum_{j\ge0}\sup_{z\in K}\AbsoluteValue{\Lambda((1-q)q^jz)-1}$ is dominated by a constant multiple of
$\sum_{j\ge0}\AbsoluteValue q^j<\infty$.  The standard criterion for infinite products gives locally
uniform convergence to an entire function, and every factor equals $1$ at $z=0$.

(ii)  Replacing $z$ by $qz$ shifts the index by one:
\[
 \Lambda\bigl((1-q)z\bigr)\mathcal A_q(qz)
 =\Lambda\bigl((1-q)z\bigr)\prod_{j\ge0}\Lambda\bigl((1-q)q^{\,j+1}z\bigr)
 =\prod_{j\ge0}\Lambda\bigl((1-q)q^{\,j}z\bigr)
 =\mathcal A_q(z),
\]
which is \eqref{eq:mgf-refinement}.  Comparing the coefficient of $z^n$ in
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}\alpha^{(q)}_nz^n
 =\left(\sum_{s\ge0}\frac{(1-q)^sz^s}{(s+1)!}\right)
  \left(\sum_{r\ge0}\alpha^{(q)}_rq^rz^r\right)
\]
gives $\alpha^{(q)}_n=\sum_{r=0}^{n}(1-q)^{n-r}q^r\alpha^{(q)}_r/(n-r+1)!$; the term $r=n$ on
the right is $q^n\alpha^{(q)}_n$, and moving it to the left proves
\eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence}.  Since $1-q^n\ne0$ for $n\ge1$, that recurrence determines
every coefficient from its predecessors, which is the asserted formal uniqueness.

(iii)  Induct on \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence}.  One has $\alpha^{(q)}_0=1$, and
$\alpha^{(q)}_n$ is obtained from $\alpha^{(q)}_0,\ldots,\alpha^{(q)}_{n-1}$ by rational
operations with the single new denominator $1-q^n$.  Hence $\alpha^{(q)}_n\in\RationalNumbers(q)$ and its
poles lie among the zeros of $\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1-q^j)$, all of which are roots of unity, and
none of those lies in $\AbsoluteValue q<1$.

(iv)  Let $0<q<1$.  By \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-basic} we have $0\le X_q\le1$, so
$\Expectation X_q^{\,n}\le1$ and $\sum_n\AbsoluteValue z^n\Expectation X_q^{\,n}/n!\le\EulerE^{\AbsoluteValue z}$; hence
$z\mapsto\Expectation\EulerE^{zX_q}$ is entire and term-by-term integration is legitimate.  For the
truncation $X_{q,N}$ of \eqref{eq:finite-geometric-uniform-sum}, independence gives
\[
 \Expectation\EulerE^{zX_{q,N}}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\int_0^1\EulerE^{z(1-q)q^ju}\,\Differential u
 =\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\Lambda\bigl((1-q)q^jz\bigr).
\]
By \eqref{eq:finite-uniform-tail-bound}, $0\le X_q-X_{q,N}\le q^N$ pointwise, so
$\EulerE^{zX_{q,N}}\to\EulerE^{zX_q}$ uniformly on the sample space while remaining bounded by
$\EulerE^{\AbsoluteValue z}$; dominated convergence identifies the limit with $\mathcal A_q(z)$ and proves
\eqref{eq:mgf-product}.  Substituting $z=it$ reproduces $\varphi_q$, and comparison of Taylor
coefficients gives $\alpha^{(q)}_n=\Expectation X_q^{\,n}/n!$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Dilation, refinement, Mahler]\label{rem:mgf-names}
\index{Mahler functional equation}
Equation \eqref{eq:mgf-refinement} carries three names in three literatures: it is a
\emph{dilation} or \emph{refinement} equation in approximation theory and wavelet analysis,
and a \emph{Mahler functional equation} in transcendence theory and in the theory of automatic
sequences.  This book uses them interchangeably.  Part (iii) is visible already in the first
cases, which \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence} produces as
\[
 \alpha^{(q)}_1=\frac12,
 \qquad
 \alpha^{(q)}_2=\frac{q+2}{12(q+1)},
 \qquad
 \alpha^{(q)}_3=\frac1{24(q+1)};
\]
the half-base values are collected in \Cref{ex:fabius-first-coefficients}, and the systematic
clearing of the denominators is \Cref{def:qF-moment-polynomial}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The half-base moment generating function]\label{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}
\index{Fabius function!moment generating function}
Write $X\DefinitionEquals X_{1/2}$, $\mathcal A\DefinitionEquals\mathcal A_{1/2}$ and
$\alpha_n\DefinitionEquals\alpha^{(1/2)}_n=\Expectation X^n/n!$.  Then $\mathcal A$ is entire,
$\mathcal A(it)=\varphi_{1/2}(t)$,
\begin{align}
 \mathcal A(z)&=\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\Lambda\!\left(\frac z{2^j}\right),
 \label{eq:fabius-mgf-product}\\
 \mathcal A(2z)&=\Lambda(z)\,\mathcal A(z),
 \label{eq:fabius-dilation}
\end{align}
and $\alpha_0=1$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence}
 (2^n-1)\,\alpha_n=\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\frac{\alpha_r}{(n-r+1)!}
 \qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put $q=1/2$ in \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf}.  The product
\eqref{eq:mgf-product} becomes $\prod_{j\ge0}\Lambda(2^{-1}2^{-j}z)$, which is
\eqref{eq:fabius-mgf-product}; replacing $z$ by $2z$ in \eqref{eq:mgf-refinement} gives
\eqref{eq:fabius-dilation}.  In \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence} every summand acquires the
factor $(1/2)^{n-r}(1/2)^r=2^{-n}$ and the left side is $(1-2^{-n})\alpha_n$;
multiplication by $2^n$ gives \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence}.  Finally
$\Lambda(is)=\EulerE^{is/2}\SincRad(s/2)$, so \eqref{eq:fabius-mgf-product} at
$z=it$ is \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-characteristic} at $q=1/2$; this is the sinc product
of \Cref{thm:up-sinc-product}.
\end{proof}

% ed.: self-contained replacement for \Cref{ex:fabius-first-coefficients}.  An
% ed.: incoming draft carried the same six rationals $\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_5$
% ed.: (there called $g_0,\ldots,g_5$) and converted them into the inverse-dyadic
% ed.: values of $F$; only that second row was absent here, and it is appended
% ed.: below.  The example now reaches $F(2^{-5})$, the first value a reader
% ed.: cannot check by hand.  All six coefficients recomputed in exact rational
% ed.: arithmetic from \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence}.
\begin{example}[The first coefficients and the first dyadic values]\label{ex:fabius-first-coefficients}
\index{Fabius function!value at $2^{-n}$}
Recurrence \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} gives
\[
 \alpha_0=1,\quad
 \alpha_1=\tfrac12,\quad
 \alpha_2=\tfrac5{36},\quad
 \alpha_3=\tfrac1{36},\quad
 \alpha_4=\tfrac{143}{32400},\quad
 \alpha_5=\tfrac{19}{32400},
\]
equivalently $\Expectation X^2=2!\,\alpha_2=5/18$ and $\Expectation X^4=4!\,\alpha_4=143/1350$.
These agree with the cumulant route of
\Cref{cor:geometric-uniform-moments,cor:halfbase-cumulants}: for instance
$\kappa_2(Y_{1/2})=B_2/(2\cdot3)=1/36$, so $\Expectation X^2=\tfrac1{36}+\tfrac14=\tfrac5{18}$.

By \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} below, $F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}\alpha_n$, so the same
six rationals are the first inverse-dyadic values of the Fabius function:
\[
 F(1)=1,\qquad
 F\!\left(\tfrac12\right)=\tfrac12,\qquad
 F\!\left(\tfrac14\right)=\frac5{72},\qquad
 F\!\left(\tfrac18\right)=\frac1{288},
\]
\[
 F\!\left(\tfrac1{16}\right)=\frac{143}{2073600},\qquad
 F\!\left(\tfrac1{32}\right)=\frac{19}{33177600}.
\]
The value $F(1/2)=1/2$ is forced by \eqref{eq:fabius-symmetry}; the remaining entries
are the first concrete instances of the rationality asserted, without values, by
\Cref{cor:halfbase-cumulants}.  The powers $2^{-\binom n2}$ are what makes the values
fall off so much faster than the coefficients.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Two routes to the same rationals]\label{rem:moment-routes}
Unlike the moment--cumulant recursion \eqref{eq:moment-cumulant-recurrence}, which is
stated for the centred variable and needs Bernoulli input, the recursion
\eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} is a triangular rational recursion in the raw
coefficients alone: one rational division per index, no Bernoulli numbers and no Bell
polynomials.  It is the cheaper and the numerically stable route to the values below.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M04 ----
\begin{lemma}[Derivative iterates of the Fabius function]\label{lem:fabius-derivative-iterate}
\index{Fabius function!derivative iterates}
For every $m\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-derivative-iterate}
 F^{(m)}(x)=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}F\bigl(2^mx\bigr)
 \qquad\bigl(0\le x\le2^{-m}\bigr).
\end{equation}
In particular $F^{(m)}(0)=0$ for every $m\ge0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The case $m=0$ is trivial, and \eqref{eq:fabius-local-derivative} is the case $m=1$.
Assume \eqref{eq:fabius-derivative-iterate} for $m$ and let $0\le x\le2^{-m-1}$, so that
$0\le2^mx\le1/2$.  Differentiating and applying \eqref{eq:fabius-local-derivative} at
$2^mx$ gives
\[
 F^{(m+1)}(x)=2^{\binom{m+1}{2}}2^mF'\bigl(2^mx\bigr)
 =2^{\binom{m+1}{2}+m+1}F\bigl(2^{m+1}x\bigr),
\]
and $\binom{m+1}{2}+m+1=\binom{m+2}{2}$.  Since $F(0)=0$, evaluating
\eqref{eq:fabius-derivative-iterate} at $x=0$ gives $F^{(m)}(0)=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Half-moment evaluation]\label{thm:fabius-half-moment}
\index{Fabius function!value at $2^{-n}$}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-half-moment}
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)=2^{-\binom n2}\alpha_n
 =\frac{\Expectation X^n}{n!\,2^{\binom n2}} .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $n=0$ both sides are $F(1)=1$; let $n\ge1$.  By
\Cref{lem:fabius-derivative-iterate} we have $F^{(j)}(0)=0$ for $0\le j<n$, and
$F\in C^\infty$ by \Cref{thm:probabilistic-fabius}, so Taylor's formula with integral
remainder at the point $2^{-n}$ reduces to the remainder alone:
\[
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac1{(n-1)!}\int_0^{2^{-n}}\bigl(2^{-n}-t\bigr)^{n-1}F^{(n)}(t)\,\Differential t
 =\frac{2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(n-1)!}
   \int_0^{2^{-n}}\bigl(2^{-n}-t\bigr)^{n-1}F\bigl(2^nt\bigr)\,\Differential t,
\]
where \eqref{eq:fabius-derivative-iterate} was used on the whole interval of integration.
Substituting $u=2^nt$ produces the factor $2^{-n(n-1)}$ from $(2^{-n}-t)^{n-1}$ and the
factor $2^{-n}$ from $\Differential t$, and
\[
 \binom{n+1}{2}-n(n-1)-n=\frac{n(n+1)}2-n^2=-\binom n2 ,
\]
so that
\[
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)=\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{(n-1)!}\int_0^1(1-u)^{n-1}F(u)\,\Differential u .
\]
By \Cref{prop:up-tail}, $F(u)=\RvachevUp(1-u)$ for $0\le u\le1$; substituting
$t=1-u$ and applying \Cref{cor:up-moments},
\[
 \frac1{(n-1)!}\int_0^1t^{n-1}\RvachevUp(t)\,\Differential t
 =\frac{\Expectation X^n}{n\,(n-1)!}
 =\alpha_n. \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}.  This block REPLACES the remark
% ed.: \texttt{rem:half-moment-cube-cut}: an incoming draft carried the same cube
% ed.: cut at a general base $q$, together with the sharp threshold $q\le1/2$ and
% ed.: the failure above it, so the argument is stated once, in the general form,
% ed.: and the $q=1/2$ reading is retained as the remark below.  The remark keeps
% ed.: its label and the split \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split}, which
% ed.: \Cref{rem:dyadic-formula-anatomy} and \Cref{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic} cite.
% ed.: The same statement occurred twice in that draft (once as an ``inverse-power
% ed.: value'', once as an ``inverse-geometric endpoint formula''); it is adopted
% ed.: once, in the sharp form.  Its table of first values is
% ed.: \Cref{ex:fabius-first-coefficients} and is not repeated.

\begin{proposition}[Inverse-power values at a general base]\label{prop:inverse-power-values}
\index{Fabius function!values at dyadic points}
\index{cube!simplex section of}
Let $0<q<1$ and $n\ge0$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inverse-power-inequality}
 F_q\bigl(q^n\bigr)\le\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(1-q)^n\,n!}\,\Expectation\bigl(X_q^{\,n}\bigr),
\end{equation}
with equality for every $n$ if and only if $q\le1/2$; for $q>1/2$ and $n\ge1$ the
inequality is strict.  Thus, for $0<q\le1/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inverse-power-values}
 F_q\bigl(q^n\bigr)
 =\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(1-q)^n\,n!}\,\Expectation\bigl(X_q^{\,n}\bigr)
 =\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(1-q)^n}\,\alpha^{(q)}_n ,
\end{equation}
with $\alpha^{(q)}_n$ as in \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def}, and
$F_q(1-q^n)=1-F_q(q^n)$.  At $q=1/2$ the prefactor is $2^{-\binom n2}$ and
\eqref{eq:inverse-power-values} is \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $n=0$ both sides are $F_q(1)=1$; let $n\ge1$.  Split the series
\eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-series} after $n$ terms,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inverse-power-split}
 X_q=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_jU_j+q^nX_q',
 \qquad a_j\DefinitionEquals(1-q)q^j,
 \qquad X_q'\DefinitionEquals(1-q)\sum_{i\ge0}q^iU_{n+i},
\end{equation}
with the $a_j$ of \eqref{eq:finite-geometric-uniform-sum} and $X_q'$ a copy of $X_q$
independent of $U_0,\ldots,U_{n-1}$; note $\prod_{j<n}a_j=(1-q)^nq^{\binom n2}$.
Conditionally on $X_q'=y$, the event $\SetOf{X_q\le q^n}$ reads $\sum_{j<n}a_jU_j\le t$
with $t=q^n(1-y)\in[0,q^n]$, so its conditional probability is the cube section
\[
 V(t)\DefinitionEquals\operatorname{vol}
 \Bigl\{u\in[0,1]^n:\ \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_ju_j\le t\Bigr\}.
\]
Dropping the constraints $u_j\le1$ enlarges the region to a simplex, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:simplex-bound}
 V(t)\le\frac{t^n}{n!\prod_{j<n}a_j}
      =\frac{t^n}{n!\,(1-q)^nq^{\binom n2}},
\end{equation}
with equality precisely when the simplex lies inside the cube, that is when
$t\le\min_ja_j=(1-q)q^{\,n-1}$: if $t>(1-q)q^{\,n-1}$, the point with
$u_{n-1}=t/a_{n-1}>1$ and all other coordinates $0$ lies in the simplex but not in the
cube, and so does a neighbourhood of it, of positive volume.  Averaging
\eqref{eq:simplex-bound} over $y$ with $t=q^n(1-y)$ and using
$1-X_q\overset d=X_q$ from \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry},
\[
 F_q(q^n)\le\frac{q^{\,n^2}\,\Expectation\bigl[(1-X_q)^n\bigr]}
                 {n!\,(1-q)^nq^{\binom n2}}
 =\frac{q^{\,n^2-\binom n2}}{n!\,(1-q)^n}\,\Expectation\bigl(X_q^{\,n}\bigr),
\]
and $n^2-\binom n2=\binom{n+1}{2}$; this is \eqref{eq:inverse-power-inequality}.

If $q\le1/2$, then every admissible threshold satisfies
$t\le q^n=q\cdot q^{\,n-1}\le(1-q)q^{\,n-1}$, so \eqref{eq:simplex-bound} is an equality
almost surely and \eqref{eq:inverse-power-values} follows.  If $q>1/2$ and $n\ge1$, then
$(1-q)q^{\,n-1}<q^n$ and
\[
 \Probability\bigl(t>(1-q)q^{\,n-1}\bigr)
 =\Probability\Bigl(1-X_q'>\frac{1-q}q\Bigr)>0,
\]
because $(1-q)/q<1$ and $1-X_q'$ has support $[0,1]$ by
\Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-basic}; on that event \eqref{eq:simplex-bound} is strict, and
so therefore is the average.  Finally $X_q$ has no atoms and $X_q\overset d=1-X_q$, which
gives $F_q(1-q^n)=1-F_q(q^n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The half-base cube cut]\label{rem:half-moment-cube-cut}
At $q=1/2$, \Cref{prop:inverse-power-values} is a second proof of
\eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment} which uses neither \Cref{prop:up-tail} nor the smoothness
of $F$.  In scaled coordinates it reads as follows.  Write $X=\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j}V_j$ with
$V_1,V_2,\ldots$ independent and uniform on $[0,1]$, multiply by $2^n$ and split the
series after $n$ terms:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-scaled-split}
 2^nX=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_jV_j+X',
 \qquad c_j\DefinitionEquals2^{\,n-j},
 \qquad X'\DefinitionEquals\sum_{i\ge1}2^{-i}V_{n+i},
\end{equation}
where $X'$ is independent of $V_1,\ldots,V_n$ and distributed as $X$.  Conditionally on
$X'=x$, the event $2^nX\le1$ is $\sum_jc_jV_j\le t$ with $t\DefinitionEquals1-x\in[0,1]$.  Each $c_j$
is an integer with $c_j\ge1$, so no upper constraint is active, the admissible region is
the entire simplex, and its volume is
$t^n/\bigl(n!\prod_jc_j\bigr)=(1-x)^n/\bigl(n!\,2^{\binom n2}\bigr)$ since
$\prod_{j=1}^{n}2^{\,n-j}=2^{\binom n2}$.  Averaging over $X'$ and using
$X\overset d=1-X$ returns \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Bell-polynomial form of the Fabius values]\label{cor:fabius-bell-values}
\index{Bell polynomial!Fabius values}
Put $\kappa_1\DefinitionEquals1/2$ and $\kappa_r\DefinitionEquals\kappa_r(Y_{1/2})$ for $r\ge2$, the half-base
cumulants \eqref{eq:halfbase-cumulants}.  Then for every $n\ge0$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-bell-value}
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\,\mathcal B_n(\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_n)
 =2^{-\binom n2}
 \sum_{\substack{\nu_1,\ldots,\nu_n\ge0\\ \nu_1+2\nu_2+\cdots+n\nu_n=n}}
 \prod_{r=1}^{n}\frac1{\nu_r!}\left(\frac{\kappa_r}{r!}\right)^{\nu_r}.
\end{equation}
Only $\kappa_1$ and the even $\kappa_r$ contribute, and every value $F(2^{-n})$ is
rational.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Cumulants of order $r\ge2$ are translation invariant, so $X=Y_{1/2}+1/2$ has
$\kappa_1(X)=\Expectation X=1/2$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry} and
$\kappa_r(X)=\kappa_r$ for $r\ge2$.  The moment generating function is the exponential of
the cumulant generating function, so the defining identity
\eqref{eq:complete-bell-definition} gives
$\Expectation(X^n)=\mathcal B_n(\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_n)$, exactly as in the proof of
\Cref{cor:geometric-uniform-moments} but for the uncentred variable; now apply
\eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment}.  The second expression is the explicit expansion of
$\mathcal B_n$ obtained by multiplying out $\exp\bigl(\sum_r\kappa_rt^r/r!\bigr)$, and the
odd cumulants beyond the first vanish by \eqref{eq:halfbase-cumulants}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Sharpness of the hypothesis $q\le1/2$]\label{rem:inverse-power-sharpness}
For $q>1/2$ the closed form \eqref{eq:inverse-power-values} fails, and can fail grossly:
at $q=4/5$ and $n=1$ its right side is $q/(2(1-q))=2$, which is not even a probability.
At $q=3/5$ the right sides for $n=1,2,3$ exceed the true values by
$1.3\cdot10^{-2}$, $5.6\cdot10^{-4}$ and $1.2\cdot10^{-5}$; in particular
$F_{3/5}(3/5)=0.73738\ldots$ against $3/4$.  The reason is the one isolated in the proof:
for $q>1/2$ the smallest coefficient $(1-q)q^{\,n-1}$ drops below the threshold $q^n$ and
the cube truncates the simplex, the deficit being exactly the inclusion--exclusion terms
of \Cref{thm:finite-uniform-spline} that \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split} annihilates.
% ed.: the values quoted here were computed by iterating
% ed.: \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-cdf-equation} on a grid of $4\cdot10^5$ points; the
% ed.: same computation reproduces $F(1/4)=5/72$, $F(1/8)=1/288$ and
% ed.: $F(1/16)=143/2073600$ to $10^{-12}$, and confirms equality at $q=1/2,1/3,1/4$.
\end{remark}
% ed.: this block merges two independent proofs of one value theorem.  The
% ed.: sibling's \texttt{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic} stated
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment} as its first assertion and proved
% ed.: it by the cube cut; that theorem now states only the closed form
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} and cites
% ed.: \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} here.  Both equation labels are attached to
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment} so that references from either chain resolve.
% ed.: \Cref{lem:fabius-derivative-iterate} is needed later and has no counterpart
% ed.: in the cube-cut argument.

% ---- merged block A01 ----
\section{Reciprocal nodes and the leading-coefficient filter}
\index{geometric interpolation!reciprocal nodes}
\index{finite difference!geometric}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!reciprocal row}

The filter $\mathcal G_{n,q}$ of \eqref{eq:geometric-filter-definition} reproduces the \emph{constant} coefficient of a series and annihilates the degrees $1,\ldots,n$.  Its companion reproduces the coefficient of degree $n$ and annihilates the degrees $0,\ldots,n-1$.  That companion lives on the reciprocal nodes $-q^{-k}$, its weights are again a signed Gaussian row, and its residual tail is again controlled by a single Gaussian row.  It is this second normalization, not the evaluation-at-zero one, that produces the exact dyadic formulas of \Cref{chap:geometric-uniform-fabius}.

Throughout this section $q$ lies in a field $K$ and $q^j\ne1$ for $1\le j\le n$; from \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} onwards $K$ is also assumed to have characteristic zero.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocal-nodes-weights}
 x_k\DefinitionEquals-q^{-k},
 \qquad
 W_{n,k}(q)\DefinitionEquals(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 \qquad(0\le k\le n).
\end{equation}
The nodes are distinct, because $q^i=q^j$ with $0\le i<j\le n$ would force $q^{\,j-i}=1$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact geometric moment transform]\label{thm:geometric-moment-transform}
For every integer $d\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-moment-all}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,x_k^{\,d}
 =(-1)^d(q^{-d};q)_n.
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-moment-zero}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,x_k^{\,d}=0
 \qquad(0\le d<n),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-moment-top}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,x_k^{\,n}
 =q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $x_k^{\,d}=(-1)^dq^{-kd}$, the left side of \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-all} equals
\[
 (-1)^d\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\bigl(q^{-d}\bigr)^{k},
\]
which is $(-1)^d(q^{-d};q)_n$ by \eqref{eq:finite-qbinomial-minus} at $z=q^{-d}$.  If $0\le d<n$, the factor with $j=d$ in
\[
 (q^{-d};q)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1-q^{\,j-d}\bigr)
\]
is zero, which gives \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-zero}.  For $d=n$, extract $-q^{\,j-n}$ from each factor:
\[
 (q^{-n};q)_n
 =(-1)^nq^{-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(n-j)}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1-q^{\,n-j}\bigr)
 =(-1)^nq^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n,
\]
because $\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(n-j)=\binom{n+1}{2}$.  Multiplication by the outer sign $(-1)^n$ proves \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-top}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Leading-coefficient functional]\label{cor:reciprocal-leading-coefficient}
If $p\in K[x]$ has degree at most $n$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocal-leading-functional}
 [x^n]p(x)
 =\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_n}
  \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,p\bigl(-q^{-k}\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write $p(x)=\sum_{d=0}^{n}b_dx^d$ and apply the functional termwise.  Every term with $d<n$ vanishes by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-zero}, and the term $d=n$ contributes $b_n$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-top}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Three faces of one weight row]\label{rem:reciprocal-weight-faces}
The functional \eqref{eq:reciprocal-leading-functional} is not a new interpolation device.  It is the divided difference $y[x_0,\ldots,x_n]$ of \Cref{thm:geometric-newton} after the substitution $q\mapsto q^{-1}$, $x\mapsto-x$; in particular the closed evaluation of $\prod_{j\ne k}(x_k-x_j)$ needed for a Lagrange derivation, and the uniqueness of a functional returning the leading coefficient, are already contained in that theorem and in \Cref{thm:geometric-evaluation-zero}.  The Vandermonde determinant of the nodes differs only by $x\mapsto-x$ and a column reversal from the alternant $a_\delta(1,q^{-1},\ldots,q^{-n})$ evaluated inside the proof of \Cref{thm:schur-principal}.  At $q=1/2$ the data \eqref{eq:reciprocal-nodes-weights} become the data \eqref{eq:halfbase-prouhet-data}: $x_k=\xi_k$ and $W_{n,k}(1/2)=w_{n,k}$, so \Cref{thm:geometric-prouhet} is the case $q=1/2$ of \Cref{cor:reciprocal-leading-coefficient}.
% ed.: an incoming draft restated the Lagrange-denominator evaluation, the
% ed.: uniqueness of the functional and the geometric Vandermonde determinant
% ed.: as fresh results; all three are the above, transported.  Only the
% ed.: arbitrary-$d$ moment \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-all} and its
% ed.: consequence \Cref{thm:reciprocal-exact-tail} are genuinely new here.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block A02 ----
\begin{theorem}[The reciprocal filter and its exact tail]\label{thm:reciprocal-exact-tail}
For a formal power series $f(X)=\sum_{m\ge0}a_mX^m$ over $K$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocal-filter-definition}
 (\mathcal D_{n,q}f)(X)
 \DefinitionEquals\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_n}
   \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,f(x_kX).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 [X^m]\mathcal D_{n,q}f
 &=\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_n}\,(-1)^m(q^{-m};q)_n\,a_m,
 \label{eq:reciprocal-filter-diagonal}\\
 (\mathcal D_{n,q}f)(X)
 &=\sum_{r\ge0}(-1)^rq^{-nr}\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q}\,a_{n+r}X^{\,n+r}.
 \label{eq:reciprocal-filter-tail}
\end{align}
Thus $\mathcal D_{n,q}$ deletes the coefficients of degrees $0,\ldots,n-1$, reproduces $a_n$, and multiplies every later coefficient by an explicit signed Gaussian polynomial.  If $\deg f\le n$, then $(\mathcal D_{n,q}f)(1)=a_n$, in agreement with \Cref{cor:reciprocal-leading-coefficient}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The outer sum in \eqref{eq:reciprocal-filter-definition} is finite, so coefficient extraction commutes with it, and \eqref{eq:reciprocal-filter-diagonal} is \Cref{thm:geometric-moment-transform}.  The multiplier vanishes for $m<n$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-zero}.  Let $m=n+r$ with $r\ge0$.  Extracting $-q^{\,j-n-r}$ from each factor of $(q^{-n-r};q)_n$ gives
\[
 (q^{-n-r};q)_n
 =(-1)^nq^{\binom n2-n(n+r)}\bigl(q^{\,r+1};q\bigr)_n,
\]
since $\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(j-n-r)=\binom n2-n(n+r)$ and $\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-q^{\,n+r-j})=(q^{\,r+1};q)_n$.  Hence the sign becomes $(-1)^{n+r}(-1)^n=(-1)^r$, the total exponent of $q$ becomes
\[
 \binom{n+1}{2}+\binom n2-n(n+r)=-nr,
\]
and
\[
 \frac{(q^{\,r+1};q)_n}{(q;q)_n}
 =\frac{(q;q)_{n+r}}{(q;q)_r(q;q)_n}
 =\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:reciprocal-filter-tail}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The two exact tails]\label{rem:two-exact-tails}
\Cref{thm:geometric-filter-diagonal,thm:reciprocal-exact-tail} are companions, not restatements.  Both multiply the surviving coefficients by one Gaussian row, but they extract different coefficients on different node sets: $\mathcal G_{n,q}$ evaluates at $0$ on the nodes $q^k$ and returns $a_0$ with tail multiplier $(-1)^nq^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q}$, whereas $\mathcal D_{n,q}$ extracts the top coefficient on the nodes $-q^{-k}$ and returns $a_n$ with tail multiplier $(-1)^rq^{-nr}\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q}$.  The $r$-dependent sign and the negative power of $q$ have no counterpart in \eqref{eq:filter-exact-tail}: reversing a polynomial exchanges the two functionals only in the finite-degree case, and a power series has no top coefficient to reverse.  For $0<q<1$ the estimate $\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q}\le1/(q;q)_n$ used in \Cref{thm:geometric-filter-bound} applies verbatim to \eqref{eq:reciprocal-filter-tail}: if $f$ is analytic on $\AbsoluteValue z\le R$ with $R>q^{-n}$ and $M_R=\max_{\AbsoluteValue z=R}\AbsoluteValue{f}$, then
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{(\mathcal D_{n,q}f)(1)-a_n}
 \le\frac{M_R}{(q;q)_nR^{\,n}}\cdot
    \frac{q^{-n}/R}{1-q^{-n}/R}.
\]
% ed.: an incoming draft carried this bound as a separate corollary with its
% ed.: own proof; it is the mirror of \Cref{thm:geometric-filter-bound} and is
% ed.: recorded here as a remark instead.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block A05 ----
\begin{corollary}[Integrality and the exact $2$-adic valuation]\label{cor:halfbase-gaussian-valuation}
\index{valuation!$2$-adic}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!at $q=2$}
For $0\le k\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:halfbase-two-reciprocity}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}=2^{-k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{2} .
\end{equation}
The integer $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{2}$ is odd; consequently $2^{\,k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}$ is an odd integer and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:halfbase-valuation}
 \TwoAdicValuation\!\left(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}\right)=-k(n-k).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Reciprocity, \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}(ii), reads $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}=q^{-k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$; taking $q=2$ gives \eqref{eq:halfbase-two-reciprocity}.  By \Cref{cor:positivity} the Gaussian coefficient lies in $\NaturalNumbers[q]$, and by \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}(iii) its constant term is $1$; every other monomial $q^j$ with $j\ge1$ is even at $q=2$, so $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{2}\equiv1\pmod2$.  An odd integer has $2$-adic valuation $0$, so \eqref{eq:halfbase-valuation} follows from \eqref{eq:halfbase-two-reciprocity}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Reading the valuation]\label{rem:halfbase-valuation-reading}
Comparing \eqref{eq:halfbase-two-reciprocity} with \eqref{eq:halfbase-gaussian} identifies the odd integer explicitly:
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{2}=\frac{\mathfrak m_n}{\mathfrak m_k\,\mathfrak m_{n-k}},
\]
and by \Cref{thm:subspace-count} it counts the $k$-dimensional subspaces of $\FiniteField_{2}^{n}$.  Thus the half-base Gaussian row is a finite-geometric count divided by an explicit power of two, and the exponent of that power is $k(n-k)$, the dimension of the Grassmannian.  In the same way \eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial} exhibits $(1/2;1/2)_n$ as a power-of-two normalization of a product of Mersenne numbers, with $\TwoAdicValuation\!\left(\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}\right)=-\binom{n+1}{2}$ because $\mathfrak m_n$ is odd.
% ed.: an incoming draft restated reciprocity and the $\FiniteField_{2}$-subspace count in
% ed.: place; both are cross-referenced here instead.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M08 ----
% ed.: self-contained replacement for \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer},
% ed.: \Cref{rem:transfer-normalization} and \Cref{cor:transfer-translation}.
% ed.: The incoming draft carried this transfer principle at $q=1/2$ only, but
% ed.: with two independent series $U$ and $V$ in place of the single series $A$;
% ed.: the two generalizations are orthogonal and both are kept here.  The
% ed.: decoupling is not cosmetic: \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula} applies the
% ed.: theorem with $U(z)=\mathcal A(-z)$ and $V=\mathcal P_m\ne\mathcal A$, which
% ed.: the self-referential statement cannot reach.  The incoming draft's repair
% ed.: of its own hypothesis ($u_0$ invertible rather than $u_0=1$) is
% ed.: \Cref{rem:transfer-normalization}, which already said this for $a_0$.
\begin{theorem}[Gaussian coefficient transfer]\label{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer}
\index{coefficient transfer}
\index{coefficient extraction!dyadic Gaussian}
\index{double cancellation}
Let $K$ have characteristic zero, let $q\in K^{\times}$ satisfy $q^j\ne1$ for $1\le j\le n$, and let
\[
 U(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}u_mz^m,\qquad u_0=1,
 \qquad
 V(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}v_mz^m
\]
be power series in $K[[z]]$.  For each $0\le k\le n$ there is exactly one series $\Psi_k(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}c_{k,m}z^m\in K[[z]]$ satisfying the bridge relation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-bridge}
 \Psi_k(z)\,U(z)=\EulerE^{-z}\,V\bigl(-q^{-k}z\bigr),
\end{equation}
namely $\Psi_k(z)=\EulerE^{-z}V(-q^{-k}z)/U(z)$, the quotient being legitimate in $K[[z]]$ because $U$ has constant term $1$.  For that family, with the weights of \eqref{eq:reciprocal-nodes-weights},
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,c_{k,d}&=0
 \qquad(0\le d<n),\label{eq:transfer-lower-zero}\\
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,c_{k,n}
 &=q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n\,v_n.\label{eq:transfer-top}
\end{align}
Of the two series, only the single coefficient $v_n$ is ever seen; $U$ enters through its constant term alone.  In the self-referential case $U(z)=A(-z)$, $V=A$ with $a_0=1$, in which \eqref{eq:general-bridge} reads $\Psi_k(z)A(-z)=\EulerE^{-z}A(-q^{-k}z)$ and $v_n=a_n$, the theorem therefore extracts the $n$th coefficient of $A$ from the family attached to $A$ itself; that is the form used in \Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $S_d\DefinitionEquals\sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)c_{k,d}$.  Comparing the coefficient of $z^d$ on the two sides of \eqref{eq:general-bridge} gives, for each fixed $k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bridge-coeff}
 \sum_{i=0}^{d}u_{d-i}\,c_{k,i}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{d}\frac{(-1)^j}{j!}\,v_{d-j}\bigl(-q^{-k}\bigr)^{d-j}.
\end{equation}
Multiply \eqref{eq:bridge-coeff} by $W_{n,k}(q)$ and sum over $k$.  Since $x_k=-q^{-k}$, the right-hand side becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bridge-coeff-summed}
 \sum_{j=0}^{d}\frac{(-1)^j}{j!}\,v_{d-j}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,x_k^{\,d-j},
\end{equation}
and the left-hand side becomes $\sum_{i=0}^{d}u_{d-i}S_i$.

Let $0\le d<n$ and induct on $d$.  Every exponent occurring in \eqref{eq:bridge-coeff-summed} satisfies $0\le d-j\le d<n$, so the entire right-hand side vanishes by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-zero}.  On the left, the terms with $i<d$ vanish by the induction hypothesis (for $d=0$ there are none), and the term $i=d$ equals $u_0S_d=S_d$.  Hence $S_d=0$, which is \eqref{eq:transfer-lower-zero}.

Now take $d=n$.  In \eqref{eq:bridge-coeff-summed} every term with $j\ge1$ carries an exponent $n-j<n$ and vanishes, while the term $j=0$ equals
\[
 v_n\sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,x_k^{\,n}
 =q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n\,v_n
\]
by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-top}.  On the left, the terms with $i<n$ vanish by \eqref{eq:transfer-lower-zero} and the term $i=n$ equals $u_0S_n=S_n$.  This proves \eqref{eq:transfer-top}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[On the normalization $u_0=1$]\label{rem:transfer-normalization}
The proof uses $u_0=1$ twice: once for the invertibility of $U$, and once at the last step of each induction stage.  Only invertibility of $u_0$ is actually needed: for arbitrary $u_0\ne0$ the same argument gives $u_0S_d=0$ for $d<n$ and $u_0S_n=q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_nv_n$.  This is not a restriction, because \eqref{eq:general-bridge} is unchanged when the pair $(U,V)$ is divided by $u_0$.  In every application below the normalization is automatic, $U$ being $\mathcal A(-z)$ or $A(-z)$ with $A(0)=1$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Invariance under a common translation]\label{cor:transfer-translation}
Under the hypotheses of \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer}, let $\alpha\in K$ and put $\widetilde\Psi_k(z)=\EulerE^{\alpha z}\Psi_k(z)$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transfer-translation-invariance}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,[z^n]\widetilde\Psi_k(z)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(q)\,c_{k,n}
 =q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n\,v_n .
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Convolution gives $[z^n]\widetilde\Psi_k=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\alpha^jc_{k,n-j}/j!$.  Weighting by $W_{n,k}(q)$ and summing over $k$, the term with index $j$ carries the factor $S_{n-j}$; every $j\ge1$ has $n-j<n$ and therefore vanishes by \eqref{eq:transfer-lower-zero}.  Only $j=0$ survives, and \eqref{eq:transfer-top} evaluates it.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two nested triangular systems]\label{rem:double-cancellation}
The proof runs two triangular cancellations in series.  First the finite $q$-binomial theorem annihilates every node power of degree below $n$.  Second, the coefficient comparison \eqref{eq:bridge-coeff} propagates that annihilation successively through $c_{k,0},c_{k,1},\ldots,c_{k,n-1}$.  The first quantity surviving both is $a_n$.  This double cancellation is what makes \Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} possible, and \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} is the reason a free parameter may be inserted there.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M06 ----
\section{The normalized Thue--Morse block}
\index{Thue--Morse sequence!normalized exponential block}

The Thue--Morse polynomial of \Cref{thm:thue-morse-product} has a zero of order exactly
$k$ at $z=1$.  Dividing that zero out produces an entire function whose Taylor
coefficients are precisely the Thue--Morse power sums, and which bridges $\mathcal A$ to
its own dilate: that bridge is the hypothesis \eqref{eq:general-bridge} of
\Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} at $q=1/2$, where $q^{-k}=2^k$.  Throughout,
$\mathcal A\DefinitionEquals\mathcal A_{1/2}$, $\alpha_n\DefinitionEquals\alpha^{(1/2)}_n$ and
$\tau_r=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}$.

For $k\ge0$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Psi-k-definition}
 \Psi_k(z)\DefinitionEquals\EulerE^{-z}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\Lambda\bigl(-2^jz\bigr)
 =\sum_{m\ge0}c_{k,m}z^m,
\end{equation}
and, for a translation parameter $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$ and an integer $d\ge0$, write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thue-morse-power-sums}
 \widetilde T_{k,d}(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,(r+\theta)^d,
 \qquad
 T_{k,d}(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\widetilde T_{k,d}\bigl(\theta-2^k\bigr)
 =\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,\bigl(r-2^k+\theta\bigr)^d .
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Thue--Morse blocks as dilation bridges]\label{lem:thue-morse-dilation-bridge}
For every $k\ge0$ the function $\Psi_k$ is entire,
\begin{align}
 \Psi_k(z)&=\EulerE^{-2^kz}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\Lambda\bigl(2^jz\bigr),
 \label{eq:psi-positive-form}\\
 \Psi_k(z)\,\mathcal A(-z)&=\EulerE^{-z}\,\mathcal A\bigl(-2^kz\bigr),
 \label{eq:TM-A-bridge}
\end{align}
and for every $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Psi-TM-expansion}
 \EulerE^{(1-\theta)z}\Psi_k(z)
 =\frac1{2^{\binom k2}z^k}\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,\EulerE^{-(r+\theta)z},
\end{equation}
the singularity of the right-hand side at $z=0$ being removable.  Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Psi-coefficients}
 [z^m]\,\EulerE^{(1-\theta)z}\Psi_k(z)
 =\frac{(-1)^{m+k}}{2^{\binom k2}(m+k)!}\,\widetilde T_{k,m+k}(\theta)
 \qquad(m\ge0).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$\Lambda$ is entire, hence so is $\Psi_k$.  The reflection identity
\[
 \Lambda(-u)=\frac{\EulerE^{-u}-1}{-u}=\EulerE^{-u}\,\frac{\EulerE^{u}-1}{u}=\EulerE^{-u}\Lambda(u)
\]
turns $\prod_{j<k}\Lambda(-2^jz)$ into $\EulerE^{-(2^k-1)z}\prod_{j<k}\Lambda(2^jz)$, because
$\sum_{j<k}2^j=2^k-1$; multiplying by $\EulerE^{-z}$ gives \eqref{eq:psi-positive-form}.

Iterating \eqref{eq:fabius-dilation} at the arguments $-z,-2z,\ldots,-2^{k-1}z$ gives
\[
 \mathcal A\bigl(-2^kz\bigr)=\mathcal A(-z)\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\Lambda\bigl(-2^jz\bigr),
\]
and multiplication by $\EulerE^{-z}$ is \eqref{eq:TM-A-bridge}.

Next, $\Lambda(-2^jz)=(1-\EulerE^{-2^jz})/(2^jz)$ and $\sum_{j<k}j=\binom k2$, so
\[
 \EulerE^{(1-\theta)z}\Psi_k(z)
 =\frac{\EulerE^{-\theta z}}{2^{\binom k2}z^k}
  \prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(1-\EulerE^{-2^jz}\bigr).
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:thue-morse-product} with its variable set to $\EulerE^{-z}$ turns the
product into $\sum_{r<2^k}\tau_r\EulerE^{-rz}$, and multiplication by $\EulerE^{-\theta z}$ gives
\eqref{eq:Psi-TM-expansion}.  The numerator vanishes to order exactly $k$ at $z=0$ by
\eqref{eq:thue-morse-moments}, so the quotient is entire.

Finally, expand $\EulerE^{-(r+\theta)z}=\sum_{p\ge0}(-1)^p(r+\theta)^pz^p/p!$.  For $p<k$ the
polynomial $X\mapsto(X+\theta)^p$ has degree $p<k$, so
$\widetilde T_{k,p}(\theta)=0$ by \eqref{eq:thue-morse-polynomial-functional} with $n=k$,
$a=\theta$, $b=1$.  Hence
\[
 \sum_{r<2^k}\tau_r\EulerE^{-(r+\theta)z}
 =\sum_{p\ge k}\frac{(-1)^pz^p}{p!}\,\widetilde T_{k,p}(\theta),
\]
and dividing by $2^{\binom k2}z^k$ and writing $p=m+k$ gives \eqref{eq:Psi-coefficients}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Centred form and the coefficient dictionary]\label{prop:psi-normalization}
For every $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thue-morse-normalized}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,\EulerE^{(r-2^k)z}
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}z^k\,\Psi_k(z),
\end{equation}
and the Taylor coefficients of $\Psi_k$ itself are the centred Thue--Morse power sums:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:psi-coefficient-dictionary}
 c_{k,m}
 =(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\frac{T_{k,m+k}(0)}{(m+k)!}
 =\frac{(-1)^{m+k}}{2^{\binom k2}(m+k)!}\,\widetilde T_{k,m+k}(1)
 \qquad(m\ge0).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Substituting $z\mapsto\EulerE^{z}$ in \eqref{eq:thue-morse-product} gives
$\sum_{r<2^k}\tau_r\EulerE^{rz}=\prod_{j<k}(1-\EulerE^{2^jz})$, and $1-\EulerE^{u}=-u\Lambda(u)$, so
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(1-\EulerE^{2^jz}\bigr)
 =\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigl(-2^jz\,\Lambda(2^jz)\bigr)
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}z^k\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\Lambda\bigl(2^jz\bigr).
\]
Multiplying by $\EulerE^{-2^kz}$ and applying \eqref{eq:psi-positive-form} proves
\eqref{eq:thue-morse-normalized}.  Its left-hand side is the exponential generating
function $\sum_{d\ge0}T_{k,d}(0)z^d/d!$, so comparing the coefficient of $z^{m+k}$ gives
the first equality in \eqref{eq:psi-coefficient-dictionary}.  For the second, note that
$2^k-1-r$ is the bitwise complement of $r$ in $k$ binary digits, whence
$\BinaryDigitSum(2^k-1-r)=k-\BinaryDigitSum(r)$ and $\tau_{2^k-1-r}=(-1)^k\tau_r$; replacing $r$ by $2^k-1-r$ in
$T_{k,d}(0)=\sum_r\tau_r(r-2^k)^d$ therefore gives
$T_{k,d}(0)=(-1)^{d+k}\widetilde T_{k,d}(1)$.  Alternatively, the second equality is
\eqref{eq:Psi-coefficients} at $\theta=1$, where the factor $\EulerE^{(1-\theta)z}$ is $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Centred and raw coordinates]\label{rem:psi-two-coordinates}
The two displays \eqref{eq:Psi-TM-expansion} and \eqref{eq:thue-morse-normalized} are one
identity in two coordinate systems.  Indeed \eqref{eq:psi-positive-form} says
$\Psi_k(z)=\EulerE^{-(2^k+1)z}\Psi_k(-z)$, and substituting $z\mapsto-z$ in
\eqref{eq:Psi-TM-expansion} at $\theta=-2^k$ returns \eqref{eq:thue-morse-normalized}.
The centred sums $T_{k,d}$ are the natural coefficients of $\Psi_k$ itself, while the raw
sums $\widetilde T_{k,d}(\theta)$ with a free $\theta$ are what
\Cref{cor:transfer-translation} feeds into \Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}; by
\eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-sums} the dictionary between them is
$T_{k,d}(\theta)=(-1)^{d+k}\widetilde T_{k,d}(1-\theta)$.
\end{remark}
% ed.: this block merges the sibling lemma with the incoming
% ed.: \texttt{prop:psi-normalization}: the two define the same $\Psi_k$, and the
% ed.: incoming centred identity and dictionary are the case $\theta=1$ of
% ed.: \eqref{eq:Psi-coefficients} after the complement antisymmetry.  Only
% ed.: \eqref{eq:psi-positive-form} and the notation \eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-sums}
% ed.: are genuinely new, and both are retained.

% ---- merged block A09 ----
\begin{theorem}[Dyadic Gaussian--Thue--Morse coefficient formula]\label{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}
For every $n\ge0$ and every $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}
 \boxed{\;
 a_n=\frac{(-1)^n}{\mathfrak m_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}(r+\theta)^{\,n+k}.\;}
\end{equation}
Every inner power sum depends on $\theta$; the total does not.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\theta$ and set $\widetilde\Psi_k(z)=\EulerE^{(1-\theta)z}\Psi_k(z)$.  By \eqref{eq:TM-A-bridge} the family $\Psi_0,\ldots,\Psi_n$ satisfies the bridge relation \eqref{eq:general-bridge} with $q=1/2$, since then $q^{-k}=2^k$; and $A$ has constant term $1$ by \Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}.  Hence \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} applies, and \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} with $\alpha=1-\theta$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-transfer-top}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(1/2)\,[z^n]\widetilde\Psi_k(z)
 =2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n a_n
 =\mathfrak m_n\,a_n,
\end{equation}
the last equality by \eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial}.  Comparing \eqref{eq:reciprocal-nodes-weights} with \eqref{eq:halfbase-prouhet-data},
\[
 W_{n,k}(1/2)=(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}=w_{n,k},
\]
while \eqref{eq:Psi-coefficients} at $m=n$ gives
\[
 [z^n]\widetilde\Psi_k(z)
 =\frac{(-1)^{n+k}}{2^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
  \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r(r+\theta)^{\,n+k}.
\]
In the product of the two, the signs combine to $(-1)^k(-1)^{n+k}=(-1)^n$, independent of $k$, and the powers of two combine to $2^{-\binom k2}2^{-\binom k2}=4^{-\binom k2}$.  Substituting into \eqref{eq:dyadic-transfer-top} and dividing by $\mathfrak m_n$ proves \eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}.  The left side of \eqref{eq:dyadic-transfer-top} does not depend on $\theta$, by \Cref{cor:transfer-translation}; this is the asserted invariance.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The first two cases]\label{ex:dyadic-formula-small}
Take $\theta=1$.  For $n=1$ one has $\mathfrak m_1=1$ and $\GaussianBinomial{1}{0}{1/2}=\GaussianBinomial{1}{1}{1/2}=1$; the inner sums are $1^1=1$ at $k=0$ and $1^2-2^2=-3$ at $k=1$, so
\[
 a_1=-\left(\frac{1}{1!}+\frac{-3}{2!}\right)=-\left(1-\frac32\right)=\frac12 .
\]
For $n=2$ one has $\mathfrak m_2=3$ and the half-base Gaussian row $1,\tfrac32,1$; the inner sums are $1$, $1^3-2^3=-7$, and $1^4-2^4-3^4+4^4=160$, so
\[
 a_2=\frac13\left(\frac{1}{2!}+\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{-7}{3!}
      +\frac{160}{4^1\cdot4!}\right)
    =\frac13\left(\frac12-\frac74+\frac53\right)
    =\frac13\cdot\frac5{12}=\frac5{36},
\]
in agreement with the value produced by \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence}.
\end{example}

% ---- merged block M09 ----
\begin{theorem}[Inverse-dyadic values of the Fabius function]\label{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic}
\index{Fabius function!closed formula at $2^{-n}$}
% ed.: this theorem formerly restated the moment evaluation
% ed.: $F(2^{-n})=\Expectation(X^n)/(n!\,2^{\binom n2})$ and proved it by the
% ed.: cube cut.  Both are now \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}, which also
% ed.: carries the derivative-iterate proof; only the closed form is stated
% ed.: here.
By \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}, $F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}a_n$ with
$a_n=\Expectation(X^n)/n!$.  Consequently, for every $n\ge0$ and every
$\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed}
 \boxed{\;
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac{(-1)^n}{2^{\,n^2}\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}(r+\theta)^{\,n+k}.\;}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
% ed.: the cube-cut derivation of the moment form that stood here is now
% ed.: the second proof of \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}, together with the
% ed.: scaled split \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split} it introduces; repeating
% ed.: it would duplicate both the argument and those two equation labels.
Only the closed form has to be proved.

For \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed}, multiply \eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} by $2^{-\binom n2}$ and use \eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial} together with $\binom n2+\binom{n+1}{2}=n^2$:
\[
 2^{\binom n2}\mathfrak m_n
 =2^{\binom n2+\binom{n+1}{2}}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n
 =2^{\,n^2}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}
% ed.: since $n^2\equiv n\pmod2$ one may absorb the sign into the denominator
% ed.: and write $(-1)^n2^{-n^2}=(-2)^{-n^2}$; an incoming draft stated
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} in that packaging.

The blocks of \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} may also be centred at their own right endpoints.  This is the normalization in which the inner sums are the bare Taylor coefficients of the Thue--Morse blocks $\Psi_k$, and it costs one application of digit complementation.

% ed.: self-contained replacement for \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}, which
% ed.: stated the case $m=1$ only.  An incoming draft carried the same centred
% ed.: formula for an arbitrary dyadic numerator $m/2^n$; since its $m=1$ case is
% ed.: verbatim the corollary, the two are stated here once, in the general form,
% ed.: under the corollary's own label.  The digit-complementation computation
% ed.: that produced the $k$-independent sign $(-1)^n$ is retained in the last
% ed.: paragraph of the proof, where \Cref{rem:dyadic-shift-common} cites it.
% ed.: Notation: the incoming draft wrote $\mathcal H_m$ for the numerator factor,
% ed.: which collides with the Hessenberg matrix of \Cref{thm:fabius-hessenberg};
% ed.: renamed $\mathcal K_m$ here.
% ed.: Retained from the replaced text: \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula} at
% ed.: $\theta=0$ is what the transfer theorem produces when applied to $\Psi_k$
% ed.: with no auxiliary factor, the inner sums then being
% ed.: $(n+k)!\,(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}[z^n]\Psi_k(z)$.  For even $n$ the reflection is
% ed.: sign-free, so the centred sums at $\theta=0$ coincide term by term with the
% ed.: raw sums at $\theta=1$; this is why \Cref{ex:dyadic-formula-small} serves
% ed.: both displays at $n=2$, and an incoming draft's separate centred worked
% ed.: example at $n=2$ (inner sums $1,-7,160$, total $5/12$) is that same
% ed.: computation.

The blocks of \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} may also be centred at their
own right endpoints.  This is the normalization in which the inner sums are the
bare Taylor coefficients of the Thue--Morse blocks $\Psi_k$, it costs one
application of digit complementation, and --- unlike the raw normalization --- it
extends from the inverse powers $2^{-n}$ to every dyadic point of $[0,1]$ at
resolution $2^{-n}$, the numerator entering only through the length of the
Thue--Morse block.

For integers $m,k,d\ge0$ and $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$ define the \emph{long} Thue--Morse block
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-long-block}
 T_{m;k,d}(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}\tau_r\,\bigl(r-m2^k+\theta\bigr)^d,
 \qquad \tau_r=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)},
\end{equation}
an empty sum being $0$; thus $T_{0;k,d}=0$ and $T_{1;k,d}=T_{k,d}$ is the centred
power sum of \eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-sums}.

\begin{corollary}[Centred form, at an arbitrary dyadic point]\label{cor:fabius-centered-formula}
\index{Thue--Morse sequence!digit complement}
\index{Fabius function!value at a dyadic point}
For $n,m\ge0$ and $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-numerator}
 \mathcal N_{m,n}(\theta)
 \DefinitionEquals\sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}\,T_{m;k,n+k}(\theta),
 \qquad
 \mathcal N_n(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\mathcal N_{1,n}(\theta).
\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal N_{m,n}(\theta)$ does not depend on $\theta$, and for every integer $m$
with $0\le m\le2^n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-dyadic-value}
 F\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{\mathcal N_{m,n}(\theta)}{2^{\binom n2}\mathfrak m_n}
 =\frac1{2^{\,n^2}\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}\tau_r\,\bigl(r-m2^k+\theta\bigr)^{\,n+k}.
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathcal N_n(\theta)=\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$ for every $\theta$, where
$\alpha_n=\Expectation X^n/n!$ is the coefficient called $a_n$ in
\Cref{thm:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}, and the case $m=1$ reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-centered-formula}
 F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac{\mathcal N_n(\theta)}{2^{\binom n2}\mathfrak m_n}
 =\frac1{2^{\,n^2}\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,\bigl(r-2^k+\theta\bigr)^{\,n+k},
\end{equation}
with no sign $(-1)^n$ in front.  The hypothesis $m\le2^n$ cannot be dropped: at
$n=2$, $m=5$ the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} equals $67/72$,
whereas $F(5/4)=1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $n=0$ the admissible numerators are $m=0$ and $m=1$, and
$\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_0=1$; the single term $k=0$ is $\sum_{r<m}\tau_r$,
which is $0$ for $m=0$ and $\tau_0=1$ for $m=1$, in agreement with $F(0)=0$ and
$F(1)=1$.  Assume $n\ge1$.

\emph{Step 1: the value is a moment functional.}  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-numerator-series}
 \mathcal K_m(z)\DefinitionEquals\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}\tau_h\,\EulerE^{(m-h-1)z},
 \qquad
 \mathcal P_m(z)\DefinitionEquals\mathcal A(z)\,\mathcal K_m(z)=\sum_{d\ge0}p_{m,d}z^d,
\end{equation}
so that $\mathcal P_1=\mathcal A$ and, by \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-numerator-moment}
 p_{m,n}=\frac1{n!}\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}\tau_h\,\Expectation\,(X+m-h-1)^n .
\end{equation}
We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}
 F\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)=2^{-\binom n2}\,p_{m,n}
 \qquad(0\le m\le2^n).
\end{equation}
Integrating \eqref{eq:dyadic-thue-morse-spline} term by term, with
$\int_0^x(t-c)_+^{\,n-1}\Differential t=(x-c)_+^{\,n}/n$, gives the distribution function of the
truncation $X_{1/2,n}$ of \eqref{eq:finite-geometric-uniform-sum}:
\[
 F_{1/2,n}(x)
 =\frac{2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}\sum_{r=0}^{2^n-1}\tau_r
   \left(x-\frac r{2^n}\right)_+^{\,n}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split}, $X=X_{1/2,n}+2^{-n}X'$ with $X'$ an independent
copy of $X$, so conditioning on $X'$ and using $x\mapsto2^{-n}(m-x)$,
\[
 F\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)
 =\Expectation\Bigl[F_{1/2,n}\bigl(2^{-n}(m-X')\bigr)\Bigr]
 =\frac{2^{\binom{n+1}{2}-n^2}}{n!}
  \sum_{r=0}^{2^n-1}\tau_r\,\Expectation\bigl[(m-r-X')_+^{\,n}\bigr],
\]
the factor $2^{-n\cdot n}$ coming from the $n$th power of $2^{-n}$.  Since
$0\le X'\le1$ and $m\le2^n$, a term with $r\ge m$ has $m-r-X'\le0$ and drops out,
while a term with $r<m$ has $m-r-X'\ge0$ and keeps its positive part.  With
$\binom{n+1}{2}-n^2=-\binom n2$ and $1-X'\overset d=X$ from
\eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry},
\[
 F\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}\tau_r\,\Expectation\,(m-r-X')^n
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}\tau_r\,\Expectation\,(X+m-r-1)^n,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment} by \eqref{eq:fabius-numerator-moment}.  At
$m=1$ this is \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} again, since $p_{1,n}=\alpha_n$.

\emph{Step 2: the long block is a dilated bridge.}  Write $r=h2^k+s$ with
$0\le h<m$ and $0\le s<2^k$; this is a bijection onto $\SetOf{0,\ldots,m2^k-1}$, and the
binary digits of $h2^k$ and of $s$ occupy disjoint positions, so
$\tau_{h2^k+s}=\tau_h\tau_s$.  Hence
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{d\ge0}T_{m;k,d}(0)\frac{z^d}{d!}
 &=\EulerE^{-m2^kz}\sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}\tau_r\EulerE^{rz}
  =\EulerE^{-m2^kz}
   \Bigl(\sum_{h=0}^{m-1}\tau_h\EulerE^{h2^kz}\Bigr)
   \Bigl(\sum_{s=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_s\EulerE^{sz}\Bigr)\\
 &=\mathcal K_m\bigl(-2^kz\bigr)
   \Bigl(\EulerE^{-2^kz}\sum_{s=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_s\EulerE^{sz}\Bigr)
  =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}z^k\,\Psi_{m,k}(z),
\end{align*}
where $\Psi_{m,k}(z)\DefinitionEquals\mathcal K_m(-2^kz)\Psi_k(z)$.  The third equality
uses
\[
 \mathcal K_m(-2^kz)
 =\EulerE^{-(m-1)2^kz}\sum_{h<m}\tau_h\EulerE^{h2^kz},
\]
and the fourth is \eqref{eq:thue-morse-normalized}.  Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:long-block-coefficient}
 [z^n]\,\Psi_{m,k}(z)
 =\frac{(-1)^k}{2^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}\,T_{m;k,n+k}(0),
\end{equation}
and multiplying \eqref{eq:TM-A-bridge} by $\mathcal K_m(-2^kz)$ gives
\[
 \Psi_{m,k}(z)\,\mathcal A(-z)
 =\EulerE^{-z}\,\mathcal K_m\bigl(-2^kz\bigr)\mathcal A\bigl(-2^kz\bigr)
 =\EulerE^{-z}\,\mathcal P_m\bigl(-2^kz\bigr).
\]

\emph{Step 3: transfer.}  Apply \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} at $q=1/2$,
where $q^{-k}=2^k$, with $U(z)=\mathcal A(-z)$, whose constant term is $1$ by
\Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}, with $V=\mathcal P_m$, and with the family
$\Psi_{m,0},\ldots,\Psi_{m,n}$, for which Step 2 is the bridge relation
\eqref{eq:general-bridge}.  By \eqref{eq:transfer-top} and \eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial},
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(1/2)\,[z^n]\Psi_{m,k}
 =2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_np_{m,n}
 =\mathfrak m_n\,p_{m,n},
\]
and inserting \eqref{eq:long-block-coefficient} together with
$W_{n,k}(1/2)=(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}$ makes the two signs cancel and the
two triangular powers of $2$ combine into $4^{-\binom k2}$:
\[
 \mathcal N_{m,n}(0)=\mathfrak m_n\,p_{m,n}.
\]
Dividing by $2^{\binom n2}\mathfrak m_n$ and using \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}
proves \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} at $\theta=0$; the second expression follows from
$2^{\binom n2}\mathfrak m_n=2^{\,n^2}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n$, which is
\eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial} with $\binom n2+\binom{n+1}{2}=n^2$.

\emph{The free parameter.}  Translating the inner argument multiplies the exponential
generating function of Step 2 by $\EulerE^{\theta z}$, so
$[z^n]\bigl(\EulerE^{\theta z}\Psi_{m,k}\bigr)
 =(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}T_{m;k,n+k}(\theta)/(n+k)!$, while
\Cref{cor:transfer-translation} with $\alpha=\theta$ leaves the weighted sum
unchanged.  Hence $\mathcal N_{m,n}(\theta)=\mathcal N_{m,n}(0)$ for every $\theta$.

\emph{Consistency with the raw form.}  For $m=1$ the two normalizations are exchanged
by digit complementation.  Substituting $r\mapsto2^k-1-r$ in the inner sum of
$\mathcal N_n(1-\theta)$ and using $\BinaryDigitSum(2^k-1-r)=k-\BinaryDigitSum(r)$, hence
$\tau_{2^k-1-r}=(-1)^k\tau_r$, together with
$(2^k-1-r)-2^k+(1-\theta)=-(r+\theta)$, gives
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-2^k+1-\theta\bigr)^{\,n+k}
 =(-1)^{n+2k}\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r(r+\theta)^{\,n+k},
\]
and $(-1)^{n+2k}=(-1)^n$ does not depend on $k$, so it factors out of the sum over
$k$: $\mathcal N_n(1-\theta)$ is $(-1)^n$ times the sum in
\eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}, which is $\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$.  This
recovers the case $m=1$ a second time, and it is the $k$-independent sign referred to
in \Cref{rem:dyadic-shift-common}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Resolution and numerator are separated]\label{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture}
\index{Fabius function!value at a dyadic point}
In \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} the half-base Gaussian row
$\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}\big/4^{\binom k2}$ depends only on the resolution $2^{-n}$, while the
numerator $m$ enters only through the length $m2^k$ of the Thue--Morse block.  It is
instructive to compare \Cref{thm:dyadic-thue-morse-spline}, which writes the density of
the truncation $X_{1/2,N}$ as a Thue--Morse signed spline: there the Thue--Morse signs
appear alone and the identity is exact only for the truncation, whereas here they
appear weighted by a Gaussian row and the identity is exact for the limit function $F$
itself.  The formula returns $0$ at $m=0$ and $1$ at $m=2^n$, and since
$F(m/2^n)=F(2m/2^{n+1})$ it is heavily overdetermined.  The row $n=3$ is
\[
 0,\quad\frac1{288},\quad\frac5{72},\quad\frac{73}{288},\quad\frac12,
 \quad\frac{215}{288},\quad\frac{67}{72},\quad\frac{287}{288},\quad1,
\]
symmetric about $1/2$ as \eqref{eq:fabius-symmetry} requires.
% ed.: verified in exact rational arithmetic for every $n\le5$ and every
% ed.: $0\le m\le2^n$ at $\theta\in\set{0,1,-3/2,7/3,11/5}$ ($\theta$-independence,
% ed.: endpoints, symmetry, strict monotonicity, and agreement across resolutions),
% ed.: and against Fourier inversion of \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} at
% ed.: $m/2^n=1/4,\,1/8,\,3/8,\,5/16,\,7/32,\,11/16$ to $10^{-17}$.
\end{remark}
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula} at $\theta=0$ is what the transfer
% ed.: theorem produces when applied to $\Psi_k$ with no auxiliary factor, the
% ed.: inner sums then being $(n+k)!\,(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}[z^n]\Psi_k(z)$.  For
% ed.: even $n$ the reflection is sign-free, so the centred sums at $\theta=0$
% ed.: coincide term by term with the raw sums at $\theta=1$; this is why
% ed.: \Cref{ex:dyadic-formula-small} serves both displays at $n=2$, and an
% ed.: incoming draft's separate centred worked example at $n=2$ (inner sums
% ed.: $1,-7,160$, total $5/12$) is that same computation.

% ---- merged block M11 ----
% ---- merged block M11 ----
% ed.: self-contained replacement for \Cref{rem:dyadic-shift-common}.  It reuses
% ed.: that label and \eqref{eq:dyadic-numerator-blockwise} and introduces no new
% ed.: label.  The two displayed families, the counterexample and the sentence
% ed.: about the $k$-independent sign (cited from the proof of
% ed.: \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}) are all retained verbatim; what is
% ed.: corrected is the closing assertion, for the second time.  The first
% ed.: correction added the family $\theta_k=2^k+\theta$; the present one records
% ed.: that it and the common shift are the members $\vartheta=-1$ and
% ed.: $\vartheta=0$ of one two-parameter family, on all of which the double
% ed.: cancellation survives and \Cref{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift}
% ed.: evaluates the sum.  All three displayed values recomputed in exact
% ed.: rational arithmetic.
\begin{remark}[The shift must be common, or geometric]\label{rem:dyadic-shift-common}
\index{translation invariance!of the dyadic formula}
The freedom in $\theta$ in \eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse},
\eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} and \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula}
is \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} at $q=1/2$: one exponential $\EulerE^{\alpha z}$ is
pulled out of all $n+1$ blocks \emph{simultaneously}, after which
\eqref{eq:transfer-lower-zero} annihilates every convolution term but one.  It is
not a symmetry of an individual block.  To see what is and is not allowed, give
each block its own shift and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-numerator-blockwise}
 \mathcal N_n(\theta_0,\ldots,\theta_n)
 \DefinitionEquals\sum_{k=0}^{n}
 \frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-2^k+\theta_k\bigr)^{\,n+k}.
\end{equation}
Two one-parameter families of shifts leave this constant, and they are exactly
the two displays above:
\[
 \mathcal N_n(\theta,\ldots,\theta)=\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n,
 \qquad
 \mathcal N_n\bigl(2^0+\theta,\ldots,2^n+\theta\bigr)
 =(-1)^n\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n ,
\]
the first by \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}, the second by
\eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} after cancelling $-2^k$ against $2^k$.  The
second family is genuinely block-dependent, and it survives only because digit
complementation converts it into the first, at the cost of the $k$-independent
sign $(-1)^n$ computed in the proof of \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}.

These two families are not independent of each other, and they are not the only
structured ones.  Both are members of the two-parameter geometric family
$\theta_k=\eta-2^k\vartheta$ of \Cref{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift},
at $\vartheta=0$ and at $\vartheta=-1$ respectively; and on the whole of that
family the double cancellation of \Cref{rem:double-cancellation} survives and
evaluates the sum in closed form,
\[
 \mathcal N_n\bigl(\eta-2^0\vartheta,\ldots,\eta-2^n\vartheta\bigr)
 =\mathfrak m_n\,\frac{A_n(\vartheta)}{n!},
\]
a polynomial of degree $n$ in $\vartheta$, free of $\eta$, whose value at
$\vartheta=0$ is the invariant $\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$.  What fails away from
$\vartheta\in\SetOf{0,-1}$ is therefore not the cancellation but the constancy: the
transfer theorem still extracts exactly one coefficient, only of
$\EulerE^{\vartheta u}\mathcal A(u)$ rather than of $\mathcal A(u)$.

Outside that family, unequal shifts do destroy the extraction.  For $n=3$ take
$(\theta_0,\theta_1,\theta_2,\theta_3)=(0,1,2,3)$, which is not of the form
$\eta-2^k\vartheta$: matching the first two entries forces
$\vartheta=\eta=-1$, and that choice produces $(0,1,3,7)$ instead.  One finds
\[
 \mathcal N_3(0,1,2,3)=\frac1{32},
 \qquad
 \mathcal N_3(0,1,3,7)=-\frac7{12},
 \qquad
 \mathfrak m_3\alpha_3=21\cdot\frac1{36}=\frac7{12},
\]
the middle value being $\mathfrak m_3A_3(-1)/3!$, as
\Cref{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift} requires, and the first being nothing in
particular.
% ed.: an incoming draft stated the invariance as a separate theorem -- it is
% ed.: \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} at $q=1/2$ -- and asserted that any
% ed.: per-block translation destroys the cancellation.  The counterexample is
% ed.: kept.  That assertion was corrected once, for the shifts
% ed.: $\theta_k=2^k+\theta$, and again here, for the geometric family.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M13 ----
\begin{remark}[Anatomy and conditioning of the dyadic formula]\label{rem:dyadic-formula-anatomy}
\index{Fabius function!closed formula at $2^{-n}$}
\index{numerical stability!of the dyadic formula}
\index{valuation!$2$-adic}
The ingredients of \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} separate cleanly, and each has a
single identifiable origin.  One factor $2^{-\binom k2}$, together with the row
$\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}$, is the geometric difference stencil $w_{n,k}$ on the nodes $-2^{k}$ of
\Cref{thm:geometric-prouhet}; the second factor $2^{-\binom k2}$ appears when the order-$k$
Thue--Morse zero is removed in \eqref{eq:Psi-TM-expansion}.  Their product is the
$4^{-\binom k2}$ of the statement: the duplication is structural, not a misprint.  The
product $\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n$ is the normalized geometric Vandermonde
denominator of that stencil, equal by \eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial} to the odd Mersenne
product $\mathfrak m_n=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(2^{j}-1)$ divided by $2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}$, and the
remaining $2^{-\binom n2}$ is the Jacobian $\prod_{j=1}^{n}2^{\,n-j}$ of the cube cut
\eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split}.  The factorials $(n+k)!$ are contributed by the
exponential generating functions, and each inner sum is an integer, being a power sum of
Thue--Morse signs whose first $k$ moments vanish.

Arithmetically, therefore, four independent sources meet in one line: dyadic dilation
powers, of which \eqref{eq:halfbase-two-reciprocity} isolates the part carried by the
Gaussian row, $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}=2^{-k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{2}$ with an \emph{odd} integer on the
right (\Cref{cor:halfbase-gaussian-valuation}); the odd Mersenne product $\mathfrak m_n$;
ordinary factorials; and integer Thue--Morse power sums.  The freedom in $\theta$ is not
an accident among those power sums: by \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} it says precisely
that the Gaussian stencil has already annihilated everything below degree $n$, so only
the top coefficient of the shifted test polynomial is ever seen.

The formula is an exact rational certificate, but it is ill conditioned in floating
point, and the loss occurs \emph{inside} the blocks rather than between them.  The outer
cancellation over $k$ is mild: at $\theta=1$ the largest block of
\eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} exceeds the value of the whole sum by the factors
\[
 \begin{gathered}
  3.00,\;4.20,\;4.69,\;4.42,\;4.09,\\
  3.84,\;3.68,\;3.59,\;3.53,\;3.50
 \end{gathered}
\]
for
$n=1,\ldots,10$, peaking at $n=3$ and decreasing thereafter, and other choices of
$\theta$ alter these ratios only by a bounded factor.  The digits are lost within a
single block, in the alternating Thue--Morse power sum: for $k=n$ the largest summand
exceeds $\sum_{r<2^{k}}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}(r+\theta)^{\,n+k}$ by a factor $4.8$ at $n=4$,
$5.2\cdot10^{3}$ at $n=8$, $3.9\cdot10^{6}$ at $n=10$ and $2.9\cdot10^{10}$ at $n=12$,
and this ratio grows without bound.  For numerical work the triangular recursion
\eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} is preferable: it delivers $a_n$, hence
$F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}a_n$, with one rational division per index and no cancellation
at all.
% ed.: one incoming draft attributed the instability to the outer sum over $k$.  Measured
% ed.: in exact arithmetic the outer cancellation is mild and bounded, while the inner
% ed.: Thue--Morse sums lose arbitrarily many digits; corrected here, with the figures.
% ed.: two sibling drafts carried this remark under the same label, one with the
% ed.: stencil/Vandermonde/Jacobian reading and one with the $2$-adic reading and the
% ed.: conditioning analysis; they are merged above.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M14 ----
\begin{proposition}[The block is a Taylor polynomial]\label{prop:dyadic-taylor-reduction}
\index{Taylor polynomial!dyadic Gaussian block}
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-normalized-block}
 \mathcal M_n(\theta):=\frac{\mathcal N_n(\theta)}{2^{\binom n2}(1/2;1/2)_n}
 =2^n\alpha_n=2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}F(2^{-n}),
\end{equation}
and, for $m\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-taylor-assembly}
 \mathcal T_m(\theta,y)
 :=2^{-\binom{m+1}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{m}
   \frac{(2^{m+1}y)^{m-n}}{(m-n)!}\,\mathcal M_n(\theta).
\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal T_m$ does not depend on $\theta$, contains no $q$-object, and is exactly
the $m$th Taylor polynomial of $F$ at the point $2^{-m}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dyadic-taylor-identification}
 \mathcal T_m(\theta,y)
 =\sum_{r=0}^{m}2^{\binom{r+1}{2}-\binom{m-r}{2}}\alpha_{m-r}\frac{y^r}{r!}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{m}F^{(r)}(2^{-m})\frac{y^r}{r!}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Independence of $\theta$ and the first equality in \eqref{eq:dyadic-normalized-block}
are \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} combined with
$\mathcal N_n(0)=\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$ and
$\mathfrak m_n=2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}(1/2;1/2)_n$; the second equality is
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}.  Substituting
$\mathcal M_n=2^n\alpha_n$ into \eqref{eq:dyadic-taylor-assembly} and writing $r=m-n$
gives the exponent
\[
 -\binom{m+1}{2}+(m+1)r+(m-r)=mr-\binom m2
 =\binom{r+1}{2}-\binom{m-r}{2},
\]
which is the first equality in \eqref{eq:dyadic-taylor-identification}.  For the second,
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} gives
$2^{-\binom{m-r}{2}}\alpha_{m-r}=F(2^{r-m})$, while
\eqref{eq:fabius-derivative-iterate} at $x=2^{-m}$, legitimate because $r\le m$, gives
$F^{(r)}(2^{-m})=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}F(2^{r-m})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\Cref{prop:dyadic-taylor-reduction} explains \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} concretely: a
common translation perturbs each block by terms of lower degree, and those are exactly
the terms annihilated by the order-$n$ geometric divided difference of
\Cref{thm:geometric-prouhet}.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M15 ----
\section{Composition and determinant forms}
\index{composition (of an integer)!Fabius values}

Expanding the product \eqref{eq:mgf-product} instead of inverting it produces two
further closed forms.  The combinatorial input is an ordered geometric sum.

\begin{lemma}[Geometric simplex sum]\label{lem:geometric-simplex}
Let $0<\AbsoluteValue q<1$, let $r_1,\ldots,r_\ell$ be positive integers, and put
$t_h:=r_h+r_{h+1}+\cdots+r_\ell$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-simplex}
 \sum_{1\le j_1<j_2<\cdots<j_\ell}
 q^{\,j_1r_1+\cdots+j_\ell r_\ell}
 =\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\frac{q^{t_h}}{1-q^{t_h}} .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Every strictly increasing $\ell$-tuple of positive integers has a unique gap
representation $j_i=i+g_1+\cdots+g_i$ with $g_h\ge0$.  Then
\[
 \sum_{i=1}^{\ell}j_ir_i
 =\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}ir_i+\sum_{h=1}^{\ell}g_ht_h,
\]
because $g_h$ occurs in $j_i$ exactly for $i\ge h$.  Summing the independent geometric
series in $g_1,\ldots,g_\ell$ gives
\[
 q^{\sum_iir_i}\prod_{h=1}^{\ell}\frac1{1-q^{t_h}},
\]
and $\sum_iir_i=\sum_ht_h$, which converts the prefactor into $\prod_hq^{t_h}$.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M16 ----
An ordered composition $\rho=(r_1,\ldots,r_\ell)\vDash n$ is a sequence of positive
integers with $r_1+\cdots+r_\ell=n$; write $\ell(\rho)=\ell$ and
$s_j:=r_1+\cdots+r_j$.

\begin{theorem}[Composition representation]\label{thm:fabius-composition}
For $0<q<1$ and $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:q-composition-moments}
 \alpha_n^{(q)}
 =\left(\frac{1-q}{q}\right)^{\!n}
  \sum_{\rho\vDash n}\ \prod_{j=1}^{\ell(\rho)}
  \frac{q^{s_j}}{(1-q^{s_j})\,(r_j+1)!}.
\end{equation}
In particular, at $q=1/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-composition}
 F(2^{-n})
 =2^{-\binom n2}\sum_{\rho\vDash n}\
 \prod_{j=1}^{\ell(\rho)}\frac1{(2^{s_j}-1)\,(r_j+1)!}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Expand each factor of \eqref{eq:mgf-product} as
$\Lambda((1-q)q^jz)=\sum_{r\ge0}(1-q)^rq^{jr}z^r/(r+1)!$ and collect the coefficient of
$z^n$.  A nonzero contribution chooses an index set $0\le j_1<\cdots<j_\ell$ of factors
and positive exponents $r_1,\ldots,r_\ell$ with $\sum r_i=n$, so that
\[
 \alpha_n^{(q)}
 =(1-q)^n\sum_{\ell\ge1}
  \sum_{\substack{r_1+\cdots+r_\ell=n\\ r_i\ge1}}
  \frac1{\prod_i(r_i+1)!}
  \sum_{0\le j_1<\cdots<j_\ell}q^{\sum_ij_ir_i}.
\]
Shifting $j_i\mapsto j_i+1$ multiplies the inner sum by $q^{-n}$ and puts it in the form
\eqref{eq:geometric-simplex}, so the inner sum equals
$q^{-n}\prod_hq^{t_h}/(1-q^{t_h})$.  Reversing the composition
$(r_1,\ldots,r_\ell)\mapsto(r_\ell,\ldots,r_1)$ is a bijection of the compositions of
$n$ that turns the tail sums $t_h$ into the prefix sums $s_j$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:q-composition-moments}.  At $q=1/2$ the prefactor is $1$ and
$q^{s_j}/(1-q^{s_j})=1/(2^{s_j}-1)$; multiply by $2^{-\binom n2}$ and apply
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M17 ----
\begin{theorem}[Lower-Hessenberg determinant]\label{thm:fabius-hessenberg}
\index{determinant!Hessenberg, Fabius values}
Put $\beta_{n,k}:=\dfrac1{(2^n-1)(n-k+1)!}$ for $0\le k\le n-1$, and let
