$\mathcal H_n$ be the $n\times n$ lower-Hessenberg matrix whose $(i,k)$ entry is
$\beta_{i,k}$ for $0\le k\le i-1$, is $-1$ for $k=i$, and is $0$ for $k>i$
(rows indexed by $1\le i\le n$, columns by $0\le k\le n-1$):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-hessenberg-matrix}
 \mathcal H_n=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \beta_{1,0}&-1&0&\cdots&0\\
 \beta_{2,0}&\beta_{2,1}&-1&\ddots&\vdots\\
 \beta_{3,0}&\beta_{3,1}&\beta_{3,2}&\ddots&0\\
 \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&-1\\
 \beta_{n,0}&\beta_{n,1}&\beta_{n,2}&\cdots&\beta_{n,n-1}
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Then, for $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-hessenberg}
 \alpha_n=\det\mathcal H_n,
 \qquad
 F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}\det\mathcal H_n .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $D_0:=1$ and $D_n:=\det\mathcal H_n$.  Note that $\mathcal H_k$ is the leading
$k\times k$ principal submatrix of $\mathcal H_n$ for $k\le n$.  Expand $D_n$ along the
last row, whose entries are $\beta_{n,k}$ for $0\le k\le n-1$, and let $M_{n,k}$ be the
minor obtained by deleting row $n$ and column $k$.  In $M_{n,k}$ the rows $1,\ldots,k$
have zero entries in every column of index greater than $k$, because the superdiagonal
entry $-1$ of row $i\le k$ sits in column $i\le k$.  Hence $M_{n,k}$ is block lower
triangular with diagonal blocks
\[
 \bigl(\text{rows }1..k,\ \text{columns }0..k-1\bigr)=\mathcal H_k,
 \qquad
 \bigl(\text{rows }k{+}1..n{-}1,\ \text{columns }k{+}1..n{-}1\bigr),
\]
and the second block is lower triangular with every diagonal entry equal to $-1$.
Therefore $\det M_{n,k}=(-1)^{n-1-k}D_k$.  The cofactor sign of the entry in row $n$ and
column $k$, that is, in matrix position $(n,k+1)$, is $(-1)^{n+k+1}$, so
\[
 D_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\beta_{n,k}(-1)^{n+k+1}(-1)^{n-1-k}D_k
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\beta_{n,k}D_k .
\]
This is \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} written as
$\alpha_n=\sum_{k<n}\beta_{n,k}\alpha_k$, with the same initial value.  Induction gives
$D_n=\alpha_n$, and \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} gives the second formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Expanding $\det\mathcal H_n$ over its nonzero Hessenberg paths reproduces
\eqref{eq:fabius-composition}: a path that descends from row $n$ to row $0$ using the
steps $r_1,\ldots,r_\ell$ contributes exactly the corresponding composition term.  The
determinant and the composition sum are two descriptions of the same triangular
resolvent, and the recursion \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} is its unrolled form.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M18 ----
\section{A Fourier formula for the Fabius function}

\begin{corollary}[Sinc-product representation of $F$]\label{cor:fabius-fourier-formula}
\index{sinc product!Fabius distribution function}
For $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-fourier-formula}
 F(x)=\frac12
 +\frac1\pi\int_0^{\infty}
 \frac{\sin\bigl((2x-1)t\bigr)}{t}
 \prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\SincRad\!\left(\frac t{2^k}\right)\Differential t,
\end{equation}
the integral being absolutely convergent.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $Y=2X-1$, whose density is $\RvachevUp$.  By
\Cref{thm:up-sinc-product}, its Fourier transform is
\[
 \FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-t)=\prod_{k\ge1}\SincRad(t/2^k),
 \qquad
 \FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-t)
 =\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit t}\varphi_{1/2}(2t).
\]
Consequently,
\Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-fourier-decay} shows that
$\FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-\,\cdot\,)$ is integrable, and indeed decays faster than every
negative power of $t$.  Fourier inversion and evenness of $\RvachevUp$ give
\[
\RvachevUp(y)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\RealNumbers}\EulerE^{-\ImaginaryUnit ty}\FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-t)\,\Differential t
=\frac1\pi\int_0^{\infty}\FourierAngular{\RvachevUp}(-t)\cos(ty)\,\Differential t .
\]
Because $\RvachevUp$ is a probability density and is symmetric,
$F(x)=\Probability(Y\le2x-1)=\tfrac12+\int_0^{2x-1}\RvachevUp(y)\,\Differential y$ for
$0\le x\le1$.  Integrating the last display in $y$ over $[0,2x-1]$ and interchanging the
order of integration, which is permitted by absolute convergence, replaces
$\cos(ty)$ by $\sin((2x-1)t)/t$ and proves \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The two exact descriptions of $F$ are complementary.  Formula
\eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} is global, analytic, and well suited to quadrature and
to smoothness statements; formula \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula} is finite,
rational, and adapted to dyadic arguments.  Their agreement encodes a highly nonlocal
cancellation in the sinc integral.  At $x=1/4$, for instance, numerical evaluation of
\eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} reproduces $5/72$ to more than twenty digits.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block A06 ----
\section{Digital cancellation in an arbitrary radix}
\index{Prouhet problem!radix $b$}
\index{Thue--Morse sequence!radix generalization}
\index{root of unity!as a digit weight}

The signs $\tau_r=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)}$ are the case $b=2$, $\omega=-1$ of a roots-of-unity construction available in every radix.  Separating the radix from the special real signs that exist only at $b=2$ shows which half of \Cref{thm:thue-morse-product,thm:thue-morse-moments} comes from binary digits and which half comes from having a digit expansion at all.

For $b\ge2$ let $s_b(r)$ denote the sum of the base-$b$ digits of $r$, and for $\omega\in\ComplexNumbers$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radix-b-signs}
 \tau^{(b)}_r\DefinitionEquals\omega^{\,s_b(r)}
 \qquad(r\ge0),
\end{equation}
so that $\tau^{(2)}_r=\tau_r$ when $\omega=-1$.

\begin{theorem}[Radix-$b$ Prouhet filter]\label{thm:radix-b-prouhet}
Let $b\ge2$ and let $\omega\in\ComplexNumbers$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radix-b-omega}
 \omega^b=1,\qquad\omega\ne1 .
\end{equation}
Then for every $k\ge0$ the polynomial identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radix-b-product}
 \sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r z^r
 =\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}
   \left(\sum_{a=0}^{b-1}\omega^az^{\,ab^j}\right)
\end{equation}
holds, and for every $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r(r+\theta)^m&=0
 \qquad(0\le m<k),\label{eq:radix-b-lower}\\
 \sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r(r+\theta)^k
 &=\frac{k!\;b^{\,k(k+1)/2}}{(\omega-1)^k}.\label{eq:radix-b-top}
\end{align}
Consequently, for every polynomial $p$ of degree at most $k$ and all $\alpha,\beta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radix-b-functional}
 \sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r\,p(\alpha+\beta r)
 =\frac{k!\;b^{\,k(k+1)/2}}{(\omega-1)^k}\;\beta^k\,[X^k]p(X).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
% ed.: the incoming draft required $\omega$ to be a \emph{primitive} $b$th root
% ed.: of unity.  Only \eqref{eq:radix-b-omega} is used, and the weaker
% ed.: hypothesis is genuinely weaker: for composite $b$ it admits several
% ed.: inequivalent sign systems, e.g. $b=4$, $\omega=-1$.

\begin{proof}
Every $r$ with $0\le r<b^k$ has a unique expansion $r=a_0+a_1b+\cdots+a_{k-1}b^{k-1}$ with $0\le a_j<b$, and then $s_b(r)=a_0+\cdots+a_{k-1}$.  Hence
\[
 \tau^{(b)}_rz^r=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\omega^{a_j}z^{\,a_jb^j},
\]
and summing independently over the $k$ digits gives \eqref{eq:radix-b-product}.

Substitute $z\mapsto\EulerE^{z}$ in \eqref{eq:radix-b-product} and multiply by $\EulerE^{\theta z}$.  The left side becomes the exponential generating function
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r\EulerE^{(r+\theta)z}
 =\sum_{m\ge0}
   \left(\sum_{r=0}^{b^k-1}\tau^{(b)}_r(r+\theta)^m\right)\frac{z^m}{m!},
\]
and the right side becomes $\EulerE^{\theta z}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}G_j(z)$, where
$G_j(z)=\sum_{a=0}^{b-1}\omega^a\EulerE^{\,ab^jz}$.

Because $\omega^b=1$ and $\omega\ne1$, the finite geometric sum gives
\[
 G_j(0)=\sum_{a=0}^{b-1}\omega^a=\frac{\omega^b-1}{\omega-1}=0 .
\]
Differentiating the identity $\sum_{a=0}^{b-1}Z^a=(Z^b-1)/(Z-1)$ and multiplying by $Z$ gives
\[
 \sum_{a=0}^{b-1}aZ^a
 =\frac{bZ^b(Z-1)-Z(Z^b-1)}{(Z-1)^2},
\]
so at $Z=\omega$, where $\omega^b=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-first-moment}
 \sum_{a=0}^{b-1}a\,\omega^a=\frac{b}{\omega-1}\ne0 .
\end{equation}
Hence $G_j'(0)=b^j\sum_{a<b}a\omega^a=b^{\,j+1}/(\omega-1)$, so each $G_j$ has a zero of order exactly one at the origin and $\prod_{j<k}G_j$ has a zero of order exactly $k$, with
\[
 [z^k]\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}G_j(z)
 =\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{b^{\,j+1}}{\omega-1}
 =\frac{b^{\,k(k+1)/2}}{(\omega-1)^k}.
\]
Multiplying by $\EulerE^{\theta z}=1+\BigO(z)$ changes neither the order of the zero nor the coefficient of $z^k$.  Comparing the coefficients of $z^m/m!$ for $0\le m\le k$ gives \eqref{eq:radix-b-lower} and \eqref{eq:radix-b-top}.

For \eqref{eq:radix-b-functional}, expand $q(X)\DefinitionEquals p(\alpha+\beta X)=\sum_{m=0}^{k}\gamma_mX^m$, a polynomial of degree at most $k$ with $\gamma_k=\beta^k[X^k]p$.  Applying \eqref{eq:radix-b-lower} with $\theta=0$ to each $m<k$ and \eqref{eq:radix-b-top} to $m=k$ leaves only the term $\gamma_k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The binary case]\label{cor:radix-two-recovers-thue-morse}
For $b=2$ and $\omega=-1$, identity \eqref{eq:radix-b-product} is \eqref{eq:thue-morse-product}, identity \eqref{eq:radix-b-functional} is \eqref{eq:thue-morse-polynomial-functional}, and the top value \eqref{eq:radix-b-top} is
\[
 \frac{k!\,2^{\,k(k+1)/2}}{(-2)^k}
 =(-1)^kk!\,2^{\binom k2},
\]
in agreement with \eqref{eq:thue-morse-moments}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $b=2$ and $\omega=-1$, and use $k(k+1)/2-k=\binom k2$.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M22 ----
\begin{remark}[Rigidity of the binary radix]\label{rem:binary-radix-rigidity}%
\label{rem:radix-prime-power}
\index{Prouhet problem!radix $b$}
\index{radix!what is special about $b=2$}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!at a prime-power base}
It is worth separating what in \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} needs the radix $2$ from what does not.  Less needs it than one might expect.

\emph{The $q$-calculus is radix-free.}  \Cref{chap:geometric-filters} is written at general $q$: for real $b>1$ the annihilator $(z;1/b)_n=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1-zb^{-j}\bigr)$ has the roots $1,b,\ldots,b^{\,n-1}$ and supplies divided-difference weights on the nodes $-1,-b,\ldots,-b^{\,n}$.  This is \Cref{cor:reciprocal-leading-coefficient} at $q=1/b$, whose top moment \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-top} is
\[
 \mathfrak m^{(b)}_n\DefinitionEquals\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(b^{\,j}-1\bigr),
\]
the Mersenne product $\mathfrak m_n$ at $b=2$.  When $b$ is a prime power the same $b$ carries in addition a finite-field meaning through $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{b}$ (\Cref{thm:subspace-count}), so that at such a radix three constructions share one parameter: digital cancellation \eqref{eq:radix-b-functional}, geometric interpolation on the nodes $-b^{\,k}$, and subspace enumeration.  For $b=2$ this is the coincidence exploited in \Cref{prin:two-prouhet-filters}.

\emph{The digital signs are radix-free.}  \Cref{thm:radix-b-prouhet} produces, for every $b\ge2$ and every $\omega$ with $\omega^b=1$, $\omega\ne1$, a sign system $\tau^{(b)}_r=\omega^{\,s_b(r)}$ whose block exponential sum has a zero of order exactly $k$ at the origin --- the property of \eqref{eq:thue-morse-moments} that removes the apparent pole in \eqref{eq:Psi-TM-expansion}.  Since \eqref{eq:radix-b-omega} does not force $\omega$ to be primitive, a composite $b$ carries several inequivalent such systems; $b=4$, $\omega=-1$ gives $\tau^{(4)}_r=(-1)^{s_4(r)}$ with constant $k!\,4^{\,k(k+1)/2}/(-2)^k$.

\emph{The coefficient formula is radix-free as well, in a shape that must be stated carefully.}  Put
\[
 Y_b\DefinitionEquals\sum_{j\ge1}b^{-j}V_j=\frac{X_{1/b}}{b-1},
 \qquad
 a^{(b)}_n\DefinitionEquals\frac{\Expectation\bigl(Y_b^{\,n}\bigr)}{n!},
\]
with $V_1,V_2,\ldots$ independent and uniform on $[0,1]$, so that $Y_2=X$ and $a^{(2)}_n=a_n$.  Everything in \Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation,lem:thue-morse-dilation-bridge} runs verbatim with $2$ replaced by $b$: the moment generating function satisfies $A_b(bz)=\Phi(z)A_b(z)$, and $\Psi^{(b)}_k(z)=\EulerE^{-z}\prod_{j<k}\Phi\bigl(-b^{\,j}z\bigr)$ satisfies the bridge relation \eqref{eq:general-bridge} at $q=1/b$, since then $q^{-k}=b^{\,k}$.  Expanding $\prod_{j<k}\bigl(1-\EulerE^{-b^{j}z}\bigr)$ over subsets and applying \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} together with \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} gives, for every $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula}
 a^{(b)}_n
 =\frac{(-1)^n}{\mathfrak m^{(b)}_n}
  \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/b}}{b^{\,2\binom k2}\,(n+k)!}
  \sum_{r\in\mathcal B^{(b)}_k}(-1)^{\sigma(r)}(r+\theta)^{\,n+k},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal B^{(b)}_k$ is the set of $r=\sum_{j<k}\varepsilon_jb^{\,j}$ with every $\varepsilon_j\in\SetOf{0,1}$, and $\sigma(r)\DefinitionEquals\varepsilon_0+\cdots+\varepsilon_{k-1}$.  The inner sums of \eqref{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula} are \emph{not} the radix-$b$ Prouhet sums of \Cref{thm:radix-b-prouhet}: the refinement equation delivers the factor $1-y^{\,b^{j}}$, whose digit alphabet is $\SetOf{0,1}$, whereas $\tau^{(b)}$ arises from $\sum_{a<b}\omega^ay^{\,ab^{j}}$, whose digit alphabet is $\SetOf{0,\ldots,b-1}$.  The two coincide exactly when $\SetOf{0,1}=\SetOf{0,\ldots,b-1}$, that is when $b=2$; there $\mathcal B^{(2)}_k=\SetOf{0,1,\ldots,2^k-1}$, $(-1)^{\sigma(r)}=\tau_r$, $b^{2\binom k2}=4^{\binom k2}$, and \eqref{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula} is \eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}.

\emph{What is genuinely rigid is the passage from coefficients to values.}  The simplex cut in the proof of \Cref{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic} uses only $b^{\,n-j}\ge1$ and a threshold at most $1$, so it survives at every radix:
\[
 \Probability\bigl(Y_b\le b^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac{\Expectation\bigl[(1-Y_b)^n\bigr]}{n!\;b^{\binom n2}} .
\]
Turning the right-hand side into the single moment $\Expectation\bigl(Y_b^{\,n}\bigr)$ uses the reflection $1-Y_b\overset d=Y_b$.  But $Y_b$ has support $[0,1/(b-1)]$ while $1-Y_b$ has support $[1-1/(b-1),1]$, and these agree only when $1/(b-1)=1$.  Only at $b=2$, where the geometric mass $\sum_{j\ge1}b^{-j}$ is exactly $1$ and $Y_2=X_{1/2}$ is symmetric about $1/2$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry}, does \eqref{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula} evaluate a distribution function.  For $b\ge3$ one still gets every value, since
\[
 F_{1/b}\bigl((b-1)b^{-n}\bigr)
 =\Probability\bigl(Y_b\le b^{-n}\bigr)
 =\frac1{b^{\binom n2}}\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^m\binom nm\frac{m!}{n!}\,a^{(b)}_m\cdot n!\Big/n!,
\]
a binomial combination of $a^{(b)}_0,\ldots,a^{(b)}_n$ rather than the single coefficient $a^{(b)}_n$.  The $q$-binomial and the digital ingredients are therefore automatic at every radix; what singles out $b=2$ is the one normalization in which the dilation factor of \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-fixed-point}, the digit alphabet of the refinement factor, and the right endpoint of the support all agree.
% ed.: this remark absorbs the incoming \emph{Rigidity of the binary radix} and
% ed.: the prime-power remark that closed the radix-$b$ section.  The incoming
% ed.: version named 'a digital sign whose block exponential sum has a zero of
% ed.: controlled order' as the obstruction; \Cref{thm:radix-b-prouhet} supplies
% ed.: one at every radix, and \eqref{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula} shows the
% ed.: coefficient formula itself generalizes, so the obstruction had to be
% ed.: relocated to the endpoint bridge.  Formula
% ed.: \eqref{eq:radix-b-coefficient-formula} was verified as an exact rational
% ed.: identity for $b=2,3,4,5$, all $n\le6$ and $\theta=0,1,-3/7$.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M19 ----
\section{The geometric row as a summability kernel}
\index{Toeplitz theorem!bounded-variation rows}

The Lagrange row $\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}$ of \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-weight} was used
in \Cref{chap:geometric-filters} for exactness.  It also has an exact and uniformly
bounded $\ell^1$ norm, which turns it into a regular summability kernel.

% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, section ``The geometric row as a
% ed.: summability kernel''.  This block REPLACES \Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation}
% ed.: (label and all three equation labels retained, so
% ed.: \Cref{cor:gaussian-row-spline-average,rem:halfbase-row-reversal,prin:row-exactness-stability}
% ed.: need no change); the corollary and the remark after it are new.  An incoming
% ed.: draft carried the same $\ell^1$ identity a third time -- it is the identity
% ed.: already proved here -- together with the reciprocal-Pochhammer form, strict
% ed.: monotonicity, the sharper half-base bound $<9$ (against $3\e^{4/3}=11.38\ldots$),
% ed.: a conditioning corollary and a comparison with harmonic nodes; only those five
% ed.: are added.  The limit is written $\mathcal L(q)$ because $\Lambda$ is the
% ed.: uniform kernel \eqref{eq:uniform-mgf-kernel} in this chapter.  The half-base
% ed.: display is now restricted to $n\ge1$: at $n=0$ its left side is $1$, not $3$.

\begin{theorem}[Row mass, total variation, and the geometric Lebesgue constant]\label{thm:geometric-row-variation}
\index{Toeplitz theorem!bounded-variation rows}
\index{Lebesgue constant!geometric nodes}
\index{extrapolation!conditioning}
Let $0<q<1$.  The weights \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-weight} alternate in sign,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-lagrange-signs}
 \Sign\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}=(-1)^{n-k}
 \qquad(0\le k\le n),
\end{equation}
and for every $n\ge0$
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}&=1,
 \label{eq:geometric-row-mass}\\
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl|\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}\bigr|
 &=\frac{\QPochhammer{-q}{q}{n}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}
  =\frac{\QPochhammer{q^2}{q^2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}^{2}} .
 \label{eq:geometric-row-variation}
\end{align}
The right-hand side of \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation} increases strictly with $n$ to
the finite limit
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-lebesgue-constant}
 \mathcal L(q)\DefinitionEquals\frac{\QPochhammer{-q}{q}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{\infty}}
              =\frac{\QPochhammer{q^2}{q^2}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{\infty}^{2}},
\end{equation}
so that $\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl|\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}\bigr|<\mathcal L(q)<\infty$ for every
$n$.  At the half base,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:halfbase-row-variation-bound}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl|\lambda_{n,k}^{(1/2)}\bigr|
 =3\prod_{r=2}^{n}\frac{1+2^{-r}}{1-2^{-r}}
 <\mathcal L(1/2)=8.2559879357782500655\ldots<9
 \qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:reversed-qbinomial} and \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-weight},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-row-polynomial}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}z^k
 =\frac1{(q;q)_n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(z-q^j).
\end{equation}
Evaluating at $z=1$ gives $(q;q)_n/(q;q)_n=1$, which is
\eqref{eq:geometric-row-mass}.  For $0<q<1$ the three factors $\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}$,
$q^{\binom{n-k+1}{2}}$ and $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ occurring in
\eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-weight} are positive, which is
\eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-signs}; hence
$\bigl|\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}\bigr|=(-1)^{n}(-1)^{k}\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}$ and, by
\eqref{eq:geometric-row-polynomial} at $z=-1$,
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigl|\lambda_{n,k}^{(q)}\bigr|
 =\frac{(-1)^n}{(q;q)_n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(-1-q^j\bigr)
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{-q}{q}{n}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}} .
\]
The second form in \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation} follows from
\[
 \QPochhammer{-q}{q}{n}\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(1+q^j\bigr)\bigl(1-q^j\bigr)
 =\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bigl(1-q^{2j}\bigr)=\QPochhammer{q^2}{q^2}{n}.
\]
Passing from $n$ to $n+1$ multiplies the quotient by $(1+q^{n+1})/(1-q^{n+1})>1$, so it
increases strictly; it converges because
\[
 \sum_{j\ge1}\log\frac{1+q^j}{1-q^j}
 =\sum_{j\ge1}\log\Bigl(1+\frac{2q^j}{1-q^j}\Bigr)
 \le\frac2{1-q}\sum_{j\ge1}q^j<\infty,
\]
both products in \eqref{eq:geometric-lebesgue-constant} being convergent for $0<q<1$.

At $q=1/2$ the factor $r=1$ contributes $(1+1/2)/(1-1/2)=3$, which is the displayed
prefactor.  For the bound, split the infinite product at $r=4$: the factors $r=1,2,3$ give
$3\cdot\frac53\cdot\frac97=\frac{45}7$, while for $r\ge4$ one has $x=2^{-r}\le1/16$ and
\[
 \frac{1+x}{1-x}=1+\frac{2x}{1-x}\le\exp\Bigl(\frac{2x}{1-x}\Bigr)
 \le\exp\Bigl(\frac{32x}{15}\Bigr),
\]
so, since $\sum_{r\ge4}2^{-r}=1/8$, the tail is at most $\EulerE^{4/15}<1.3063$ and
$\mathcal L(1/2)\le\frac{45}7\EulerE^{4/15}<8.4<9$.  The decimal expansion in
\eqref{eq:halfbase-row-variation-bound} is obtained by evaluating the product
numerically.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Conditioning of geometric extrapolation]\label{cor:geometric-extrapolation-conditioning}
Let $0<q<1$, let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $n$, and let $y_0,\ldots,y_n$ be
data with $\AbsoluteValue{y_k-p(q^k)}\le\varepsilon$ for every $k$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:geometric-extrapolation-conditioning}
 \Bigl|\sum_{k=0}^{n}\lambda^{(q)}_{n,k}y_k-p(0)\Bigr|\le\mathcal L(q)\,\varepsilon .
\end{equation}
The amplification of data error is therefore bounded independently of the number of
nodes.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:geometric-evaluation-zero}, $\sum_k\lambda^{(q)}_{n,k}p(q^k)=p(0)$;
subtract, take absolute values, and apply \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation} together
with \eqref{eq:geometric-lebesgue-constant}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Geometric versus harmonic node sequences]\label{rem:geometric-vs-harmonic-nodes}
The uniform bound is special to geometric nodes.  For the harmonic sequence
$v_k=1/(k+1)$, $0\le k\le n$, the corresponding evaluation weights
$\ell_k(0)=\prod_{j\ne k}v_j/(v_j-v_k)$ have absolute sums
\[
 9,\quad 91.67,\quad 1.007\cdot10^{3},\quad 1.151\cdot10^{4},
 \quad 1.600\cdot10^{6},\quad 3.495\cdot10^{10}
\]
at $n=2,4,6,8,12,20$ (computed in exact rational arithmetic), growing geometrically in
$n$, whereas at $q=1/2$ the same quantity never reaches $9$.  Combined with
\Cref{thm:geometric-filter-diagonal}, this says that the filters $\mathcal G_{n,q}$ are
exact and simultaneously uniformly bounded on the sampled values: raising the order of a
geometric filter costs nothing in stability.  This is the analytic half of
\Cref{prin:row-exactness-stability}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The half-base row in reversed coordinates]\label{rem:halfbase-row-reversal}
Put
\[
 W_{n,j}:=\frac{(-1)^j2^{-\binom{j+1}{2}}}{(1/2;1/2)_n}\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{1/2},
 \qquad 0\le j\le n.
\]
The symmetry $\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{1/2}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{n-j}{1/2}$ gives
$W_{n,j}=\lambda_{n,n-j}^{(1/2)}$, and \eqref{eq:geometric-row-polynomial} becomes
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}W_{n,j}z^j=\frac{(z/2;1/2)_n}{(1/2;1/2)_n}.
\]
The two rows are reversals of one another, so \Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation} applies
verbatim to either.
\end{remark}

% ---- merged block M20 ----
\begin{theorem}[Bounded-variation tail rows]\label{thm:tail-row-toeplitz}
Let $(x_m)_{m\ge0}$ converge to $L$ in a normed vector space.  For each $n$ let
$u_{n,0},\ldots,u_{n,n}$ be scalars and $r(n,0),\ldots,r(n,n)$ nonnegative integers with
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{n,j}=1,
 \qquad
 \sup_n\sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{u_{n,j}}\le M<\infty,
 \qquad
 \min_{0\le j\le n}r(n,j)\longrightarrow\infty .
\]
Then $\sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{n,j}x_{r(n,j)}\to L$ as $n\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Unit row mass gives
\[
 \Bigl\|\sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{n,j}x_{r(n,j)}-L\Bigr\|
 =\Bigl\|\sum_{j=0}^{n}u_{n,j}\bigl(x_{r(n,j)}-L\bigr)\Bigr\|
 \le\sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{u_{n,j}}\,\bigl\|x_{r(n,j)}-L\bigr\| .
\]
Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose $N$ with $\Norm{x_m-L}<\varepsilon/M$ for all $m\ge N$.
For all $n$ large enough, $r(n,j)\ge N$ for every $j$, so the right side is at most
$M\cdot\varepsilon/M=\varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Gaussian averages of the spline approximants]\label{cor:gaussian-row-spline-average}
Let $0<q<1$ and let $F_{q,N}$ be the distribution function of $X_{q,N}$.  For any
integers $r(n,j)\ge n$,
\[
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}\left|\sum_{j=0}^{n}\lambda_{n,j}^{(q)}F_{q,r(n,j)}(x)-F_q(x)\right|
 \longrightarrow0
 \qquad(n\to\infty).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \Cref{thm:tail-row-toeplitz} in the Banach space of bounded functions on $\RealNumbers$ with
the supremum norm, to the sequence $x_m:=F_{q,m}$, which converges to $F_q$ by
\eqref{eq:cdf-uniform-rate}.  The hypotheses hold by
\Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation}, with $M=(-q;q)_\infty/(q;q)_\infty$, and
$\min_jr(n,j)\ge n\to\infty$.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M21 ----
\begin{principle}[One polynomial, two roles]\label{prin:row-exactness-stability}
The single finite product $\prod_{j=1}^{n}(z-q^j)$, normalized by $(q;q)_n$, controls
both exactness and stability of the geometric row:
\begin{enumerate}
\item its \emph{roots} $q,q^2,\ldots,q^n$ make the row an order-$n$ geometric
finite-difference stencil, annihilating $q^{kd}$ for $1\le d\le n$
(\Cref{thm:geometric-evaluation-zero}) and extracting one leading coefficient
(\Cref{thm:geometric-prouhet});
\item its \emph{endpoint values} at $z=1$ and $z=-1$ give unit row mass and the exact
$\ell^1$ norm $(-q;q)_n/(q;q)_n$ (\Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation}), which are
precisely the two hypotheses of \Cref{thm:tail-row-toeplitz}.
\end{enumerate}
Roots control vanishing moments; endpoint values control norms.
\end{principle}

\begin{proof}
Combine \Cref{thm:geometric-evaluation-zero,thm:geometric-prouhet,thm:geometric-row-variation,thm:tail-row-toeplitz}.
\end{proof}

% ---- merged block M23 ----
\begin{remark}[Where the analysis actually enters]\label{rem:dyadic-algebraic-layer}
\index{formalization!dyadic Fabius layer}
The dyadic results of this chapter split along the boundary recommended in
\Cref{prin:proof-architecture}, and the split is sharper than it may look.  Each of
\Cref{thm:geometric-prouhet,thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer,cor:fabius-centered-formula,cor:transfer-translation,thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic,prop:dyadic-taylor-reduction}
is a finite identity between rational numbers, proved in polynomial rings and formal
power series over $\RationalNumbers$; none of them uses a convergence hypothesis, and each survives
mapping into an arbitrary commutative $\RationalNumbers$-algebra, exactly as in
\Cref{rem:denominator-free-reversed}.  Analysis enters at precisely two points: the
identification of $\mathcal A$ with $\Expectation\EulerE^{zX}$ in
\Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf}, and \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}, which converts the
formal coefficient $\alpha_n$ into the value $F(2^{-n})$.  Every closed formula above is
the algebraic core composed with that single analytic bridge.  The formalization status
of the individual statements is recorded in \Cref{app:lean-status}.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block B17 ----
\subsection{Negative parameters}

The series \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-series} and the convergence estimate in
the proof of \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-basic} use only $\AbsoluteValue q<1$, so $X_q$ is
a well-defined bounded random variable for every real $q\in(-1,1)$.  For negative
$q$ it is no longer supported in $[0,1]$, but it is an affine image of a
positive-parameter member of the same family.

\begin{proposition}[Negative parameters as affine positive laws]\label{prop:qF-negative-affine}
\index{geometric uniform sum!negative parameter}
Let $0<a<1$ and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-lambda-beta}
 \lambda_a\DefinitionEquals\frac{1+a}{1-a},
 \qquad
 \beta_a\DefinitionEquals\frac{a}{1-a},
 \qquad\text{so that }\lambda_a=1+2\beta_a.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-negative-affine}
 X_{-a}\overset d=\lambda_aX_a-\beta_a,
 \qquad
 M_{-a}(t)=\EulerE^{-\beta_at}M_a(\lambda_at).
\end{equation}
Consequently $\Expectation X_{-a}=\tfrac12$ and $X_{-a}\overset d=1-X_{-a}$, and the
topological support of $X_{-a}$ is the interval
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-negative-support}
 \Bigl[-\frac a{1-a},\ \frac1{1-a}\Bigr].
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $V_j\DefinitionEquals U_j-\tfrac12$.  The $V_j$ are independent and symmetric, so the
sequences $(V_j)_{j\ge0}$ and $\bigl((-1)^jV_j\bigr)_{j\ge0}$ have the same
joint law.  Since $(1-q)\sum_{j\ge0}q^j=1$ for every $\AbsoluteValue q<1$,
\[
 X_q-\frac12=(1-q)\sum_{j\ge0}q^jV_j .
\]
Taking $q=-a$ and using $(1+a)(-a)^j=(1+a)a^j\cdot(-1)^j$,
\begin{align*}
 X_{-a}-\frac12
 &=(1+a)\sum_{j\ge0}a^j\,(-1)^jV_j
 \overset d=(1+a)\sum_{j\ge0}a^jV_j\\
 &=\frac{1+a}{1-a}\Bigl[(1-a)\sum_{j\ge0}a^jV_j\Bigr]
 =\lambda_a\Bigl(X_a-\frac12\Bigr),
\end{align*}
all three series converging absolutely.  Hence
$X_{-a}\overset d=\lambda_aX_a-\tfrac{\lambda_a-1}2$, and
$\tfrac{\lambda_a-1}2=\beta_a$ by \eqref{eq:qF-lambda-beta}.  Taking moment
generating functions gives the second identity in
\eqref{eq:qF-negative-affine}.

For the remaining assertions, recall from \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-basic}
that $X_a$ has support $[0,1]$ and satisfies $X_a\overset d=1-X_a$.  An
increasing affine map carries the support onto its image, so the support of
$X_{-a}$ is $[-\beta_a,\lambda_a-\beta_a]$, and
$\lambda_a-\beta_a=\frac{(1+a)-a}{1-a}=\frac1{1-a}$, which is
\eqref{eq:qF-negative-support}.  Finally
$\Expectation X_{-a}=\tfrac{\lambda_a}2-\beta_a=\frac{(1+a)-2a}{2(1-a)}=\tfrac12$,
and, using $\lambda_a-1-\beta_a=\beta_a$,
\[
 1-X_{-a}\overset d=1-\lambda_aX_a+\beta_a
 =\lambda_a(1-X_a)-\beta_a
 \overset d=\lambda_aX_a-\beta_a
 \overset d=X_{-a}. \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

For instance $X_{-1/2}\overset d=3X_{1/2}-1$, so the law of $X_{-1/2}$ is the
Fabius law stretched by $3$ and supported on $[-1,2]$; likewise
$X_{-1/4}\overset d=\tfrac53X_{1/4}-\tfrac13$.


% ---- merged block B18 ----
\subsection{Reciprocal germs and reciprocity}

For $\AbsoluteValue q>1$ the series \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-series} diverges and there
is no random variable to expand.  The functional equation \eqref{eq:mgf-refinement}
survives, however, and pins down a unique normalized germ.

\begin{theorem}[The Mahler equation with a normalization is rigid]\label{thm:qF-germ-uniqueness}
Let $q\in\ComplexNumbers$ with $q\ne0$ and $\AbsoluteValue q\ne1$, and put
$\rho\DefinitionEquals2\pi\min(1,\AbsoluteValue q)/\AbsoluteValue{1-q}$.  Then there is exactly one function
$M$, analytic on the disc $\AbsoluteValue t<\rho$, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-mahler-abstract}
 M(0)=1,
 \qquad
 M(t)=\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)t\bigr)M(qt)
 \ \text{ for }\AbsoluteValue t<\rho,\ \AbsoluteValue{qt}<\rho,
\end{equation}
namely
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-germ-def}
 M(t)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{j}t\bigr),
  &\AbsoluteValue q<1,\\[12pt]
  \displaystyle\Bigl[\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{-j}t\bigr)\Bigr]^{-1},
  &\AbsoluteValue q>1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Both products converge locally uniformly, and in the second case no factor
vanishes on $\AbsoluteValue t<\rho$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Convergence of the first product is \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf}; the second
is identical after replacing $q$ by $q^{-1}$ in the estimate, since
$\sum_{j\ge1}\AbsoluteValue q^{-j}<\infty$.  When $\AbsoluteValue q>1$ one has
$\rho=2\pi/\AbsoluteValue{1-q}$, so for $\AbsoluteValue t<\rho$ and $j\ge1$
\[
 \AbsoluteValue{(1-q)q^{-j}t}
 \le\AbsoluteValue{1-q}\AbsoluteValue q^{-1}\AbsoluteValue t
 <\AbsoluteValue{1-q}\AbsoluteValue q^{-1}\rho
 =\frac{2\pi}{\AbsoluteValue q}<2\pi .
\]
Since every zero of $\mathcal E$ has modulus at least $2\pi$, no factor vanishes
on that disc and the reciprocal is analytic there.

That \eqref{eq:qF-germ-def} solves \eqref{eq:qF-mahler-abstract} is the
computation of \Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-mgf} in the first case.  In the second
case write $P(t)=\prod_{j\ge1}\mathcal E((1-q)q^{-j}t)$ and split off $j=1$ after
replacing $t$ by $qt$:
\[
 P(qt)=\prod_{j\ge1}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{1-j}t\bigr)
      =\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)t\bigr)P(t).
\]
Taking reciprocals gives $M(t)=\mathcal E((1-q)t)M(qt)$ with $M=P^{-1}$.

For uniqueness, let $M$ be any solution.  If $\AbsoluteValue q<1$, then $\AbsoluteValue{q^kt}<\rho$
for all $k\ge0$, so \eqref{eq:qF-mahler-abstract} may be iterated $N$ times:
\[
 M(t)=\Bigl[\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{j}t\bigr)\Bigr]M(q^Nt).
\]
Let $N\to\infty$: $q^Nt\to0$, so $M(q^Nt)\to M(0)=1$ by continuity, and the
bracket converges to the first product in \eqref{eq:qF-germ-def}.

If $\AbsoluteValue q>1$, apply \eqref{eq:qF-mahler-abstract} at the point $q^{-1}t$, which
again lies in the disc, to get
$M(q^{-1}t)=\mathcal E((1-q)q^{-1}t)M(t)$, that is,
\[
 M(t)=\frac{M(q^{-1}t)}{\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{-1}t\bigr)} .
\]
Iterating $N$ times,
\[
 M(t)=M(q^{-N}t)\Bigl[\prod_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{-j}t\bigr)\Bigr]^{-1},
\]
and letting $N\to\infty$ gives the second line of \eqref{eq:qF-germ-def}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The reciprocal germ]\label{def:qF-germ}
For $\AbsoluteValue q>1$, $M_q$ denotes the unique germ of
\Cref{thm:qF-germ-uniqueness}, that is, the reciprocal product in
\eqref{eq:qF-germ-def}; its Taylor coefficients are again written
$a_n(q)=d_n(q)/n!$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:qF-germ-not-mgf}
% ed.: the source draft called this germ "canonical".  The Mahler equation by
% ed.: itself determines a solution only up to a multiplicative $q$-periodic
% ed.: factor, so the normalization that actually pins it down --- analyticity
% ed.: at the origin together with $M_q(0)=1$ --- is now stated explicitly in
% ed.: \Cref{thm:qF-germ-uniqueness}.
The symbol $M_q$ is used in two senses.  For real $q$ with $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ it is a
genuine moment generating function and $d_n(q)=\Expectation X_q^n$.  For $\AbsoluteValue q>1$
there is no underlying random variable, and $d_n(q)$ are merely normalized
Taylor coefficients.  What is common to both regimes is \eqref{eq:mgf-refinement},
and therefore also \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence}: the derivation of the
recurrence used the Mahler equation and nothing else, so a single rational
function $a_n$ of $q$ governs both regimes.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Reciprocity]\label{thm:qF-reciprocity}
For every $q\in\ComplexNumbers$ with $q\ne0$ and $\AbsoluteValue q\ne1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-reciprocity}
 M_q(t)\,M_{1/q}(-t)=1
\end{equation}
as analytic germs at $t=0$.  Equivalently, at the level of coefficients,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-moment-convolution}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk(-1)^{n-k}\,d_k(q)\,d_{n-k}(1/q)=\delta_{n0}
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By symmetry it suffices to treat $\AbsoluteValue q<1$, so that $\AbsoluteValue{1/q}>1$ and
$M_{1/q}$ is the germ of \Cref{def:qF-germ}.  Writing $Q=1/q$ in
\eqref{eq:qF-germ-def} and evaluating at $-t$,
\[
 M_{1/q}(-t)^{-1}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal E\bigl((1-Q)Q^{-j}(-t)\bigr)
 =\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathcal E\bigl(-(1-q^{-1})q^{j}t\bigr).
\]
Now $-(1-q^{-1})q^{j}=(q^{-1}-1)q^{j}=q^{j-1}-q^{j}=(1-q)q^{j-1}$, so after the
substitution $i=j-1$ the right-hand side is
$\prod_{i\ge0}\mathcal E\bigl((1-q)q^{i}t\bigr)=M_q(t)$, which is
\eqref{eq:qF-reciprocity}.

For \eqref{eq:qF-moment-convolution}, multiply the two Taylor series
\[
 M_q(t)=\sum_{k\ge0}d_k(q)\frac{t^k}{k!},
 \qquad
 M_{1/q}(-t)=\sum_{m\ge0}(-1)^md_m(1/q)\frac{t^m}{m!},
\]
and read off the coefficient of $t^n/n!$ in the product; by
\eqref{eq:qF-reciprocity} it must equal $\delta_{n0}$.
\end{proof}


% ---- merged block B19 ----
\subsection{The spectral \texorpdfstring{$q^2$}{q2}-Pochhammer factorization}

Splitting $\mathcal E$ into its centered part is the step that converts the
geometric ratio $q$ of the random series into the \emph{nome} $q^2$ of a
$q$-Pochhammer product.  From \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-characteristic},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-Ecal-centered}
 \mathcal E(z)
 =\EulerE^{z/2}\,\frac{\EulerE^{z/2}-\EulerE^{-z/2}}{z}
 =\EulerE^{z/2}\,\frac{\sinh(z/2)}{z/2},
\end{equation}
and Euler's product for the sine, in its hyperbolic form, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-sinh-product}
 \frac{\sinh(z/2)}{z/2}
 =\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac{z^2}{4\pi^2m^2}\right).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Spectral Pochhammer factorization]\label{thm:qF-spectral}
\index{Pochhammer symbol!spectral factorization}
For $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and every $t\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-spectral-inside}
 M_q(t)=\EulerE^{t/2}\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \QPochhammer{-\dfrac{(1-q)^2t^2}{4\pi^2m^2}}{q^2}{\infty},
\end{equation}
and the associated double product converges normally on compact subsets of
the $t$-plane.

For $\AbsoluteValue q>1$, put
\[
 \mathcal D_q(t):=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \QPochhammer{-\dfrac{(1-q^{-1})^2t^2}{4\pi^2m^2}}{q^{-2}}{\infty}.
\]
Then $\mathcal D_q$ is entire, its defining double product converges normally on
compact subsets of $\ComplexNumbers$, and, on the germ disk of
\Cref{def:qF-germ},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-spectral-outside}
 M_q(t)=\frac{\EulerE^{t/2}}{\mathcal D_q(t)}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the quotient in \eqref{eq:qF-spectral-outside} is the meromorphic
continuation of $M_q$ to $\ComplexNumbers$.  Its pole divisor is the multiset
\[
 t=\frac{2\pi i kq^j}{1-q},
 \qquad k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\},\quad j\ge1,
\]
where multiplicities add when listed points coincide.  The reciprocal product
converges locally uniformly on compact subsets avoiding this divisor.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\AbsoluteValue q<1$ and substitute \eqref{eq:qF-Ecal-centered} and
\eqref{eq:qF-sinh-product} with $z=(1-q)q^jt$ into \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mgf-def}.  The
exponential factors multiply to
\[
 \exp\Bigl(\frac t2(1-q)\sum_{j\ge0}q^{j}\Bigr)=\EulerE^{t/2},
\]
leaving the double product
$\prod_{j\ge0}\prod_{m\ge1}\bigl(1+(1-q)^2q^{2j}t^2/(4\pi^2m^2)\bigr)$.  On
$\AbsoluteValue t\le T$ the sum of the absolute values of the perturbations is at most
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\AbsoluteValue{1-q}^2\AbsoluteValue q^{2j}T^2}{4\pi^2m^2}
 =\frac{\AbsoluteValue{1-q}^2T^2}{24\,(1-\AbsoluteValue q^2)}<\infty,
\]
using $\sum_mm^{-2}=\pi^2/6$.  The double product is therefore normally
convergent and may be rearranged.  Fixing $m$ and multiplying over $j$,
\[
 \prod_{j\ge0}\Bigl(1+\frac{(1-q)^2t^2}{4\pi^2m^2}q^{2j}\Bigr)
 =\QPochhammer{-\dfrac{(1-q)^2t^2}{4\pi^2m^2}}{q^{2}}{\infty}
\]
by the definition $(x;p)_\infty=\prod_{j\ge0}(1-xp^j)$ with $p=q^2$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:qF-spectral-inside}.

Now let $\AbsoluteValue q>1$ and write
\[
 P(t)=\prod_{j\ge1}\mathcal E((1-q)q^{-j}t),
 \qquad M_q=P^{-1}
\]
on the germ disk.  The same estimate, with $q^{-1}$ in place of $q$, proves
normal convergence on all compacta of the direct product obtained from the
quadratic factors; hence $\mathcal D_q$ is entire.  The linear arguments sum to
\[
 \frac t2(1-q)\sum_{j\ge1}q^{-j}
 =\frac t2(1-q)\frac{q^{-1}}{1-q^{-1}}
 =\frac t2\,\frac{1-q}{q-1}
 =-\frac t2,
\]
and, for the quadratic part, reindexing by $i=j-1\ge0$ gives
\[
 (1-q)^2q^{-2j}=(1-q)^2q^{-2}\,q^{-2i}=(1-q^{-1})^2\,(q^{-2})^{i},
\]
because $(1-q)^2q^{-2}=(q^{-1}-1)^2=(1-q^{-1})^2$.  Thus
\[
 P(t)=\EulerE^{-t/2}\mathcal D_q(t),
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:qF-spectral-outside} wherever the reciprocal germ is
defined and supplies its meromorphic continuation.

The zeros of $P$ arise from the simple nonzero zeros $2\pi i k$ of
$\mathcal E$: they are precisely the solutions of
\[
 (1-q)q^{-j}t=2\pi i k,
 \qquad k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\},\quad j\ge1.
\]
This is the stated pole divisor, with multiplicities added at collisions; no
cancellation is possible because $\EulerE^{t/2}$ never vanishes.  Finally, on
a compact set disjoint from the divisor, $\mathcal D_q$ has a positive minimum.
Uniform convergence of the direct partial products then implies uniform
convergence of their reciprocals.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Lean boundary for \Cref{thm:qF-spectral}.}
The current formal counterpart proves the general inside Pochhammer product,
but not the whole compound theorem.  The module
\path{RvachevPochhammerFactorization.lean} defines
\path{complexQPochhammerInf} and proves its defining identity,
multipliability, named product, and finite-prefix convergence through
\path{complexQPochhammerInf_eq_tprod},
\path{multipliable_one_sub_mul_pow_complex},
\path{hasProd_complexQPochhammerInf}, and
\path{tendsto_finiteQPochhammerIn_complex}; the last three statements allow
arbitrary complex $a$ and assume exactly $\lVert q\rVert<1$.
For arbitrary complex $q,z$ with $\lVert q\rVert<1$, the next three theorems,
\path{summable_norm_sineTerm_qpow_pair},
\path{geometricSincProduct_eq_tprod_pair}, and
\path{geometricSincProduct_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf}, prove absolute
summability of the paired Euler perturbations, identify the scale--zero
product, and justify the rearrangement
\[
 S_q(z)=\prod_{k\ge0}
 \left(\frac{z^2}{(k+1)^2};q^2\right)_{\!\infty}.
\]
This is global in $z$, includes $q=0$, negative and nonreal contractions, and
requires no nonvanishing premise.  The last two theorems,
\path{rvachevFourierProduct_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf} and
\path{rvachevFourier_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf}, specialize to dyadic
scale and nome $1/4$ and transfer the product to a bounded Fabius witness
satisfying \path{IsFabius}.

The zero-definition, three-theorem module
\path{GeometricPochhammerNormalConvergence.lean} strengthens that pointwise
identity to the exact outer-product convergence used here.  For every complex
$q$ with $\lVert q\rVert<1$,
\path{hasProdLocallyUniformly_geometricSincProduct_complexQPochhammerInf}
proves locally uniform convergence on the whole $z$-plane of
\[
 \prod_{k\ge0}
 \left(\frac{z^2}{(k+1)^2};q^2\right)_\infty
\]
to $S_q(z)$, including $q=0$.  The declarations
\path{hasProdLocallyUniformly_rvachevFourierProduct_complexQPochhammerInf}
and
\path{hasProdLocallyUniformly_rvachevFourier_complexQPochhammerInf}
give respectively the nome-$1/4$ Rvachev product and its bounded-Fabius
Fourier specialization.  Thus the outer locally-uniform, equivalently normal
convergence, portion of the inside product is now an exact Lean subclaim.

In centered characteristic-function coordinates,
$z=(1-q)t/(2\pi)$ identifies the general theorem with the inside product.
At the dyadic scale this is $t=4\pi z$.  In the uncentered MGF normalization
of \eqref{eq:qF-spectral-inside}, the corresponding dyadic substitution is
$t=4\pi\mathrm i z$: its Pochhammer product becomes $\Phi(z)$ and the retained
phase gives $M_{1/2}(4\pi\mathrm i z)=\EulerE^{2\pi\mathrm i z}\Phi(z)$.

The complementary sinc-product API supplies the analytic convergence and
characteristic-function bridge around this general factorization.  For
complex $q,z$ with $\lVert q\rVert<1$, the theorems
\path{hasProdLocallyUniformly_geometricSincProduct},
\path{geometricSincProductFactors_multipliable},
\path{hasProd_geometricSincProduct}, and
\path{geometricSincProduct_differentiable} prove locally uniform convergence,
pointwise \path{Multipliable}/\path{HasProd}, and entire-ness of
$S_q(z)=\prod_{n\ge0}\SincRad(\pi q^nz)$.  For real
$\AbsoluteValue q<1$, $t\in\RealNumbers$, and $z_q(t)=(1-q)t/(2\pi)$, the two theorems
\path{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricSincProduct}
and
\path{charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricReciprocalGamma}
give
\[
 \varphi_q(t)=\EulerE^{\mathrm i t/2}S_q(z_q(t))
 =\EulerE^{\mathrm i t/2}G_q(z_q(t))G_q(-z_q(t)),
\]
where $G_q(z)=\operatorname{geometricReciprocalGamma}(q,z)$.  This includes
$q=0$ and negative contractions.  The additional declarations
\path{tendstoLocallyUniformly_prefix_sinc_charFun} and
\path{tendstoUniformlyOn_prefix_sinc_charFun} give local uniform convergence
of the full prefixes on the real frequency line and uniform convergence on
every compact real frequency set.  There is still no named centered or MGF
bridge, no packaged complex-frequency full-phase-prefix statement, and no
outside formula, pole divisor, or zero--pole exchange.  Thus
\Cref{thm:qF-spectral} remains only partially formalized even though its
inside-disk pointwise factorization and outer normal convergence are exact.

\begin{remark}[The nome, and where the $q$-objects finally appear]\label{rem:qF-nome}
The geometric ratio $q$ of the random series \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-series}
becomes the nome $q^2$ of the spectral product: at $q=\pm\tfrac12$ the nome is
$\tfrac14$, and at $q=\pm\tfrac14$ it is $\tfrac1{16}$.  Reciprocal parameters
exchange the zeros of \eqref{eq:qF-spectral-inside} with the poles of
\eqref{eq:qF-spectral-outside}, in accordance with \Cref{thm:qF-reciprocity}.
\Cref{thm:qF-spectral} is the point at which the two halves of this Part meet:
the transform of the geometric uniform sum --- and hence, at $q=1/2$, of the
Fabius law --- is literally an infinite product of $q$-Pochhammer symbols in the
nome $q^2$, with the $m$th factor supplied by the $m$th spectral line of Euler's
sine product.

Taking logarithms in \eqref{eq:qF-spectral-inside} and expanding $\log(1+x)$
gives, for small $\AbsoluteValue t$,
\[
 \log M_q(t)-\frac t2
 =\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{r-1}}{r}\,
   \frac{\zeta(2r)}{1-q^{2r}}\left(\frac{(1-q)^2t^2}{4\pi^2}\right)^{\!r},
\]
which, by $\zeta(2r)=(-1)^{r+1}B_{2r}(2\pi)^{2r}/\bigl(2\,(2r)!\bigr)$, is
exactly the cumulant expansion \eqref{eq:q-cumulant-log-mgf} of
\Cref{thm:q-cumulants-uniform}.  The spectral factorization and the Bernoulli
cumulants are two readings of the same product.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block B20 ----
\section{The moment polynomial and the endpoint values}
\index{moment polynomial}
\index{Fabius function!inverse-dyadic values}

The recurrence \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence} carries apparent poles at all
roots of unity.  A single finite $q$-Pochhammer factor removes them.

\begin{definition}[The moment polynomial]\label{def:qF-moment-polynomial}
For $n\ge0$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-moment-polynomial}
 \mathcal P_n(q)\DefinitionEquals\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}{(1-q)^n}\,a_n(q)
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}{(1-q)^n}\,\frac{d_n(q)}{n!}.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomiality and recurrence]\label{thm:qF-moment-polynomial}
Each $\mathcal P_n$ lies in $\RationalNumbers[q]$.  One has $\mathcal P_0=1$ and, for $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-recurrence}
 \mathcal P_n(q)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
 \frac{q^{k}}{(n-k+1)!}\,\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-1}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{k}}\,\mathcal P_k(q),
\end{equation}
and moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-degree}
 \deg\mathcal P_n\le\binom n2,
 \qquad
 \mathcal P_n(0)=\frac1{(n+1)!}.
\end{equation}
The first cases are
\begin{gather*}
 \mathcal P_0=1,\qquad
 \mathcal P_1=\frac12,\qquad
 \mathcal P_2=\frac{q+2}{12},\qquad
 \mathcal P_3=\frac{q^2+q+1}{24},\\
 \mathcal P_4=\frac{(q^2+q+1)\bigl(6+3q+5q^2+2q^3-q^4\bigr)}{720}.
\end{gather*}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Inverting \eqref{eq:qF-moment-polynomial} gives
$a_k(q)=(1-q)^k\mathcal P_k(q)/\QPochhammer{q}{q}{k}$.  Substituting this into
\eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence} and multiplying through by
$\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-1}/(1-q)^n$ turns the left-hand side into
\[
 \frac{(1-q^n)\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-1}}{(1-q)^n}a_n(q)
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}{(1-q)^n}a_n(q)
 =\mathcal P_n(q),
\]
and the $k$th term on the right into
$\frac{q^k}{(n-k+1)!}\frac{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-1}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{k}}\mathcal P_k(q)$.  This is
\eqref{eq:qF-P-recurrence}.

Each quotient $\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n-1}/\QPochhammer{q}{q}{k}=\prod_{j=k+1}^{n-1}(1-q^j)$ occurring in
\eqref{eq:qF-P-recurrence} is a polynomial of degree
$\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}j=\binom n2-\binom{k+1}2$.  Assuming inductively that
$\mathcal P_k\in\RationalNumbers[q]$ with $\deg\mathcal P_k\le\binom k2$ for all $k<n$, the
$k$th summand is a polynomial of degree at most
\[
 k+\Bigl(\binom n2-\binom{k+1}2\Bigr)+\binom k2
 =\binom n2,
\]
because $\binom{k+1}2-\binom k2=k$.  Hence $\mathcal P_n\in\RationalNumbers[q]$ and
$\deg\mathcal P_n\le\binom n2$.  Setting $q=0$ in \eqref{eq:qF-P-recurrence}
kills every term with $k\ge1$ and leaves
$\mathcal P_n(0)=\mathcal P_0(0)/(n+1)!=1/(n+1)!$.  The displayed values follow
by evaluating \eqref{eq:qF-P-recurrence} for $n\le4$.
\end{proof}

% ed.: the source draft stated only the bound $\deg\mathcal P_n\le\binom n2$ and
% ed.: did not record that it is not attained (already $\deg\mathcal P_3=2<3$).
% ed.: The exact degree is determined below.
\begin{proposition}[Exact degree and the two leading coefficients]\label{prop:qF-P-degree-sharp}
Let $B_m$ be the Bernoulli numbers in the convention
\eqref{eq:bernoulli-convention-fabius}, so that $B_1=-\tfrac12$.  For every
$n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-leading}
 [q^{\binom n2}]\,\mathcal P_n(q)=\frac{(-1)^nB_n}{n!},
\end{equation}
and for every $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-subleading}
 [q^{\binom n2-1}]\,\mathcal P_n(q)
 =-\frac{(-1)^nB_n}{n!}+\frac{(-1)^{n-1}B_{n-1}}{2\,(n-1)!}.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-exact-degree}
 \deg\mathcal P_n=
 \begin{cases}
  \binom n2, & n\text{ even, or }n=1,\\[2pt]
  \binom n2-1, & n\ge3\text{ odd.}
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $R_n(q)\DefinitionEquals\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}/(1-q)^n=\prod_{j=2}^{n}(1+q+\cdots+q^{j-1})$, a
polynomial of degree $\sum_{j=2}^n(j-1)=\binom n2$.  Factoring the top power out
of each of its $n-1$ factors,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-Rn-at-infinity}
 R_n(q)=q^{\binom n2}\prod_{j=2}^{n}\bigl(1+q^{-1}+\cdots+q^{-(j-1)}\bigr)
       =q^{\binom n2}\bigl(1+(n-1)q^{-1}+O(q^{-2})\bigr)
\end{equation}
as $q\to\infty$.  Since $\mathcal P_n=R_na_n$ is a polynomial of degree at most
$\binom n2$, the rational function $a_n=\mathcal P_n/R_n$ is regular at
$q=\infty$; let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-an-at-infinity}
 a_n(q)=L_n+M_nq^{-1}+O(q^{-2})
 \qquad(q\to\infty)
\end{equation}
be its expansion there.  Multiplying \eqref{eq:qF-Rn-at-infinity} by
\eqref{eq:qF-an-at-infinity} and comparing coefficients gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-P-coeffs-from-LM}
 [q^{\binom n2}]\mathcal P_n=L_n,
 \qquad
 [q^{\binom n2-1}]\mathcal P_n=M_n+(n-1)L_n\quad(n\ge2).
\end{equation}

It remains to compute $L_n$ and $M_n$.  Multiply
\eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence} by $q^{-n}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-recurrence-scaled}
 (q^{-n}-1)\,a_n(q)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(-1)^{n-k}(1-q^{-1})^{n-k}}{(n-k+1)!}\,a_k(q),
\end{equation}
using $q^{-n}q^k(1-q)^{n-k}=(-1)^{n-k}(1-q^{-1})^{n-k}$.  Letting
$q\to\infty$ in \eqref{eq:qF-recurrence-scaled} gives
\[
 -L_n=\sum_{k<n}(-1)^{n-k}\frac{L_k}{(n-k+1)!},
\]
that is,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-L-relation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{n-k}}{(n-k+1)!}\,L_k=0
 \qquad(n\ge1),
 \qquad L_0=1 .
\end{equation}
Set $L(s)=\sum_{n\ge0}L_ns^n$ and
$\Psi(s)=\sum_{r\ge0}(-s)^r/(r+1)!=(1-\EulerE^{-s})/s$.  Relation
\eqref{eq:qF-L-relation} says precisely that $L(s)\Psi(s)=1$, whence
\[
 L(s)=\frac{s}{1-\EulerE^{-s}}=\frac{-s}{\EulerE^{-s}-1}
      =\sum_{n\ge0}B_n\frac{(-s)^n}{n!}
\]
by \eqref{eq:bernoulli-convention-fabius}.  Thus $L_n=(-1)^nB_n/n!$, which with
\eqref{eq:qF-P-coeffs-from-LM} is \eqref{eq:qF-P-leading}.

For $M_n$, note first that $a_1\equiv\tfrac12$, so $M_0=M_1=0$.  For $n\ge2$ the
term $q^{-n}a_n(q)$ on the left of \eqref{eq:qF-recurrence-scaled} is
$O(q^{-2})$, so comparing coefficients of $q^{-1}$ there gives
\[
 -M_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(-1)^{n-k}\bigl(M_k-(n-k)L_k\bigr)}{(n-k+1)!},
\]
that is, $[s^n]\bigl(M\Psi\bigr)=[s^n]\bigl(L\Theta_{\mathrm{aux}}\bigr)$ for $n\ge2$, where
$M(s)=\sum M_ns^n$ and
$\Theta_{\mathrm{aux}}(s)=\sum_{r\ge0}(-1)^rr\,s^r/(r+1)!$.  Since
$\sum_{r\ge0}(r+1)(-s)^r/(r+1)!=\EulerE^{-s}$, we have
$\Theta_{\mathrm{aux}}=\EulerE^{-s}-\Psi$.  The two
remaining coefficients are checked directly:
$[s^0](M\Psi)=M_0=0=[s^0](L\Theta_{\mathrm{aux}})$, while $[s^1](M\Psi)=M_1=0$ and
$[s^1](L\Theta)=L_0\Theta_1=-\tfrac12$.  Hence $M\Psi=L\Theta+\tfrac s2$, and
since $1/\Psi=L$,
\[
 M=L^2\Theta+\frac{sL}2=L^2\EulerE^{-s}-L+\frac{sL}2 .
\]
Differentiating $L=s/(1-\EulerE^{-s})$ gives $sL'=L-L^2\EulerE^{-s}$, so
$L^2\EulerE^{-s}=L-sL'$ and therefore $M=-sL'+\tfrac{sL}2$.  Reading off
coefficients, $M_n=-nL_n+\tfrac12L_{n-1}$, and substituting into
\eqref{eq:qF-P-coeffs-from-LM},
\[
 [q^{\binom n2-1}]\mathcal P_n=-nL_n+\tfrac12L_{n-1}+(n-1)L_n
 =-L_n+\tfrac12L_{n-1},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:qF-P-subleading}.

Finally, $B_n\ne0$ for $n$ even and $B_n=0$ for odd $n\ge3$, while
$L_1=\tfrac12\ne0$.  So \eqref{eq:qF-P-leading} is nonzero exactly for $n$ even
and for $n=1$; and when $n\ge3$ is odd, \eqref{eq:qF-P-subleading} reduces to
$\tfrac12L_{n-1}=B_{n-1}/\bigl(2(n-1)!\bigr)\ne0$, since $n-1$ is even and at
least $2$.  This is \eqref{eq:qF-P-exact-degree}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}\label{ex:qF-P-degrees}
For $n=3,\ldots,8$ the degrees of $\mathcal P_n$ are
\[
 2,\ 6,\ 9,\ 15,\ 20,\ 28,
 \qquad\text{against}\qquad
 \binom n2=3,\ 6,\ 10,\ 15,\ 21,\ 28 ,
\]
so the bound \eqref{eq:qF-P-degree} is missed by exactly one at each odd $n$.
The leading coefficients of $\mathcal P_4$, $\mathcal P_6$, $\mathcal P_8$ are
$-\tfrac1{720}$, $\tfrac1{30240}$, $-\tfrac1{1209600}$, in agreement with
$B_4/4!$, $B_6/6!$, $B_8/8!$.
\end{example}

% ed.: the incoming item stated two identities, of which the second,
% ed.: $F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}\Expectation X^n/n!$, is \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment}
% ed.: of \Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}; only the first is new and only it is kept
% ed.: here.  The general-$q$ display \eqref{eq:qF-endpoint-poch} is
% ed.: \eqref{eq:inverse-power-values} rewritten through \Cref{def:qF-moment-polynomial},
% ed.: and is the form in which the $q$-Pochhammer denominator becomes visible.
% ed.: Placement: immediately after \Cref{ex:qF-P-degrees}.

\begin{corollary}[Pochhammer-cleared endpoint form and the half-base dictionary]
\label{cor:qF-halfbase-dictionary}
\index{Mersenne product!and Fabius values}
\index{Pochhammer symbol!as endpoint denominator}
For $0<q\le\tfrac12$ and $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-endpoint-poch}
 F_q\bigl(q^{\,n}\bigr)=\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}2}}{\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}}\,\mathcal P_n(q).
\end{equation}
At $q=1/2$ this is a dictionary between the moment polynomial and the values of the Fabius
function:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qF-halfbase-dictionary}
 \mathcal P_n(1/2)=\mathfrak m_n\,F\bigl(2^{-n}\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $\mathfrak m_n=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(2^j-1)$ is the Mersenne product
\eqref{eq:mersenne-product}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:qF-moment-polynomial}, $\alpha^{(q)}_n/(1-q)^n=\mathcal P_n(q)/\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}$, so
\eqref{eq:qF-endpoint-poch} is \eqref{eq:inverse-power-values} rewritten.  At $q=\tfrac12$,
\eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial} gives
$\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}=2^{-\binom{n+1}2}\mathfrak m_n$, and the two powers of $2$ in
\eqref{eq:qF-endpoint-poch} cancel, leaving
$F(2^{-n})=\mathcal P_n(1/2)/\mathfrak m_n$.
\end{proof}

The first values, with $F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}\alpha_n$ from
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment} and $\alpha_n$ as in \Cref{ex:fabius-first-coefficients}, are
\[
 \begin{array}{c|ccccc}
  n & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\[2pt]\hline
  \rule{0pt}{16pt}
  \mathcal P_n(1/2) & \frac12 & \frac5{24} & \frac7{96}
    & \frac{1001}{46080} & \frac{4123}{737280}\\[6pt]
  \mathfrak m_n & 1 & 3 & 21 & 315 & 9765\\[6pt]
  F(2^{-n}) & \frac12 & \frac5{72} & \frac1{288}
    & \frac{143}{2073600} & \frac{19}{33177600}
 \end{array}
\]
% ed.: the last row also appears in the sibling item drafted as
% ed.: \texttt{ex:fabius-dyadic-values} (the first dyadic values attached to the
% ed.: composition sum \eqref{eq:fabius-composition}).  Whichever of the two is
% ed.: adopted second should drop its copy; the row is kept here because it is
% ed.: the third factor of \eqref{eq:qF-halfbase-dictionary} and the table is
% ed.: unreadable without it.

\begin{remark}[The Pochhammer denominator is not imported notation]\label{rem:qF-not-imported}
\Cref{cor:qF-halfbase-dictionary} closes the circle of this part.  The finite $q$-Pochhammer
symbol $\QPochhammer{q}{q}{n}$ in \eqref{eq:qF-endpoint-poch} was not chosen for cosmetic reasons: by
\Cref{def:qF-moment-polynomial,thm:qF-moment-polynomial} it is exactly the pole-clearing
factor of the recurrence \eqref{eq:q-moment-recurrence}, whose denominators $1-q^n$ are forced
by the self-similarity \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-fixed-point}.  At $q=1/2$ it specializes,
through \Cref{thm:halfbase-normalization}, to the Mersenne product $\mathfrak m_n$ of
\Cref{chap:halfbase-thuemorse} --- the same constant that
\Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} produces on the right of \eqref{eq:transfer-top}, and
that normalizes the dyadic numerator $\mathcal N_n(\theta)=\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$ of
\Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}.  The three occurrences are one occurrence: the geometric
Vandermonde denominator of the order-$n$ stencil on the nodes $-2^k$, the pole-clearing factor
of the moment recurrence, and the Mersenne product are the same object read three ways.
\end{remark}

\section{The universal division-free definition}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!universal Pascal definition}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!over a semiring}
\index{semiring}
\index{specialization!denominator-free}

The factorial quotient is a formula, not a definition: it presupposes that every factor $1-q^j$ with $j\le n$ is invertible.  Either recurrence of \Cref{thm:qpascal} is free of that hypothesis, and free of subtraction as well.  It therefore defines the Gaussian coefficient over an arbitrary commutative semiring, and it is this universal object, rather than the quotient, that makes the root-of-unity specializations of \Cref{chap:cyclotomic} and the layered architecture of \Cref{prin:proof-architecture} legitimate.

\begin{definition}[Universal Gaussian polynomials]\label{def:universal-gaussian}
Let $u$ be an indeterminate.  Define $G_{n,k}(u)$ for $n\in\NaturalNumbers$ and $0\le k\le n$ by
$G_{0,0}(u)=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-pascal}
 G_{n+1,k}(u)=G_{n,k}(u)+u^{\,n+1-k}G_{n,k-1}(u)
 \qquad(0\le k\le n+1),
\end{equation}
with the convention that $G_{n,k}(u)=0$ whenever $k<0$ or $k>n$.  In the displayed range the exponent $n+1-k$ is nonnegative, so no negative power occurs.  For a commutative semiring $R$ and $a\in R$, write $G_{n,k}(a)\in R$ for the element produced by the same recursion with $u$ replaced by $a$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Existence, uniqueness, and functoriality]\label{thm:universal-functoriality}
% ed.: the cross-reference in the first item is an unbreakable box in an
% ed.: italic list of this width; \sloppy absorbs it locally.
\sloppy
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item There is exactly one family satisfying \eqref{eq:universal-pascal}
 with $G_{0,0}=1$, and every $G_{n,k}(u)$ lies in $\NaturalNumbers[u]$.
\item $G_{n,k}(u)=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ under $q=u$; that is, the factorial
 quotient is the value of the universal polynomial.
\item If $\phi\colon R\to S$ is a homomorphism of commutative semirings, then
\[
 \phi\bigl(G_{n,k}(a)\bigr)=G_{n,k}\bigl(\phi(a)\bigr)
 \qquad(a\in R,\ 0\le k\le n).
\]
\end{enumerate}
Hence the Gaussian coefficient is definable, and specializable, with no division, no subtraction, and no invertibility hypothesis on the factors $1-a^j$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i)  Row $n+1$ is determined by row $n$ through \eqref{eq:universal-pascal}, and row $0$ is prescribed; induction on $n$ gives existence and uniqueness.  The recursion uses only $0$, $1$, addition, multiplication, and nonnegative powers of $u$, so every $G_{n,k}$ lies in $\NaturalNumbers[u]$.

(ii)  Replacing $n$ by $n+1$ in the second recurrence \eqref{eq:qpascal2} of \Cref{thm:qpascal} gives
\[
 \GaussianBinomial{n+1}{k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}+q^{\,n+1-k}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k-1}{q},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:universal-pascal} with $u=q$, and the boundary value $\GaussianBinomial{0}{0}{q}=1$ agrees.  The uniqueness argument of (i) is valid over any commutative ring, in particular over the field $\RationalNumbers(u)$; hence the two families coincide there, and therefore in $\NaturalNumbers[u]$ by \Cref{cor:positivity}.

(iii)  Apply $\phi$ to \eqref{eq:universal-pascal}.  A semiring homomorphism preserves $0$, $1$, sums, products, and hence powers, so the family $\bigl(\phi(G_{n,k}(a))\bigr)$ satisfies the same boundary condition and the same recursion in $S$ with parameter $\phi(a)$.  Uniqueness in $S$, again by (i), gives the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Denominator-free factorial identities]\label{cor:universal-factorial-identities}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring and let $a\in R$.  For $0\le k\le n$,
\begin{align}
 (a;a)_k\,G_{n,k}(a)&=(a^{\,n-k+1};a)_k,\label{eq:universal-short-factorial}\\
 (a;a)_n&=(a;a)_k\,(a;a)_{n-k}\,G_{n,k}(a).\label{eq:universal-full-factorial}
\end{align}
No factor $1-a^j$ is assumed invertible; both identities hold even when every product displayed in them vanishes.  Thus \Cref{prop:qbinom-products} is a consequence of a polynomial identity rather than a prerequisite for the definition.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:universal-functoriality}(ii), $G_{n,k}(u)=(u;u)_n/\bigl((u;u)_k(u;u)_{n-k}\bigr)$ in $\RationalNumbers(u)$.  Both sides of \eqref{eq:universal-full-factorial} with $a=u$ are polynomials, so the equality holds in $\IntegerNumbers[u]$.  By \Cref{prop:concat} with $m=n-k$,
\[
 (u;u)_n=(u;u)_{n-k}\,(u^{\,n-k+1};u)_k;
\]
since $\IntegerNumbers[u]$ is an integral domain and $(u;u)_{n-k}\ne0$, cancelling it in \eqref{eq:universal-full-factorial} gives \eqref{eq:universal-short-factorial} in $\IntegerNumbers[u]$.  Finally apply the unique ring homomorphism $\IntegerNumbers[u]\to R$ with $u\mapsto a$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A primitive root of unity]\label{ex:gaussian-root-of-unity}
Let $a=\EulerE^{2\pi i/3}$, so $(a;a)_3=(1-a)(1-a^2)(1-a^3)=0$ while $(a;a)_2=3$.  The quotient $\GaussianBinomial{6}{3}{q}$ at $q=a$ displays $0/0$, but the polynomial value is unambiguous: $G_{6,3}(a)=2$, in agreement with the $q$-Lucas congruence of \Cref{chap:cyclotomic}.  Identity \eqref{eq:universal-full-factorial} reads $0=0\cdot0\cdot2$, and it is true.  For a nondegenerate instance take $n=5$, $k=2$: here $G_{5,2}(a)=1$ and \eqref{eq:universal-short-factorial} reads $(a;a)_2\cdot1=(a^4;a)_2$, both sides equal to $3$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
The transport statement \Cref{thm:universal-functoriality}(iii) is what licenses steps 1--2 and 6 of \Cref{prin:proof-architecture}: an identity established without division descends to finite characteristic, to quotient rings, to truncated power-series rings, and to roots of unity with no cancellation theorem interposed.  The same observation applies verbatim to \Cref{thm:finite-qbinomial}, whose proof is an induction from \Cref{thm:qpascal} and therefore valid over every commutative ring, and to the reversed row of \Cref{chap:geometric-filters}; compare \Cref{rem:denominator-free-reversed}.
\end{remark}

\section{The M\"obius function of the subspace lattice}
\index{Mobius function@M\"obius function!subspace lattice}
\index{incidence algebra}
\index{subspace lattice}

Counting is only the first half of the finite-field picture.  The subspaces of $\FiniteField_{Q}^{n}$ form a lattice, and its M\"obius function is again a Gaussian datum: a sign and a quadratic power of $Q$.  This is the structural reason for the shape of the inverse kernel in \Cref{thm:qbinomial-inversion}.

For an ambient vector space $V$, the notation
$\SubspaceMobius{V}{U}{W}$ denotes the interval-poset M\"obius function on
the subspace lattice $\mathcal L(V)$.  It is characterized by
\[
 \SubspaceMobius{V}{U}{U}=1,
 \qquad
 \sum_{U\subseteq X\subseteq W}\SubspaceMobius{V}{U}{X}=0
 \quad(U\subsetneq W).
\]
It is neither the arithmetic M\"obius function nor a measure.

\begin{theorem}[M\"obius function of the subspace lattice]\label{thm:subspace-mobius}
Let $Q$ be a prime power and let $V$ be a $d$-dimensional vector space over $\FiniteField_{Q}$.  In the lattice of subspaces of $V$, ordered by inclusion,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:subspace-mobius}
 \SubspaceMobius{V}{0}{V}=(-1)^dQ^{\binom d2}.
\end{equation}
More generally, if $U\subseteq W$ are subspaces and $r=\dim(W/U)$, then
\[
 \SubspaceMobius{V}{U}{W}=(-1)^rQ^{\binom r2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The correspondence $X\mapsto X/U$ is an isomorphism from the interval $[U,W]$ onto the lattice of subspaces of $W/U$, and the M\"obius function of a poset depends only on the isomorphism type of the interval.  It therefore suffices to prove \eqref{eq:subspace-mobius}.

Induct on $d$.  For $d=0$ the value is
$\SubspaceMobius{V}{0}{0}=1$, as asserted.  Let $d\ge1$ and assume the
formula for all smaller dimensions.  The defining relation for the M\"obius
function of the interval $[0,V]$ is
\[
 \sum_{X\subseteq V}\SubspaceMobius{V}{0}{X}=0,
\]
the sum running over all subspaces of $V$.  Grouping the subspaces by dimension and using \Cref{thm:subspace-count} together with the induction hypothesis,
\[
 \SubspaceMobius{V}{0}{V}
 =-\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\GaussianBinomial{d}{j}{Q}(-1)^jQ^{\binom j2}.
\]
By \Cref{cor:alternating-qbinom} at base $Q$, which is \eqref{eq:finite-qbinomial-minus} at $z=1$,
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{d}\GaussianBinomial{d}{j}{Q}(-1)^jQ^{\binom j2}=(1;Q)_d=0
 \qquad(d\ge1),
\]
since the factor with index $0$ in $(1;Q)_d$ vanishes.  Hence the truncated sum through $j=d-1$ equals the negative of the omitted term $j=d$, and therefore
$\SubspaceMobius{V}{0}{V}=(-1)^dQ^{\binom d2}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Where this kernel is met again]\label{rem:mobius-inversion-kernel}
The inverse kernel $(-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}$ of \Cref{thm:qbinomial-inversion} is exactly $\SubspaceMobius{V}{U}{W}$ of \Cref{thm:subspace-mobius} with $r=n-k$, multiplied by the number $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{Q}$ of intermediate subspaces: for $q=Q$ a prime power, $q$-binomial inversion \emph{is} M\"obius inversion over the subspace lattice for functions depending only on dimension, and the polynomial identity then extends it to an indeterminate $q$.  The reversed Gaussian row $N_{n,k}(q)$ of \eqref{eq:reversed-gaussian-row} carries the additional monomial $q^{\,n-k}$, because
$\binom{n-k}{2}+(n-k)=\binom{n-k+1}{2}$ and its nodes are $q^1,\dots,q^n$ rather than $q^0,\dots,q^{n-1}$.  This accounts for the sign and the quadratic exponent that recur throughout \Cref{chap:geometric-filters}.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block N10 ----
% ed.: drafted as \Cref{prop:thue-morse-power-recurrence} inside the synthesis section above;
% ed.: reproduced here for convenience.  It is meaningful only if \Cref{thm:fabius-inverse-dyadic}
% ed.: is imported, since it is what makes \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} evaluable.

\begin{proposition}[Recurrence for the translated Thue--Morse power sums]\label{prop:thue-morse-power-recurrence}
For all $k,d\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thue-morse-power-base}
 M_{0,d}(c)=c^d,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thue-morse-power-recurrence}
 M_{k+1,d}(c)=-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom dj2^{kj}M_{k,d-j}(c).
\end{equation}
Consequently all the quantities $M_{k,d}(c)$ with $k\le n$ and $d\le2n$ needed in \eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed} can be tabulated with $O(n^3)$ arithmetic operations, rather than the $2^k$ terms of the defining sum \eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-sums}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The base case is the single term $r=0$.  For the recurrence, split $\{0,\dots,2^{k+1}-1\}$ into $\{0,\dots,2^k-1\}$ and its translate by $2^k$.  Adding $2^k$ to an integer $r<2^k$ turns off no binary digit and turns on exactly one, so $\tau_{r+2^k}=-\tau_r$.  Hence
\[
 M_{k+1,d}(c)
 =\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\Bigl((r+c)^d-\bigl(r+c+2^k\bigr)^d\Bigr)
 =-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\binom dj2^{kj}\sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r(r+c)^{d-j},
\]
by the binomial theorem, the term $j=0$ cancelling.  This is \eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-recurrence}.  Filling the table in increasing $k$ and $d$ costs $O(d)$ operations per entry, hence $O(n\cdot n\cdot n)$ in total for the stated ranges.
\end{proof}
