\section{A ring-level residual principle}
\index{residual moment!ring-level principle}
\index{symmetric function!complete homogeneous}
\index{moment!first surviving}
\index{commutative ring!moment residual}

\Cref{thm:universal-lagrange-residual} is stated for the Lagrange weights of $N$
distinct nodes in a field, and the free target $x$ carried by both sides of
\eqref{eq:universal-residual} is what makes it a statement about interpolation.
The mechanism it uses is narrower than its hypotheses.  The field, the
distinctness of the nodes, the Lagrange normalization of the weights, and the
invertibility of the differences $v_i-v_j$ can all be discarded if one asks only
for the moments of a finite weight system: the single hypothesis that the first
$p$ moments vanish already determines every later moment.  In that form the
statement holds over an arbitrary commutative ring, and both exact tails of this
part become instances of it.

\begin{theorem}[All-degree residual from the first surviving moment]
\label{thm:ring-moment-residual}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring and let $x_0,\ldots,x_p\in R$ and
$w_0,\ldots,w_p\in R$ be arbitrary elements.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-moment-definition}
 M_m\DefinitionEquals\sum_{i=0}^{p}w_ix_i^{\,m}
 \qquad(m\ge0),
\end{equation}
with the convention $x^0=1$ for every $x\in R$.  If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses}
 M_0=M_1=\cdots=M_{p-1}=0,
 \qquad
 M_p=K,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-moment-residual}
 M_{p+r}=K\,h_r(x_0,\ldots,x_p)
 \qquad(r\ge0),
\end{equation}
where $h_r$ is the complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial in the $p+1$
displayed variables, evaluated in $R$.  No node is assumed distinct from another,
no weight and no difference $x_i-x_j$ is assumed invertible, and $R$ may have
zero divisors.  For $p=0$ the hypothesis \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses} is
empty, $K=w_0$, and \eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual} is the definition of $M_r$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Work in $R[[t]]$ and put
\[
 M(t)=\sum_{m\ge0}M_mt^m,
 \qquad
 E(t)=\prod_{i=0}^{p}(1-x_it).
\]
Each factor $1-x_it$ is invertible in $R[[t]]$, with inverse
$\sum_{m\ge0}x_i^{\,m}t^m$.  Interchanging the finite sum over $i$ with the sum
over $m$ in \eqref{eq:ring-moment-definition} gives
$M(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{p}w_i(1-x_it)^{-1}$, and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-moment-transform}
 E(t)M(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{p}w_i\prod_{j\ne i}(1-x_jt).
\end{equation}
The right-hand side of \eqref{eq:ring-moment-transform} is a polynomial in $t$ of
degree at most $p$, each of its $p+1$ summands being a product of $p$ linear
factors.

Compute the same coefficients from the left.  By \eqref{eq:weighted-e-gen} with
$z=-t$ one has $E(t)=\sum_{l=0}^{p+1}(-1)^le_lt^l$, where
$e_l=e_l(x_0,\ldots,x_p)$, so
\[
 [t^m]\bigl(E(t)M(t)\bigr)=\sum_{l=0}^{m}(-1)^le_lM_{m-l}
\]
involves only $M_0,\ldots,M_m$.  For $m<p$ every moment occurring here has index
at most $m<p$ and vanishes by \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses}; for $m=p$ the
only surviving term is $e_0M_p=K$.  Comparing with the degree bound just
obtained,
\[
 E(t)M(t)=Kt^p.
\]
Finally $E(t)$ has constant term $1$, and by \eqref{eq:weighted-h-gen} its inverse
in $R[[t]]$ is $\sum_{r\ge0}h_r(x_0,\ldots,x_p)t^r$.  Multiplying the last display
by that inverse gives
\[
 M(t)=Kt^p\sum_{r\ge0}h_r(x_0,\ldots,x_p)t^r,
\]
and comparison of the coefficients of $t^{p+r}$ is
\eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The exact scope of the hypotheses]\label{rem:ring-residual-scope}
Three comments delimit the statement.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \emph{The hypotheses are satisfiable with non-invertible data.}  In
 $R=\IntegerNumbers/12\IntegerNumbers$ take nodes $(x_0,x_1,x_2)=(0,1,2)$ and weights
 $(w_0,w_1,w_2)=(1,-2,1)$.  Then $M_0=M_1=0$ and $K=M_2=2$, a zero divisor; no
 weight and no node difference is a unit.  Here
 $h_r(0,1,2)=h_r(1,2)=2^{\,r+1}-1$ and
 $M_{2+r}=2^{\,r+2}-2=2h_r(0,1,2)$, an identity of integers and hence one in
 every quotient of $\IntegerNumbers$.
\item \emph{Coincident nodes make the conclusion empty rather than false.}
 Group the nodes by their distinct values $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ and let $W_j$ be the
 sum of the weights carried by the value $v_j$, so that $M_m=\sum_jW_jv_j^{\,m}$.
 If two nodes coincide then $s\le p$, and the first $s$ hypotheses say that the
 $s\times s$ Vandermonde matrix of $v_1,\ldots,v_s$ annihilates
 $(W_1,\ldots,W_s)$.  Whenever its determinant $\prod_{i<j}(v_j-v_i)$ is not a
 zero divisor --- in particular over a field --- the adjugate identity forces
 $W_1=\cdots=W_s=0$, hence $K=0$, and both sides of
 \eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual} vanish.  Distinctness is thus not needed as a
 hypothesis, and adding it would exclude nothing of substance.
\item \emph{Neither residual theorem contains the other.}
 \Cref{thm:universal-lagrange-residual} is not a corollary of
 \Cref{thm:ring-moment-residual}: its two sides carry a free target $x$, and its
 right-hand side involves the $(N+1)$-variable symmetric function
 $h_r(x,v_1,\ldots,v_N)$, of which \eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual} sees only the
 specialization $x=0$.  Conversely \eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual} is not a
 corollary of \eqref{eq:universal-residual}, which presupposes a field, distinct
 nodes, and one particular weight vector.  The present development uses
 \eqref{eq:universal-residual} only at $x=0$, in the proof of
 \Cref{thm:complete-geometric-moments}, and
 \Cref{rem:one-principle-two-filters}\,(i) recovers exactly that use from
 \eqref{eq:ring-moment-residual}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D02 ----
% ---- merged block D02 ----
% ed.: continues the section opened by block D01; place immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{rem:ring-residual-scope}.
\begin{corollary}[Geometric Gaussian residual for arbitrary weights]
\label{cor:ring-geometric-residual}
\index{Gaussian coefficient!as a residual multiplier}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring, let $c,q\in R$, and let $w_0,\ldots,w_p\in R$
satisfy \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses} on the nodes $x_i=cq^i$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-geometric-residual}
 \sum_{i=0}^{p}w_i\,(cq^i)^{\,p+r}
 =K\,c^{\,r}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}
 \qquad(r\ge0),
\end{equation}
the Gaussian coefficient being read in $R$ as the value at $q$ of the universal
polynomial of \Cref{def:universal-gaussian}, so that no factor $1-q^j$ is
assumed invertible.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:ring-moment-residual} the left-hand side of
\eqref{eq:ring-geometric-residual} equals $K\,h_r(c,cq,\ldots,cq^p)$.  Since
$h_r$ is homogeneous of degree $r$, scaling every variable by $c$ multiplies it
by $c^{\,r}$, so $h_r(c,cq,\ldots,cq^p)=c^{\,r}h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^p)$.

It remains to identify $h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^p)$.  In the notation of
\Cref{chap:weighted} this is $S_r^{\,p+1}(\mathbf w)$ for $w_i=q^{\,i-1}$, which
by \Cref{thm:weighted-specializations}\,(ii) with $n=p+1$ and $k=r$ equals
$\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{r}{q}$, hence $\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}$ by the symmetry
\Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}\,(i).  Both sides of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-principal-specialization}
 h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^p)=\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}
\end{equation}
lie in $\IntegerNumbers[q]$ --- the left one by inspection of
\eqref{eq:weighted-h-gen}, the right one by
\Cref{thm:universal-functoriality}\,(i),(ii) --- and they agree in $\RationalNumbers(q)$, so
they agree in $\IntegerNumbers[q]$.  Applying the evaluation homomorphism $\IntegerNumbers[q]\to R$ with
$q\mapsto q$ and using the functoriality
\Cref{thm:universal-functoriality}\,(iii) transports
\eqref{eq:ring-principal-specialization} to $R$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:ring-geometric-vs-lagrange}
\Cref{cor:scaled-geometric-moments} is the case of
\eqref{eq:ring-geometric-residual} in which the weights are the Lagrange row
$\lambda^{(q)}_{n,k}$, after the deflation described in
\Cref{rem:one-principle-two-filters}\,(i); what is new here is that no property
of the weight row is used beyond \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses}, and that
$c$, $q$ and the $w_i$ need not lie in a field.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D03 ----
% ---- merged block D03 ----
% ed.: continues the section opened by block D01; place immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{rem:ring-geometric-vs-lagrange}.  This block closes
% ed.: \Cref{chap:geometric-filters}.
\begin{corollary}[Formal-series filter for arbitrary weights]
\label{cor:ring-series-filter}
\index{filter!ring-level}
\index{power series!coefficient filter}
Let $R$ be a commutative ring and let $x_0,\ldots,x_p$ and $w_0,\ldots,w_p$ in
$R$ satisfy \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses}.  For every
$f(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}a_mz^m\in R[[z]]$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ring-series-filter}
 \sum_{i=0}^{p}w_if(x_iz)
 =K\sum_{r\ge0}a_{p+r}\,h_r(x_0,\ldots,x_p)\,z^{\,p+r}.
\end{equation}
On a geometric node set $x_i=cq^i$ the multiplier of $a_{p+r}$ is
$Kc^{\,r}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The left-hand side is a sum of \emph{finitely many} elements of $R[[z]]$, so
coefficient extraction commutes with it:
\[
 [z^m]\sum_{i=0}^{p}w_if(x_iz)
 =a_m\sum_{i=0}^{p}w_ix_i^{\,m}
 =a_mM_m.
\]
By \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses} this vanishes for $m<p$, and for $m=p+r$ it
is $Ka_{p+r}h_r(x_0,\ldots,x_p)$ by \Cref{thm:ring-moment-residual}.  The last
assertion is \Cref{cor:ring-geometric-residual}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[One principle, two exact tails]\label{rem:one-principle-two-filters}
The two exact tails of this part are instances of
\eqref{eq:ring-series-filter}.  This does not merge them --- what keeps them
apart is recorded in \Cref{rem:two-exact-tails} --- but it does replace two
separate residual computations by one.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \emph{Evaluation at zero, after deflation.}  The Lagrange row
 $\lambda^{(q)}_{n,k}$ has $M_0=1$, so \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses} fails
 for it.  Adjoin one further node $x_{n+1}=0$ with weight $w_{n+1}=-1$.  The
 augmented system has $n+2$ nodes, so $p=n+1$; its moments are $M_0=1-1=0$ and
 $M_m=0$ for $1\le m\le n$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-eval-zero}, since $0^m=0$
 there.  Its value of $K$ can be read off \eqref{eq:ring-moment-transform}
 without any prior knowledge of the row: among the $n+2$ summands only the one
 indexed by the adjoined node has degree $n+1$, namely
 $-\prod_{k=0}^{n}(1-q^kt)$, whose coefficient of $t^{\,n+1}$ is
 $-(-1)^{n+1}q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}$, so
 \[
 K=(-1)^nq^{\binom{n+1}{2}}.
 \]
 A variable equal to $0$ contributes the factor $1$ to
 \eqref{eq:weighted-h-gen}, so
 \[
  h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^n,0)=h_r(1,q,\ldots,q^n),
 \]
 and \eqref{eq:ring-geometric-residual} returns
 \eqref{eq:complete-geometric-moments} while \eqref{eq:ring-series-filter}
 returns \eqref{eq:filter-exact-tail}.  Running the same argument on the nodes
 $cq^k$ together with $0$ returns \Cref{cor:scaled-geometric-moments}.  This is
 a proof of \Cref{thm:complete-geometric-moments} that does not pass through
 \Cref{thm:universal-lagrange-residual} and uses no symmetric-function
 orthogonality.
\item \emph{Extraction of the top coefficient.}  The data
 \eqref{eq:reciprocal-nodes-weights} satisfy \eqref{eq:ring-moment-hypotheses}
 with $p=n$ and $K=q^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}(q;q)_n$, by \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-zero}
 and \eqref{eq:geometric-moment-top}; no deflation is needed, because that row
 already annihilates the constants.  Its nodes are $-q^{-k}=c\,\tilde q^{\,k}$
 with $c=-1$ and $\tilde q=q^{-1}$, so
 \eqref{eq:ring-principal-specialization} gives
 \[
 h_r\bigl(-1,-q^{-1},\ldots,-q^{-n}\bigr)
 =(-1)^r\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q^{-1}}
 =(-1)^rq^{-nr}\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{n}{q},
 \]
 the last step being the reciprocity \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}\,(ii) with
 $k(n-k)=nr$.  Multiplying \eqref{eq:ring-series-filter} by
 $q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}/(q;q)_n$ therefore reproduces
 \eqref{eq:reciprocal-filter-tail} exactly, giving a second proof of
 \Cref{thm:reciprocal-exact-tail} in which no shifted factorial
 $(q^{-n-r};q)_n$ is ever rewritten.
\end{enumerate}
The two instances differ in precisely one structural respect: the first has a
nonzero moment of order $0$ and must be deflated by one extra node, the second
does not.  Everything else --- the vanishing block, the single surviving
constant $K$, and the Gaussian row that multiplies the tail --- is supplied by
\Cref{thm:ring-moment-residual}.
\end{remark}

% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula}, and before \section{Digital cancellation in
% ed.: an arbitrary radix}.  The two blocks that follow (the local reduction identity
% ed.: and the digit expansion) depend on this one.
% ed.: The case $\theta=0$ of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} is the filed
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}, proved in Step~1 of

\section{Cells, a local reduction, and the digit expansion}
\index{Fabius function!value at a non-dyadic point}

Formula \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} evaluates $F$ exactly, but only at dyadic
rationals; formula \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} evaluates it at every point, but
only by quadrature.  This section supplies the missing third description: an exact,
digit-driven expansion valid at every point of $[0,1)$, whose partial sums are finite
rational combinations of the moments of $X$ and whose remainder is itself a value of
$F$, at an argument smaller by a factor $2^{-N}$.  The mechanism is the moment
functional of \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula} carried through with a fractional part
attached.

\begin{definition}[Shifted moment polynomials]\label{def:fabius-shift-polynomial}
\index{Appell sequence!Fabius moments}
For $n\ge0$ and $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-shift-polynomial}
 \mathfrak a_n(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\Expectation\,(X+\theta)^n
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom nj\,\Expectation X^{\,n-j}\,\theta^{\,j},
\end{equation}
a monic polynomial of degree $n$ with
\[
 \mathfrak a_n(0)=\Expectation X^n=n!\,\alpha_n
\]
and exponential generating function
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}\mathfrak a_n(\theta)z^n/n!
 =\EulerE^{\theta z}\mathcal A(z).
\]
\end{definition}

Thus $(\mathfrak a_n)$ is the Appell sequence attached to $\mathcal A$: differentiating
the generating function gives $\mathfrak a_n'=n\,\mathfrak a_{n-1}$, multiplying it by
$\EulerE^{cz}$ gives the binomial translation law, of which
\eqref{eq:raw-moments-from-centered} is the instance $c=1/2$ for the centred variable,
and $X\overset d=1-X$ from \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry} gives the
reflection $\mathfrak a_n(-1-\theta)=(-1)^n\mathfrak a_n(\theta)$, the moment
counterpart of the $k$-independent digit-complementation sign $(-1)^n$ computed at the
end of the proof of \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}.  Only
\eqref{eq:fabius-shift-polynomial} itself is
used below; in this notation the numerator coefficient
\eqref{eq:fabius-numerator-moment} reads
$p_{m,n}=\frac1{n!}\sum_{h<m}\tau_h\,\mathfrak a_n(m-h-1)$.

\begin{lemma}[Spline expectation at an arbitrary argument]\label{lem:fabius-spline-expectation}
\index{spline!truncated-power expectation}
Write $y_+\DefinitionEquals\max(y,0)$.  For every $n\ge1$ and every real $t$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-spline-expectation}
 F\!\left(\frac t{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\sum_{r=0}^{2^n-1}\tau_r\,
   \Expectation\bigl(t-r-X\bigr)_+^{\,n}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the scaled splitting \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split}:
$2^nX=\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_jV_j+X'$ with $c_j=2^{\,n-j}$, with $V_1,\ldots,V_n$ independent
and uniform on $[0,1]$, and with $X'$ independent of them and distributed as $X$.  Since
$(V_1,\ldots,V_n)$ is uniform on the unit cube, conditioning on $X'$ gives
\[
 \Probability\bigl(2^nX\le t\mid X'\bigr)
 =\Probability\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_jV_j\le t-X'\;\Big|\;X'\Bigr)
 =V_{\mathbf c}\bigl(t-X'\bigr),
\]
where $V_{\mathbf c}$ is the weighted cube cut \eqref{eq:weighted-cube-cut}.  For the
integer weights $c_j=2^{\,n-j}$ that cut is \eqref{eq:dyadic-cube-cut}, an identity
valid at every real argument.  Substituting it, averaging over $X'$, and exchanging the
expectation with the finite sum --- legitimate because the sum has $2^n$ terms, each
bounded on the bounded range of $X'$ --- gives \eqref{eq:fabius-spline-expectation},
with $X$ written for $X'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the unrestricted argument buys]\label{rem:spline-expectation-scope}
At an integer $t=m$ with $0\le m\le2^n$ every term with $r\ge m$ of
\eqref{eq:fabius-spline-expectation} vanishes and every term with $r<m$ loses its
positive part; that is exactly the computation performed in Step~1 of the proof of
\Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}, and it produces \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}.
The content of the lemma is that no restriction on $t$ is needed.  At a non-integer $t$
one term keeps its positive part, and \Cref{thm:fabius-dyadic-cell} identifies it.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Exact formula on every dyadic cell]\label{thm:fabius-dyadic-cell}
\index{Fabius function!value at a non-dyadic point}
Let $n\ge1$, let $m$ be an integer with $0\le m\le2^n$, and let $\theta$ be real with
$0\le\theta<1$ and $m+\theta\le2^n$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell}
 F\!\left(\frac{m+\theta}{2^n}\right)
 =\tau_m\,F\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2^n}\right)
  +\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}
   \sum_{h=0}^{m-1}\tau_h\,\mathfrak a_n\bigl(m-h-1+\theta\bigr),
\end{equation}
the sum being empty when $m=0$.  At $\theta=0$ the first term vanishes and
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} is \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \Cref{lem:fabius-spline-expectation} at $t=m+\theta$ and classify the $2^n$
terms, using $0\le X\le1$ and $0\le\theta<1$.

If $r\ge m+1$, then $t-r-X\le m+\theta-(m+1)=\theta-1<0$ and the term vanishes.

If $r\le m-1$, then $t-r-X\ge m+\theta-(m-1)-1=\theta\ge0$, so the positive part is
inactive and
\[
 \Expectation\bigl(m+\theta-r-X\bigr)_+^{\,n}
 =\Expectation\bigl((1-X)+m-r-1+\theta\bigr)^{n}
 =\mathfrak a_n\bigl(m-r-1+\theta\bigr),
\]
because $1-X\overset d=X$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry}.  These terms
contribute the sum in \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell}.

There remains the term $r=m$, which occurs in the range $0\le r\le2^n-1$ precisely when
$m\le2^n-1$.  In that case its contribution is
$\tau_m\,2^{-\binom n2}\Expectation(\theta-X)_+^{\,n}/n!$.  Apply
\Cref{lem:fabius-spline-expectation} once more, now at $t=\theta$: since
$0\le\theta<1$, every index $r\ge1$ gives $\theta-r-X\le\theta-1<0$ and drops out, so
only $r=0$ survives and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-cell-residual-term}
 F\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\,\Expectation\bigl(\theta-X\bigr)_+^{\,n} .
\end{equation}
The $r=m$ term is therefore $\tau_m F(\theta/2^n)$, which is the first summand of
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell}.

If instead $m=2^n$, then $\theta=0$ by the hypothesis $m+\theta\le2^n$; the index $r=m$
is absent from the range of summation, and the first summand of
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} is $\tau_{2^n}F(0)=0$, so the formula again holds.  This
proves \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} in all cases.  Finally, at $\theta=0$ one has
$F(0)=0$ and $\mathfrak a_n(m-h-1)$ in place of $\mathfrak a_n(m-h-1+\theta)$, so
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} becomes
$F(m/2^n)=2^{-\binom n2}p_{m,n}$ by \eqref{eq:fabius-numerator-moment}, which is
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-moment}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Both hypotheses on $\theta$ are needed, and how sharp they are]\label{rem:fabius-cell-sharpness}
The restriction $m+\theta\le2^n$ cannot be dropped: at $n=1$, $m=2$, $\theta=1/4$ the
right-hand side of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} is
$-F(1/8)+\mathfrak a_1(5/4)-\mathfrak a_1(1/4)=1-\frac1{288}=\frac{287}{288}$, whereas
$F(9/8)=1$.  The restriction $\theta<1$ can be relaxed to $\theta\le1$ but no further.
At $\theta=1$ with $m+1\le2^n$ the identity persists, because
$\mathfrak a_n(0)=n!\,\alpha_n$ and \eqref{eq:fabius-half-moment} turn the extra term
$\tau_m\,2^{-\binom n2}\mathfrak a_n(0)/n!$ of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-cell} at the
numerator $m+1$ into $\tau_mF(2^{-n})$.  At $\theta=3/2$ it fails: for $n=2$, $m=1$ the
right-hand side is
$-F(3/8)+\frac14\mathfrak a_2(3/2)=-\frac{73}{288}+\frac{145}{144}=\frac{217}{288}$,
whereas $F(5/8)=\frac{215}{288}$.
% ed.: all three numbers verified in exact rational arithmetic.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D06 ----
% ed.: this block kept only its worked example and description: the
% ed.: digit-expansion theorem it also proved is stated in the fuller
% ed.: Appell form of \Cref{thm:fabius-digit-expansion} below, which the
% ed.: same sibling supplies from another chapter.
% ed.: PLACEMENT: immediately after \Cref{cor:fabius-local-reduction}, closing the new
% ed.: section.  \Cref{rem:fabius-third-description} completes the unlabelled remark
% ed.: that follows \Cref{cor:fabius-fourier-formula}, which lists only two exact
% ed.: descriptions of $F$.

Let $x\in[0,1)$ and let $x=0.b_1b_2b_3\ldots$ in base two be its canonical binary
expansion, the one that is not eventually constant equal to $1$.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-digit-prefix}
 m_j\DefinitionEquals\Floor{2^jx},
 \qquad
 \theta_j\DefinitionEquals2^jx-m_j\in[0,1)
 \qquad(j\ge0),
\end{equation}
so that $m_0=0$ and $\theta_0=x$.  The binary digits of $m_j$ are $b_1\ldots b_j$;
hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-digit-recursions}
 m_j=2m_{j-1}+b_j,
 \qquad
 \tau_{m_j}=(-1)^{b_j}\tau_{m_{j-1}},
 \qquad
 \theta_{j-1}=\frac{b_j+\theta_j}2
 \qquad(j\ge1),
\end{equation}
the middle relation because appending a digit $0$ leaves $\BinaryDigitSum$ unchanged and appending
a digit $1$ raises it by one.


\begin{example}[A non-dyadic argument]\label{ex:fabius-digit-onethird}
Let $x=1/3$, whose binary expansion is $0.\overline{01}$.  Then $b_j=1$ exactly for
even $j$, and $\theta_j=1/3$ for even $j$, $\theta_j=2/3$ for odd $j$, while
$m_1=0$, $m_3=2$, $m_5=10$, with $\tau_{m_1}=1$, $\tau_{m_3}=-1$,
$\tau_{m_5}=1$.  Hence \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell} reads
\[
 F\!\left(\tfrac13\right)
 =\frac{\mathfrak a_2(1/3)}{4}
 -\frac{\mathfrak a_4(1/3)}{2^{6}\cdot4!}
 +\frac{\mathfrak a_6(1/3)}{2^{15}\cdot6!}-\cdots
 =\frac{13}{72}-\frac{2429}{6220800}+\frac{198007}{8427641241600}-\cdots,
\]
whose partial sums are
\[
 S_2=\tfrac{13}{72}=0.180555\ldots,\qquad
 S_4=0.1801650913\ldots,\qquad
 S_6=0.18016511480153\ldots,
\]
against $F(1/3)=0.1801651148014819\ldots$ obtained from
\eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula}.  Three terms already agree with $F(1/3)$ to within
$5.2\cdot10^{-14}$.  The guaranteed bound of \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-residual} at $N=6$
is $2^{-15}/6!=4.2\cdot10^{-8}$; it is far from sharp here because the residual is
$F(1/192)$ and $1/192$ lies well inside the interval $[0,2^{-6}]$ on which $F$ is
extremely flat.
% ed.: partial sums computed in exact rational arithmetic; the comparison value
% ed.: computed from \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} to $25$ digits.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Three exact descriptions of $F$]\label{rem:fabius-third-description}
The remark following \Cref{cor:fabius-fourier-formula} contrasts two exact descriptions
of $F$: the global analytic one \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} and the finite
rational one \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value}, which applies only at dyadic arguments.
Formula \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell} is a third, and it is the only one that is
simultaneously exact, elementary, and valid at every argument.  Its cost structure is
also different from both.  The $j$th term needs the moments
$\Expectation X^0,\ldots,\Expectation X^j$, which \eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence}
produces through $\Expectation X^n=n!\,\alpha_n$ at one rational division each; the
accuracy is governed by the superfactorial
weight $2^{-\binom N2}/N!$ of \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-residual}, which is
$7.8\cdot10^{-21}$ at $N=10$ and $2.6\cdot10^{-76}$ at $N=20$, uniformly in $x$; and
only the indices $j$ with $b_j=1$ contribute a term, so the number of nonzero terms is
the binary weight of the truncated argument and not its length.  By contrast
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} needs a Thue--Morse block of length $m2^k$, and
\eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} needs quadrature of an oscillatory integral.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D07 ----
% ---- merged block Q01 ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:geometric-uniform-fabius, section ``Finite spline
% ed.: approximants'', immediately after \Cref{cor:finite-uniform-convergence}
% ed.: and before \Cref{rem:halfbase-density-normalizations}.  NEW.
% ed.: An incoming draft introduced the midpoint-centred truncation and recorded
% ed.: two things about it: the half-base spline formula, which it reproved by a
% ed.: second inclusion--exclusion argument --- it is \eqref{eq:dyadic-cube-cut}
% ed.: re-centred, and is obtained that way in (i) below, the duplicate proof
% ed.: being dropped --- and the bound $\sup\abs{S_p-F}\le2^{-p}$, obtained by
% ed.: coupling against an ad hoc re-proof of $\norm f_\infty\le2$, which is
% ed.: \Cref{prop:geometric-uniform-density-band}(ii)--(iii) and is cited here.
% ed.: That bound is true but is off by the factor $9\cdot2^{p}$: recentring at
% ed.: the tail midpoint annihilates the first-order term, and the exact rate is
% ed.: $q^{2N-1}/(24(1-q^2))$.  Its hypothesis $N\ge2$ is necessary, by (iv).

The truncation $X_{q,N}$ of \eqref{eq:finite-geometric-uniform-sum} discards a
nonnegative tail, so \Cref{cor:finite-uniform-convergence} is a one-sided
coupling and its rate $q^N$ is the length of the discarded interval.  Adding back
the \emph{mean} of that tail centres the coupling and removes the first-order
term; the resulting rate is second order in $q^N$, and it can be computed
exactly.

\begin{proposition}[Midpoint-centred truncations, and an exact second-order rate]\label{prop:midpoint-truncation}
\index{spline!midpoint-centred}
\index{Fabius function!midpoint spline}
\index{coupling!second-order}
Let $0<q<1$ and $N\ge1$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-truncation-def}
 X_{q,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}\DefinitionEquals X_{q,N}+\tfrac12q^N,
 \qquad
 F_{q,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}(x)\DefinitionEquals\Probability\bigl(X_{q,N}^{\mathrm{mid}}\le x\bigr)
  =F_{q,N}\bigl(x-\tfrac12q^N\bigr).
\end{equation}
At the half base write $S_p\DefinitionEquals F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{1/2,p}$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $p\ge1$ and every real $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-spline}
 S_p(x)=\frac{2^{-\binom p2}}{p!}
  \sum_{r=0}^{2^p-1}\tau_r\bigl(2^px-\tfrac12-r\bigr)_+^{\,p},
 \qquad \tau_r=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(r)};
\end{equation}
\item for every $N\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-rate}
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}\bigl|F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}(x)-F_q(x)\bigr|
 \le\frac{q^{\,2N-1}}{24\bigl(1-q^{2}\bigr)};
\end{equation}
\item if moreover $0<q\le1/2$, then \eqref{eq:midpoint-rate} is an equality, the
extreme value being attained at $x^\ast\DefinitionEquals q(1-q)$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-extremum}
 F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}\bigl(x^\ast\bigr)-F_q\bigl(x^\ast\bigr)
 =-\frac{q^{\,2N-1}}{24\bigl(1-q^{2}\bigr)} .
\end{equation}
At $q=1/2$ this reads
$\sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}\AbsoluteValue{S_p(x)-F(x)}=\tfrac19\,4^{-p}$ for $p\ge2$, attained at
$x=1/4$;
\item the hypothesis $N\ge2$ cannot be dropped: at $q=1/2$ and $N=1$ the variable
$X^{\mathrm{mid}}_{1/2,1}=\tfrac12U_0+\tfrac14$ is uniform on $[1/4,3/4]$, so
$S_1(1/4)=0$ while $F(1/4)=5/72$ by \Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture},
whereas the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:midpoint-rate} would be $1/36<5/72$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{(i).}  By \Cref{rem:dyadic-cube-cut} with $n=p$, the weights $c_j=2^{\,p-j}$
are $2^p$ times the half-base weights $a_{j-1}=2^{-j}$, so
$V_{\mathbf c}(t)=\Probability\bigl(2^pX_{1/2,p}\le t\bigr)=F_{1/2,p}\bigl(2^{-p}t\bigr)$
and \eqref{eq:dyadic-cube-cut} reads
\[
 F_{1/2,p}\bigl(2^{-p}t\bigr)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom p2}}{p!}\sum_{r=0}^{2^p-1}\tau_r\,(t-r)_+^{\,p}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:midpoint-truncation-def}, $S_p(x)=F_{1/2,p}\bigl(x-2^{-p-1}\bigr)$,
which is the above at $t=2^px-\tfrac12$.  This is \eqref{eq:midpoint-spline}.

\emph{An exact error integral.}  Iterating \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-fixed-point}
$N$ times gives $X_q\overset d=X_{q,N}+q^NX_q'$ with $X_q'$ an independent copy of
$X_q$; at $q=1/2$ this is \eqref{eq:fabius-scaled-split} after scaling.  Write
$Y\DefinitionEquals X_{q,N}$, let $g_{q,N}$ be its density
(\Cref{thm:finite-uniform-spline}), and put
\[
 H_q(u)\DefinitionEquals\IndicatorOf{[1/2,\infty)}(u)-F_q(u),
\]
which vanishes for $u<0$ and for $u>1$.  Conditioning on $Y$,
\[
 F_q(x)=\Expectation\Bigl[F_q\bigl(q^{-N}(x-Y)\bigr)\Bigr],
 \qquad
 F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}(x)
 =\Probability\bigl(Y\le x-\tfrac12q^N\bigr)
 =\Expectation\Bigl[\IndicatorOf{[1/2,\infty)}\bigl(q^{-N}(x-Y)\bigr)\Bigr],
\]
so, substituting $u=q^{-N}(x-y)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-error-integral}
 F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}(x)-F_q(x)
 =\Expectation\Bigl[H_q\bigl(q^{-N}(x-Y)\bigr)\Bigr]
 =q^N\int_0^1g_{q,N}\bigl(x-q^Nu\bigr)H_q(u)\Differential u .
\end{equation}

\emph{The two moments of $H_q$.}  Since $\Expectation X_q=\int_0^1(1-F_q)$ and
$\Expectation X_q=1/2$ by \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-H-moment-zero}
 \int_0^1H_q(u)\Differential u=\tfrac12-\int_0^1F_q(u)\Differential u
 =\tfrac12-\bigl(1-\Expectation X_q\bigr)=0 .
\end{equation}
Integrating by parts, $\int_0^1uF_q(u)\Differential u=\tfrac12-\tfrac12\Expectation X_q^2$, whence
\[
 \int_0^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)H_q(u)\Differential u
 =\int_{1/2}^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)\Differential u-\int_0^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)F_q(u)\Differential u
 =\tfrac12\Expectation X_q^2-\tfrac18
 =\tfrac12\Variance(X_q).
\]
By \eqref{eq:q-cumulants-uniform} at $m=1$, with $B_2=1/6$,
$\Variance(X_q)=\kappa_2(Y_q)=(1-q)/\bigl(12(1+q)\bigr)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:midpoint-H-moment-one}
 \int_0^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)H_q(u)\Differential u=\frac{1-q}{24(1+q)} .
\end{equation}
Finally $H_q\le0$ on $[0,1/2)$ and $H_q\ge0$ on $[1/2,1]$, so
$\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)H_q(u)=\bigl|u-\tfrac12\bigr|\,\AbsoluteValue{H_q(u)}$ pointwise and
\eqref{eq:midpoint-H-moment-one} also computes
$\int_0^1\AbsoluteValue{u-\tfrac12}\AbsoluteValue{H_q(u)}\Differential u$.

\emph{The density and its Lipschitz constant.}  For $M\ge1$ write
$X_{q,M}=(1-q)U_0+qX_{q,M-1}$ with the two summands independent and
$X_{q,0}\DefinitionEquals0$.  Conditioning on the second summand, which contributes the
uniform density $1/(1-q)$ on a window of length $1-q$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncated-density-from-cdf}
 g_{q,M}(x)=\frac1{1-q}
 \left[F_{q,M-1}\!\left(\frac xq\right)-F_{q,M-1}\!\left(\frac{x-1+q}q\right)\right],
\end{equation}
the difference of two values of a distribution function; hence
$0\le g_{q,M}\le1/(1-q)$, the finite-$M$ form of
\Cref{prop:geometric-uniform-density-band}(ii).  If $M\ge2$ then $F_{q,M-1}$ has
the bounded density $g_{q,M-1}$ and is therefore Lipschitz with constant
$1/(1-q)$, so \eqref{eq:truncated-density-from-cdf} shows that $g_{q,M}$ is
Lipschitz with constant
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncated-density-lipschitz}
 \operatorname{Lip}(g_{q,M})\le\frac1{q(1-q)^2}
 \qquad(M\ge2).
\end{equation}

\emph{(ii).}  Let $N\ge2$.  By \eqref{eq:midpoint-H-moment-zero} we may subtract
the constant $g_{q,N}\bigl(x-\tfrac12q^N\bigr)$ inside
\eqref{eq:midpoint-error-integral}, so that
\[
 \begin{aligned}
  \bigl|F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}(x)-F_q(x)\bigr|
  &\le q^N\int_0^1\Bigl|g_{q,N}\bigl(x-q^Nu\bigr)
    -g_{q,N}\bigl(x-\tfrac12q^N\bigr)\Bigr|\\[-2pt]
  &\hspace{7em}{}\cdot\AbsoluteValue{H_q(u)}\Differential u\\
  &\le\frac{q^{2N}}{q(1-q)^2}\int_0^1
    \Bigl|u-\tfrac12\Bigr|\AbsoluteValue{H_q(u)}\Differential u .
 \end{aligned}
\]
by \eqref{eq:truncated-density-lipschitz}.  Now insert
\eqref{eq:midpoint-H-moment-one}:
\[
 \frac{q^{2N-1}}{(1-q)^2}\cdot\frac{1-q}{24(1+q)}
 =\frac{q^{2N-1}}{24(1-q^2)} ,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:midpoint-rate}.

\emph{(iii).}  Let $0<q\le1/2$ and $N\ge2$.  We claim that $g_{q,N}$ is affine
with slope exactly $1/\bigl(q(1-q)^2\bigr)$ on
$I\DefinitionEquals[x^\ast-q^N,\,x^\ast]$, where $x^\ast=q(1-q)$.

First, for $M\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncated-density-plateau}
 g_{q,M}(y)=\frac1{1-q}
 \qquad\text{for (almost) every }y\in\bigl[1-q-q^M,\,1-q\bigr].
\end{equation}
For $M=1$ the variable $X_{q,1}=(1-q)U_0$ is uniform on $[0,1-q]$ and
$1-q-q\ge0$ because $q\le1/2$, so the interval lies in the support and the
density is $1/(1-q)$ almost everywhere on it.  For $M\ge2$ use
\eqref{eq:truncated-density-from-cdf}: such a $y$ has $y-1+q\le0$, and
$X_{q,M-1}$ has a density, so $F_{q,M-1}\bigl((y-1+q)/q\bigr)=0$; moreover
$y/q\ge(1-q)/q-q^{M-1}\ge1-q^{M-1}$ because $(1-q)/q\ge1$, while
$X_{q,M-1}\le1-q^{M-1}$, so $F_{q,M-1}(y/q)=1$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:truncated-density-plateau}, and hence $F_{q,M}$ is affine with slope
$1/(1-q)$ on $\bigl[1-q-q^M,1-q\bigr]$.

Now let $x\in I$.  Then $x\le q(1-q)<1-q$, so $x-1+q<0$ and the second term of
\eqref{eq:truncated-density-from-cdf} vanishes, giving
$g_{q,N}(x)=F_{q,N-1}(x/q)/(1-q)$.  As $x$ runs through $I$, the point $x/q$ runs
through $\bigl[1-q-q^{N-1},\,1-q\bigr]$, on which $F_{q,N-1}$ is affine with slope
$1/(1-q)$ by \eqref{eq:truncated-density-plateau} with $M=N-1\ge1$.  Composing
the two affine maps proves the claim.

Write therefore
$g_{q,N}(x^\ast-q^Nu)=g_{q,N}(x^\ast)-q^Nu\big/\bigl(q(1-q)^2\bigr)$ for
$0\le u\le1$ and substitute into \eqref{eq:midpoint-error-integral}.  The
constant term is annihilated by \eqref{eq:midpoint-H-moment-zero}, which also
gives $\int_0^1uH_q=\int_0^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)H_q$, so by
\eqref{eq:midpoint-H-moment-one}
\[
 F^{\mathrm{mid}}_{q,N}(x^\ast)-F_q(x^\ast)
 =-\frac{q^{2N}}{q(1-q)^2}\int_0^1\bigl(u-\tfrac12\bigr)H_q(u)\Differential u
 =-\frac{q^{2N-1}}{24(1-q^2)} ,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:midpoint-extremum}; with (ii) it makes
\eqref{eq:midpoint-rate} an equality.  At $q=1/2$ one has $x^\ast=1/4$ and
$q^{2N-1}/\bigl(24(1-q^2)\bigr)=2^{1-2N}\big/18=\tfrac194^{-N}$.

\emph{(iv).}  The computation is the one displayed in the statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the midpoint buys]\label{rem:midpoint-versus-plain}
Against \eqref{eq:cdf-uniform-rate-explicit} the gain is a full extra factor
$q^{N-1}(1-q)/\bigl(24(1+q)\bigr)$, and at the half base the gain is
$9\cdot2^{N}$: the plain truncation obeys
$\sup_x\AbsoluteValue{F_{1/2,N}-F}=2^{-N}$, while
$\sup_x\AbsoluteValue{S_N-F}=\tfrac194^{-N}$.  The mechanism is visible in
\eqref{eq:midpoint-error-integral}: the shift $\tfrac12q^N$ is exactly the one
that makes the kernel $H_q$ have vanishing mean, after which only its first
moment $\tfrac12\Variance(X_q)$ survives.  The same identity shows why no
further shift helps: the next obstruction is the second moment of $H_q$, which
cannot be removed by a translation.
% ed.: the exact half-base values $\sup_x\abs{S_p-F}=\tfrac194^{-p}$ were
% ed.: confirmed in exact rational arithmetic for $2\le p\le7$ (extremum $-1/144$,
% ed.: $-1/576$, $-1/2304$, $-1/9216$, $-1/36864$, $-1/147456$, always at
% ed.: $x=1/4$), and \eqref{eq:midpoint-rate} was confirmed by Monte Carlo at
% ed.: $q=1/3,1/2,7/10$ and $N=2,3$ with $4\cdot10^{7}$ samples.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D08 ----
% ---- merged block Q02 ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, new section ``An off-grid
% ed.: continuation of the dyadic formula'', inserted after
% ed.: \Cref{prin:row-exactness-stability} and before
% ed.: \Cref{rem:dyadic-algebraic-layer}.  NEW.  This block and
% ed.: \Cref{thm:offgrid-dyadic,thm:offgrid-uniform-rate} are the surviving
% ed.: content of an incoming draft's half-base Toeplitz section; the row
% ed.: identities it also stated (generating polynomial, unit mass, $\ell^1$
% ed.: norm) are \Cref{rem:halfbase-row-reversal} together with
% ed.: \eqref{eq:geometric-row-mass} and \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation}, and
% ed.: are cited rather than restated.

\section{An off-grid continuation of the dyadic formula}

\Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula} evaluates $F$ at the dyadic points
$m/2^n$ with $0\le m\le2^n$, and \Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture} shows that
the hypothesis $m\le2^n$ cannot simply be dropped: at $n=2$, $m=5$ the right-hand
side of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} returns $67/72$ instead of $F(5/4)=1$.
Both restrictions disappear at once if the block length $m2^k$ is replaced by a
cutoff that is \emph{clamped} at the top.  The resulting function is defined for
every real argument, agrees with $F$ on the grid, and is an explicit Gaussian
average of the midpoint splines of \Cref{prop:midpoint-truncation}.

\begin{definition}[The clamped midpoint cutoff]\label{def:clamped-cutoff}
\index{cutoff!clamped midpoint}
For an integer $p\ge1$ and $x\in\RealNumbers$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:clamped-cutoff}
 N_p(x)\DefinitionEquals\min\Bigl(2^p,\ \max\bigl(0,\ \Floor{2^px+\tfrac12}\bigr)\Bigr)
 \in\SetOf{0,1,\ldots,2^p} .
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Cutoff form of the midpoint spline]\label{lem:cutoff-to-spline}
\index{spline!cutoff form}
Let $p\ge1$.  For every real $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cutoff-to-spline}
 \frac1{p!}\sum_{r=0}^{N_p(x)-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-2^px+\tfrac12\bigr)^{\,p}
 =(-1)^p2^{\binom p2}\,S_p(x),
\end{equation}
an empty sum being $0$.  Moreover, if $0\le n\le p$ and $m$ is an integer with
$0\le m\le2^n$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:clamped-cutoff-on-grid}
 N_p\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)=m\,2^{\,p-n}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $t\DefinitionEquals2^px-\tfrac12$.  In \eqref{eq:midpoint-spline} the term of index $r$
is nonzero exactly when $t-r>0$; since $r$ is an integer this says
$r<\Ceiling t$, so the terms that survive are those with
$0\le r<\min\bigl(2^p,\max(0,\Ceiling t)\bigr)$.

We check that $\Ceiling t$ may be replaced by $\Floor{t+1}=\Floor{2^px+\tfrac12}$.
The two integers agree unless $t\in\IntegerNumbers$, in which case
$\Floor{t+1}=\Ceiling t+1$; the single extra index is then $r=t$, whose contribution
to \eqref{eq:cutoff-to-spline} is $\tau_t\,(t-t)^p=0$ because $p\ge1$.  Neither
side of \eqref{eq:cutoff-to-spline} changes, so the summation range in
\eqref{eq:cutoff-to-spline} is exactly the active range of
\eqref{eq:midpoint-spline}.
% ed.: this is the one point where the floor convention of
% ed.: \eqref{eq:clamped-cutoff} needs an argument; an incoming draft asserted it
% ed.: without comment.

Consequently, using $(t-r)^p=(-1)^p(r-t)^p$ and $r-t=r-2^px+\tfrac12$,
\[
 S_p(x)=\frac{2^{-\binom p2}}{p!}\sum_{r=0}^{2^p-1}\tau_r(t-r)_+^{\,p}
 =\frac{(-1)^p2^{-\binom p2}}{p!}
  \sum_{r=0}^{N_p(x)-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-2^px+\tfrac12\bigr)^{\,p},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:cutoff-to-spline} after multiplying by
$(-1)^p2^{\binom p2}$.

For \eqref{eq:clamped-cutoff-on-grid}, $2^p\cdot m/2^n=m2^{\,p-n}$ is a
nonnegative integer, so $\Floor{m2^{\,p-n}+\tfrac12}=m2^{\,p-n}$; and
$0\le m2^{\,p-n}\le2^n2^{\,p-n}=2^p$, so neither clamp is active.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The clamp repairs the hypothesis $m\le2^n$]\label{rem:clamp-repairs-hypothesis}
The upper clamp in \eqref{eq:clamped-cutoff} is not cosmetic.  Without it the
right-hand side of \eqref{eq:cutoff-to-spline} would continue to accumulate terms
for $x>1$ and would describe a signed global extension rather than a bounded
distribution function; the failure recorded in
\Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture} at $n=2$, $m=5$ is precisely the case
$N_{n+k}(x)$ replaced by an unclamped $m2^k>2^{\,n+k}$.  The lower clamp plays
the symmetric role for $x<0$.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D09 ----
% ---- merged block Q03 ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, section ``An off-grid
% ed.: continuation of the dyadic formula'', immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{rem:clamp-repairs-hypothesis}.  NEW.  An incoming draft stated this
% ed.: theorem together with the row-generating polynomial, the unit row mass and
% ed.: the $\ell^1$ norm of $\omega_{n,\cdot}$; those three are
% ed.: \Cref{rem:halfbase-row-reversal} with \eqref{eq:geometric-row-mass},
% ed.: \eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-signs} and
% ed.: \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation}, and are cited in (ii) instead of being
% ed.: proved a second time.  Part (iv) is added here; the endpoint exponent is
% ed.: $2n+1$ at both ends, the binding constraint being the longest spline
% ed.: $S_{2n}$ and not the shortest.

\begin{theorem}[Off-grid continuation of the dyadic formula]\label{thm:offgrid-dyadic}
\index{Fabius function!off-grid continuation}
\index{Toeplitz theorem!bounded-variation rows}
For $n\ge1$ define, for every $x\in\RealNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-dyadic-def}
 D_n(x)\DefinitionEquals\frac1{2^{\,n^2}\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{N_{n+k}(x)-1}
 \tau_r\bigl(r-2^{\,n+k}x+\tfrac12\bigr)^{\,n+k},
\end{equation}
with $N_p$ as in \eqref{eq:clamped-cutoff}, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-weights}
 \omega_{n,j}\DefinitionEquals\frac{(-1)^j2^{-\binom{j+1}{2}}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{1/2}
 \qquad(0\le j\le n).
\end{equation}
Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $x\in\RealNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-decomposition}
 D_n(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}\,S_{2n-j}(x);
\end{equation}
\item $\omega_{n,j}=W_{n,j}=\lambda^{(1/2)}_{n,\,n-j}$ in the notation of
\Cref{rem:halfbase-row-reversal}; in particular
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}=1,
 \qquad \Sign\omega_{n,j}=(-1)^j,
\]
and
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}z^j
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{z/2}{1/2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}},
 \qquad
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{\omega_{n,j}}
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}};
\]
\item $D_n$ interpolates $F$ on the grid of resolution $2^{-n}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-interpolation}
 D_n\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)=F\!\left(\frac m{2^n}\right)
 \qquad(m\in\IntegerNumbers,\ 0\le m\le2^n);
\end{equation}
\item $D_n$ is a continuous piecewise polynomial of degree at most $2n$, its
knots are the odd multiples of $2^{-2n-1}$, it satisfies
$D_n(1-x)=1-D_n(x)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-endpoints}
 D_n(x)=0\ \ (x\le2^{-2n-1}),
 \qquad
 D_n(x)=1\ \ (x\ge1-2^{-2n-1}).
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{(i).}  Apply \Cref{lem:cutoff-to-spline} with $p=n+k$: the inner sum of
\eqref{eq:offgrid-dyadic-def}, divided by $(n+k)!$, equals
$(-1)^{n+k}2^{\binom{n+k}{2}}S_{n+k}(x)$.  Hence
\[
 D_n(x)=\frac1{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+k}
 2^{\binom{n+k}{2}-n^2-2\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}\,S_{n+k}(x),
\]
since $4^{\binom k2}=2^{2\binom k2}$.  Substitute $j=n-k$, so that $n+k=2n-j$.
The sign is $(-1)^{2n-j}=(-1)^j$; the Gaussian coefficient is unchanged because
$\GaussianBinomial{n}{n-j}{1/2}=\GaussianBinomial{n}{j}{1/2}$, by the symmetry visible in
\eqref{eq:halfbase-gaussian}; and the exponent of $2$ becomes
\begin{align*}
 \binom{2n-j}{2}-2\binom{n-j}{2}-n^2
 &=\tfrac12(2n-j)(2n-j-1)\\
 &\qquad -(n-j)(n-j-1)-n^2\\
 &=-\tfrac12 j(j+1)
 =-\binom{j+1}{2},
\end{align*}
an identity in $n$ and $j$ verified by expanding both sides.  This is exactly
\eqref{eq:offgrid-weights}, and \eqref{eq:offgrid-decomposition} follows.

\emph{(ii).}  The displayed $\omega_{n,j}$ is the row $W_{n,j}$ of
\Cref{rem:halfbase-row-reversal}, where it is identified with
$\lambda^{(1/2)}_{n,n-j}$ and its generating polynomial is computed.  Unit mass
is \eqref{eq:geometric-row-mass}, the sign pattern is
\eqref{eq:geometric-lagrange-signs} read in reversed coordinates, and the
$\ell^1$ norm is \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation}, all at $q=1/2$.

\emph{(iii).}  Let $0\le m\le2^n$.  By \eqref{eq:clamped-cutoff-on-grid} with
$p=n+k\ge n$ we have $N_{n+k}(m/2^n)=m2^k$, while
$2^{\,n+k}\cdot m/2^n=m2^k$; hence \eqref{eq:offgrid-dyadic-def} becomes
\[
 \frac1{2^{\,n^2}\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{m2^k-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-m2^k+\tfrac12\bigr)^{\,n+k},
\]
which is the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} at
$\theta=\tfrac12$.  By \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula} its value is
$F(m/2^n)$.

\emph{(iv).}  Each $S_p$ is, by \eqref{eq:midpoint-spline}, a continuous
piecewise polynomial of degree $p$ whose knots are the numbers
$(2r+1)/2^{\,p+1}$; the degrees occurring in
\eqref{eq:offgrid-decomposition} are $n,\ldots,2n$ and the finest knot set is
that of $S_{2n}$.  The variable $X^{\mathrm{mid}}_{1/2,p}$ of
\eqref{eq:midpoint-truncation-def} is symmetric about $\tfrac12$, because
$X_{1/2,p}$ is symmetric about its mean $(1-2^{-p})/2$ and
$(1-2^{-p})/2+2^{-p-1}=\tfrac12$; as it has no atoms, $S_p(1-x)=1-S_p(x)$, and
the unit row mass of (ii) transfers this to $D_n$.  Finally
$X^{\mathrm{mid}}_{1/2,p}$ takes values in $[2^{-p-1},1-2^{-p-1}]$, so $S_p=0$ on
$(-\infty,2^{-p-1}]$ and $S_p=1$ on $[1-2^{-p-1},\infty)$; both hold
simultaneously for all $p\in\SetOf{n,\ldots,2n}$ exactly on the two rays of
\eqref{eq:offgrid-endpoints}, the binding index being $p=2n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Which part of the dyadic formula is being continued]\label{rem:offgrid-architecture}
The separation of \Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture} survives the
continuation: in \eqref{eq:offgrid-dyadic-def} the half-base Gaussian row
$\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}\big/4^{\binom k2}$ still depends only on the resolution
$2^{-n}$, and the argument $x$ enters only through the block length $N_{n+k}(x)$
and the common shift.  What changes is that the block length is now a
step function of a real variable instead of an integer parameter, and
\eqref{eq:offgrid-decomposition} identifies the resulting object as a
\emph{fixed} Gaussian average of box splines --- one Lagrange row of
\Cref{chap:geometric-filters} applied to the family
$S_{2n},S_{2n-1},\ldots,S_n$.  The choice $\theta=\tfrac12$ of the free shift in
\eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} is what forces the midpoint centring: no other
value of $\theta$ produces a combination of distribution functions.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D10 ----
% ---- merged block Q04 ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, section ``An off-grid
% ed.: continuation of the dyadic formula'', immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{rem:offgrid-architecture}.  NEW.  An incoming draft stated this as
% ed.: $\sup\abs{D_n-F}\le V_n2^{-n}$ with $V_n$ the $\ell^1$ row norm.  That is
% ed.: true but not of the right order: it bounds every $\sup\abs{S_{2n-j}-F}$ by
% ed.: $2^{-(2n-j)}$ and then by $2^{-n}$.  With the sharp spline rate of
% ed.: \Cref{prop:midpoint-truncation}(iii), and keeping the dependence on $j$,
% ed.: the rate is $16^{-n}$.  The improved bound requires $n\ge2$; see
% ed.: \Cref{rem:offgrid-first-row} for the exact failure at $n=1$.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform half-base Toeplitz limit, with the sharp rate]\label{thm:offgrid-uniform-rate}
\index{Toeplitz theorem!bounded-variation rows}
\index{Fabius function!uniform approximation}
For every $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-uniform-rate}
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}\AbsoluteValue{D_n(x)-F(x)}
 \le\frac1{9\cdot16^{\,n}}\,
   \frac{\QPochhammer{-2}{1/2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 <\frac{2\mathcal L(1/2)}{3}\,16^{-n}
 <6\cdot16^{-n},
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal L(1/2)=8.2559879357782500655\ldots$ as in
\eqref{eq:halfbase-row-variation-bound}.  In particular $D_n\to F$ uniformly on
the whole real line, and the convergence is geometric of ratio $16^{-1}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:offgrid-dyadic}(i) and the unit row mass of
\Cref{thm:offgrid-dyadic}(ii),
\[
 D_n(x)-F(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}\bigl(S_{2n-j}(x)-F(x)\bigr)
 \qquad(x\in\RealNumbers).
\]
For $0\le j\le n$ one has $2n-j\ge n\ge2$, so
\Cref{prop:midpoint-truncation}(iii) at $q=1/2$ applies to every summand and
gives $\sup_x\AbsoluteValue{S_{2n-j}-F}=\tfrac194^{-(2n-j)}$.  Hence
\[
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}\AbsoluteValue{D_n(x)-F(x)}
 \le\frac19\sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{\omega_{n,j}}4^{\,j-2n}
 =\frac{16^{-n}}9\sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{\omega_{n,j}}4^{\,j}.
\]
By \Cref{thm:offgrid-dyadic}(ii) the signs alternate,
$\AbsoluteValue{\omega_{n,j}}=(-1)^j\omega_{n,j}$, so the last sum is the value at $z=-4$
of the row polynomial of \Cref{rem:halfbase-row-reversal}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:offgrid-weighted-row-norm}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}\AbsoluteValue{\omega_{n,j}}4^{\,j}
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\omega_{n,j}(-4)^j
 =\frac{\QPochhammer{-2}{1/2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}} .
\end{equation}
This proves the first inequality in \eqref{eq:offgrid-uniform-rate}.

For the second, split off the first two factors of the numerator: for $n\ge2$,
\[
 \QPochhammer{-2}{1/2}{n}=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1+2^{\,1-i}\bigr)
 =3\cdot2\cdot\prod_{h=1}^{n-2}\bigl(1+2^{-h}\bigr)
 =6\,\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{n-2}.
\]
Both infinite products in \eqref{eq:geometric-lebesgue-constant} converge at
$q=1/2$, by \Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation}; since $\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{m}$
increases in $m$ to $\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{\infty}$ and $\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{m}$ decreases
in $m$ to $\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{\infty}>0$, we get
\[
 \frac{\QPochhammer{-2}{1/2}{n}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 =6\,\frac{\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{n-2}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{n}}
 <6\,\frac{\QPochhammer{-1/2}{1/2}{\infty}}{\QPochhammer{1/2}{1/2}{\infty}}
 =6\,\mathcal L(1/2).
\]
Dividing by $9$ gives $\tfrac23\mathcal L(1/2)=5.5039919571\ldots<6$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The first row, and what the abstract theorem gives]\label{rem:offgrid-first-row}
The hypothesis $n\ge2$ in \Cref{thm:offgrid-uniform-rate} is necessary.  For
$n=1$ the decomposition \eqref{eq:offgrid-decomposition} reads
$D_1=2S_2-S_1$, and $S_1$ is outside the range of
\Cref{prop:midpoint-truncation}(iii); indeed $S_1(1/4)=0$ and $S_2(1/4)=1/16$,
so $D_1(1/4)=1/8$, whereas $F(1/4)=5/72$ by
\Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture}.  Thus
\[
 D_1(1/4)-F(1/4)=\frac18-\frac5{72}=\frac1{18},
\]
against the value $6/144=1/24$ that the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:offgrid-uniform-rate} would take at $n=1$.  Replacing the term $j=1$
by the exact bound $\sup_x\AbsoluteValue{S_1-F}=5/72$ restores a valid estimate
$\AbsoluteValue{D_1-F}\le1/12$.

It is worth separating what is and is not an instance of the abstract machinery.
For any exponents $r(n,j)\ge n$, the uniform convergence
\[
 \sum_j\omega_{n,j}F_{1/2,r(n,j)}\longrightarrow F
\]
is \Cref{cor:gaussian-row-spline-average} at $q=1/2$, itself an
application of \Cref{thm:tail-row-toeplitz} with the two hypotheses supplied by
\Cref{thm:geometric-row-variation}; that argument gives no rate beyond the rate
of its input, and applied to \eqref{eq:fabius-truncation-rate} it yields only
$O(2^{-n})$.  What \Cref{thm:offgrid-uniform-rate} adds is, first, that the
average being estimated is the concrete off-grid formula
\eqref{eq:offgrid-dyadic-def} --- an object that exists only because of the
clamped cutoff --- and second, that feeding the sharp midpoint rate
\eqref{eq:midpoint-extremum} into the same sum improves the exponent from
$2^{-n}$ to $16^{-n}$.  The improvement is entirely due to the input: the row
is unchanged, and \eqref{eq:offgrid-weighted-row-norm} is the same endpoint
evaluation as \eqref{eq:geometric-row-variation}, taken at $z=-4$ instead of
$z=-1$.
% ed.: exact rational computation on the grid $2^{-10}$ gives
% ed.: $\sup_x\abs{D_n-F}=1/18$, $3.3396\cdot10^{-4}$ and $1.5006\cdot10^{-6}$ for
% ed.: $n=1,2,3$, attained at $x=1/4$, $101/1024$ and $1/16$; the true decay is
% ed.: faster still than $16^{-n}$, so \eqref{eq:offgrid-uniform-rate} is not
% ed.: claimed to be sharp.
\end{remark}


% ---- merged block D11 ----
% ---- merged block ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, immediately after
% ed.: \Cref{rem:fabius-dyadic-architecture} (monograph line 8113), just before the
% ed.: line "% ---- merged block M11 ----" that opens \Cref{rem:dyadic-shift-common};
% ed.: the replacement of that remark cites the theorem below.
% ed.: NEW.  The monograph so far evaluates the transfer functional on exactly two
% ed.: shift patterns: the common shift of \eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula} and
% ed.: the shift $2^k+\theta$ of \eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}.  Both are
% ed.: members of the single geometric family below, which is what
% ed.: \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} yields when its second series is
% ed.: taken to be $\e^{\theta u}\mathcal A(u)$ instead of $\mathcal A(u)$ --- the
% ed.: decoupled case $U\ne V$ that the editorial note at merged block M08 records
% ed.: as kept deliberately, but that no result until now instantiated.
% ed.: The two incoming statements (a centred and a raw display) are one identity
% ed.: in the two coordinate systems of \Cref{rem:psi-two-coordinates}, exchanged
% ed.: by $\theta\mapsto-1-\theta$; they are stated here once, as one theorem with
% ed.: two displays.

Both shift patterns evaluated so far --- the common shift of
\eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula} and the shift $2^k+\theta$ of
\eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse} --- belong to a single two-parameter
family, on which the weighted sum is not constant at all, but is a polynomial:
the Appell polynomial attached to the Fabius law.  The mechanism is the
decoupling $U\ne V$ permitted by \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer}.

\begin{definition}[Fabius Appell polynomials]\label{def:fabius-appell}
\index{Appell polynomial!Fabius moments}
For $n\ge0$ and $\theta\in\ComplexNumbers$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-appell-def}
 A_n(\theta)\DefinitionEquals\Expectation\,(X+\theta)^n
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom nj\,\Expectation X^{\,n-j}\,\theta^{\,j},
\end{equation}
a monic polynomial of degree $n$ in $\theta$ with
$A_n(0)=\Expectation X^n=n!\,\alpha_n$.  Since $0\le X\le1$, the exchange of
expectation and summation in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-appell-egf}
 \sum_{n\ge0}A_n(\theta)\frac{z^n}{n!}
 =\Expectation\,\EulerE^{z(X+\theta)}
 =\EulerE^{\theta z}\,\mathcal A(z)
\end{equation}
is legitimate for every $z\in\ComplexNumbers$, by \eqref{eq:mgf-product} at $q=1/2$.  The
roman $A_n$ is not the calligraphic $\mathcal A$ of
\Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}: the latter is one entire function of $z$, the
former the coefficient sequence of its exponential shift, and
$A_n(0)/n!=\alpha_n$ recovers it.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Appell and reflection laws]\label{prop:fabius-appell-laws}
For $n\ge1$ and $\theta,c\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 A_n'(\theta)&=n\,A_{n-1}(\theta),
 \qquad
 A_n(\theta+c)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom njA_{n-j}(\theta)\,c^{\,j},
 \label{eq:fabius-appell-derivative}\\
 A_n(-1-\theta)&=(-1)^nA_n(\theta).
 \label{eq:fabius-appell-reflection}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiating \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-egf} in $\theta$ multiplies it by $z$,
which shifts the coefficient index by one; multiplying it by $\EulerE^{cz}$ and
expanding the product of the two exponential series gives the translation law.
For the reflection, \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry} at $q=1/2$ gives
$1-X\overset d=X$, whence
\[
 A_n(-1-\theta)=\Expectation\,(X-1-\theta)^n
 =(-1)^n\Expectation\,(1-X+\theta)^n
 =(-1)^n\Expectation\,(X+\theta)^n. \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Geometric block shifts and the Appell polynomials]\label{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift}
\index{Appell polynomial!Fabius moments}
\index{translation invariance!of the dyadic formula}
\index{coefficient transfer}
For every $n\ge0$ and all $\theta,\eta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-appell-geometric}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r
 \bigl(r-2^k+\eta-2^k\theta\bigr)^{\,n+k}
 =\mathfrak m_n\,\frac{A_n(\theta)}{n!} ;
\end{equation}
the left-hand side depends on $\theta$, but not on $\eta$.  Equivalently, in the
centred normalization $\eta=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-appell-centred}
 \boxed{\;
 \frac{A_n(\theta)}{n!}
 =\frac{2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}}{\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\bigl(r-2^k(1+\theta)\bigr)^{\,n+k},\;}
\end{equation}
and, in the raw normalization, for all $\theta,\eta\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-appell-raw}
 \boxed{\;
 \frac{A_n(\theta)}{n!}
 =\frac{(-1)^n2^{-\binom{n+1}{2}}}{\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_n}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\bigl(r+\eta+2^k\theta\bigr)^{\,n+k}.\;}
\end{equation}
The specialization $\theta=0$ of \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-geometric} is
$\mathcal N_n(\eta)=\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$, that is
\eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula}; the specialization $\theta=-1$ is
\eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}, with $\eta$ in the role of the dummy
parameter there.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer} at $q=1/2$, where $q^{-k}=2^k$,
with
\[
 U(z)=\mathcal A(-z),\qquad V(u)=\EulerE^{\theta u}\mathcal A(u).
\]
The normalization $u_0=1$ holds because $\mathcal A(0)=1$
(\Cref{lem:fabius-moment-dilation}), and $v_n=A_n(\theta)/n!$ by
\eqref{eq:fabius-appell-egf}.  The unique family satisfying the bridge relation
\eqref{eq:general-bridge} is
\[
 \Psi^{\theta}_k(z)\DefinitionEquals\frac{\EulerE^{-z}\,V(-2^kz)}{\mathcal A(-z)}
 =\EulerE^{-2^k\theta z}\,\frac{\EulerE^{-z}\mathcal A(-2^kz)}{\mathcal A(-z)}
 =\EulerE^{-2^k\theta z}\,\Psi_k(z),
\]
the last step by the dilation bridge \eqref{eq:TM-A-bridge}.

Multiply the centred Thue--Morse identity \eqref{eq:thue-morse-normalized} by
$\EulerE^{(\eta-2^k\theta)z}$:
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r\,\EulerE^{(r-2^k+\eta-2^k\theta)z}
 =(-1)^k2^{\binom k2}z^k\,\EulerE^{\eta z}\Psi^{\theta}_k(z).
\]
The left-hand side is the exponential generating function
$\sum_{d\ge0}T_{k,d}(\eta-2^k\theta)z^d/d!$ of the centred power sums
\eqref{eq:thue-morse-power-sums}, so comparing the coefficient of $z^{n+k}$
gives
\[
 [z^n]\bigl(\EulerE^{\eta z}\Psi^{\theta}_k(z)\bigr)
 =\frac{(-1)^k}{2^{\binom k2}(n+k)!}\,
  T_{k,n+k}\bigl(\eta-2^k\theta\bigr).
\]
By \Cref{cor:transfer-translation} with $\alpha=\eta$, together with
\eqref{eq:transfer-top} and the Mersenne evaluation
\eqref{eq:halfbase-factorial},
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}W_{n,k}(1/2)\,[z^n]\bigl(\EulerE^{\eta z}\Psi^{\theta}_k\bigr)
 =2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n\frac{A_n(\theta)}{n!}
 =\mathfrak m_n\,\frac{A_n(\theta)}{n!},
\]
and the same corollary states that this value does not depend on $\eta$.
Inserting
\[
 W_{n,k}(1/2)=(-1)^k2^{-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{1/2}
\]
makes the two signs cancel and combines the two triangular powers of two into
$4^{-\binom k2}$; that
is \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-geometric}.

Putting $\eta=0$, writing $r-2^k-2^k\theta=r-2^k(1+\theta)$ and dividing by
$\mathfrak m_n=2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\left(\tfrac12;\tfrac12\right)_n$ gives
\eqref{eq:fabius-appell-centred}.  For \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-raw}, replace
$\theta$ by $-1-\theta$ in \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-geometric}: the inner argument
becomes $r-2^k+\eta+2^k(1+\theta)=r+\eta+2^k\theta$, while the right-hand side
becomes $\mathfrak m_nA_n(-1-\theta)/n!=(-1)^n\mathfrak m_nA_n(\theta)/n!$ by
\eqref{eq:fabius-appell-reflection}.

Finally, $\theta=0$ in \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-geometric} is
\eqref{eq:fabius-centered-formula}, because $A_n(0)=n!\alpha_n$ and the inner
argument is then $r-2^k+\eta$; and $\theta=-1$ turns the inner argument into
$r+\eta$ and the right-hand side into $(-1)^n\mathfrak m_n\alpha_n$, which is
\eqref{eq:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why $\theta$ is a variable and $\eta$ a dummy]
\Cref{cor:transfer-translation} says that a shift common to all $n+1$ blocks is
invisible; \Cref{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift} says that a shift scaled by
$2^k$ in the $k$th block is visible, and is seen exactly once, in the top
coefficient of $\EulerE^{\theta u}\mathcal A(u)$.  The distinction is precisely the
decoupling $U\ne V$ of \Cref{thm:gaussian-coefficient-transfer}: the
self-referential instance $V=\mathcal A$ attached to $U(z)=\mathcal A(-z)$ can
only ever return $\alpha_n$, whereas the factor $\EulerE^{\theta u}$ inserted into $V$
alone survives the double cancellation of \Cref{rem:double-cancellation}.  The
two displays \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-centred} and \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-raw}
are one identity in the two coordinate systems of
\Cref{rem:psi-two-coordinates}, exchanged by the digit complementation carried
out in the last paragraph of the proof of \Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[The case $n=2$]\label{ex:fabius-appell-small}
Here $\mathfrak m_2=3$ and the half-base Gaussian row is $1,\tfrac32,1$.  At
$\eta=0$ the three inner sums of \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-centred} are
\[
 (1+\theta)^2,\qquad
 -8(1+\theta)^3+(1+2\theta)^3,\qquad
 c^4-(1-c)^4-(2-c)^4+(3-c)^4\ \ \text{with } c=4(1+\theta),
\]
that is $\theta^2+2\theta+1$, $-12\theta^2-18\theta-7$ and
$384\theta^2+480\theta+160$.  Weighted by $1/2!$, $\tfrac32/3!$ and
$1/(4\cdot4!)$ they sum to
\[
 \tfrac32\theta^2+\tfrac32\theta+\tfrac5{12}
 =3\left(\frac{\theta^2}2+\frac\theta2+\frac5{36}\right)
 =\mathfrak m_2\,\frac{A_2(\theta)}{2!},
\]
as \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-geometric} asserts.  At $\theta=1$ the inner sums are
the integers $4,-37,1024$, the terms are $2,-\tfrac{37}4,\tfrac{32}3$, the total
is $41/12$, and $A_2(1)/2!=41/36=\tfrac12+\tfrac12+\tfrac5{36}$.  At $\theta=0$
they are $1,-7,160$ with total $5/12=3\alpha_2$, which is
\Cref{ex:dyadic-formula-small} once more.
% ed.: all displayed polynomials and integers recomputed in exact arithmetic; the
% ed.: symbolic identity was checked for every $n\le7$ and, at rational $\theta$
% ed.: and $\eta$, for every $n\le8$.
\end{example}


% ---- merged block D13 ----
% ---- merged block ----
% ed.: PLACEMENT: chap:dyadic-gaussian-thue-morse, a new section inserted after
% ed.: the section "A Fourier formula for the Fabius function" and immediately
% ed.: before \section{Digital cancellation in an arbitrary radix} (monograph
% ed.: line 8419).
% ed.: NEW.  Until now the monograph evaluates $F$ at exactly two kinds of point:
% ed.: at dyadic rationals in closed finite form (\Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula})
% ed.: and at an arbitrary $x$ analytically, by the sinc integral
% ed.: \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula}, the two being framed as complementary in
% ed.: the remark after that corollary.  The expansion below is a third route,
% ed.: finite and rational term by term, driven by the binary digits of $x$.  It
% ed.: uses \Cref{thm:fabius-appell-geometric-shift} and must follow it.

\section{A digit-sparse expansion at an arbitrary argument}
\index{Fabius function!digit-sparse expansion}
\index{binary expansion!of the argument of $F$}

The dyadic formula \eqref{eq:fabius-dyadic-value} is exact but confined to the
grid; the sinc integral \eqref{eq:fabius-fourier-formula} reaches every $x$ but
is an integral of an infinite product.  Between them lies a third
representation: an exact series whose $j$th term is a finite rational expression
attached to the $j$th binary digit of $x$, which terminates on the grid and
converges superexponentially off it.

The unrestricted spline identity \eqref{eq:fabius-spline-expectation} already
gives this representation for every real $t$, and therefore in particular on
$0\le t\le2^n$.  The binary expansion below uses that stronger canonical
lemma directly.

\begin{corollary}[Local reduction identity]\label{cor:fabius-local-reduction}
For every $n\ge1$ and every $\theta\in[0,1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-local-reduction}
 F\!\left(\frac{1+\theta}{2^n}\right)+F\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\,A_n(\theta).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Both $t=\theta$ and $t=1+\theta$ lie in $[0,2]\subseteq[0,2^n]$, so
\Cref{lem:fabius-spline-expectation} applies to each.  Since $0\le X\le1$, a term with
$r\ge1$ in the sum for $t=\theta$ has $\theta-r-X\le\theta-1<0$ and drops out,
leaving
\[
 F\!\left(\frac{\theta}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\,\Expectation(\theta-X)_+^{\,n}.
\]
In the sum for $t=1+\theta$ a term with $r\ge2$ has $1+\theta-r-X\le\theta-1<0$
and drops out; the term $r=0$ has $1+\theta-X\ge\theta\ge0$, so its positive part
is the plain power, and $1-X\overset d=X$ by
\eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-mean-symmetry} turns
$\Expectation(1+\theta-X)^n$ into $\Expectation(X+\theta)^n=A_n(\theta)$; the term
$r=1$ carries $\tau_1=-1$ and contributes $-\Expectation(\theta-X)_+^{\,n}$.  Hence
\[
 F\!\left(\frac{1+\theta}{2^n}\right)
 =\frac{2^{-\binom n2}}{n!}\Bigl(A_n(\theta)-\Expectation(\theta-X)_+^{\,n}\Bigr),
\]
and adding the two displays proves \eqref{eq:fabius-local-reduction}.
\end{proof}

Let $x\in[0,1)$ and let $x=\sum_{j\ge1}b_j2^{-j}$, $b_j\in\SetOf{0,1}$, be its
canonical binary expansion, the one that is not eventually constant equal to
$1$.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-prefix-tail}
 m_j\DefinitionEquals\Floor{2^jx},
 \qquad
 \theta_j\DefinitionEquals2^jx-m_j\in[0,1),
 \qquad
 m_0=0,\quad\theta_0=x,
\end{equation}
the binary \emph{prefix} and \emph{tail} of $x$ at depth $j$; then
$m_j=2m_{j-1}+b_j$ and $\theta_{j-1}=(b_j+\theta_j)/2$, and the floor in
\eqref{eq:fabius-prefix-tail} selects the canonical expansion automatically.

\begin{theorem}[Digit-sparse Appell expansion]\label{thm:fabius-digit-expansion}
\index{Fabius function!digit-sparse expansion}
For every $x\in[0,1)$, with the notation \eqref{eq:fabius-prefix-tail},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-digit-appell}
 \boxed{\;
 F(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
 b_j\,\tau_{m_{j-1}}\,\frac{2^{-\binom j2}}{j!}\,A_j(\theta_j).\;}
\end{equation}
Writing $S_N(x)$ for the sum of the first $N$ terms,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-digit-residual}
 F(x)-S_N(x)=\tau_{m_N}\,F\!\left(\frac{\theta_N}{2^N}\right),
 \qquad
 \AbsoluteValue{F(x)-S_N(x)}\le\frac{2^{-\binom N2}}{N!}\,.
\end{equation}
The series therefore converges absolutely and uniformly on $[0,1)$, and at a
dyadic rational $m/2^n$, taken in its canonical terminating expansion, it
terminates after $n$ terms.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $R_N\DefinitionEquals\tau_{m_N}F(\theta_N/2^N)$, so that $R_0=\tau_0F(x)=F(x)$.  Fix
$j\ge1$.  If $b_j=0$ then $m_j=2m_{j-1}$, and doubling shifts every binary digit
without creating or destroying one, so $\tau_{m_j}=\tau_{m_{j-1}}$; moreover
$\theta_{j-1}=\theta_j/2$, hence
$\theta_{j-1}/2^{\,j-1}=\theta_j/2^{\,j}$ and $R_{j-1}=R_j$, which is also the
$j$th term of \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell}, namely $0$.

If $b_j=1$ then $m_j=2m_{j-1}+1$, so $\BinaryDigitSum(m_j)=\BinaryDigitSum(m_{j-1})+1$ and
$\tau_{m_j}=-\tau_{m_{j-1}}$, while
$\theta_{j-1}/2^{\,j-1}=(1+\theta_j)/2^{\,j}$.  Therefore
\[
 R_{j-1}-R_j
 =\tau_{m_{j-1}}\left[
   F\!\left(\frac{1+\theta_j}{2^{\,j}}\right)
  +F\!\left(\frac{\theta_j}{2^{\,j}}\right)\right]
 =\tau_{m_{j-1}}\,\frac{2^{-\binom j2}}{j!}\,A_j(\theta_j)
\]
by \Cref{cor:fabius-local-reduction}, which is again the $j$th term.  Summing
over $j=1,\ldots,N$ telescopes to $R_0-R_N=S_N(x)$, which is the first half of
\eqref{eq:fabius-digit-residual}.

For the bound, $0\le\theta_N<1$ and $F$ is nondecreasing, so
$F(\theta_N/2^N)\le F(2^{-N})=2^{-\binom N2}\alpha_N$ by
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}, and $\alpha_N=\Expectation X^N/N!\le1/N!$ because
$0\le X\le1$.  This bound is independent of $x$ and tends to $0$, so
$S_N\to F$ uniformly on $[0,1)$ and \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell} holds.  The
convergence is absolute because $0\le X+\theta_j<2$ gives
$\AbsoluteValue{A_j(\theta_j)}\le2^j$, so the terms are dominated by the summable sequence
$2^{\,j-\binom j2}/j!$.

Finally, if $x=m/2^n$ then $2^jx\in\IntegerNumbers$ for every $j\ge n$, so $b_j=0$ and
$\theta_j=0$ there; hence $R_N=\tau_{m_N}F(0)=0$ for $N\ge n$ and
$F(x)=S_N(x)=S_n(x)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Digit-sparse half-base $q$-series]\label{cor:fabius-digit-qseries}
\index{$q$-binomial coefficient!base one half}
For every $x\in[0,1)$, with the notation \eqref{eq:fabius-prefix-tail},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fabius-digit-qseries}
 \boxed{\;
 F(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
 \frac{b_j\,\tau_{m_{j-1}}}
      {2^{\,j^2}\left(\frac12;\frac12\right)_j}
 \sum_{k=0}^{j}\frac{\GaussianBinomial{j}{k}{1/2}}{4^{\binom k2}(j+k)!}
 \sum_{r=0}^{2^k-1}\tau_r
 \bigl(r-2^k(1+\theta_j)\bigr)^{\,j+k},\;}
\end{equation}
with the convergence, residual and termination properties of
\Cref{thm:fabius-digit-expansion}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Insert \eqref{eq:fabius-appell-centred} with $n=j$ and $\theta=\theta_j$ into
\eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell}.  The two triangular powers of two combine as
$2^{-\binom j2}2^{-\binom{j+1}{2}}=2^{-j^2}$.  This is a termwise replacement by
an exact finite identity, so every analytic assertion is unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Three ways to evaluate $F$]\label{rem:fabius-digit-vs-fourier}
\index{Fabius function!value at a dyadic point}
The monograph now evaluates $F$ by three mechanisms.
\Cref{cor:fabius-centered-formula} is finite, rational and exact, but only on the
dyadic grid; \Cref{cor:fabius-fourier-formula} is global and analytic, an
integral of an infinite sinc product;
\Cref{cor:fabius-digit-qseries} is global and \emph{arithmetic}, each term being
a finite half-base Gaussian sum indexed by one binary digit of $x$.  The three
agree where they overlap: at $x=2^{-n}$ only the digit $b_n$ is nonzero, with
$m_{n-1}=0$ and $\theta_n=0$, so \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-appell} collapses to
$F(2^{-n})=2^{-\binom n2}A_n(0)/n!=2^{-\binom n2}\alpha_n$, which is
\Cref{thm:fabius-half-moment}.

At $x=\tfrac13=0.\overline{01}_2$ the odd digits vanish and
$\theta_j=\tfrac23$ or $\tfrac13$ according as $j$ is odd or even, so the first
six terms contain only three nonzero summands:
\[
 S_2=\frac{13}{72}=0.18055\ldots,\quad
 S_4=\frac{1120771}{6220800}=0.18016509\ldots,\quad
 S_6=0.1801651148015\ldots,
\]
against $F(\tfrac13)=0.180165114801481906955\ldots$: thirteen correct digits
from three terms, where \eqref{eq:fabius-digit-residual} guarantees only
$2^{-15}/6!<4.3\cdot10^{-8}$.  Unlike
\eqref{eq:fabius-inverse-dyadic-closed}, whose conditioning is analysed in
\Cref{rem:dyadic-formula-anatomy}, the outer series here has no cancellation at
all for $x<\tfrac12$ beyond the Thue--Morse signs $\tau_{m_{j-1}}$; the
ill-conditioning of the inner blocks is inherited unchanged, so for numerical
work one evaluates $A_j(\theta_j)$ from
\eqref{eq:fabius-appell-def} and the recurrence
\eqref{eq:fabius-coefficient-recurrence} rather than from
\eqref{eq:fabius-digit-qseries}.
% ed.: the partial sums, the exact residual identity and the termination were
% ed.: verified in exact rational arithmetic; $F(1/3)$ was independently bracketed
% ed.: by $F(5461/2^{14})<F(1/3)<F(5462/2^{14})$ and cross-checked against
% ed.: Gil-Pelaez inversion of the characteristic function to the accuracy of the
% ed.: quadrature ($\approx10^{-10}$).
\end{remark}
