\chapter{Formula atlas}\label{app:atlas}

This appendix is a navigation table, not a substitute for hypotheses and proofs.  Each formula points back to the theorem where its exact domain and derivation are given.

\index{$q$-Pochhammer symbol}
\index{Gaussian coefficient}
\index{basic hypergeometric series}
\index{cyclotomic polynomial}
\index{Jacobi triple product}
\index{Rogers--Ramanujan identities}
\index{Bailey pair}
\index{Jackson integral}
\index{$q$-gamma function}
\index{Lambert series}
\index{cyclic sieving}
\index{Newton interpolation}

\footnotesize
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.23\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.55\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.14\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Name & Formula & Location\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Name & Formula & Location\\
\midrule
\endhead
Concatenation & $(a;q)_{m+n}=(a;q)_m(aq^m;q)_n$ & \Cref{prop:concat}\\[1pt]
Dissection & $(a;q)_{rn}=\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}(aq^j;q^r)_n$ & \Cref{prop:dissection}\\[1pt]
Dissection with remainder & $(a;q)_{rn+u}=\prod_{s<u}(aq^s;q^r)_{n+1}\prod_{s\ge u}(aq^s;q^r)_n$ & \Cref{cor:dissection-remainder}\\[1pt]
Base reversal & $(a;q)_n=(-a)^nq^{\binom n2}(a^{-1}q^{1-n};q)_n$, \(aq\ne0\) & \Cref{prop:base-reversal}\\[1pt]
Negative index & $\GeneralizedQPochhammer{a}{q}{-n}=1/\QPochhammer{aq^{-n}}{q}{n}$ & \Cref{prop:negative-index}\\[1pt]
Infinite logarithm & $\log(a;q)_\infty=-\sum_{r\ge1}a^r/(r(1-q^r))$ & \Cref{thm:lambert-log}\\[1pt]
Gaussian product & $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=(q;q)_n/((q;q)_k(q;q)_{n-k})$ & \Cref{prop:qbinom-products}\\[1pt]
Pascal I & $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=q^k\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}+\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}$ & \Cref{thm:qpascal}\\[1pt]
Pascal II & $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k}{q}+q^{n-k}\GaussianBinomial{n-1}{k-1}{q}$ & \Cref{thm:qpascal}\\[1pt]
Reciprocity & $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=q^{k(n-k)}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q^{-1}}$ & \Cref{thm:qbinom-structure}\\[1pt]
Finite theorem & $(z;q)_n=\sum_k(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}z^k$ & \Cref{thm:finite-qbinomial}\\[1pt]
Reciprocal theorem & $(z;q)_{n+1}^{-1}=\sum_{k\ge0}\GaussianBinomial{n+k}{k}{q}z^k$ & \Cref{thm:reciprocal-finite}\\[1pt]
$q$-Vandermonde & $\GaussianBinomial{m+n}{r}{q}=\sum_k q^{(m-k)(r-k)}\GaussianBinomial{m}{k}{q}\GaussianBinomial{n}{r-k}{q}$ & \Cref{thm:q-vandermonde}\\[1pt]
$q$-binomial inversion & $b_n=\sum_k\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}a_k\iff a_n=\sum_k(-1)^{n-k}q^{\binom{n-k}2}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}b_k$ & \Cref{thm:qbinomial-inversion}\\[1pt]
Infinite theorem & $\sum_{n\ge0}(a;q)_nz^n/(q;q)_n=(az;q)_\infty/(z;q)_\infty$ & \Cref{thm:infinite-qbinomial}\\[1pt]
Euler I & $(z;q)_\infty=\sum_{n\ge0}(-1)^nq^{\binom n2}z^n/(q;q)_n$ & \Cref{cor:euler-infinite}\\[1pt]
Euler II & $1/(z;q)_\infty=\sum_{n\ge0}z^n/(q;q)_n$, $\AbsoluteValue z<1$ & \Cref{cor:euler-infinite}\\[1pt]
$q$-Gauss & ${}_2\phi_1(a,b;c;q,c/(ab))=(c/a,c/b;q)_\infty/(c,c/(ab);q)_\infty$ & \Cref{thm:q-gauss}\\[1pt]
Heine & ${}_2\phi_1(a,b;c;q,z)=\dfrac{(b,az;q)_\infty}{(c,z;q)_\infty}\,{}_2\phi_1(c/b,z;az;q,b)$ & \Cref{thm:heine}\\[1pt]
$q$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz & Terminating balanced ${}_3\phi_2$ product evaluation & \Cref{thm:q-pfaff}\\[1pt]
$q$-Chu--Vandermonde & Two terminating ${}_2\phi_1$ evaluations & \Cref{cor:q-chu}\\[1pt]
Ramanujan ${}_1\psi_1$ & ${}_1\psi_1(a;b;q,z)=(q,b/a,az,q/(az);q)_\infty/(b,q/a,z,b/(az);q)_\infty$ & \Cref{thm:1psi1}\\[1pt]
Finite triple product & $(z;q)_N(q/z;q)_N=\sum_{k=-N}^{N}(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{2N}{N+k}{q}z^k$ & \Cref{thm:finite-jtp}\\[1pt]

Jacobi triple product & $\sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}(-1)^nq^{\binom n2}z^n=(q,z,q/z;q)_\infty$ & \Cref{thm:jtp}\\[1pt]
Rogers--Ramanujan pair &
$\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{q^{n^2}}{(q;q)_n}=\frac1{(q,q^4;q^5)_\infty}$,
$\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{q^{n^2+n}}{(q;q)_n}=\frac1{(q^2,q^3;q^5)_\infty}$
& \Cref{thm:rogers-ramanujan}\\[1pt]
Andrews--Gordon family &
$\displaystyle\sum_{n_1,\ldots,n_{k-1}\ge0}
\frac{q^{\sum_jN_j^2+\sum_{j=i}^{k-1}N_j}}
{\prod_j(q;q)_{n_j}}
=\frac{(q^i,q^{2k+1-i},q^{2k+1};q^{2k+1})_\infty}
{(q;q)_\infty}$, $N_j=n_j+\cdots+n_{k-1}$
& \Cref{qg:thm-andrews-gordon}\\[1pt]
Rogers--Ramanujan fraction &
$\displaystyle\frac{F_1(q)}{F_0(q)}
=\cfrac1{1+\cfrac q{1+\cfrac{q^2}{1+\ddots}}}$
& \Cref{qg:thm-rrcf}\\[1pt]
Pentagonal theorem & $(q;q)_\infty=\sum_{n\in\IntegerNumbers}(-1)^nq^{n(3n-1)/2}$ & \Cref{thm:pentagonal}\\[1pt]
$q$-derivative & $D_qf(x)=(f(x)-f(qx))/((1-q)x)$ & \Cref{prop:qderivative-rules}\\[1pt]
Jackson integral & $\int_0^af(x)\,\JacksonDifferential{q}{x}=(1-q)a\sum_{n\ge0}q^nf(aq^n)$ & \Cref{thm:q-fundamental}\\[1pt]
$q$-gamma & $\Gamma_q(x)=(1-q)^{1-x}(q;q)_\infty/(q^x;q)_\infty$ & \Cref{thm:qgamma-basic}\\[1pt]
Cyclotomic factorization & $\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=\prod_d\Phi_d(q)^{\Floor{n/d}-\Floor{k/d}-\Floor{(n-k)/d}}$ & \Cref{thm:cyclotomic-pochhammer}\\[1pt]
$q$-Lucas & $\GaussianBinomial{ad+b}{rd+s}{q}\equiv\binom ar\GaussianBinomial{b}{s}{q}\pmod{\Phi_d(q)}$ & \Cref{thm:q-lucas}\\[1pt]
$q$-Babbage & $\GaussianBinomial{an}{bn}{q}\equiv\GaussianBinomial{a}{b}{q^{n^2}}\pmod{\Phi_n(q)^2}$ & \Cref{thm:q-babbage}\\[1pt]
Negative upper index & $\GeneralizedGaussianBinomial{-m}{k}{q}=(-1)^kq^{-mk-\binom k2}\GaussianBinomial{m+k-1}{k}{q}$ & \Cref{prop:negative-gaussian}\\[1pt]
Geometric Newton basis & $P(x)=\sum_kc_k(-1)^kq^{\binom k2}(x;q^{-1})_k$ & \Cref{thm:geometric-newton}\\[1pt]
Central reduction & $\GaussianBinomial{2k}{k}{q^2}/(-q;q)_{2k}=(q;q^2)_k/(q^2;q^2)_k$ & \Cref{lem:central-reduction}\\[1pt]
Bernoulli expansion & All-orders expansion of $\log(\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{\EulerE^{-t}}/\binom nk)$ & \Cref{thm:gaussian-bernoulli}\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\normalsize

\chapter{Limit and specialization dictionary}\label{app:limits}

\section{Elementary limits}

For fixed nonnegative integers, the following transitions are direct consequences of the product formulas:
\begin{align*}
 \lim_{q\to1}[n]_q&=n,
 &\lim_{q\to1}(1-q)^{-n}(q^a;q)_n&=(a)_n,\\
 \lim_{q\to1}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=\binom nk,
 &\lim_{n\to\infty}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=\frac1{(q;q)_k}\quad(\AbsoluteValue q<1),\\
 \lim_{q\to1^-}\Gamma_q(x)&=\Gamma(x),
 &\lim_{q\to0}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}&=1\quad(0\le k\le n).
\end{align*}
Each has been proved in the main text; the relevant references are \Cref{prop:classical-poch-limit,cor:qbinom-classical,thm:fixed-column-limit,thm:qgamma-classical-limit,prop:extreme-specializations}.

\medskip
% ed.: the first draft of this paragraph claimed the dictionary recorded no
% ed.: finite-to-infinite transition at all, and conflated the columnwise
% ed.: limit 1/(q;q)_k with the central limit 1/(q;q)_infty.  Both are
% ed.: corrected here.
The dictionary also contains transitions in the size parameter.  For example,
$\lim_{n\to\infty}\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}=1/(q;q)_k$ appears above.  Its central counterpart is
\[
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\GaussianBinomial{2N}{N+k}{q}=\frac1{(q;q)_\infty}
 \qquad(k\in\IntegerNumbers\text{ fixed},\ 0<\AbsoluteValue q<1),
\]
which is \Cref{thm:central-fixed-q} when $k=0$ and follows for every fixed $k$
by the same argument; it is what carries the finite triple product
\eqref{eq:finite-jtp} to Jacobi's \eqref{eq:jtp-minus}.  See
\Cref{thm:finite-jtp,cor:jtp-from-finite}, and compare
\Cref{prop:partition-stabilization}, which records the same stabilization
coefficientwise.

\section{Classical shadows of the main summations}

Under the standard scaling $a=q^A$, $b=q^B$, and $q\to1^-$, terminating basic hypergeometric sums tend to ordinary hypergeometric sums.  The mechanism is
\[
 \frac{(q^A;q)_n}{(1-q)^n}\longrightarrow(A)_n,
 \qquad
 \frac{(q;q)_n}{(1-q)^n}\longrightarrow n!.
\]
Thus:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the finite $q$-binomial theorem tends to Newton's binomial theorem;
\item $q$-Chu--Vandermonde tends to Chu--Vandermonde;
\item $q$-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz tends to Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz;
\item the Jackson $q$-beta integral tends to Euler's beta integral;
\item \Cref{thm:regular-central-sum} tends to \Cref{cor:regular-central-classical};
\item \Cref{thm:singular-central} tends, at the level of its Lambert tail and prefactor, to \Cref{thm:classical-singular-central}.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Roots of unity}

At a root of unity, factorial quotients should be replaced by polynomial formulas before evaluation.  The basic dictionary is:
\begin{align*}
 \Phi_d(q)\mid\GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}
 &\Longleftrightarrow k\bmod d>n\bmod d,\\
 \GaussianBinomial{ad+b}{rd+s}{q}
 &\equiv\binom ar\GaussianBinomial{b}{s}{q}\pmod{\Phi_d(q)},\\
 \GaussianBinomial{n}{k}{q}\bigg|_{q=-1}
 &\text{ is given by \Cref{cor:qminusone}.}
\end{align*}
These statements turn apparently singular substitutions into finite enumerations and are the algebraic basis of the subset cyclic-sieving theorem.

\chapter{Proof-dependency guide}\label{app:dependencies}

The monograph can be read linearly, but several shorter routes are useful.

\section*{Route to Gaussian combinatorics}
\[
 \text{finite products}
 \longrightarrow q\text{-Pascal}
 \longrightarrow \text{finite }q\text{-binomial theorem}
 \longrightarrow \text{partitions, paths, subspaces}.
\]
Read \Cref{chap:pochhammer,chap:gaussian,chap:finite-qbinomial,chap:combinatorics,chap:finite-geometry}.

\section*{Route to product--sum identities}
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \text{infinite }q\text{-binomial theorem}
 \longrightarrow \text{Heine transformation}
 \longrightarrow q\text{-Gauss},\\
 \text{negative indices and bilateral convergence}
 \longrightarrow {}_1\psi_1,\\
 \text{Euler expansions}
 \longrightarrow \text{Jacobi triple product}
 \longrightarrow \text{Bailey pairs}.
\end{gathered}
\]
Read \Cref{chap:infinite-qbinomial,chap:basic-hypergeometric,chap:bilateral,chap:jtp,chap:bailey}.

\section*{Route to arithmetic}
\[
 1-q^n=\prod_{d\mid n}\Phi_d(q)
 \longrightarrow \text{carry criterion}
 \longrightarrow q\text{-Lucas}
 \longrightarrow \text{cyclic sieving and }q\text{-Babbage}.
\]
Read \Cref{chap:cyclotomic}.

\section*{Route to analytic deformation}
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \log(a;q)_\infty
 \longrightarrow \text{Lambert series and dilogarithms}
 \longrightarrow q\text{-gamma}\\
 \longrightarrow \text{central sums and Bernoulli asymptotics}.
\end{gathered}
\]
Read \Cref{chap:pochhammer,chap:qgamma,chap:central,chap:asymptotics}.

\section*{Route to geometric filters and the Fabius bridge}
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \text{reversed Gaussian rows}
 \longrightarrow \text{geometric Lagrange moments}
 \longrightarrow \text{exact coefficient filters}\\
 \longrightarrow \text{dyadic Prouhet cancellation}
 \longrightarrow \text{Fabius--Rvachev sinc products}.
\end{gathered}
\]
Read \Cref{chap:geometric-filters,chap:halfbase-thuemorse,chap:geometric-uniform-fabius}.

\chapter{Formalization status}\label{app:lean-status}
\index{formalization status}

Every mathematical result in this book is intended, eventually, to have a
machine-checked proof in the Lean development that accompanies the Fabius
project~\cite{ReshetnikovProveIt2026}.  The table below is the exhaustive Lean
crosswalk for the \emph{forward} part: it covers the labelled results inherited
from the forward backbone together with all labelled definitions and results
retained from the three general guides.  The branch-aware inverse part has a
separate exhaustive source-to-canonical register in
\Cref{sec:ref-concordance}; the machine-readable
\path{theorem_concordance.csv} records its 260 historical source results.
Finally, \path{source_concordance.csv} maps all 547 result environments from
the five publications merged into this volume, including duplicates and
retired source assertions, to their surviving canonical destinations.  These
three views are complementary: the table below answers the Lean-status
question for each labelled forward result, while the concordances preserve
complete merger provenance without counting a repeated theorem twice.

The classification is deliberately severe, and it is the same rule the
project uses to decide whether a claim may leave its quarantine boundary.
A result counts as formalized only when a current Lean declaration proves
the \emph{exact} statement: same hypotheses, same domain, same
normalization, same conclusion.  A related definition, a theorem with
stronger hypotheses or a weaker conclusion, an executable computation, a
numerical match, or an apparently immediate derivation is \emph{not} an
exact counterpart.  In particular a Lean lemma that appears as a step
inside the proof below does not formalize the result the proof concludes.

\begin{description}
\item[\statusexact] a named Lean declaration proves the same statement; the
  declaration and its module are given.
\item[\statuspartial] Lean has the result only in a special case, at a fixed
  parameter, on a restricted domain, or with a weaker conclusion.  The named
  declaration is the nearest one, and the shortfall is real.
\item[\statusnone] nothing in the development proves it, even partially.
\item[\statusna] the environment states a convention or an interface rather
  than a mathematical claim.
\end{description}

Of the 282 labelled forward results, 64 are \statusexact, 78 are
\statuspartial, 132 are \statusnone, and 8 are \statusna.  The original
191-result pre-Fabius core had 36 \statusexact, 29 \statuspartial, 123
\statusnone, and 3 \statusna{} entries.  The four integrated guide chapters
add 31 human-proved but not Lean-formalized assertions and five labelled
definitions.  The later Fabius bridge contributes the remaining 57 rows.
The formalized part is concentrated exactly where the Fabius development
needed it: the finite Gaussian-coefficient core, the geometric Lagrange and
filter calculus, half-base and Thue--Morse cancellation, and the geometric
uniform law.  The complementary modules
\texttt{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating} and
\texttt{SymmetricFunctionGenerating} now formalize both finite algebraic
halves of the weighted generating-product theorem as formal-power-series
identities; its analytic evaluation and convergence clause is not
formalized.  The finite-family theorem in
\texttt{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics} separately transfers coordinatewise
Big-O bounds through a fixed complete homogeneous degree, without asserting
formal-series convergence.  Nonterminating basic hypergeometric summation,
Bailey inversion and the Bailey lemma, and the Rogers--Ramanujan identities
remain untouched.  The pointwise infinite \(q\)-binomial theorem and its Euler
specializations now have named counterparts, as do the finite and infinite
Jacobi triple products and the pentagonal specialization; the Rogers--Szegő
recurrences remain unformalized.  The algebraic \(q\)-difference annihilation
input to the Bailey chapter is now exact.  The elementary base-reversal,
terminating-factor, and adjacent-ratio
claims are now exact as well: their field forms record \(a\ne0\), \(q\ne0\),
and every displayed nonzero denominator, while their cleared and
cross-multiplied polynomial forms remain valid at roots of unity; both
adjacent cross identities are total in all natural indices by zero extension.
The former
claim that every infinite \(q\)-Pochhammer symbol in
the development was a finite \texttt{Finset.range} product is now false:
\texttt{Fabius.qPochhammerInf} from \texttt{LimitConditionNumber.lean} is the genuine real
\(\prod'_{j\ge0}(1-aq^j)\); for \(0\le q<1\) the factors are multipliable for
every real \(a\), the finite symbols converge to it, and the positive-factor
specializations used for \((q;q)_\infty\) and \((-q;q)_\infty\) are strictly
positive.  This real contractive-base product layer does not formalize the
book's general complex analytic or basic-hypergeometric infinite-product
theory.  The newer \texttt{QPochhammerInfinite} module defines
\texttt{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn} over every topological commutative ring and,
for strict contractions in complete normed commutative rings, proves
multipliability, finite-prefix convergence, concatenation, factor removal,
positive-modulus residue-class dissection, and the exact factor-zero locus
under a multiplicative norm.  It further gives locally uniform convergence
and continuity over complete locally compact normed fields, and over
\(\ComplexNumbers\) gives entireness and an explicit nonzero derivative at
every reciprocal-power zero for \(q\ne0\).  It now also proves actual
derivative nonvanishing directly from every raw factor equation \(aq^j=1\),
including \(q=0\), and analytic order exactly one at every zero.  Its predecessor
\texttt{QPochhammerDissection} proves the finite exact and remainder
dissections over an arbitrary commutative ring.  The separate
\texttt{RvachevPochhammerFactorization} module
defines the complex symbol and proves its pointwise convergence for every
contracting complex nome.  It also identifies every geometric sinc product
at a strict complex contraction with the corresponding nome-\(q^2\)
Pochhammer product, pointwise and globally in the complex argument; only its
final two Rvachev wrappers fix the nome to \(1/4\).  The downstream
five-theorem \texttt{QPochhammerEntire} module now proves locally uniform
convergence in the symbol variable, entireness, the exact division-free
factor-zero locus, the reciprocal-power zero lattice for nonzero \(q\), and
analytic order one at every zero for every strict complex contraction.  The
factor-zero and simple-order results include the boundary \(q=0\).  The
zero-definition, three-theorem
\texttt{GeometricPochhammerNormalConvergence} leaf further proves locally
uniform convergence of the \emph{outer rearranged} Pochhammer-product
right-hand side for every strict complex contraction, its nome-\(1/4\)
Rvachev specialization, and the bounded-Fabius Fourier specialization.  The
combined interface still does not supply the book's named centered/MGF
wrapper or its outside-disk reciprocal, pole-divisor, and zero--pole clauses.
The generic module owns the analytic-order theorem, including \(q=0\);
\texttt{QPochhammerEntire} preserves the legacy naming surface.

symmetric-function rows in this edition were statically checked against the
complete public declaration lists:

The imported \path{QBinomialTheoremInfinite.lean} surface has one definition
and 22 theorems.  In particular,
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_zero_left} is canonically declared in
\path{GaussianBinomialAtOne.lean} and merely made available by that import; it
is not redeclared in \path{QBinomialTheoremInfinite.lean}.

The added finite \(q\)-algebra and geometric layers have the following
exhaustive public surfaces.
\begin{itemize}
\item The four theorems of \path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean} are
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_reciprocity_units},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_reciprocity},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_eq_zero_of_odd_degree}, and
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero}.  Unit reciprocity is
total over every commutative semiring; its nonzero-base wrapper needs only a
semifield.  The two \(q=-1\) consequences are universal commutative-ring
identities, transported from the integer Gaussian polynomial, and the
even-row odd-column statement includes columns above the row by zero
extension.

\item The five theorems of \path{GaussianBinomialAtNegOne.lean} are
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_even},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_odd_even},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_odd_odd},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_neg_one_even}, and
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_neg_one_odd}.  The first three, together
with
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_neg_one_even_odd_eq_zero} from
\path{QBinomialReciprocity.lean}, give all four parity cases of
\Cref{cor:qminusone} exactly.  Every identity uses only a commutative ring;
the Gaussian formulas are total in all natural parameters, including
columns above the row by zero extension, and the last two theorems give the
paired even- and odd-length finite $q$-Pochhammer products without any
domain, characteristic, or nonvanishing assumption.

\item The 13 theorems of
\path{QPochhammerElementaryIdentities.lean} are
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_base_reversal_units},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_base_reversal_units},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_base_reversal},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_base_reversal},
\path{Fabius.prod_pow_sub_pow_eq_finiteQPochhammerIn},
\path{Fabius.pow_mul_finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq_self_div},
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_eq_zero_of_lt},
\path{Fabius.one_sub_mul_gaussianBinomial_one},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_adjacent_mul},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_row_adjacent_mul},
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_adjacent_div}, and
\path{Fabius.gaussianBinomial_row_adjacent_div}.  The two unit reversals,
the terminating numerator, the first-column clearer, and the adjacent cross
identities are commutative-ring statements.  The field reversals require
exactly \(a\ne0\) and \(q\ne0\); the cleared terminating formula and
above-range zero clause require \(q\ne0\), and the terminating quotient also
requires \((q;q)_{N-k}\ne0\).  The adjacent quotients require exactly their
displayed Gaussian and linear-factor denominators, retain \(k<n\), and do not
require \(q\ne0\); the two adjacent cross identities are total in all
\(n,k\), including on and above the row boundary.

\item The zero-definition, two-theorem module
\path{QPochhammerDissection.lean} consists exactly of
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_dissection} and
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_dissection_remainder}.  Both are identities
over every commutative ring, without topology or a condition on $q$.  The
first holds for all natural $r,n$; the second assumes exactly $u\le r$ and
therefore includes the endpoint $u=r$.  Together with the infinite
dissection named below, these declarations formalize all of
\Cref{prop:dissection,cor:dissection-remainder}.

\item The one-definition, twenty-nine-theorem module
\path{QPochhammerInfinite.lean} starts with the total topological
commutative-ring definition \path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn} and its defining
theorem \path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_tprod}.  In a normed ring with
$\lVert1\rVert=1$, the elementary inputs are
\path{Fabius.summable_norm_mul_pow},
\path{Fabius.one_sub_ne_zero_of_norm_lt_one}, and
\path{Fabius.norm_mul_pow_self_lt_one};
\path{Fabius.finiteQPochhammerIn_self_ne_zero} additionally assumes a
commutative ring with no zero divisors.

For a complete normed commutative ring with $\lVert1\rVert=1$ and exactly
$\lVert q\rVert<1$, the seven convergence/factorization theorems are
\path{Fabius.multipliable_one_sub_mul_pow_of_norm_lt_one},
\path{Fabius.hasProd_qPochhammerInfIn},
\path{Fabius.tendsto_finiteQPochhammerIn_qPochhammerInfIn},
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_finite_mul_shift},
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_succ_shift},
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_factor_mul}, and
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_dissection}; only the last adds $0<r$.
Adding a multiplicative norm gives
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_ne_zero},
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_zero_iff}, and
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_self_ne_zero}, including the division-free
factor-zero locus at $q=0$.

The unconditional normed-field support theorems are
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_tprod_smul} and
\path{Fabius.isBigO_one_sub_sub_one};
\path{Fabius.summable_norm_pow_of_norm_lt_one} additionally assumes
$\lVert q\rVert<1$, while
\path{Fabius.differentiable_finiteQPochhammerIn} needs only a nontrivially
normed field.  A complete normed field, $\lVert q\rVert<1$, and $q\ne0$ give
\path{Fabius.qPochhammerInfIn_eq_zero_iff_exists_inv_pow}.  Local compactness
and the same strict contraction are required by
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_qPochhammerInfIn} and
\path{Fabius.continuous_qPochhammerInfIn}.  The cofactor normalization
\path{Fabius.pow_sq_mul_finiteQPochhammerIn_inv_pow_self} is purely algebraic
over a field with $q\ne0$.

Finally, over $\ComplexNumbers$,
\path{Fabius.differentiable_qPochhammerInfIn} proves entireness for a strict
contraction, and
\path{Fabius.hasDerivAt_qPochhammerInfIn_of_mul_pow_eq_one} computes the
derivative from the raw factor equation $a_0q^j=1$, including $q=0,j=0$.
Under $q\ne0$,
\path{Fabius.hasDerivAt_qPochhammerInfIn_inv_pow} identifies the derivative
at $a=q^{-j}$ as
$(-1)^{j+1}(q^{\binom j2})^{-1}(q;q)_j(q;q)_\infty$, and
\path{Fabius.deriv_qPochhammerInfIn_inv_pow_ne_zero} proves this displayed
coefficient nonzero.  The latter theorem literally states coefficient
nonvanishing and uses the preceding \path{HasDerivAt} theorem for its
derivative interpretation.  The division-free theorem
\path{Fabius.deriv_qPochhammerInfIn_ne_zero_of_mul_pow_eq_one} instead states
that the actual derivative is nonzero whenever $aq^j=1$; it includes $q=0$,
where the equation forces $j=0$ and $a=1$.  Finally,
\path{Fabius.analyticOrderAt_qPochhammerInfIn_of_eq_zero} states that every
zero has analytic order exactly one for every strict complex contraction,
again including $q=0$.

\item \path{RvachevPochhammerFactorization.lean} has the one definition
\path{Fabius.complexQPochhammerInf} and exactly nine theorems:
\path{Fabius.complexQPochhammerInf_eq_tprod},
\path{Fabius.multipliable_one_sub_mul_pow_complex},
\path{Fabius.hasProd_complexQPochhammerInf},
\path{Fabius.tendsto_finiteQPochhammerIn_complex},
\path{Fabius.summable_norm_sineTerm_qpow_pair},
\path{Fabius.geometricSincProduct_eq_tprod_pair},
\path{Fabius.geometricSincProduct_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf},
\path{Fabius.rvachevFourierProduct_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf}, and
\path{Fabius.rvachevFourier_eq_tprod_complexQPochhammerInf}.  The complex
symbol is total; its named convergence results require exactly
\(\lVert q\rVert<1\).  For every complex \(q,z\) under that same contraction
hypothesis, the three new theorems prove absolute summability of the paired
Euler perturbations, the paired scale--spectral-zero tprod, and
\[
 S_q(z)=\prod_{k\ge0}
 \left(\frac{z^2}{(k+1)^2};q^2\right)_\infty .
\]
This identity includes \(q=0\), negative and nonreal contractions, and zero
factors, and it has no nonvanishing hypothesis.  The final two factorizations
specialize to the fixed nome
\(1/4\); the Fourier form assumes exactly a bounded Fabius witness and
\path{IsFabius}.  What remains outside the API from
\Cref{thm:qF-spectral} is its named centered/MGF packaging, outside-disk
reciprocal formula, pole divisor, and zero--pole clauses.

\item The zero-definition, three-theorem module
\path{GeometricPochhammerNormalConvergence.lean} consists exactly of
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_geometricSincProduct_complexQPochhammerInf},
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_rvachevFourierProduct_complexQPochhammerInf},
and
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_rvachevFourier_complexQPochhammerInf}.
The first gives locally uniform convergence on the whole complex plane of the
outer nome-$q^2$ Pochhammer product to $S_q$ for every complex strict
contraction, including $q=0$.  The second fixes the dyadic scale and nome
$1/4$; the third transfers that result to the Fourier transform of every
bounded Fabius witness satisfying \path{IsFabius}.  This is precisely the
outer normal-convergence subclaim of the inside-disk formula after its linear
change of variables; it asserts no exterior reciprocal or pole statement.

\item The zero-definition, five-theorem module
\path{QPochhammerEntire.lean} consists exactly of
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_complexQPochhammerInf},
\path{Fabius.complexQPochhammerInf_differentiable},
\path{Fabius.complexQPochhammerInf_eq_zero_iff},
\path{Fabius.complexQPochhammerInf_eq_zero_iff_eq_inv_pow}, and
\path{Fabius.analyticOrderAt_complexQPochhammerInf_of_eq_zero}.
They transfer the generic \path{qPochhammerInfIn} results to the older
\path{complexQPochhammerInf} declaration names; no analytic product,
zero-cofactor, derivative-nonvanishing, or analytic-order proof is duplicated
here.  For every complex \(q\) under exactly \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), the first
wrapper gives locally uniform convergence on the whole \(a\)-plane to
\(a\mapsto(a;q)_\infty\), and the second proves that this function is entire.
The third is the division-free equivalence
\[
 (a;q)_\infty=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists j\in\NaturalNumbers,\;1-aq^j=0,
\]
the fourth gives the literal reciprocal-power lattice \(a=(q^j)^{-1}\) when
\(q\ne0\), and the fifth says that every zero has analytic order one.  Thus
the five theorems cover all of \Cref{thm:poch-entire}; the division-free third
and simple-order fifth theorem also cover the boundary \(q=0\), where the sole
zero is \(a=1\).  The Lean surface asserts nothing for
\(\lVert q\rVert\ge1\), no joint holomorphy in \(q\), closed derivative or
growth formula, or reciprocal meromorphic continuation.  The separate
outer-product leaf above owns the locally uniform convergence over spectral
indices.

\item The final geometric-sinc tranche contributes four public theorems in
\path{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean}, namely
\path{Fabius.hasProdLocallyUniformly_geometricSincProduct},
\path{Fabius.geometricSincProductFactors_multipliable},
\path{Fabius.hasProd_geometricSincProduct}, and
\path{Fabius.geometricSincProduct_differentiable}, and the zero-definition,
four-theorem module \path{GeometricSincCharacteristicFunction.lean} consists of
\path{Fabius.charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricSincProduct}
and
\path{Fabius.charFun_geometricUniformDistribution_eq_phase_mul_geometricReciprocalGamma},
\path{Fabius.tendstoLocallyUniformly_prefix_sinc_charFun}, and
\path{Fabius.tendstoUniformlyOn_prefix_sinc_charFun}.
For complex $q,z$ with $\lVert q\rVert<1$, the first four prove locally
uniform convergence, pointwise \path{Multipliable}/\path{HasProd}, and
entire-ness of
$S_q(z)=\prod_{n\ge0}\SincRad(\pi q^nz)$.  For real
$\AbsoluteValue q<1$, $t\in\RealNumbers$, and $z_q(t)=(1-q)t/(2\pi)$, including $q=0$ and
negative contractions, the first two characteristic-function declarations state
\[
 \varphi_q(t)=\EulerE^{\mathrm i t/2}S_q(z_q(t))
 =\EulerE^{\mathrm i t/2}G_q(z_q(t))G_q(-z_q(t)).
\]
Here $G_q(z)=\operatorname{geometricReciprocalGamma}(q,z)$.
The last two declarations assert local uniform convergence of the full
phase-bearing prefixes on \(\RealNumbers\) and its uniform-on-every-compact-set
corollary.
These are exact subclaims of
\Cref{thm:geometric-uniform-characteristic,thm:qF-spectral}; neither compound
label is promoted, because no declaration packages the former's
complex-frequency full phase-prefix convergence or the latter's centered/MGF
wrapper, reciprocal outside-disk formula, pole divisor, and zero--pole
exchange.  The latter's outer normal convergence is exact through
\path{GeometricPochhammerNormalConvergence.lean} as recorded above.

\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanCoefficient_zero},

quadratic inverse.  The downstream module
\path{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean} connects this algebraic shadow to the
actual smooth inverse through exactly two public theorems,
\path{Fabius.iteratedDeriv_centeredFabiusInv_quarter_eq_quadraticInverse}
and
\path{Fabius.iteratedDeriv_fabiusInv_five_seventy_two_succ}.  For every
bounded Fabius solution they prove the full centered jet identity at
\(5/72=F(1/4)\) and the Catalan formula
\[
 G^{(m+1)}(5/72)=(m+1)!(-4)^m C_m.
\]
These are smooth derivative identities, not convergence of the formal series,
equality on a neighborhood, or analyticity of the inverse at the anchor.

\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_eq_zero},
\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_eq_zero_of_le},
\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_eq_qBinomial},
\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_coeff_succ_eq_qBinomial}, and
\path{Fabius.quarterCatalanRichardsonFilter_firstUncancelled_coeff}.  These are
coefficientwise formal-power-series identities only: no convergence, real
error sign, remainder estimate, or analytic acceleration is asserted.
\end{itemize}
Finally,
\path{Fabius.sum_lagrangeEvalWeight_mul_pow_card_add_zero} in
\path{LagrangeResidualMoments.lean} gives every higher evaluation-at-zero
moment of a nonempty distinct field-valued node family as the negative signed
nodal product times its complete homogeneous function.  Its nonempty premise
is exactly what removes the exceptional empty-row \(0^0\) term.

The theorem statements were checked at arbitrary parameters and
degrees, including the empty and degree-zero boundaries.  Weighted inversion
is now \statusexact.  Weighted generating products and reciprocal finite
products are each \statuspartial: their formal power-series identities are
proved, but their analytic evaluation and convergence clauses are not.  The
nearby \(q\)-Pfaff--Saalsch\"utz summation remains \statusnone{} and is not
inferred from a related finite identity.

\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}mul},
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}mul'},
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}comp\_\allowbreak{}C\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}X},
\texttt{eval\_\allowbreak{}qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}mul}\newline
{\footnotesize\texttt{PolynomialQDerivative}; on polynomials over every
commutative semiring, division-free, with the scaling rule for an arbitrary
scale factor and the difference-quotient characterization
$(1-q)x\,D_qp(x)=p(x)-p(qx)$ over every commutative ring}\\[2pt]
\Cref{thm:q-leibniz} & \statusexact &
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}iterate\_\allowbreak{}mul},
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}iterate\_\allowbreak{}comp\_\allowbreak{}C\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}X}\newline
{\footnotesize\texttt{PolynomialQLeibniz}; on polynomials over every
commutative semiring, from the product rule and the first $q$-Pascal
summation}\\[2pt]
\Cref{lem:qfalling-derivative} & \statusexact &
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}qFallingPower\_\allowbreak{}succ},
\texttt{qDerivative\_\allowbreak{}iterate\_\allowbreak{}qFallingPower}\newline
{\footnotesize\texttt{PolynomialQTaylor}; over every commutative ring, the
iterated form for every $j$ as
$D_q^j(x-a)_q^{\langle m\rangle}=[m]_q[m-1]_q\cdots[m-j+1]_q\,(x-a)_q^{\langle m-j\rangle}$,
division-free, and vanishing automatically for $j>m$}\\[2pt]
\Cref{thm:q-taylor} & \statusexact &
\texttt{qTaylor}\newline
{\footnotesize\texttt{PolynomialQTaylor}; over every field, under exactly the
stated hypothesis $[j]_q\ne0$ for $1\le j\le N$; the formalized proof peels
off the leading falling power $c\,(x-a)_q^{\langle N+1\rangle}$ and inducts on
$N$, rather than invoking a basis}\\[2pt]
\Cref{thm:quantum-binomial} & \statusexact &
\texttt{quantum\_\allowbreak{}binomial},
\texttt{quantumPlane\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}pow}\newline
{\footnotesize\texttt{QuantumBinomial}; every semiring, with $q$ assumed only
to commute with $X$ and $Y$; see \Cref{rem:quantum-binomial-generality}}\\[2pt]
\Cref{cor:quantum-multinomial} & \statusnone & ---\\[2pt]
\Cref{cor:qexp-factor} & \statusnone & ---\\[2pt]
\Cref{prop:qexp-eigen} & \statusnone & ---\\[2pt]
\Cref{thm:q-fundamental} & \statusnone & ---\\[2pt]
\Cref{cor:q-integration-parts} & \statusnone & ---\\[2pt]

normed field including $q=0$}\\[2pt]
